CNPLE Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

Case C001

A 34-year-old woman arrives at a rural walk in clinic reporting two days of right sided pleuritic chest pain and breathlessness on climbing stairs.

She returned nine days ago from a fourteen hour flight and takes a combined oral contraceptive. She speaks in short phrases.

Vital signs are blood pressure 108/68 mmHg, heart rate 118 beats per minute, respiratory rate 26 breaths per minute, temperature 37.2 degrees Celsius, and oxygen saturation 91 percent on room air. Chest auscultation is clear bilaterally and her calves are non tender.

  1. The correct answer is C (Apply supplemental oxygen and arrange urgent emergency department transfer now): hypoxemia with tachycardia and tachypnoea after a long flight on estrogen containing contraception places this woman in the emergent band, where treatment and transfer run in parallel with diagnosis. Oxygen corrects the immediate physiological threat while transport is organized. Imaging and d dimer are unavailable or too slow in a rural clinic and delay definitive care. A next day ultrasound accepts an overnight risk of sudden deterioration that this presentation does not permit.
  2. The correct answer is A (Oxygen saturation of 91 percent with a respiratory rate of 26): objective gas exchange failure is not produced by a chest wall strain, and it moves this presentation out of the routine band regardless of a clear chest and soft calves. Pleuritic quality is common to many causes and does not discriminate between them. A near normal temperature neither supports nor excludes thromboembolism. Estrogen exposure raises pretest probability but is a risk factor rather than evidence of current physiological compromise.
  3. The correct answer is D (Describe the specific risk of death, the alternatives, and help solve childcare): a capable person may refuse, but refusal is only valid once she has received the material risks, the alternatives and the consequences of declining, and the barrier she named is practical rather than a settled value. Treating a socially driven refusal as a fixed decision abandons her. Labelling a clearly reasoning person incapable because she disagrees misapplies the capacity test. Transporting a capable adult against her stated wish is battery.
  4. The correct answer is B (Recent long haul travel, estrogen use, and room air saturation): a handover carries the data that changes the receiving team's immediate decisions, which here are the pretest probability drivers and the objective measure of gas exchange. Remote atopy has no bearing on suspected thromboembolism. A stated preference for a female clinician is worth recording but does not alter urgency or investigation. Attendance history invites a judgment about the person rather than informing acute care.
Case C002

An 82-year-old man with moderate Alzheimer disease lives with his daughter and attends your clinic for a chronic venous leg ulcer that now needs debridement.

He greets you warmly, cannot state the year, and repeats the same question three times. His daughter says she holds power of attorney for personal care and asks you to proceed without upsetting him.

The chart records no capacity assessment and no discussion of his wishes. He says the dressing hurts and he wants it fixed.

  1. The correct answer is B (Assess his understanding and appreciation of this specific proposed procedure): capacity is decision specific and time specific, so it must be tested against the actual treatment proposed rather than inferred from a diagnosis or a chart entry. Alzheimer disease does not by itself remove the ability to consent to wound care. A cognitive screening score measures domains such as attention, executive function, memory and orientation, not the functional consent test, and cannot substitute for it. Arranging an external capacity assessment before doing any bedside assessment yourself delays care that he is asking for.
  2. The correct answer is D (He has capacity for this decision and may consent or refuse himself): he demonstrates both understanding of the proposed treatment and appreciation of the foreseeable consequences of accepting or declining, which is the whole functional test. Disorientation to time is a cognitive deficit, not a legal disqualification. A power of attorney for personal care takes effect only once incapacity for the decision has been established. Capacity found for wound care says nothing about financial or residential decisions, which would each need their own assessment.
  3. The correct answer is A (Obtain his express consent before releasing any result to his daughter): a capable person controls disclosure of his own personal health information, and a family caregiver outside the circle of care needs express permission rather than assumed permission. An attorney for personal care has no authority while the person remains capable for the decision. Practical involvement in giving medications does not create a right of access to the record. A blanket refusal is also wrong, since disclosure is permitted the moment he agrees.
  4. The correct answer is C (Explore with him who should speak for him and what matters most): advance care planning belongs in routine assessment while the person is stable and capable, and it begins with naming a substitute and expressing values rather than with a form. Waiting for an acute admission produces rushed decisions that poorly reflect real values. Recording a relative's preferences replaces his voice with hers. A resuscitation order signed without his articulated goals reverses the proper order of the conversation.
Case C003

A 15-year-old arrives alone at your clinic asking for contraception and says her mother must not find out. She is well, has no chronic illness, and takes no medications.

She answers questions readily, describes a relationship with a 16-year-old classmate, and reports missing several school days recently.

Blood pressure is 112/70 mmHg and her body mass index sits at the 40th percentile for age. Your province applies the mature minor doctrine and sets no fixed minimum age for consent to treatment.

  1. The correct answer is A (Explain confidentiality and its limits, then complete a HEEADSSS interview): stating what stays private and what must be reported before the interview begins is what makes honest answers possible, and the psychosocial screen surfaces the school absences, safety and mood issues that a contraception request can conceal. Contacting a parent without permission breaches confidentiality and ends the therapeutic relationship. Prescribing without the psychosocial assessment treats the request and misses the person. Age alone is not a barrier where the mature minor doctrine applies.
  2. The correct answer is C (She meets the mature minor test and may consent to this treatment): she has demonstrated understanding of the nature of the treatment and appreciation of the consequences of accepting or refusing, which is exactly what the doctrine requires. The province in question sets no fixed age threshold, so a parental permission rule does not apply here. Specialist assessment is reserved for genuinely uncertain capacity, not for youth alone. A countersignature adds nothing legally and would breach her confidentiality.
  3. The correct answer is B (Assess her immediate safety and connect her to youth housing supports): unstable housing and food insecurity are social determinants that predict outcomes more strongly than most clinical variables, and where a young person is sleeping tonight is both a safety question and an actionable one. Bloodwork does not change what happens to her this week. Documenting and waiting three months accepts a season of preventable risk. Contacting her school discloses personal information she has not agreed to share.
  4. The correct answer is D (Whether she now has stable housing and reliable access to food): the intervention with the largest effect on her health was the referral for housing and food, so evaluation has to test whether that referral actually produced a change. A stable blood pressure was never the problem being treated. Pharmacy dispensing records tell you a prescription moved, not whether her circumstances improved. A percentile shift over six weeks is too small and too slow to inform anything.
Case C004

A 7-week-old infant is brought to your clinic by both parents because she has fed poorly since yesterday and feels warm. She was born at 39 weeks after an uncomplicated delivery and her newborn screening was normal.

Today she is sleepy but rousable and has had three wet diapers in twenty four hours. Her rectal temperature is 38.4 degrees Celsius.

Heart rate is 178 beats per minute and respiratory rate is 58 breaths per minute. The anterior fontanelle is soft and flat.

  1. The correct answer is D (Arrange immediate transfer for a full septic workup and parenteral antibiotics): any rectal temperature of 38.0 degrees Celsius or higher in an infant under three months is emergent, and sleepiness with poor feeding raises the concern further. Antipyretics treat a number and delay the workup that decides outcome. A urine sample alone misses meningitis and bacteremia, and oral antibiotics are inadequate for possible serious bacterial infection at this age. Next morning review accepts an overnight deterioration risk that this age group cannot tolerate.
  2. The correct answer is A (Young infants show few localizing signs, so absent signs cannot exclude sepsis): infants of this age present with non specific feeding change, temperature instability and lethargy rather than focal findings, so the absence of classic signs carries almost no negative predictive value. A soft fontanelle is common early in meningitis and cannot rule it out. Consolability is reassuring in an older child but does not constitute a neurological assessment here. Bacterial and viral illness overlap completely in appearance at seven weeks.
  3. The correct answer is C (Establish vascular access and begin an isotonic fluid bolus): delayed capillary refill with tachycardia and reduced intake indicates impaired perfusion, and restoring circulating volume is the intervention that changes physiology within minutes. A lumbar puncture in an unstable infant is unsafe and belongs to the receiving team. Plotting growth is useful surveillance but does nothing for perfusion now. Oral feeding is unreliable in a sleepy infant and carries an aspiration risk.
  4. The correct answer is B (Plot weight on the growth chart and confirm renal imaging is arranged): recovery is judged by feeding and weight trajectory, and a febrile urinary tract infection in a young infant requires the imaging pathway to be completed rather than assumed. Repeat cultures in a well afebrile infant add cost without changing management. Routine long term prophylaxis is not supported after a first uncomplicated episode. A resolved infection is not a contraindication to immunization on the provincial schedule.
Case C005

A 58-year-old First Nations man travels four hours from his home community for a diabetes review. His most recent glycated hemoglobin was 10.2 percent.

He says he stopped one of his pills months ago but is not sure which one, and he also takes something his aunt prepares for him.

He mentions that the pharmacy in town is closed on the day he travels, and that his fridge broke in the spring. He is polite but gives short answers and avoids eye contact.

  1. The correct answer is B (Take a full medication history including traditional preparations and access barriers): he has an unnamed discontinued drug, an unidentified preparation from a family member, a pharmacy he cannot reach, and no working refrigeration, and each of those changes what can safely be prescribed. Repeating laboratory work confirms a number already known and alters nothing. Written education assumes the barrier is knowledge rather than access. Prescribing insulin to a man without reliable refrigeration or pharmacy access sets up a predictable failure.
  2. The correct answer is C (Communication style varies by culture and cannot be read as disengagement): brevity and reduced eye contact carry different meanings across cultures and may also reflect prior unsafe experiences of health care, so reading them as a character trait records an assumption the chart cannot support. Labelling him unmotivated shifts a system access problem onto the person. Mood and cognition each require actual assessment rather than inference from conversational manner. Cultural safety is judged by the person receiving care, not by the clinician.
  3. The correct answer is D (Simplify to a once daily regimen deliverable through the nursing station): a plan is only as good as the person's ability to execute it, and the weekly nursing station contact is the reliable supply route while the town pharmacy is not. A four times daily regimen multiplies the number of doses he cannot obtain. A city referral adds travel he has already told you is a barrier. Leaving glycated hemoglobin at 10.2 percent unchanged for six months accepts avoidable harm.
  4. The correct answer is A (Whether he actually received and took the medication every week): the change made was a supply and simplification change, so the measure that tests it is whether medication reached him and was taken. A target below seven percent is neither realistic in three months from 10.2 percent nor necessarily his own goal. Attendance at a referral that was deliberately not made cannot measure anything. Traditional preparations are worth documenting for interactions, but stopping them was never the treatment goal.
  5. The correct answer is D (Sit back down and assess her immediate safety before she leaves): a doorknob disclosure of feeling unsafe is an acuity question, and whether she is safe to go home tonight cannot be deferred to a future appointment. Rebooking signals that the concern matters less than the schedule, and she may not return. A printed list without assessment neither establishes risk nor makes a plan. A telephone call later leaves the interval between now and then completely unassessed.
  6. The correct answer is B (Interruption narrows the story and hides concerns you must later recover): closing the narrative before it finishes commits you to one hypothesis and pushes the rest of the agenda to the end of the visit or out of it entirely. Framing this as mere discourtesy misses the diagnostic cost of the lost information. Repetition is a symptom of the problem rather than the reason it matters. Forming a working diagnosis in the first seconds is precisely the premature closure an open invitation prevents.
  7. The correct answer is A (Whether similar exertional episodes have occurred over recent weeks): a crescendo pattern of exertional discomfort separates unstable from stable angina and decides whether he goes home or to hospital today, which is the definition of pertinent history. Childhood asthma has no bearing on this presentation. Paternal longevity is too vague to modify risk, unlike a specific premature cardiovascular family history. Colorectal screening status is good preventive care but changes nothing about today's decision.
  8. The correct answer is A (Back pain with urinary retention, saddle numbness and bilateral leg weakness): that combination is cauda equina syndrome, which sits in the emergent band because delayed decompression produces permanent bladder, bowel and sexual dysfunction. A new breast mass needs prompt investigation but the outcome does not turn on hours. A first seizure in someone now fully alert requires same day assessment rather than immediate action. A slow fall in hemoglobin over six months is urgent, and the timeline itself argues against minutes mattering.
  9. The correct answer is C (Obtain an electrocardiogram now and arrange emergency department transfer): a potassium of 6.3 mmol/L with acute kidney injury is life threatening regardless of how well she looks, and cardiac conduction must be assessed immediately while transfer is organized. Repeating the sample tomorrow accepts a night of arrhythmia risk. Stopping the offending drug is necessary but far too slow on its own. Dietary advice addresses chronic intake and does nothing for an acute critical value.
  10. The correct answer is D (Confirm what she knows, then state material risks, alternatives and consequences): informed consent requires that the person actually received the nature of the treatment, its material risks, the alternatives and the result of declining, and checking her existing understanding lets you fill the gaps efficiently. A casual statement that she needs no more information is not an effective waiver and does not relieve you of the disclosure obligation. A signature on a form is evidence of a conversation rather than a substitute for one. Postponing a wanted procedure over information she may already hold is disproportionate.
  11. The correct answer is B (Emergency treatment may proceed, with consent sought as soon as possible): where a person is incapable, delay would risk serious bodily harm, and no substitute is available, the emergency exception authorizes necessary treatment across Canadian jurisdictions. No jurisdiction makes a second signature a precondition of urgent treatment, and where a second opinion is asked for at all, as in British Columbia, it applies only where practicable and never delays necessary care. Waiting for a public guardian during hypoglycemia would cause the exact harm the exception exists to prevent. The exception covers the emergency only, so ongoing care needs consent once capacity or a substitute is available.
  12. The correct answer is C (The spouse, unless separation meets the statutory test excluding him): substitute decision making follows a ranked hierarchy in which spouse or partner outranks child and sibling, and estrangement removes a spouse only if the jurisdiction's definition of separation is actually met. Involvement in care is not a ranking criterion and does not promote a child above a spouse. Disagreement among family does not transfer authority to the clinical team. Absence of conflict is not a legal basis for selecting a substitute.
  13. The correct answer is C (Share the indication, since the pharmacist is within the circle of care): provincial privacy statutes generally permit implied consent for sharing information among clinicians providing that person's care, and a dispensing pharmacist counselling on the same prescription sits inside that circle. Requiring written consent for every internal exchange would make coordinated care impossible. Sending the whole chart exceeds what is needed and breaches the limiting principle. Pushing the paperwork onto the pharmacist misstates where the obligation actually sits.
  14. The correct answer is A (Arrange ambulatory blood pressure monitoring before making a diagnosis): Hypertension Canada directs that out of office measurement confirm the diagnosis when office readings sit in this range, because white coat hypertension is common and a diagnosis commits people to lifelong therapy. Starting a drug on office readings alone risks treating an artefact. Six more months of the same flawed measurement adds delay without adding certainty. Readings consistently near 150/90 mmHg are not normal and cannot be dismissed.
  15. The correct answer is D (Reclassify it as intolerance and prescribe amoxicillin with clear counselling): gastrointestinal upset without rash, angioedema or respiratory symptoms is a known side effect rather than an immune mediated reaction, and leaving the label unchallenged narrows her options for life. Broad spectrum alternatives such as fluoroquinolones carry greater harm and resistance cost. Delaying treatment of an abscess for allergy testing is disproportionate to the history described. Recording an unverified label as confirmed entrenches the error in her record.
  16. The correct answer is B (Welcome the Elder, then ask how she wants the visit to proceed): safety is judged by the person receiving care, and offering choice about who is present and how the encounter unfolds is what Call to Action 22 asks for when it directs the health system to involve Indigenous Elders and healers wherever the person requests them. Citing policy to exclude a support person reproduces the experience that drove her away. Removing the Elder for the examination imposes an assumption at the moment she is most vulnerable. Redirecting her elsewhere transfers responsibility rather than providing safe care.
  17. The correct answer is A (Take an organ inventory and offer cervical screening on the provincial schedule): screening follows the organs a person actually has rather than the sex marker on a card, and a cervix that has not been removed remains at risk. Applying a male schedule would omit screening he still needs. Deferring to a specialist fragments primary care that a nurse practitioner is fully able to provide. Ultrasound does not detect cervical dysplasia and is not a screening substitute.
  18. The correct answer is D (Offer choice about pacing, a chaperone, and deferring to another visit): trauma informed practice assumes any person may have a history and builds the encounter around predictability, choice and control without requiring disclosure. Asking directly about assault at the moment of exposure can retraumatize and is not needed to practise safely. Speed removes her control and makes future attendance less likely. Permanently cancelling screening treats one tense moment as a fixed refusal and removes a needed service.
  19. The correct answer is C (Explore what has given his life meaning and offer spiritual care): existential distress is a distinct domain of suffering that calls for exploration and appropriate referral rather than pharmacological treatment, particularly when depression screening is negative and pain is controlled. Prescribing an antidepressant medicalizes a spiritual question and closes the conversation he opened. Premature reassurance tells him his concern is unwelcome. Escalating opioids for non physical suffering risks sedation without touching the source.
  20. The correct answer is C (Weight has faltered since six months and needs a feeding assessment): the trajectory flattens and then falls across the second half of the first year, which is a crossing of percentile bands rather than normal variation, and excessive cow's milk intake displacing solids is the leading explanation to assess. Being above birth weight says nothing about trajectory. Jumping to a malabsorption workup skips the dietary history that most often explains it. Increased activity does not produce sustained weight loss in a well infant.
  21. The correct answer is B (Explain the harms of testing without indication, then offer proven screening): untargeted panels generate incidental findings, confirmatory cascades, cost and anxiety without improving outcomes, and the useful response substitutes what the Canadian Task Force on Preventive Health Care actually recommends at his age. Ordering on request alone abandons stewardship. Splitting the difference by picking the cheap tests still produces the false positives that drive the cascade. Refusing outright leaves real preventive opportunities unused.
  22. The correct answer is A (A focused respiratory and cardiovascular assessment alongside immediate stabilising treatment): acuity drives the choice, and a person who cannot complete sentences with a saturation of 88 percent needs the systems that explain and complicate his presentation examined while treatment begins. A head to toe examination in respiratory distress costs time he does not have and is itself a harm. Functional and cognitive assessment matter for his longer term plan but not before stabilization. Medication reconciliation is necessary later and cannot precede treatment.
  23. The correct answer is B (Whether the tool performs reliably in this population and setting): validity and reliability are properties of a tool in a specific population, and a measure that performs well in community dwelling adults may misclassify people who are acutely unwell, in pain, or delirious after surgery. Completion time is a practical constraint rather than a measurement property. Family agreement bears on consent, not on whether the instrument works. Comparing to community norms compounds the problem by importing the wrong reference group.
  24. The correct answer is D (A sensitive screen produces false positives, so diagnostic assessment must follow): screening tools generate probabilities rather than diagnoses, and a tool chosen for sensitivity necessarily identifies people who do not have the condition. Treating on a screening score alone exposes her to medication she may not need. Dismissing the result because she looks well discards the very cases screening exists to find. Denial of self harm thoughts is a single item and does not invalidate the instrument.
  25. The correct answer is A (Stop the newly started sedating medication and reassess her insomnia): a tricyclic started at discharge in an older woman with syncope, prior falls and a prolonged QT interval combines anticholinergic burden, orthostatic hypotension and arrhythmia risk, which is the most modifiable hazard on the page. A hemoglobin of 128 grams per litre is normal and needs no iron studies. Echocardiography may follow but does not outrank removing an active harm today. A normal troponin excludes infarction, not arrhythmia or orthostatic syncope.
  26. The correct answer is C (Ask him directly how much he wants to know and who decides): the capable person controls both the information he receives and who participates, so the way through a family request like this is to ask him rather than to accept or override it. Deferring to another clinician avoids a conversation that belongs in primary care. Recording a family refusal misattributes a decision that was never his to begin with. Waiting for another admission guarantees the conversation happens during a crisis.
  27. The correct answer is D (Explore what prompted the question and give accurate information about eligibility): a question is not a request, and understanding what lies behind it, whether fear of choking, loss of control, or unmanaged symptoms, often changes what he actually needs. Refusing to discuss it and routing him to psychiatry pathologizes a reasonable inquiry. Starting formal paperwork skips assessment of what he is really asking for. Nothing in the Criminal Code prevents an informational conversation before any request is made.
  28. The correct answer is B (It must be reassessed now, since capacity is time and decision specific): a person delirious overnight may be fully capable the following morning, and continuing to treat her as incapable strips rights she currently holds. Treating a finding as fixed for an admission ignores the fluctuating nature of delirium. Tribunal review exists for disputes about a finding, not as a routine step to restore capacity. Waiting for a specialist to certify resolution imposes a delay the functional test does not require.
  29. The correct answer is C (Jordan's Principle requires the service now, with payment resolved afterwards): the principle exists precisely to stop jurisdictional disputes from delaying services to First Nations children, and the government of first contact pays and settles the dispute later. Asking the family to navigate both systems places the burden on the people the principle protects. Waiting for jurisdiction to be settled is the exact harm the principle prohibits. Responsibility on reserve is not automatically provincial, which is why the dispute arises.
  30. The correct answer is A (Findings change management directly, including what you prescribe and where you refer): knowing that a person cannot afford a medication, has no refrigeration, or has no fixed address changes the prescription, the follow up interval and the referral, which is the definition of an assessment that alters management. Documenting inequity without acting is surveillance rather than care. No college mandates a specific screening item at every review. Satisfaction is a weak justification compared with a change in clinical decisions.
  31. The correct answer is B (Any medication started or changed in the last two weeks): euvolemic hypo osmolar hyponatremia developing over the same interval as the symptoms points strongly at a drug cause, and selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors, thiazides and carbamazepine are common culprits in this age group. Dietary sodium restriction alone rarely produces a sodium of 121 mmol/L. A normal thyroid stimulating hormone already makes thyroid disease an unlikely explanation. Alcohol matters for other reasons but does not fit the timeline described.
  32. The correct answer is D (Give intramuscular epinephrine into the anterolateral thigh right away): anaphylaxis with airway involvement and hypotension is life threatening, and epinephrine is the only intervention that reverses both the airway edema and the vasodilation. Establishing access first is the most common fatal delay in these events. Antihistamines act on urticaria and have no effect on airway compromise or shock. Salbutamol treats bronchospasm alone and does nothing for laryngeal edema or hypotension.
  33. The correct answer is D (Measure thyroid stimulating hormone while exploring her caregiving and work situation): both a biological and a psychosocial explanation fit this picture, and a single inexpensive test settles one branch while the conversation addresses the other. Attributing the symptoms to strain without testing risks missing thyrotoxicosis, which can present without tremor or goitre. A broad panel of rare endocrine tests generates false positives before the common cause is excluded. Suppressing symptoms with a beta blocker masks the findings that would guide diagnosis.
  34. The correct answer is C (Set the agenda openly and ask what he most wants addressed today): a pattern of repeat visits with a concern raised only at the end is the classic signal of an unspoken agenda, and naming it directly is what surfaces the real reason for the encounter. Deferring again repeats the cycle that has already failed four times. Ordering a sleep study investigates a symptom before the underlying concern is even known. Prescribing a hypnotic treats the stated complaint and closes the door on the actual one.
  35. The correct answer is A (Do not screen, since his life expectancy is below the benefit horizon): colorectal screening takes roughly ten years to produce a mortality benefit while the procedural risk is immediate, so severe frailty inverts the balance. Doing it once still exposes him to bowel preparation, sedation and perforation risk with no realistic gain. Stool testing avoids the procedure but only has value if a positive result would lead to a colonoscopy he should not have. At 88 he already sits beyond the 50 to 74 age range in which screening is recommended, and eligibility by age would not equal benefit for this person in any case.
  36. The correct answer is B (Replace the teratogenic medications and start high dose folic acid now): angiotensin converting enzyme inhibitors and statins are contraindicated in pregnancy and must be switched before conception rather than after a positive test, and diabetes places her in the higher folic acid dose group. The standard 0.4 milligram dose is insufficient for a woman with diabetes. Referral does not remove the primary care responsibility to act on a teratogen today. Waiting for a positive test means exposure occurs during organogenesis.
  37. The correct answer is C (Possible sepsis, so begin resuscitation and arrange urgent hospital transfer): tachycardia, tachypnoea, hypotension and new confusion together indicate an emergent presentation, and older adults frequently mount no fever or even become hypothermic. Attributing the picture to dehydration alone ignores the perfusion failure the numbers describe. None of these findings represent normal ageing. Committing to a urinary source and oral antibiotics narrows the differential before it has been assessed and delays definitive care.
  38. The correct answer is D (Refer urgently for developmental assessment, since loss of skills is abnormal): regression of previously acquired language and social skills is never a normal variant and requires prompt specialist evaluation regardless of how well the child appears. Language plateaus do not involve losing words that were already in use. Waiting for the next routine visit wastes a window in which early intervention has the greatest effect. Screen time advice addresses a possible contributor but cannot account for skill loss.
  39. The correct answer is A (Ask what made attending difficult and document only what is supported): missed appointments most often reflect transportation, childcare, work or cost barriers, and recording an unsupported assumption about substance use follows the person through the system and shapes how others treat them. A drug screen investigates the assumption rather than the barrier and cannot be justified without indication. Copying the previous characterization entrenches the error. Discharge punishes the person for barriers you have not yet identified.
  40. The correct answer is B (Explain why this design cannot support the claim, then discuss proven strategies): critical appraisal is part of assessment, and a small unblinded manufacturer funded trial with a surrogate endpoint and no comparator cannot establish prevention of anything. Endorsing it because harm seems unlikely ignores cost, false reassurance and interaction risk. A blanket dismissal of all supplements is inaccurate and damages the relationship. Deferring the decision to future memory testing implies the evidence would somehow become stronger.
  41. The correct answer is B (Decline and explain that express client consent is required for disclosure): an employer sits outside the circle of care, so disclosure of a diagnosis requires the person's express authorization rather than an assertion of administrative need. Possessing a note that states fitness for work does not entitle the employer to the underlying diagnosis. Changing the format of the disclosure does not change its legality. A request on letterhead is still a request without consent.
  42. The correct answer is D (Explain these findings are common and incidental, and continue active management): disc desiccation and facet arthropathy are present in most asymptomatic adults of this age, and imaging for uncomplicated back pain without red flags is exactly the practice Choosing Wisely Canada advises against because it drives cascades like this one. Surgical referral for incidental degenerative change exposes him to intervention without indication. Repeat imaging tracks findings that do not correlate with symptoms. Further imaging compounds the original error.
  43. The correct answer is C (Start high dose corticosteroid now and arrange urgent ophthalmology review): giant cell arteritis with visual symptoms threatens irreversible blindness within hours, so treatment precedes confirmation and the biopsy stays informative for a short period after steroids begin. Waiting for inflammatory markers accepts a risk that cannot be undone. Anti inflammatory analgesia does not prevent arteritic optic neuropathy. Delaying treatment until biopsy results return is the classic error that costs sight.
  44. The correct answer is A (Ask everyone the same normalising questions as part of routine history): asking the same questions of everyone removes the signal that you suspect this person in particular, and normalising language makes disclosure of borrowed medications, cannabis and alcohol far more likely. Asking only when you suspect something guarantees you miss the people who do not fit your expectation. Deferring to the end of the visit frequently means the questions are never asked. Seeking corroboration from a partner without consent breaches privacy.
  45. The correct answer is D (Review prior investigations and diagnoses relevant to fatigue before examining her): targeted retrieval of what bears on the presenting concern prevents you from repeating tests already done and reveals patterns the person may not recall. Reading chronologically from the beginning consumes the visit and rarely reaches the relevant material. A single recent note gives a snapshot without the trend that makes the data meaningful. Ignoring the record entirely discards years of information and guarantees duplicate testing.
  46. The correct answer is C (Start iron and refer urgently for investigation of gastrointestinal blood loss): iron deficiency anemia in a postmenopausal woman is occult gastrointestinal bleeding until proven otherwise, and replacing iron without finding the source can mask a malignancy while the number improves. Treating with iron alone and rechecking in three months delays the diagnosis that matters. Dietary advice cannot correct a ferritin of 6 micrograms per litre. Transfusion is not indicated in a stable person with a chronic slow fall in hemoglobin.
  47. The correct answer is B (The airway, breathing, circulation and distress you can already observe): the initial assessment is a rapid physiological survey that happens before any history and decides whether this encounter is an interview or a resuscitation. A booking reason describes what he intended to discuss, not his current stability. The problem list is historical and cannot describe the state he is in as he walks toward you. Previous bloodwork tells you nothing about his work of breathing right now.
  48. The correct answer is A (Accept his refusal as valid and document the discussion and reasoning): capacity is not diminished by a psychiatric diagnosis or by disagreement with a recommendation, and he has articulated an understanding of the risk he is accepting. Triggering a capacity assessment because of the diagnosis alone is the discriminatory reasoning the functional test exists to prevent. Asking a psychiatrist to vouch for him treats capacity as a property of the person rather than of the decision. Prescribing over a valid refusal disregards his autonomy.
  49. The correct answer is C (Agree together which issues matter most and safety screen the rest): shared agenda setting with a rapid check for red flags in the deferred items is the only approach that respects both her priorities and her safety within the time available. Covering everything superficially risks doing all four badly and missing something serious behind the cough. Working through them in the order raised gives no weight to urgency or to what matters to her. Choosing the chronic risk factor unilaterally overrides her stated reason for coming.
Case C006

A 31-year-old woman attends your clinic to plan a pregnancy in the next six months.

She has type 2 diabetes treated with metformin and ramipril, a body mass index of 34,

and a first pregnancy that ended at 20 weeks with an open neural tube defect.

She smokes five cigarettes daily. Her most recent glycated hemoglobin was 8.4 percent.

She takes no vitamins and reports no other medications.

  1. The correct answer is C (Replace ramipril with an antihypertensive considered safe in pregnancy): angiotensin converting enzyme inhibitors are fetotoxic and can cause renal dysgenesis, oligohydramnios and skull hypoplasia if continued into pregnancy, so the switch must happen before conception rather than after a missed period. Metformin is generally continued in preconception care and does not carry the same fetal risk. Bariatric surgery would delay conception by well over a year and is not the immediate priority. Smoking cessation matters but nicotine replacement does not need to precede removal of a known teratogen.
  2. The correct answer is A (Poor control at conception raises the risk of major congenital malformation): organogenesis is complete by roughly the eighth week, so hyperglycemia at the moment of conception is what drives cardiac, renal and neural tube malformation risk, and that risk rises steeply as the glycated hemoglobin rises. Waiting while control improves therefore changes outcomes in a way that later tightening cannot. Third trimester control drives macrosomia and neonatal hypoglycemia, which is a different set of harms. Obesity independently raises risk but does not replace glucose as the dominant modifiable factor here.
  3. The correct answer is D (Folic acid 4 mg daily starting now and through the first trimester): a previous neural tube defect affected pregnancy places her in the high dose group, and her diabetes and obesity reinforce that, so high dose supplementation should begin at least two to three months before conception. Starting after a positive pregnancy test misses the window in which the neural tube closes. A dose of 0.4 mg daily is the routine low risk recommendation and is inadequate here. Withholding supplementation until glucose targets are met leaves her unprotected during the very months she is trying to conceive.
  4. The correct answer is B (Confirm the pregnancy and date it, then review all current medications): accurate dating anchors every subsequent screening decision, and a fresh medication review catches anything reintroduced or bought over the counter since the preconception plan was made. Anatomy ultrasound is performed near 18 to 20 weeks and cannot be done at this gestation. Referral without any assessment abandons the preconception work already done and delays the medication check. Monthly glycated hemoglobin without dating leaves the pregnancy unstaged and gives no information that changes care this week.
Case C007

A 5-week-old breastfed infant is brought to your clinic by her mother for a weight check.

Birth weight was 3.5 kg at 39 weeks by spontaneous vaginal delivery.

She weighed 3.2 kg at her two week visit and today she weighs 3.4 kg.

The mother reports feeding every four to five hours, six wet diapers daily,

and two small yellow stools daily. The infant is alert, with normal tone and no jaundice.

  1. The correct answer is B (The feeding interval, since breastfed infants this age feed more often): a five week old breastfed infant typically feeds eight to twelve times in twenty four hours, so a four to five hour interval yields roughly five or six feeds and is the most likely explanation for inadequate intake. Six wet diapers daily is within the acceptable range and does not signal dehydration. An uncomplicated term vaginal birth five weeks ago does not explain current weight gain. Absence of jaundice is a reassuring finding rather than a marker of poor clearance.
  2. The correct answer is D (Growth is inadequate because she has not regained birth weight by five weeks): term infants are expected to return to birth weight by two to three weeks of age, and remaining below it at five weeks is faltering growth regardless of the small rise since the last visit. Any upward movement can look reassuring in isolation, which is exactly why the trajectory rather than a single number is plotted. Breastfed infants do follow the World Health Organization curves rather than gaining slowly overall, so feeding method does not excuse this pattern. Corrected age applies to preterm infants and is irrelevant at 39 weeks gestation.
  3. The correct answer is A (Observe a full breastfeed and arrange lactation support with a feeding plan): painful cracked nipples point to a latch problem, and directly observing a feed is the assessment most likely to identify a fixable cause of low intake. The infant is clinically stable, so a hospital admission for tube feeding is disproportionate and would undermine breastfeeding further. Abandoning breastfeeding before assessing the latch discards a modifiable problem and conflicts with the mother's stated goal. Investigating malabsorption or endocrine disease before excluding inadequate intake reverses the diagnostic order and wastes resources.
  4. The correct answer is C (Two to three days, with concern if the weight has not started rising): faltering growth in a young infant demands a short interval weight check so that a failing plan is detected in days rather than weeks. Waiting four weeks or until the two month immunization visit allows a preventable decline to become severe before anyone acts. Doubling of birth weight is expected near four to six months and is useless as a short term marker. A tight recheck also lets you escalate to supplementation or pediatric referral while the infant is still well.
Case C008

A 15-year-old is brought to your clinic by her father for fatigue and declining school marks

over four months. Her father stays in the room and answers most of your questions.

He reports she has stopped playing soccer, sleeps most of the weekend,

and spends hours alone in her room. She has no chronic conditions and takes no medications.

Her weight is 6 kg lower than at her visit ten months ago.

  1. The correct answer is D (Explain confidentiality and its limits, then interview her alone): time alone with the adolescent is the single change that unlocks disclosure about mood, eating, substance use and safety, and confidentiality must be framed before questioning rather than after. Asking the parent to speak for her repeats the very dynamic that is blocking the history. A take home questionnaire returns no information today and may be seen by the family. Deferring a month leaves a possibly suicidal adolescent unassessed when the presentation already contains weight loss, withdrawal and functional decline.
  2. The correct answer is C (She has passive suicidal ideation, so risk assessment must continue today): wishing not to wake up is ideation, and the absence of a stated plan lowers but does not remove risk, so intent, access to means, protective factors and prior attempts must all be explored now. Treating a denial of planning as an endpoint is how adolescent risk is missed. Restrictive eating alongside significant weight loss is a separate problem requiring its own assessment rather than being folded into the mood diagnosis. Automatic disclosure to a parent before completing the assessment would breach the confidentiality just promised and end the disclosure.
  3. The correct answer is A (Explore what she fears about disclosure and work toward involving a safe adult): the goal is a supported adolescent rather than a technically confidential one, and negotiating who is told, what is told and when usually achieves involvement without rupturing trust. She has already named a trusted aunt, which gives a workable route if a parent feels unsafe to her. Promising unconditional secrecy is not something confidentiality allows when safety is in question. Overriding her immediately and without discussion destroys the therapeutic relationship and is not required when no imminent plan or intent has been identified.
  4. The correct answer is B (She has given away possessions and seems suddenly calm and settled): giving away belongings and an abrupt unexplained calm after a period of distress are classic warning signs that a decision has been made, and they must be treated as escalation rather than recovery. A modest fall in a symptom score is a positive trend and not a danger sign. Conflict about screen time is developmentally ordinary and does not signal suicide risk on its own. Fatigue on returning to sport is expected after months of inactivity and low intake, and it points to reconditioning needs rather than to acute danger.
Case C009

A 3-year-old boy is brought for a well child visit. His parents report he says about twenty

single words, does not combine words, and points to what he wants.

He was born at term and had normal newborn screening results.

He drinks about one litre of homogenized cow's milk daily and eats little meat.

He is up to date on immunizations and walks, runs and climbs stairs without difficulty.

  1. The correct answer is A (His expressive language is delayed and warrants standardized screening now): a three year old is expected to use several hundred words in three or four word sentences understandable to strangers, so twenty single words with no word combinations is a clear delay. Developmental surveillance at every visit escalates to standardized screening precisely when a concern like this appears. Waiting for spontaneous catch up wastes the years in which intervention works best, and sex does not make this pattern normal. Intact gross motor skills are common in isolated language delay and offer no reassurance about speech.
  2. The correct answer is B (Iron deficiency, which is linked to poorer language and cognitive outcomes): cow's milk intake above roughly 500 to 750 mL daily displaces iron rich foods, irritates the gut mucosa and blocks iron absorption, and the resulting deficiency is associated with lasting neurodevelopmental effects. That makes it directly relevant to the language concern already identified. Vitamin D toxicity does not occur from ordinary milk intake and has no link to speech. Lactose intolerance would present with symptoms he does not have, and dietary protein at this level does not cause kidney disease in a healthy child.
  3. The correct answer is C (Limit milk, start oral iron, and refer for speech language assessment): the anemia and the language delay each need their own intervention, and treating only the iron leaves a delay that will not resolve on its own. Restricting milk to about 500 mL daily plus therapeutic oral iron addresses the microcytic anemia at its source. A stable active child with iron deficiency does not need transfusion or hematology, since the cause is already evident. A general multivitamin supplies far too little iron to correct a ferritin of 6 ug/L and does nothing for speech.
  4. The correct answer is D (Refer for audiology assessment and arrange interim early intervention support): hearing loss is the most important treatable cause of language delay and must be excluded formally, since a normal newborn screen does not rule out acquired or progressive loss. A six month wait for speech services is a reason to add community early intervention now rather than a reason to do nothing. Correcting the anemia does not by itself resolve an established delay. Iron should continue for about three months after the hemoglobin normalizes so that stores are replenished, and delaying reassessment to age four wastes critical time.
Case C010

An 84-year-old woman living alone is brought by her daughter after two falls in six weeks.

She uses a cane outdoors and has osteoarthritis, hypertension, insomnia

and mild cognitive impairment. Her medications are amlodipine, hydrochlorothiazide,

zopiclone, naproxen as needed and a daily multivitamin.

She has lost 4 kg over a year and now needs help with shopping and finances.

Her daughter is worried about her staying at home.

  1. The correct answer is C (A medication review with observed gait, balance and orthostatic vital signs): falls in older adults are almost always multifactorial, and medications, postural blood pressure change and gait are the three contributors most often reversible in a single visit. Her list already contains a sedative hypnotic, a diuretic, a calcium channel blocker and a nonsteroidal agent, which makes drug related risk highly likely. Head imaging is not indicated when there is no head injury, anticoagulation or neurological change. Bone density testing addresses fracture consequences rather than the cause of falling, and an untargeted full examination consumes the time the focused assessment needs.
  2. The correct answer is D (Reversible contributors are present, including medication related hypotension): a systolic fall of 32 mmHg with symptoms meets the definition of orthostatic hypotension, and it sits alongside a sedative hypnotic, an abnormal gait and weight loss, so multiple modifiable factors are stacking. The purpose of scoring frailty is to calibrate goals and treatment burden, not to declare a person beyond intervention. Attributing everything to arthritis ignores the measured postural drop and the drug list. A seated pressure that looks unremarkable is exactly what is expected in orthostatic hypotension, so it cannot be used to dismiss the standing value.
  3. The correct answer is A (Taper the sedative hypnotic, which raises her falls and delirium risk): sedative hypnotics are among the strongest medication predictors of falls, fractures and delirium in older adults, and they must be tapered rather than stopped abruptly to avoid rebound insomnia and withdrawal. Antihypertensives also need adjustment given her postural drop, but stopping two agents simultaneously removes any way to tell which change helped or harmed. A multivitamin is not psychoactive and does not drive falls. Substituting a long acting benzodiazepine increases accumulation and daytime sedation, worsening the exact problem you are trying to solve.
  4. The correct answer is B (Assess instrumental activities of daily living and arrange coordinated home support): a structured review of shopping, finances, medication management, transport and meals identifies exactly which supports are needed and is the core of comprehensive geriatric assessment. Difficulty with instrumental tasks alone does not justify institutional care while basic self care is intact and risk has been reduced. Annual scoring without action ignores the current unmet need and the ongoing weight loss. Cancer screening in an 84 year old with frailty and weight loss offers little benefit within her likely lifespan and diverts attention from function.
  5. The correct answer is B (Screening for intimate partner violence, substance use and mental health): these three exposures are common, frequently undisclosed and directly alter safety planning, referral and monitoring, which makes them the highest yield content of a first prenatal visit in a well person. Violence often begins or escalates during pregnancy, so the first visit is the moment to ask privately. Partner cardiac history has no bearing on this pregnancy. Fundal height is not measurable or meaningful at nine weeks, and an exhaustive neurological examination in an asymptomatic woman rarely produces anything actionable.
  6. The correct answer is D (Arrange urgent neurology review to consider a less teratogenic anticonvulsant): valproate carries the highest risk of major congenital malformation and neurodevelopmental harm of the common anticonvulsants, and any switch needs specialist planning plus a period of stability before conception. Seizure freedom reflects good control rather than fetal safety, so continuing unchanged accepts an avoidable risk. Stopping an anticonvulsant abruptly in primary care risks status epilepticus, which endangers both her and a future pregnancy. Waiting until conception misses organogenesis entirely, and she also needs high dose folic acid rather than the routine dose.
  7. The correct answer is A (Proceed with routine gestational diabetes screening as scheduled at this stage): universal screening between 24 and 28 weeks is the Canadian standard, and she is already inside that window, so the dipstick finding changes nothing about timing. Glycosuria in pregnancy is common because the renal threshold falls, and it is neither sensitive nor specific enough to trigger or replace a glucose challenge. A single fasting value misses the postprandial pattern that defines most gestational diabetes. Waiting until 34 weeks leaves too little time to achieve control before delivery and increases macrosomia and neonatal risk.
  8. The correct answer is C (Her weight has crossed downward through percentile bands and needs evaluation): the plotted trajectory rises early and then flattens, so the child drifts across major percentile lines even though every individual measurement is higher than the one before. Sustained downward crossing of two or more bands is the definition of faltering growth and calls for a feeding, caloric and medical assessment. An absolute increase between visits is reassuring only when the curve keeps its shape. Introducing solids should not slow the curve, and a diagnosis of faltering growth rests on a trend rather than on any single plotted point.
  9. The correct answer is C (Refer immediately for a full septic workup and parenteral antibiotic therapy): any temperature of 38.0 degrees Celsius or higher in an infant under three months is emergent, because reliable clinical signs of serious bacterial infection are absent at this age and deterioration can be sudden. A well appearing infant carries almost no negative predictive value in this age group. A household viral contact does not exclude concurrent bacteremia, urinary tract infection or meningitis. Sending urine alone or waiting until the next day accepts a delay that can prove fatal, and antipyretics treat the number rather than the cause.
  10. The correct answer is B (Measure serum bilirubin and plot it against the hour specific nomogram): visual estimation of jaundice is unreliable, and risk can only be judged by plotting a measured value against the infant's exact age in hours to decide on phototherapy or escalation. Jaundice reaching the abdomen suggests a level well above the reassurance threshold. Sunlight exposure is ineffective, carries burn and hypothermia risk, and delays definitive assessment. Interrupting breastfeeding is not a first line diagnostic step, and assuming physiological jaundice without a number is how kernicterus is missed in an infant discharged before any measurement.
  11. The correct answer is D (Use the Rourke Baby Record to guide growth, development and anticipatory guidance): it is the Canadian evidence based well baby tool, and it sets out what to cover at each visit from growth plotting and nutrition through development, immunization and safety guidance. Working only from parental concerns systematically misses the surveillance items that parents do not know to raise. A full physical examination at every visit adds little yield once the newborn examination is complete and displaces the history that matters more. Routine bloodwork in a well infant produces false positives and cascading tests rather than useful information.
  12. The correct answer is A (Refer for formal audiology assessment despite the newborn screening result): a normal newborn screen does not exclude acquired, progressive or late onset hearing loss, and parental report of no response to voice or loud sound is one of the strongest indicators of significant loss. Early identification and amplification within the first year protects language development. Watchful waiting for two more months squanders that window when the concern is already specific. Speech therapy without hearing assessment treats the symptom while leaving the cause unaddressed, and sedated imaging is not a first line investigation for suspected deafness.
  13. The correct answer is D (Weight has plateaued since twelve months, so nutrition needs formal assessment): gaining only 300 g across a full year in the second year of life is a flat curve that has already crossed downward through percentile bands, and grazing on sweetened juice is a well recognized cause. A dietary history, iron studies and a search for an underlying medical cause are all warranted. Weight velocity does slow after infancy, but it does not stop. No plotted point is lower than the one before, so this is not weight loss, and a weight trend alone is enough to identify the problem even before length is added.
  14. The correct answer is B (Refer urgently for developmental assessment, since skill loss is never normal): loss of previously acquired language and social gestures is a red flag that requires prompt evaluation for autism spectrum disorder, hearing loss, seizure disorder and neurodegenerative and metabolic conditions. Behavioural regression after a new sibling may affect toileting or sleep, but it does not erase established communication milestones. Iron studies are reasonable in parallel and never sufficient on their own. Watchful waiting with more play delays diagnosis of a condition where the earliest intervention produces the largest functional gain.
  15. The correct answer is C (Housing stability, food security and exposure to adverse childhood experiences): repeated caries, recurrent infection, housing instability, parental exhaustion and police attendance form a pattern in which social determinants are driving the clinical picture, and identifying them opens referral pathways that treatment alone cannot. Adverse childhood experiences predict later physical and mental illness, so recognizing them now is preventive care. Diet and brushing matter but sit downstream of food insecurity and housing chaos. Hearing testing and immunization review remain part of routine care yet neither addresses the force generating the repeated presentations.
  16. The correct answer is A (Build a catch up schedule using the Canadian Immunization Guide and this record): written records from other countries are accepted as valid, and the Guide provides age appropriate catch up intervals for children whose immunization is incomplete or started early. A measles dose given at nine months does not count toward the routine series in Canada and must be repeated. Restarting everything exposes the child to unnecessary injections and delays protection. Blanket serology is costly, poorly standardized for several antigens, and unnecessary when a documented record exists, and treating the record as complete leaves him underimmunized entering group care.
  17. The correct answer is B (School performance, physical activity, screen time, sleep, bullying and body image): the Greig Health Record structures visits for ages 6 to 17 and shifts the focus from infant physical parameters to school, behaviour, activity and psychosocial wellbeing, with blood pressure interpreted against age, sex and height percentiles. Feeding and fontanelle assessment belong to the Rourke Baby Record and the first years of life. Driving and advance care planning are older adult concerns. Cervical screening and preconception counselling apply to adults and are not part of a nine year old's periodic health visit.
  18. The correct answer is D (The reading is unreliable, so repeat it with a correctly sized cuff first): a bladder that is too narrow or too short falsely elevates the recorded pressure, so no interpretation is possible until the measurement is repeated properly. Pediatric blood pressure is then judged against age, sex and height percentile tables rather than adult cutoffs, and being tall raises the threshold for this boy. Diagnosing hypertension in children requires elevated readings at three separate visits, so treatment after one measurement is premature. Ordering imaging for a technically invalid reading commits resources before the basic measurement has been repeated.
  19. The correct answer is C (Length has drifted downward across bands, so further evaluation is warranted): starting near the median and falling progressively below it while weight holds its band is linear growth failure, which points toward endocrine, skeletal, genetic or chronic disease causes rather than simple undernutrition. Where undernutrition is the driver, weight usually falters before length. Continued absolute growth does not exclude a falling velocity, which is precisely what percentile crossing detects. Familial short stature is a diagnosis of exclusion that requires measured parental heights and a mid parental target, and growth hormone testing is not a first line investigation.
  20. The correct answer is A (Explore school, peer relationships and sleep before ordering any investigations): pain confined to school mornings in a thriving child with a normal examination is functional abdominal pain until proven otherwise, and bullying, anxiety, learning difficulty and poor sleep are the findings that actually change management. Investigating first produces incidental findings, anxiety and a cascade of confirmatory tests without improving outcomes. Endoscopy is invasive and unjustified in the absence of red flags such as weight loss, nocturnal pain, vomiting or bleeding. Acid suppression treats a diagnosis that has not been established and delays the useful conversation.
  21. The correct answer is A (That the conversation is confidential, with limits you explain in advance): naming the boundaries before disclosure, including risk of serious self harm, harm to others and suspected abuse, lets the adolescent decide what to share with full knowledge and protects the relationship if a limit is later reached. Promising absolute secrecy is untrue and collapses the moment a safety concern appears. Age alone does not remove confidentiality, since the mature minor doctrine governs in the common law provinces and Quebec sets a statutory age of fourteen for consent to care. Deferring the explanation until after she speaks means she disclosed without knowing the rules, which is the situation this conversation exists to prevent.
  22. The correct answer is C (Begin with questions about home life, school and daily activities): the HEEADSSS framework is deliberately ordered from lower risk to higher risk domains so that rapport is established before sexuality, substance use and suicidality are raised. Opening with the highest risk topics in a first encounter typically produces guarded or false reassurance that is worse than no answer. Sexual history, substance use and suicide screening all remain essential, but they belong later in the same interview. Sequencing is what determines whether those later answers can be believed.
  23. The correct answer is B (Assess her capacity for this decision and prescribe if she is capable): most Canadian jurisdictions apply the mature minor doctrine, under which a young person who understands the nature and consequences of a specific treatment may consent to it independently, and capacity is judged decision by decision rather than by birthdate. Her account of the method, her reasons and the side effects she is weighing is direct evidence of understanding. Demanding parental consent where the law does not require it creates a barrier that increases unintended pregnancy. Restricting her to condoms or deferring a year substitutes an arbitrary age rule for the required capacity assessment.
  24. The correct answer is D (Assess for an eating disorder and medical instability before clearing her): amenorrhea, rapid percentile loss and marked bradycardia together suggest relative energy deficiency in sport with possible restrictive eating, and bradycardia at this level can reflect medical instability requiring urgent care rather than fitness. Vital sign, electrolyte and electrocardiographic assessment must precede any clearance decision. Assuming athletic adaptation is the error that allows these presentations to progress to arrhythmia and collapse. Pregnancy and thyroid testing are reasonable parts of the workup but do not replace the eating disorder assessment or the safety decision.
  25. The correct answer is B (Catch up growth has occurred, so support can taper with continued monitoring): the curve turns upward from three months and climbs steadily back across percentile bands, which is the definition of catch up growth and evidence the plan worked. Tapering with ongoing weight checks guards against relapse once support is withdrawn. Sitting below the median is not failure, since the healthy range spans a wide band and his own trajectory is what matters. Referral for malabsorption testing is not indicated in a child who is gaining well, and describing the curve as unchanged misreads a clear upward crossing.
  26. The correct answer is C (Explain the screening schedule and the harms of testing before that age): transient human papillomavirus infection is very common in the early twenties and clears spontaneously, so screening before the programme start age mostly detects changes that would resolve, leading to colposcopy, excisional treatment and later obstetric risk without reducing cancer deaths. Explaining that reasoning respects her autonomy while declining an intervention that would harm her. Age of first intercourse does not shift the start age in any Canadian provincial or territorial programme. Screening remains active in every province, so telling her it has ended is simply false.
  27. The correct answer is A (Offer sexually transmitted and blood borne infection testing based on his risk): chlamydia and gonorrhea are frequently asymptomatic in men yet still transmissible and capable of causing complications, and human immunodeficiency virus, syphilis and hepatitis testing is indicated by his reported exposure history. Screening asymptomatic people with risk factors is exactly what these programmes exist for. Waiting for symptoms guarantees ongoing transmission and later presentation. Broad autoimmune panels in a well person generate false positives with no benefit, which is the opposite of the targeted testing his history supports.
  28. The correct answer is D (Apply a brief validated alcohol screen and act on the resulting score): a three item instrument such as the AUDIT-C takes about a minute, has known sensitivity and specificity for hazardous drinking, and produces a score that tells you whether a fuller assessment is warranted. Liver enzymes and red cell indices are insensitive and normal in most people drinking at hazardous levels, so a normal result would falsely reassure. A single question about family complaints is neither validated nor reliable in someone already minimizing. Deferring everything to a future long appointment loses the opportunity while he is in front of you.
  29. The correct answer is A (Caregiver burden, respite options and her own gaps in preventive care): caregiving load peaks in middle adulthood and predicts burnout, depression, somatic symptoms and neglected personal health, and the missed screening appointments are the clue that her own care is being displaced. Naming the burden and connecting her to respite and community supports addresses the cause rather than the symptom. Neuroimaging for tension type headache without red flags produces incidentalomas and anxiety. Repeating already normal bloodwork adds no information, and a sleep study investigates a condition her history does not suggest.
  30. The correct answer is B (Sustained hypertension is present, so evaluate for causes and target obesity): three separate visits with correctly measured readings above the 95th percentile for a twelve year old of median height meets the pediatric definition of hypertension, and the body mass index percentile identifies the most likely driver. Evaluation should include renal function, urinalysis, lipids and glucose alongside intensive lifestyle intervention, with echocardiography reserved for the point at which drug therapy is being considered. Adult thresholds do not apply at this age, so calling the readings normal misclassifies him. Repeated elevation across visits argues against a white coat effect, and drug therapy is not first line before secondary causes and lifestyle are addressed.
  31. The correct answer is D (Measure a lipid profile and glucose, then calculate his cardiovascular risk): screening lipids and glycemia in a smoker over forty with elevated blood pressure feeds a validated risk calculator, and the resulting estimate is what determines whether statin therapy is justified, while his repeated readings of 142 over 90 already meet the threshold for antihypertensive treatment on their own. This is the cheapest test that will actually change management. Anatomical and functional cardiac testing in an asymptomatic person generates false positives, radiation exposure and invasive follow up without improving outcomes. Reassurance is unsafe, because sudden cardiac death or infarction is the first manifestation of coronary disease in a substantial minority.
  32. The correct answer is C (Discuss the benefits and harms so she can make an informed choice): screening in the fifties is a preference sensitive decision in which the modest mortality benefit is balanced against false positives, additional imaging, biopsy and overdiagnosis, so shared decision making is the recommended approach rather than an automatic order or refusal. Presenting absolute numbers rather than relative risk helps her weigh the trade off. Provincial programmes do offer screening in this age range, so refusing outright is wrong. Ultrasound is not a population screening tool and raises the false positive rate rather than lowering it.
  33. The correct answer is B (A focused genitourinary assessment with a brief medication and allergy review): a well defined acute problem in a person whose history you already know calls for a targeted assessment of the presenting system plus the few extras that change prescribing, namely current medications, allergies and renal function. Treating comprehensive as automatically safer wastes time and displaces higher acuity work without improving accuracy. Skipping the medication and allergy check to save time is the omission that causes harm here. Deferring a treatable acute infection risks progression to pyelonephritis while she waits.
  34. The correct answer is A (Use a tool validated in the population and setting you are assessing): sensitivity, specificity and predictive values are properties of a tool within a specific population and prevalence, so an instrument developed on acutely unwell inpatients can misclassify community dwelling older adults in either direction. Choosing an instrument validated for ambulatory older people keeps the score meaningful for the decision at hand. Assuming interchangeability across settings is the error being tested. Walking in unaided says nothing about fatigue, weight loss, grip strength or activity level, which is precisely why a structured measure is used.
  35. The correct answer is C (Assess his gait and balance, then advise strength and balance exercise): a single non injurious fall with an identifiable situational cause and no gait abnormality warrants a brief gait and balance evaluation, and progressive strength and balance training is the intervention with the strongest evidence for preventing further falls. Multifactorial falls clinic assessment is reserved for recurrent falls, injury or an abnormal gait, none of which he has. Head imaging without head injury or neurological change adds cost and radiation only. Telling him to move house addresses neither his balance nor his fracture risk.
  36. The correct answer is D (Treatment intensification has produced orthostatic hypotension and warrants review): the record shows sitting pressure falling steadily while the standing value falls much further, so by the third visit there is a symptomatic postural drop of 20 mmHg systolic that appeared as the regimen was intensified. In an eighty one year old, that pattern predicts falls, fracture and loss of independence, and it calls for dose reduction rather than escalation. Adding another agent would deepen the drop. Deconditioning and weight loss may contribute, yet neither explains a postural gradient that widened in step with the medication changes.
  37. The correct answer is D (Use a brief validated cognitive screen and arrange fuller testing if abnormal): an instrument such as the Mini-Cog takes a few minutes, has acceptable performance for detecting cognitive impairment in primary care, and gives a defensible basis for deciding whether to proceed to more detailed testing. Screening results are probabilities rather than diagnoses, so an abnormal score triggers assessment rather than ending it. Full neuropsychological testing takes hours and is not deliverable in a clinic appointment. Imaging before any cognitive measurement inverts the workup, and an unstructured family rating is neither valid nor reproducible.
  38. The correct answer is B (Assess fitness to drive and follow your provincial reporting obligations): getting lost on familiar routes and repeated collisions are markers of impaired driving safety in dementia, and most Canadian provinces impose a mandatory or discretionary duty to report drivers who may be unsafe. The duty runs to the licensing authority and to public safety, and it cannot be waived by a family member's preference. Restricting to daylight and local roads does not address the navigational failure that caused the events. Waiting months for testing leaves an unsafe driver on the road in the meantime.
  39. The correct answer is A (Explain that the benefit takes longer to appear than her likely lifespan): mortality benefit from colorectal screening emerges only after roughly ten years, while bowel preparation, sedation and perforation risk are immediate, so the balance clearly favours declining in someone with severe frailty and advanced organ failure. Framing the reasoning in terms of time to benefit respects her autonomy and lets her decide. Rising incidence with age does not translate into benefit when competing mortality dominates. Stool testing simply relocates the same problem, because a positive result would lead to the colonoscopy she should not have.
  40. The correct answer is C (His instrumental activities of daily living, which are the tasks now failing): cooking, banking, medication management, shopping, transport and housekeeping are the domains that decline first, and mapping exactly which ones have failed tells you what services to arrange and where safety risk currently sits. Unreliable pill taking in particular demands an immediate solution such as blister packing or supervision. Basic self care is preserved and therefore generates no new plan. A frailty score from a past admission is stale and setting specific, and a weight trend measures nutrition rather than the functional gap driving the referral.
  41. The correct answer is B (Investigate for gastrointestinal blood loss and review her anti inflammatory use): the pattern of low hemoglobin, low mean corpuscular volume and a ferritin of 8 ug/L is unambiguous iron deficiency, and in an older adult that requires a search for a gastrointestinal source rather than treatment alone. Daily nonsteroidal use is both a likely cause of occult bleeding and a modifiable one, so it must be addressed at the same time. Replacing iron without investigating can mask a colorectal cancer for months. Thyroid function is already normal, and calling significant iron deficiency an age related change is never correct.
  42. The correct answer is D (Explain how false positives lead to further testing, and offer indicated screening): with any broad panel the chance that at least one result falls outside the reference interval in a healthy person is high, and each of those results generates repeat tests, imaging, referral, cost and anxiety without improving health. Choosing Wisely Canada names routine annual bloodwork in asymptomatic adults as low value care. Redirecting her toward screening that is actually indicated for her age and risk gives her something useful. Ordering tests you have no intention of acting on is indefensible, and refusing to engage abandons the conversation that changes her mind.
  43.
