The correct answer is C (Explain that imaging without red flags does not improve outcomes, and treat): uncomplicated mechanical back pain of a few weeks resolves in most people, and early imaging finds degenerative changes that are equally common in people without pain, leading to more procedures and worse outcomes rather than better ones. Active management with education, analgesia and continued movement is what actually helps. Plain films deliver gonadal radiation and even less useful information. Referring to surgery to outsource the imaging decision adds a long wait and legitimizes an intervention that is not indicated.
  44. The correct answer is A (A sensitive screen is chosen to rule out, accepting some false positives): when the cost of a missed case is high, as with postpartum depression and its risk to parent and infant, the tool is deliberately tuned to catch nearly everyone affected, and the price is a group of well people who screen positive and need a follow up interview. Your colleague has described the intended trade off rather than a flaw. High sensitivity does not confer few false positives, since those two properties move in opposite directions. Prioritizing specificity in a potentially fatal condition inverts the logic, and no screening score is diagnostic by itself.
  45. The correct answer is D (Select an instrument validated in older adults and administer it verbally): tools such as the Geriatric Depression Scale were developed for this population, use simpler response formats and place less weight on somatic items that overlap with normal ageing and chronic disease. Administering the questions aloud, with amplification if needed, accommodates hearing loss without altering the content. Presentation genuinely differs in later life, so reusing a working age self report instrument unchanged risks systematic misclassification. Proxy completion measures the family member's perception, and skipping screening abandons a treatable condition that is both common and underdetected here.
  46. The correct answer is B (Reviewing medications and investigating the postural drop and weight loss): the report shows the consulting service has already arranged home safety, nutrition and cognitive follow up, so the items left unclaimed are the symptomatic orthostatic drop and the unexplained 7 kg loss, both of which sit squarely in primary care. Medication review is the intervention most likely to reverse the postural drop and reduce further falls. Duplicating the occupational therapy or memory referrals wastes scarce appointments and confuses the patient. Repeating a mobility test that has just been performed generates no information that changes the plan.
  47. The correct answer is A (A focused respiratory and cardiovascular assessment with immediate stabilizing measures): inability to speak in full sentences with tachypnoea and accessory muscle use signals a life threatening presentation, so airway, breathing and circulation with oxygen, positioning, monitoring and access take priority over any comprehensive process. Assessment and treatment proceed in parallel rather than in sequence. A thirty minute head to toe examination in a person in respiratory distress causes harm through delay. Pharmacy reconciliation and social history are valuable once he is stabilized, and a targeted medication question can be asked while treatment is under way.
  48. The correct answer is C (A comprehensive assessment including a full medication history and function): a new patient with several interacting conditions, an unexplained decline and a bag of medications is the classic indication for a full history and head to toe examination, and the medication history alone often reveals interactions, duplications and adherence problems driving the deterioration. Function, mood and social circumstances complete the picture. Choosing one system in advance assumes a cause that has not been established. Ordering tests before taking a history means the panel is untargeted, and rationing to one problem per visit leaves the interactions between her conditions unexamined.
  49. The correct answer is B (Assessing his health knowledge, self management skills and consent capacity): successful transition depends on knowing what this young adult understands about his condition, medications, bladder and bowel routine and warning signs, and on establishing that he is now the decision maker unless capacity is lacking. That assessment defines the teaching and support plan. Repeating imaging without a clinical question exposes him to cost and radiation for no benefit. His mother cannot consent for a capable adult, and delaying transition until he is fully independent leaves him without an adult provider during the years when complications accumulate.
Case C011

A 22-year-old university student presents to your primary care clinic with two

days of sore throat and subjective fever. She reports pain on swallowing and

fatigue. She denies cough, rhinorrhea and hoarseness. Temperature is 38.4

degrees Celsius, heart rate 96, respiratory rate 16, blood pressure 112/70 mmHg.

She takes a combined oral contraceptive and has no drug allergies. She lives in

residence, where two roommates have had similar symptoms this week.

  1. The correct answer is C (Tender anterior cervical adenopathy and tonsillar exudate or tonsillar swelling): the modified Centor score awards one point each for fever above 38 degrees Celsius, absence of cough, tender anterior cervical adenopathy, and tonsillar exudate or swelling. Posterior chain nodes point toward infectious mononucleosis, while palatal petechiae can accompany either streptococcal pharyngitis or mononucleosis, and neither finding carries any scoring weight. Splenomegaly with a rash also points toward mononucleosis rather than streptococcal risk. Trismus and uvular deviation are red flags for peritonsillar abscess, which overrides scoring altogether.
  2. The correct answer is A (Score of 4 with no age adjustment, so swab and treat by result): fever, absent cough, tender anterior nodes and tonsillar exudate each earn one point, and age 22 neither adds nor subtracts. Only ages 3 to 14 gain a point and only age 45 and older lose one, so no adjustment applies here. Choosing Wisely Canada advises against antibiotics for pharyngitis without confirmed group A streptococcus, so even a high score favours swabbing first. Reassurance without any testing belongs to scores of 0 or 1.
  3. The correct answer is D (Direct her to emergency now for urgent airway assessment and drainage): trismus, a muffled voice, unilateral pain and drooling describe peritonsillar abscess or a deep neck space infection, which threatens the airway. These red flags outrank any score, so repeat swabbing adds nothing and costs time. Oral antibiotics alone cannot decompress a collection and delay definitive care. Symptomatic home advice is unsafe once drooling and trismus are present.
  4. The correct answer is B (Even a high score is wrong about half the time, so confirmation guides treatment): even the highest score an adult can reach predicts group A streptococcus in only about half of cases, so Choosing Wisely Canada advises confirming infection before prescribing. Antibiotics are still given when the swab is positive, so a blanket refusal misstates the guidance. Allergy prevalence is not the reason testing precedes treatment. Abscess is uncommon and occurs even in treated clients, so it does not justify empiric prescribing.
Case C012

A 34-year-old carpenter inverted his right ankle stepping off a curb ninety

minutes ago. He walked into your clinic with a limp. He reports pain on the

outer side of the ankle with swelling. He has no other injuries, takes no

medications, and has never fractured this ankle. He is afebrile with normal

vital signs and removed his own work boot without assistance.

  1. The correct answer is D (Tenderness at either malleolar posterior edge or tip, plus four step inability): the malleolar component requires bone tenderness along the posterior edge or tip of either malleolus, or inability to bear weight for four steps both immediately after injury and in the assessment setting. Ligament tenderness is soft tissue and is deliberately excluded from the rule. The fifth metatarsal base belongs to the midfoot component for foot radiography. Achilles findings sit outside both Ottawa components entirely.
  2. The correct answer is B (Rule negative, so treat as a sprain with protected weight bearing): no bone tenderness at either malleolus and the ability to take four or more steps make the malleolar component negative, and absent midfoot tenderness makes the foot component negative. The rules are highly sensitive by design, so a negative result reliably excludes clinically significant fracture. Painful but successful ambulation still counts as bearing weight, so the rule remains applicable. Deferring the decision three days leaves him without a plan.
  3. The correct answer is A (The rules miss almost no fractures, so imaging now would change nothing): the Ottawa rules were derived and validated to be near perfectly sensitive, accepting low specificity so that a negative result is safe. Choosing Wisely Canada targets exactly this scenario, since routine films after a negative rule add cost and false reassurance without altering treatment. Low radiation dose does not justify a test that cannot change management. Withholding the film is a clinical judgement, not a scheduling or paperwork issue.
  4. The correct answer is C (Order foot radiographs, since midfoot bone tenderness is now present): the Ottawa Foot Rule indicates radiography for midfoot zone pain with bone tenderness at the base of the fifth metatarsal or the navicular. A rule applied at one visit does not immunise later visits, and new bony tenderness is exactly the trigger it describes. Continuing rehabilitation ignores a finding that may represent a missed or stress fracture. Advanced imaging and specialist referral follow a plain film rather than replacing it.
Case C013

A 71-year-old woman with type 2 diabetes and hypertension reports six weeks of

increasing breathlessness climbing stairs and ankle swelling that worsens by

evening. She takes metformin, amlodipine and atorvastatin. She sleeps on two

pillows, unchanged from a year ago. Blood pressure is 146/88 mmHg, heart rate 84

and regular, respiratory rate 20, oxygen saturation 95 percent on room air, and

temperature 36.8 degrees Celsius.

  1. The correct answer is B (Vertical height of the internal jugular venous pulsation above the sternal angle): jugular venous pressure reflects right sided filling pressure, so an elevated column supports a cardiac cause while a normal column points toward venous disease, drug effect or hypoalbuminemia. Hemosiderin staining suggests chronic venous insufficiency but does not exclude coexisting heart failure. Symmetry and pitting occur in all three causes and therefore cannot discriminate. A walk test measures functional limitation without identifying its mechanism.
  2. The correct answer is D (Elevated filling pressures consistent with decompensating congestive heart failure): an elevated jugular venous column, a third heart sound and fine crackles that do not clear with cough form a coherent picture of volume overload. A calcium channel blocker causes dependent edema without raising jugular venous pressure or generating a third heart sound. Coarse crackles that clear with coughing indicate secretions, whereas hers persist. Venous insufficiency produces edema and skin change while leaving central venous pressure normal.
  3. The correct answer is C (Electrocardiogram, chest radiograph, natriuretic peptide, serum electrolytes and creatinine level): these tests confirm the syndrome, identify precipitants such as ischemia or arrhythmia, and establish renal and electrolyte baselines before diuresis begins. Ambulatory pressure monitoring and stress testing address chronic risk rather than the acute decompensation in front of you. Pulmonary angiography adds contrast and radiation when the clinical picture already explains her symptoms. Watchful waiting for two weeks is unsafe once volume overload is established.
  4. The correct answer is A (Reduce the diuretic dose, replace potassium, and recheck bloodwork within one week): a modest creatinine rise with hypokalemia after brisk diuresis indicates over diuresis, managed by dose reduction and electrolyte correction rather than abandonment of therapy. Stopping the diuretic outright risks rapid recurrence of congestion in someone who has just improved. Ignoring a falling potassium in a client at arrhythmia risk is unsafe. Escalating diuresis would deepen the renal impairment, and adding spironolactone while the creatinine is rising risks swinging the potassium high rather than safely reversing over diuresis.
Case C014

A 68-year-old man arrives at your rural clinic after a low speed collision in

which his stationary car was struck from behind. He wore a seatbelt, did not

lose consciousness, and walked in unaided. He reports neck stiffness that began

about an hour after the crash and a mild headache. He is alert with a Glasgow

Coma Scale of 15, blood pressure 138/82 mmHg, heart rate 78, and no limb

weakness or paresthesias.

  1. The correct answer is A (His age of sixty eight years exceeds the rule high risk age threshold): the Canadian C-Spine Rule treats age 65 or older, a dangerous mechanism, or extremity paresthesias as high risk factors that mandate imaging without any attempt at rotation testing. Delayed onset neck pain and ambulation at any time are low risk factors that would permit safe range of motion testing. Being struck while stationary in a simple rear end collision is specifically classified as low risk. No low risk item can override an existing high risk factor.
  2. The correct answer is C (Whether there was witnessed loss of consciousness, amnesia or disorientation): the Canadian CT Head Rule applies only to minor head injury, defined as witnessed loss of consciousness, definite amnesia, or witnessed disorientation with a Glasgow Coma Scale of 13 to 15. Without one of those entry criteria the rule cannot be applied at all and imaging is decided on clinical grounds. Repeated vomiting and a score below 15 at two hours are criteria inside the rule, reached only once entry is established. Anticoagulant use excludes a client from the rule rather than determining its result.
  3. The correct answer is B (Discharge with analgesia, mobilisation advice and clear return precautions): with negative cervical imaging, a normal neurological examination and no entry criteria for head imaging, further scans and continued immobilisation add harm without benefit. Age alone does not trigger head computed tomography in someone who never met the definition of minor head injury. Prolonged collar use worsens stiffness and deconditioning and is not evidence based after a negative workup. Advanced cervical imaging is reserved for persistent neurological signs or strong suspicion of instability.
  4. The correct answer is D (Send him to emergency now for immediate computed tomography of the head): progressive drowsiness, ataxia and repetitive questioning after head trauma in an anticoagulated older adult describe a subdural hematoma until imaging proves otherwise. Anticoagulation raises both the risk and the speed of deterioration, so assessment cannot wait for an outpatient appointment. Stopping the anticoagulant does nothing about a collection that may already be expanding. Attributing new focal deterioration to post concussion symptoms ignores a timeline that is worsening rather than improving.
Case C015

You are conducting a scheduled video visit with a 46-year-old woman who lives

four hours from the nearest emergency department. She reports three days of a

productive cough and fever, and now pain in her right chest that worsens with

deep breathing. Her home thermometer reads 38.6 degrees Celsius. She owns a home

blood pressure cuff and a fingertip oximeter. She has no chronic conditions and

takes no regular medications.

  1. The correct answer is D (Counted respiratory rate, sentence length, work of breathing and oximeter reading): all four are directly observable or client generated on video and together capture the severity of a lower respiratory illness. Self percussion cannot be interpreted by the clinician and chest wall self palpation adds nothing about severity. Sputum colour does not predict bacterial infection or severity, and no validated home test exists for pneumonia. A cough recording and a subjective statement are far weaker than a counted rate and a measured saturation.
  2. The correct answer is A (These findings indicate severe illness requiring urgent in person assessment today): a respiratory rate of 28, inability to speak in full sentences and a saturation of 91 percent together describe significant respiratory compromise that cannot be managed remotely. Consumer oximeters do have limitations, including reduced accuracy at low saturations and on darker skin, but a low reading with visible distress raises concern rather than lowering it. Temperature has little bearing once oxygenation and work of breathing are abnormal. Bronchitis does not produce this degree of hypoxemia.
  3. The correct answer is C (Arrange emergency medical transport now and document what could not be assessed): hypoxemia with increased work of breathing meets the threshold for immediate in person care, and arranging transport is the practitioner responsibility rather than the client problem to solve. Documenting the auscultation and palpation that video could not deliver, plus the safety netting given, is where the standard of care is met. Antibiotics with a deferred assessment leave an unstable client unmonitored overnight. Repeating the call or seeking another oximeter reading delays treatment without adding information.
  4. The correct answer is B (A note of what could not be examined and the escalation threshold given): recording the unavailable examination components, the safety netting advice and the point at which the client should seek in person care is the virtual equivalent of a pertinent negative. Consent to the modality is necessary but says nothing about clinical reasoning or safety. A verbatim transcript documents content without demonstrating that risk was considered. Privacy compliance addresses a legal obligation rather than the adequacy of the clinical assessment.
  5. The correct answer is B (Measure visual acuity in each eye, the vital sign of the eye): acuity is the single measurement that separates benign from sight threatening red eye, and it must be recorded before drops or manipulation alter it. Lid eversion helps when a foreign body is suspected but follows acuity testing. Topical anesthetic may be needed for examination yet should never precede baseline vision, and it is never dispensed for ongoing use. Digital estimation of pressure is unreliable and risks harm in a possibly infected or perforated eye.
  6. The correct answer is D (The hearing pattern fits presbycusis but unilateral tinnitus needs investigation): symmetric high frequency sensorineural loss with disproportionate difficulty in noise is the classic age related pattern, whereas any unilateral auditory symptom raises the possibility of vestibular schwannoma. Dismissing the tinnitus as ageing would miss a retrocochlear lesion. Urgent steroids apply to sudden sensorineural loss developing over hours to days, not to gradual symmetric loss. Cerumen impaction causes conductive loss and would not explain a sensorineural audiogram.
  7. The correct answer is A (Prompt investigation for malignancy including imaging and a tissue diagnosis): a firm, fixed, non tender node above one centimetre persisting beyond four to six weeks in an adult is malignancy until proven otherwise, and the supraclavicular position with weight loss and smoking sharpens that concern. An antibiotic trial delays diagnosis in a node with no inflammatory features. Watchful serial measurement suits small soft reactive nodes in a well client. Infectious serology addresses generalised adenopathy in a younger client rather than a fixed isolated mass.
  8. The correct answer is A (Treat as sudden sensorineural loss with urgent steroids and same day referral): lateralisation away from the affected ear with a positive Rinne confirms a sensorineural pattern, and hearing loss developing over hours to days is an otologic emergency where corticosteroid benefit falls sharply after the first two weeks. Waiting six weeks for audiometry forfeits the treatment window entirely. Decongestants address a conductive middle ear problem, which the tuning fork results exclude. Imaging for a retrocochlear lesion follows later and must never delay treatment.
  9. The correct answer is C (Evolution outweighs the other criteria, so this lesion warrants excisional biopsy): change over time is the most powerful of the ABCDE criteria, and a lesion that has become raised and symptomatic has evolved regardless of its size or colour. Melanomas frequently arise below six millimetres, so diameter alone never excludes the diagnosis. Uniform pigment and a regular border reassure in a static lesion but not in a changing one. A twelve month photographic review is far too long for a lesion that has already changed.
  10. The correct answer is B (Arrange immediate surgical assessment and start intravenous antibiotics now): pain out of proportion, crepitus, rapid spread and systemic toxicity describe necrotising fasciitis, a surgical emergency where survival depends on early debridement. Marking the border is a useful adjunct in cellulitis but wastes an hour when the diagnosis already demands the operating room. Oral antibiotics cannot penetrate necrotic fascia and next day review is far too late. Imaging for thrombosis pursues a different diagnosis and delays definitive treatment.
  11. The correct answer is D (Measure the ankle brachial index to establish arterial perfusion first): the ulcer looks venous, but absent pulses and claudication raise the possibility of coexisting arterial disease, and compression applied to an ischemic limb causes tissue loss. Vascular assessment therefore precedes the treatment decision rather than following it. Swabbing a chronic ulcer without signs of infection yields colonising organisms and drives unnecessary antibiotics. A week of elevation delays both the diagnosis and the definitive management of an established wound.
  12. The correct answer is C (Give parenteral antibiotics immediately and activate emergency transfer for sepsis): a non blanching petechial rash with fever suggests meningococcemia, and the recorded trend shows falling blood pressure with rising heart and respiratory rates, which is shock developing in front of you. Blood cultures are valuable but must never delay the first antibiotic dose in suspected meningococcal disease. Oral therapy with next day review is unsafe once perfusion is deteriorating. Lumbar puncture in a shocked client in a clinic setting is unsafe and unnecessary before treatment.
  13. The correct answer is A (It signals inadequate air movement and impending respiratory arrest requiring escalation): a silent chest in an exhausted asthmatic means too little air is moving to generate sound, and the accompanying drowsiness points to carbon dioxide retention. Genuine bronchodilator response would bring improving speech, alertness and saturation rather than deterioration in all three. Cardiac wheeze does not explain a six hour exacerbation in a young adult with a known diagnosis. Physiotherapy has no role and would delay assisted ventilation in a client who is tiring.
  14. The correct answer is D (Stop testing for embolism and pursue an alternative cause of her pain): her Wells score is low and she satisfies the PERC criteria, which together drop the probability of pulmonary embolism below the testing threshold. Choosing Wisely Canada explicitly advises against D dimer or imaging in low probability clients who meet PERC, because false positives lead to unnecessary scans and anticoagulation. Computed tomography adds contrast and radiation with no expected benefit here. Ventilation perfusion scanning is a different modality, not a reason to image someone who needs no imaging.
  15. The correct answer is B (Alveoli filled with exudate transmitting sound better than aerated lung): consolidated lung conducts sound more efficiently, producing increased fremitus, bronchial breathing and whispered pectoriloquy, while loss of air makes it dull to percussion. A pneumothorax produces hyperresonance with absent breath sounds and reduced fremitus, the opposite pattern. A pleural effusion is dull to percussion but reduces fremitus and breath sounds rather than enhancing them. Small airway narrowing produces wheeze without altering percussion note or fremitus.
  16. The correct answer is B (The oximeter cannot distinguish carboxyhemoglobin, so tissue hypoxia may be severe): standard pulse oximetry reads carboxyhemoglobin as though it were oxyhemoglobin, so a normal or high saturation is expected in carbon monoxide poisoning and gives false reassurance. Confirmation requires co oximetry on a blood sample, while high concentration oxygen begins on clinical grounds. Treating the displayed number as proof of adequate oxygenation is the classic fatal error in this exposure. The unchanged reading reflects a device limitation rather than a delivery failure or a different dyshemoglobinemia.
  17. The correct answer is C (Perform immediate needle decompression of the right hemithorax on clinical grounds): hypotension with hyperresonance, absent breath sounds and tracheal deviation away from the affected side defines tension pneumothorax, treated before imaging because obstructive shock kills within minutes. Waiting for a radiograph converts a treatable emergency into an arrest. Oxygen alone does not relieve the intrapleural pressure obstructing venous return. Computed tomography is far slower and is reserved for stable clients in whom the diagnosis is uncertain.
  18. The correct answer is A (Measure both arms after rest, then arrange out of office measurement): an interarm difference above 15 mmHg suggests subclavian stenosis or dissection and should be identified at the first visit, and Canadian practice relies on ambulatory or home measurement because white coat and masked hypertension make a single office value a weak basis for lifelong therapy. Repeating immediately without a rest period simply records the same activity and anxiety effect. Positioning the arm below heart level falsely raises the reading. A standing measurement detects orthostatic drop but does not establish the diagnosis.
  19. The correct answer is D (Reduced preload during Valsalva worsens dynamic outflow obstruction in cardiomyopathy): Valsalva lowers venous return, shrinking the left ventricular cavity so the hypertrophied septum obstructs outflow more, while squatting raises preload and afterload and relieves it. Any explanation built on increased filling during Valsalva reverses the physiology. Aortic stenosis murmurs typically soften with Valsalva because less blood crosses the valve. Exertional near syncope with this maneuver pattern is never innocent and requires echocardiography before return to sport.
  20. The correct answer is C (A delayed and diminished carotid upstroke with a late peaking murmur): a slow rising, low volume carotid pulse and a murmur peaking late in systole track most closely with severity, because both reflect prolonged obstructed ejection. Loudness correlates poorly with the gradient and can actually fall as the ventricle fails. An early systolic click suggests a bicuspid valve rather than severity. Radiation to the axilla points toward mitral regurgitation rather than aortic outflow obstruction.
  21. The correct answer is A (New atrial fibrillation here should prompt thyroid function testing as a cause): the strip shows an irregularly irregular rhythm without discernible P waves, and thyrotoxicosis is a recognised precipitant that presents in older adults with weight loss, heat intolerance and tremor. Sinus tachycardia would show regular intervals with a P wave before every complex, which is not displayed. Dismissing a new arrhythmia in a symptomatic client ignores both stroke risk and a treatable underlying cause. Nothing on the strip shows progressive lengthening or dropped conduction.
  22. The correct answer is B (Arrange emergency transfer with serial twelve lead tracings and troponin testing): women, older adults and people with diabetes often present with epigastric discomfort, nausea and diaphoresis rather than classic chest pain, and a single unremarkable tracing never excludes acute coronary syndrome. Reproducible chest wall tenderness lowers probability slightly but is present in a meaningful minority of confirmed infarcts. Empiric acid suppression treats a diagnosis of exclusion while an evolving infarct goes unmonitored. Stress testing during a possible acute presentation is unsafe and delays care by days.
  23. The correct answer is D (Localised peritoneal irritation, which raises the probability of appendicitis considerably): pain felt in the right lower quadrant during left sided palpation is the Rovsing sign, produced by displacement of gas and movement of inflamed parietal peritoneum. Referred pain from a left sided process would be felt on the left rather than crossing to the right. The psoas sign is elicited by hip extension, not by contralateral palpation. Visceral appendiceal distension explains the earlier periumbilical phase rather than this parietal finding.
  24. The correct answer is A (Refer for endoscopy, because alarm features are present in this presentation): dysphagia and unintentional weight loss in a man over fifty are upper gastrointestinal alarm features that change the plan immediately from empiric therapy to direct visualisation and biopsy. An acid suppression trial may partly relieve symptoms and thereby mask a malignancy while it progresses. Testing for Helicobacter pylori suits uncomplicated dyspepsia but does not address dysphagia with weight loss. A contrast swallow cannot obtain tissue and adds a step before the definitive test.
  25. The correct answer is C (Refer for colonoscopy to identify the source of the iron loss): a microcytic anemia with a ferritin well below the reference range in a postmenopausal woman means gastrointestinal blood loss until proven otherwise, and a negative stool inspection does not exclude an occult bleeding lesion. A low ferritin always demands a search for the source, so iron replacement belongs alongside the referral rather than instead of it, and supplementing with a passive three month recheck risks delaying a colorectal cancer diagnosis. Thyroid testing may follow but cannot explain iron deficiency. Repeating a ferritin that is already unequivocally low adds delay without information.
  26. The correct answer is D (Activate emergency vascular transfer now without waiting for any imaging): a pulsatile expansile mass with back pain and hypotension is a ruptured abdominal aortic aneurysm, and imaging happens only when the client is stable enough to tolerate the delay. This client is already shocked, so definitive surgical control is the only intervention that changes the outcome. Urinalysis pursues renal colic, which does not explain a pulsatile mass with hypotension. Aggressive fluid resuscitation to a normal pressure worsens bleeding, since permissive hypotension is preferred until the aorta is controlled.
  27. The correct answer is B (Contact urology immediately for surgical exploration without waiting for ultrasound): a high riding transverse testis with an absent cremasteric reflex and acute severe pain is torsion, and salvage rates fall steeply beyond roughly six hours from onset. Ultrasound helps when the picture is equivocal but must never delay the surgical call in a classic presentation. Antibiotics treat epididymo orchitis, which usually has gradual onset, fever and a preserved cremasteric reflex. Manual detorsion may be attempted while awaiting surgery but never replaces exploration or justifies discharge.
  28. The correct answer is C (Withhold antibiotics and investigate other causes of the fall thoroughly): Choosing Wisely Canada is explicit that asymptomatic bacteriuria should not be screened for or treated outside pregnancy and certain urologic procedures, and a positive culture in an older adult without urinary symptoms does not explain a fall. Treating produces resistance, Clostridioides difficile risk and adverse drug effects while the real cause goes unexamined. A single dose regimen is still unnecessary antibiotic exposure. Repeating the culture perpetuates the original testing error rather than correcting it.
  29. The correct answer is A (Brisk deep tendon reflexes with sustained clonus at the ankle): hyperreflexia with clonus signals cerebral irritability and impending eclampsia, converting a concerning presentation into an emergency needing immediate obstetric care and magnesium sulphate. Dependent ankle edema is common in normal pregnancy and does not grade severity. A soft flow murmur reflects the physiological rise in cardiac output during pregnancy. A modest lag in fundal height warrants growth assessment but does not dictate transfer within the hour.
  30. The correct answer is D (Refer same day to obstetrics and gynecology for suspected ectopic pregnancy): an empty uterus with a separate adnexal structure and free pelvic fluid, at a hormone level above the discriminatory zone, describes an ectopic pregnancy until specialist assessment proves otherwise. Serial hormone testing has a role when imaging is genuinely indeterminate, but it is unsafe here because an adnexal mass and free fluid are already present. Reassurance about an early intrauterine pregnancy contradicts both the level and the imaging. Managing this as a miscarriage risks rupture and hemorrhage at home.
  31. The correct answer is B (Radiograph indicated, because fibular head tenderness alone satisfies the rule): the Ottawa Knee Rule requires imaging if any single criterion is met, and tenderness at the head of the fibula is one of them. A second criterion is also satisfied here, since she cannot flex to ninety degrees, which reinforces the decision. Successful weight bearing and an age below fifty five only mean those particular criteria are absent, and absent criteria never cancel a positive one. Deferring the film two weeks would delay diagnosis of a proximal fibular or tibial plateau fracture.
  32. The correct answer is A (Arrange emergency imaging and urgent surgical referral for cauda equina syndrome): saddle numbness, urinary difficulty and bilateral leg weakness together define the syndrome, and delay beyond hours risks permanent incontinence and paralysis. Corticosteroids do not decompress the cauda equina and would waste the surgical window. Routine physiotherapy with delayed imaging applies only to back pain without red flags. Plain films cannot show the neural compression causing these findings and would falsely reassure if reported as normal.
  33. The correct answer is C (Explain that imaging without red flags does not improve outcomes here): Choosing Wisely Canada identifies early imaging for uncomplicated low back pain as a low value test, since incidental degenerative findings are near universal and often lead to worry, further tests and procedures without improving pain or function. Ordering a scan for reassurance reliably produces the opposite effect once incidental findings are reported. Plain films still deliver radiation and share the same lack of benefit. A fixed twelve week rule misstates the principle, because red flags rather than elapsed time drive imaging.
  34. The correct answer is C (Arrange urgent spinal magnetic resonance imaging and blood cultures today): fever with back pain in a person who injects drugs suggests spinal epidural abscess or vertebral osteomyelitis, and magnetic resonance imaging detects it before a neurological deficit develops. A normal neurological examination is reassuring only in the sense that there is still time to act. Plain films are insensitive early, and oral antibiotics both undertreat the infection and reduce culture yield. Attributing night pain with fever to chronic pain is a stigmatising error that misses a cord threatening and life threatening infection.
  35. The correct answer is D (Proceed to lumbar puncture or angiography to exclude subarachnoid hemorrhage): a thunderclap headache reaching peak intensity within a minute demands exclusion of aneurysmal bleeding, and non contrast imaging loses sensitivity as the hours pass, so a normal study at eight hours cannot end the workup. Cerebrospinal fluid examination for xanthochromia or computed tomography angiography completes the pathway. Discharging on the basis of this report treats a sensitive early test as definitive well after its best window. Treating this as migraine or repeating the same scan leaves a potentially fatal aneurysm unexamined.
  36. The correct answer is B (This is delirium, a medical emergency requiring a search for causes): acute onset over days, a fluctuating course and impaired attention are the defining features, and delirium always signals an underlying medical problem such as infection, medication effect, urinary retention or a metabolic derangement. Dementia progresses over months to years with attention preserved early, so a two day change does not fit. Depression can cause slowing but not hour to hour fluctuation in attention. Normal ageing produces slower processing without impaired attention or a reversed sleep pattern.
  37. The correct answer is A (An upper motor neuron lesion, so image the brain and spinal cord): spasticity, hyperreflexia and an extensor plantar response localise the problem above the anterior horn cell, making central imaging the logical next investigation. Lower motor neuron disease produces flaccidity, wasting, fasciculations and reduced reflexes, none of which are present. A length dependent polyneuropathy causes distal sensory loss with depressed rather than brisk reflexes. Myopathy causes proximal weakness with normal or reduced reflexes and no plantar change.
  38. The correct answer is D (The time last known well at 07:00, which sets his therapeutic window): eligibility for thrombolysis and thrombectomy is calculated from the last time the person was seen at their neurological baseline, not from the moment symptoms were noticed. Using the discovery time would overestimate the remaining window and could lead to unsafe treatment. Blood pressure must be controlled before thrombolysis but it does not define the window. Deficit severity influences the decision without replacing the timing rule, and imaging based selection can extend the window in carefully chosen clients.
  39. The correct answer is B (Watching the client rise, walk, turn and return across the room): gait requires strength, proprioception, cerebellar coordination, vision and vestibular function to work together, so an abnormal gait immediately narrows where the problem lies. Sensory testing examines one modality and misses cerebellar and motor contributions. Reflex testing is fast but says little about balance or the ability to stand from a chair. A full cranial nerve screen rarely explains falls and would consume most of a short appointment.
  40. The correct answer is C (Repeat the thyroid stimulating hormone with antibodies in about three months): a mildly raised thyroid stimulating hormone with a normal free thyroxine is subclinical, and a substantial proportion of such values normalise on repeat, so confirmation precedes any lifelong therapy. Starting levothyroxine on a single borderline value commits the client to treatment and monitoring without evidence of benefit at this level. Rechecking after two weeks is too soon, since the axis takes about six weeks to reflect change. Referral and imaging are unnecessary without a nodule, compressive symptoms or overt biochemical disease.
  41. The correct answer is A (Thyrotoxicosis can present apathetically in older adults and must be tested): apathetic hyperthyroidism produces weight loss, lethargy, low mood and atrial fibrillation without the adrenergic features seen in younger clients, so absent tremor and heat intolerance do not exclude it. Attributing the picture to grief risks missing a treatable cause of both a new arrhythmia and ongoing weight loss. An irregularly irregular pulse is never a normal ageing finding and always warrants an electrocardiogram. Thyroid testing is inexpensive and directly changes management here.
  42. The correct answer is D (Testing protective sensation with a 10 g monofilament at defined plantar sites): loss of protective sensation is the strongest single predictor of future ulceration, and it is usually silent, which is exactly why testing happens even when the client reports no symptoms. Symptom questions miss the painless neuropathy that carries the highest risk. Pulses identify arterial disease, which matters for healing but is a separate and less common pathway to a first ulcer. Temperature comparison features in some monitoring programmes but is not the core annual screening maneuver.
  43. The correct answer is C (Hyperosmolar hyperglycemic state, so prioritise aggressive intravenous fluid replacement): very high glucose with profound dehydration, altered mentation and minimal ketosis in an older adult with type 2 diabetes defines the hyperosmolar state, where the fluid deficit is the main threat to life. Ketoacidosis presents with Kussmaul respirations, a fruity odour and significant ketones at generally lower glucose values. Insulin given before volume replacement shifts water intracellularly and worsens circulatory collapse. Oral rehydration cannot correct a deficit of this size in a confused client.
  44. The correct answer is B (Start oral iron and investigate the bowel for an occult bleeding source): a low ferritin always demands a source, and in an adult man eating a normal diet, occult gastrointestinal loss including celiac disease and malignancy must be excluded rather than assumed away. Replacing iron without a search corrects the number while the cause continues unchecked. Delaying three months allows an underlying lesion to progress. Bone marrow examination is unnecessary once the ferritin has already established iron deficiency.
  45. The correct answer is A (A coagulation factor deficiency, since deep bleeding into joints predominates here): hemarthrosis and deep muscle hematomas are the signature of impaired secondary hemostasis such as hemophilia, because clot stabilisation fails after the initial platelet plug forms. Platelet disorders instead cause petechiae, purpura and mucosal bleeding, none of which he has. Vascular fragility produces superficial bruising rather than repeated joint bleeds. Anticoagulants can cause bleeding anywhere, no such exposure is described, and they do not selectively target joints.
  46. The correct answer is C (Avoid contact sport for at least three to four weeks from symptom onset): splenic enlargement in infectious mononucleosis carries a real risk of rupture with abdominal trauma, and that risk persists after the sore throat has settled. Returning once the throat feels better misjudges the timeline, because splenomegaly outlasts the pharyngitis. Aminopenicillins commonly provoke a widespread rash in mononucleosis and do not shorten a viral illness. Serology stays positive for months and cannot be used to time a return to play.
  47. The correct answer is D (Offer human immunodeficiency virus testing as part of this workup today): recurrent unexplained thrush in an adult, poor wound healing and fatigue are recognised indicator conditions, and Canadian guidance recommends offering testing routinely rather than waiting for a client to disclose risk. Risk based testing misses a large proportion of undiagnosed infections and reinforces stigma. Repeated topical treatment addresses the symptom while an underlying immunodeficiency progresses. Immunoglobulin studies investigate a much rarer cause and should not delay a simple high yield test.
  48. The correct answer is B (Examine all node chains, spleen and liver, then arrange urgent investigation): drenching night sweats and marked weight loss with generalised rubbery adenopathy are classic constitutional symptoms of lymphoma, and mapping the full extent of nodal and organ involvement guides both urgency and the site chosen for biopsy. An antibiotic trial has no rationale when there is no inflammatory focus. Watchful waiting for six weeks is unsafe once constitutional symptoms accompany the nodes. Testing for mononucleosis alone is too narrow for a picture that also fits lymphoma or human immunodeficiency virus infection.
  49. The correct answer is A (The rules need clinician palpation, so an in person assessment is required): the Ottawa rules were derived using examiner palpation of specific bony landmarks, and self reported or self palpated tenderness has never been validated as a substitute. Applying them on video risks a false negative that defers a needed radiograph. Observing weight bearing covers only one component and cannot stand alone while the tenderness criteria remain unassessed. A client statement of no bony tenderness is not equivalent to a trained examiner localising the posterior edge or tip of a malleolus.
Case C016

An 82-year-old woman is brought to your clinic by her daughter.

Over four days she has become confused, sleeping most of the day and awake and agitated overnight. She has hypertension, osteoarthritis and mild cognitive impairment diagnosed two years ago.

Her medications are ramipril, atorvastatin, acetaminophen and oxybutynin, which was started ten days ago for urinary urgency. Her daughter reports that her attention drifts mid sentence and that she was fully oriented last week.

  1. The correct answer is C (Her attention drifting mid sentence with a four day fluctuating course): impaired attention with a waxing and waning course over days is the single most useful bedside discriminator for delirium. A prior diagnosis of mild cognitive impairment raises her baseline vulnerability to delirium rather than explaining an abrupt change. Disorientation occurs in both delirium and dementia, so it separates neither. Sleep wake reversal supports delirium but is far less specific than fluctuating attention.
  2. The correct answer is A (Oxybutynin, an anticholinergic drug recently started for her urinary urgency): anticholinergic burden is among the most common reversible precipitants of delirium in older adults, and the timing of the new prescription fits her four day change. A stable antihypertensive taken for years does not explain an abrupt confusional state. Acetaminophen at therapeutic dosing does not cause delirium through hepatic metabolites. Statin associated cognitive complaints are reported but are not a common cause of acute fluctuating confusion.
  3. The correct answer is D (Perform a targeted examination for infection, retention, hypoxia and constipation): delirium is usually multifactorial, so a single stopped medication does not complete the search for precipitants. Cognitive screens do not distinguish delirium from dementia and produce uninterpretable results during an acute confusional state. A memory clinic assesses chronic cognitive decline and cannot manage an acute reversible cause. Antipsychotics carry mortality risk in older adults and are reserved for severe distress or danger after non pharmacological measures fail.
  4. The correct answer is B (Explain that capacity is decision specific and must be reassessed now): capacity is both decision specific and time specific, so a finding made during delirium must be revisited once the delirium resolves. Capacity is presumed in Canada, and a single confusional episode does not create a permanent global finding. Cognitive screening scores measure impairment and do not establish capacity for any particular decision. A memory clinic assesses cognition and does not certify blanket incapacity.
Case C017

A 29-year-old woman attends a six week postpartum visit with her first baby.

She reports crying most days, poor concentration and constant worry that she is a bad mother. She sleeps only when the baby sleeps and feels exhausted.

Her Edinburgh Postnatal Depression Scale score today is 17, and on item 10 she indicated that the thought of harming herself has come to her sometimes. Her partner is supportive and has come to the visit with her.

  1. The correct answer is B (The score indicates probable depression, and the item 10 response needs assessment): a screening instrument identifies probability and severity, and diagnosis still rests on the clinical interview. A positive self harm item is a signal to assess directly, not a number to be averaged into a total. This scale deliberately omits somatic items precisely so that normal postpartum fatigue does not distort it, so calling it invalid is wrong. A supportive partner is a protective factor but never a substitute for assessing disclosed self harm thoughts.
  2. The correct answer is D (Interview her alone about the thoughts, their content, intent and access to means): a disclosed thought of self harm is assessed directly and in private at the visit where it appears, because a partner in the room suppresses honest disclosure. Minimization by the client is common and is not a reason to defer the conversation. Starting medication or arranging peer support may both follow, but neither tells you whether she is safe to leave today. Deferring the assessment by a week leaves the most dangerous unknown unexamined.
  3. The correct answer is A (Collaborative safety planning, means restriction, treatment and early scheduled follow up): passive ideation without plan or intent, combined with accessible stockpiled medication, calls for a written client owned plan plus removal of the means and a short follow up interval. A promise not to act has no evidence of benefit and discourages honest reporting. Involuntary assessment is not justified when she is engaged, has no intent and has capacity. Recording low risk and waiting six weeks ignores both the accessible means and the treatable depression.
  4. The correct answer is C (Sudden confusion with rapid mood shifts and strange beliefs about the baby): that triad describes postpartum psychosis, an emergency that typically appears within the first weeks and carries risk to both parent and infant. A rising screening score signals worsening severity but is managed in the community. Insomnia and tearfulness are concerning and warrant escalation of treatment, yet they remain within the depressive picture. Anhedonia is a core depressive symptom rather than a marker of an emergency.
Case C018

A 47-year-old man is seen at his family's insistence two weeks after his marriage ended and he was laid off.

He drinks six to eight beers most evenings, sleeps two to three hours a night, and describes himself as a burden to everyone.

He owns two hunting rifles stored in a locked cabinet at home. He has no psychiatric history and has never attempted suicide. He tells you he is only here to satisfy his sister.

  1. The correct answer is A (Recent losses, severe insomnia, hopelessness and heavy nightly alcohol use): these are dynamic factors, they are present right now, and they are where intervention can actually change the trajectory. Sex and age are static demographic markers that set a background rate and cannot be modified. The absence of a previous attempt is reassuring statistically but does not offset an acute cluster of dynamic drivers, and most people who die have never attempted before. Reluctant attendance affects engagement rather than the level of acute risk.
  2. The correct answer is C (Negotiate transfer of both rifles to another person outside the home): restricting access to lethal means has the strongest evidence base of any single suicide prevention intervention, and firearms carry the highest case fatality. Sedative hypnotics add an accessible overdose agent and impair judgment in someone drinking heavily. Addiction treatment matters but works over weeks, while the firearm risk is present tonight. A structured ideation scale documents what was asked and creates shared language, yet it does not predict risk and changes nothing about access to a rifle.
  3. The correct answer is B (Negotiate removal of the ammunition and a trusted person holding the key): means restriction is a negotiation, and partial steps such as separating ammunition and adding a delay to access still reduce lethality when full removal is refused. Involuntary assessment requires the criteria in the applicable mental health legislation, which owning a firearm alone does not meet. Simply documenting refusal abandons the intervention with the best evidence. Reporting to a chief firearms officer under the Firearms Act is available when risk cannot be mitigated, but it is not the first move while he is still engaged and negotiating.
  4. The correct answer is D (Warning signs, coping steps, contacts and crisis numbers, with review in days): a safety plan has a defined structure covering personal warning signs, internal coping strategies, distracting people and settings, people to contact, real crisis numbers and the agreed means restriction steps. It is collaborative and client owned, so a document written by the clinician and filed in the chart gives the person nothing to use in the moment. Crisis numbers on their own are only one element of that structure. A promise not to act has never been shown to reduce attempts and offers false reassurance.
Case C019

An 84-year-old man with moderate osteoarthritis and early Alzheimer disease lives with his son in Ontario.

He arrives for a routine visit noticeably thinner than at his last review, wearing soiled clothing, with bruises of different ages on both forearms.

His son answers every question for him and declines to leave the room. The community pharmacy reports that his medications have not been picked up for two months. He scores 21 on the MoCA.

  1. The correct answer is D (Create a clinical reason to interview the man privately without his son): a companion who will not leave the room is itself an indicator, and no valid abuse assessment is possible while the suspected person is present. Confronting the son first can escalate danger for the older adult and closes off any later disclosure. Delaying a home visit by a month leaves a possibly unsafe situation unexamined. Contacting police before the man has spoken removes his voice from a decision he may have capacity to make.
  2. The correct answer is B (The score signals impairment, but capacity is decision specific and assessed separately): a cognitive screen quantifies impairment and never determines capacity for any particular decision. Capacity turns on whether the person can understand the relevant information and appreciate the reasonably foreseeable consequences of deciding or not deciding. A dementia diagnosis lowers the probability of capacity for complex decisions but does not remove it, and capacity is presumed in Canada. The professional discipline of the administrator does not convert a screening score into a capacity finding.
  3. The correct answer is C (No blanket duty applies, so safety plan and support his capable choices): in Ontario there is no general mandatory reporting duty for a capable older adult living in a private home, so the work is assessing capacity, addressing immediate safety, documenting and respecting his decisions. Adult protection legislation with broad mandatory reporting exists in Nova Scotia, Newfoundland and Labrador, and Prince Edward Island, which is why a national claim is false. Ontario's mandatory reporting duties attach to licensed long term care homes and to retirement homes, not to private residences. Financial exploitation should still be assessed and named, since it is common, frequently hidden, and among the most commonly reported forms.
  4. The correct answer is A (Assess caregiver strain and arrange respite and community support for the son): caregiver burnout and abuse frequently share a household, and reducing strain is one of the few interventions that changes the conditions producing the harm. Framing the encounter as an investigation ends the only access you have to the home. A substitute decision maker is appointed only where capacity for the relevant decisions is absent, which has not been established. Withholding support until the abuse question resolves keeps the driving factor untouched while the older adult remains in the same home.
Case C020

A 16-year-old is brought to the clinic by a parent worried about falling grades, withdrawal from friends and a smell of cannabis in the bedroom.

The parent wants a drug test today and wants to stay for the whole visit.

The teenager sits with arms crossed and answers in single words. There is no known psychiatric history, and there have been no previous visits to this clinic in the past two years.

  1. The correct answer is B (Explain confidentiality and its limits, then interview the teenager alone): the CRAFFT depends on a confidential interview and loses validity when a parent remains in the room. Explaining both confidentiality and the safety limits on it before questioning is what makes honest answers possible. A urine screen detects a metabolite, not pattern, harm or context, and ordering it first frames the encounter as surveillance. A parent completed checklist adds collateral information but cannot substitute for the youth's own account.
  2. The correct answer is A (Two positives warrant fuller assessment, and the car item is a safety concern): two or more affirmative answers is the recognized signal for a fuller assessment, and riding with an impaired driver is a safety issue that must be addressed regardless of the total. A screening tool identifies who needs assessment and never establishes a substance use disorder on its own. Two positives sit at the threshold rather than below it, so routine follow up understates what has been found. Youth self report obtained confidentially is reasonably reliable, and dismissing it removes the only usable data.
  3. The correct answer is D (Mood symptoms with direct questions about self harm and suicidal thoughts): months of flat mood, loss of a valued activity and social withdrawal in an adolescent using cannabis require direct assessment of self harm within the confidential portion of the visit. Asking directly does not plant the idea and is the only way to find it. Further detail on quantity and route refines a picture you already have. Drug screening and school planning are both reasonable later, but neither addresses the most dangerous unknown while you still have private time.
  4. The correct answer is C (Share the safety concerns and the plan, and keep the remainder confidential): the disclosure is limited to what safety requires, it matches what the youth agreed to, and it brings the parent into the safety plan as a support. Handing over the whole interview breaks the confidentiality that produced the disclosure and makes future honesty unlikely. Withholding the safety information leaves the parent unable to help and removes a protective factor. Deferring the conversation to a separate appointment delays safety planning without protecting anything.
  5. The correct answer is B (The observed findings conflict with his report and deserve more weight): the mental status examination records what you observe now, and psychomotor slowing with restricted affect and impoverished speech is objective data. When observation and self report disagree, the observed findings usually carry more weight and drive further assessment. Accepting the self report at face value discards the strongest signal in the encounter. A questionnaire relies on the same self report that is already in question, so a low total would not resolve the mismatch.
  6. The correct answer is D (Whether food ran out in the past year with no money for more): that wording is the validated food insecurity question, and unfilled prescriptions with missed appointments in a lone parent point strongly to material deprivation driving the clinical failure. A social condition that is causing the deterioration outranks a further medication step, because titration on an empty cupboard will not work. Asking about adherence without asking why produces a blaming answer and no plan. Carbohydrate counting assumes a stable food supply that may not exist, and a positive answer creates an obligation to link her to income and food supports.
  7. The correct answer is A (A medical or medication cause, given her age and fluctuating alertness): new psychosis after age 40 with fluctuating alertness points to delirium, medication effects, substance intoxication or withdrawal, endocrine disease, infection or an intracranial cause before any primary psychiatric diagnosis. A first presentation of schizophrenia in the sixth decade is uncommon and remains a diagnosis of exclusion. Delusional disorder and brief psychotic disorder both assume clear consciousness, which the fluctuating drowsiness contradicts. Starting an antipsychotic before the workup can mask the organic cause and worsen a delirium.
  8. The correct answer is A (The Edinburgh Postnatal Depression Scale, which omits somatic symptom items): it is used antenatally as well as postpartum, and leaving out sleep, appetite and energy items is exactly why it outperforms general depression scales during pregnancy. The PHQ-9 can be used in pregnancy, but its somatic items inflate scores when broken sleep and fatigue are near universal. A generalized anxiety measure tracks worry rather than depressive severity. The Mood Disorder Questionnaire screens for a lifetime history of hypomania or mania and measures nothing about current severity.
  9. The correct answer is C (Somatic items are inflating the score, so the total needs clinical interpretation): the item detail shows that sleep, energy, appetite and concentration carry almost the whole total while the mood, interest and self worth items are low, which is the classic pattern in active cancer treatment. Treating the number as a diagnosis would start medication for a score driven by chemotherapy effects. The instrument is not invalid, and repeating it a month later would reproduce the same somatic inflation. A zero on the self harm item never clears a person of suicide risk, so that inference is unsafe as well as beside the point.
  10. The correct answer is D (Continue assessing, because the GAD-7 performs poorly for post traumatic stress): the instrument was built for generalized anxiety disorder and is much weaker for panic, social anxiety, obsessive compulsive disorder and post traumatic stress. A low score in a client who is obviously anxious closes nothing, and his symptom cluster fits a trauma related presentation. Repeating the same poorly suited tool in four weeks delays assessment without adding information. A depression measure may be useful later but does not address the presenting trauma symptoms.
  11. The correct answer is B (The score flags hazardous drinking, so a fuller assessment should now follow): the AUDIT-C measures consumption and identifies hazardous drinking, so a positive result opens an assessment of harms, dependence features and readiness rather than closing the topic. It cannot diagnose a use disorder, which requires assessment of impairment and loss of control. Her score of 5 sits above the usual threshold of 3 for women, so treating it as negative misreads the instrument. Repeating the questionnaire postpones the assessment that the positive result has already earned.
  12. The correct answer is C (The Mood Disorder Questionnaire, to detect an unrecognized history of hypomania): early onset, recurrent episodes and poor antidepressant response are the exact indications for screening for a bipolar spectrum disorder before prescribing again, because an unrecognized diagnosis changes the entire treatment plan. Hazardous drinking does undermine response and is worth screening, but it would not overturn the medication class. Comorbid anxiety may guide the choice of agent without changing the underlying strategy. A severity measure documents a baseline and adds nothing about diagnosis.
  13. The correct answer is A (Routine questionnaires for asymptomatic adults are not recommended, so use case finding): the Canadian Task Force on Preventive Health Care does not recommend routinely administering depression questionnaires to asymptomatic adults, so these tools belong in case finding and severity tracking. Applying them universally generates false positives, consumes visit time and creates follow up burden without demonstrated benefit. There is no Canadian recommendation for universal annual screening at any adult age band. No questionnaire replaces the clinical interview, which remains where diagnosis is made.
  14. The correct answer is B (Categorical stratification predicts poorly, and most who die were rated low): risk categories perform badly at the level of the individual, and the majority of people who die by suicide were rated as low risk at their last contact. What the record needs instead is a formulation naming the drivers, the protective factors, the access to means and the plan for the coming days. Time cost is a weak objection compared with the failure of prediction itself. No instrument produces a valid predictive category either, so shifting the task to a tool or to a specialist does not repair the underlying problem.
  15. The correct answer is D (Reassess his capacity for this decision now that the delirium has resolved): capacity is time specific, and a finding made on the second postoperative day during an active delirium cannot govern a decision once the delirium has cleared. The summary itself records that attention fluctuated and then improved, which is the trigger to reassess rather than to defer. A cognitive screening score measures impairment and does not determine capacity for the placement decision. A substitute decision maker acts only while the person lacks capacity for the specific decision at hand.
  16. The correct answer is C (The MoCA, because it is more sensitive to executive dysfunction): his vascular risk profile and the reported failure of planning and judgment point to executive impairment, which is what the alternating trail making task, clock drawing, verbal fluency and abstraction items are designed to expose. The MMSE leans on orientation and memory and looks falsely reassuring in vascular and frontal presentations, which is exactly this picture. Speed and lack of training requirements do not justify choosing an instrument that misses the deficit in question. Preserved conversational fluency is common in early executive impairment and excludes nothing.
  17. The correct answer is C (It is unreliable here, given language, education and the ad hoc interpreter): language, literacy, education, sensory impairment and culture all shift cognitive screening scores, and administration in a second language through a family member compounds every one of those problems. The education adjustment adds a single point and cannot repair an invalid administration. Neither a diagnosis of mild cognitive impairment nor a diagnosis of dementia can rest on a test delivered under these conditions. Repeating the screen with a validated translation and a professional interpreter is the appropriate way forward.
  18. The correct answer is A (The fifteen item Geriatric Depression Scale, which avoids somatic items): the short form uses simple yes or no answers and deliberately excludes somatic content, which suits an older adult whose fatigue and poor appetite already have physical explanations. A score of 5 or higher on that scale warrants further assessment. The PHQ-9 would be inflated by sleep, energy and appetite items driven by heart failure and arthritis. An anxiety measure does not assess depressive severity, and a cognitive screen answers a different question entirely in a woman described as cognitively intact.
  19. The correct answer is B (Gather collateral history from family and staff about observed behaviour change): self report instruments lose validity in moderate to severe dementia, so structured collateral information about withdrawal, appetite, sleep and engagement becomes the primary data. Recording unreliable answers produces a number that looks objective and means nothing. The PHQ-9 also asks the person to rate the past two weeks, so it depends on exactly the recall she lacks. Repeating a cognitive screen measures the dementia she already has and does not address the new mood change.
  20. The correct answer is D (Depression, given preserved attention, a precise onset and memory complaints): depression in older adults can present with prominent cognitive complaints while attention and orientation stay intact, and the person typically emphasizes the deficit and gives do not know answers rather than confabulating. A sharply dateable onset tied to a bereavement fits a mood disorder, not a degenerative process. Delirium requires impaired and fluctuating attention, which he does not have. Alzheimer disease develops insidiously over months to years, and affected people often minimize rather than volunteer their memory problems.
  21. The correct answer is A (Her insomnia, daily drinking and the acute housing loss she now faces): these are dynamic factors that can be targeted this week through sleep intervention, addressing the drinking, housing navigation and closer follow up. A previous attempt is the strongest single predictor but is fixed history and cannot be changed. Family history and demographic characteristics also belong to the static baseline that shapes vulnerability rather than the plan. A formulation names both, then puts the intervention where the modifiable factors are.
  22. The correct answer is C (It records last night only, and the new dynamic factors demand fresh assessment): a structured ideation scale documents what was asked at a moment in time, is not predictive, and a negative result is never a clearance. Job loss, acute intoxication, agitation and the purchase of a possible means are all new dynamic factors that have appeared since the call. Using yesterday's record as reassurance is precisely the error that makes these scales dangerous when treated as prediction. Narrowing today's visit to the drinking would leave the means and the agitation unaddressed.
  23. The correct answer is B (Avoid it, since it has no evidence of benefit and discourages honest disclosure): a promise not to act has never been shown to reduce suicide or attempts, it shifts responsibility onto the client, and it makes disclosure feel like breaking a promise. The comfort your colleague describes is exactly the false reassurance that makes the practice harmful. Restricting its use to particular subgroups or adding a witness does not repair an intervention with no evidence behind it. The defensible alternative is collaborative safety planning, means restriction and a defined short follow up interval.
  24. The correct answer is B (Weekly blister packs and family removal of the acetaminophen and opioids): limiting the quantity dispensed and having willing family hold the remaining stock removes the lethal quantity of all three agents now rather than later. Changing the antidepressant is sensible but leaves a month of tablets in the house until the next review and does nothing about the acetaminophen or the opioids. Telephone contact supports engagement without altering what he can reach in the moment. Psychotherapy is important treatment but works over weeks and does not address immediate lethality.
  25. The correct answer is D (See her within days, because the period after discharge carries very high risk): recent discharge following an attempt is one of the strongest dynamic risk factors, and the days immediately afterward are the highest risk window. A six week gap in contact is unsafe and should be bridged with early follow up, safety planning and means restriction. Apparent improvement soon after an attempt is common and does not indicate that risk has passed. Repeating bloodwork addresses the physical sequelae of the ingestion and leaves the risk itself unmanaged.
  26. The correct answer is A (It should not offset an active plan while he is intoxicated and agitated): protective factors are not subtracted from risk, and responsibility for dependants collapses as a restraint under acute intoxication and agitation. An active plan combined with disinhibition requires immediate action on safety and means, not reassurance. Dismissing reasons for living entirely is also wrong, since they inform the safety plan and the therapeutic conversation. Waiting for a psychiatric assessment to decide whether a factor is protective delays the decision that must be made tonight.
  27. The correct answer is C (Disclose the minimum needed to police and the named person, then document): all three considerations from Smith v. Jones are met here, since there is a clear risk to an identifiable person, the threatened harm is death, and the danger is imminent. Disclosure is limited to what is needed to prevent the harm and goes to those who can prevent it, usually police and the person at risk. Releasing the entire clinical record exceeds that limit and is itself a privacy breach. Confidentiality is not absolute, and no court order is required before acting on a threat of this kind.
  28. The correct answer is D (Do not disclose, since no identifiable person, serious harm or imminence exists): the duty to warn requires a clear risk to an identifiable person or group, threatened serious bodily harm or death, and imminence, and none of the three is present in a diffuse expression of anger. Disclosing anyway would be a privacy breach rather than a cautious choice. Withholding his name does not cure a disclosure that should not be made at all. A regulatory college has no role in receiving a report about a client's statement, and the appropriate work here is exploring the anger and documenting the reasoning.
  29. The correct answer is A (Arrange respite care and connect her with a caregiver support program): unrelieved caregiving and eight months of social isolation are producing the insomnia, weight loss and blood pressure change, and social isolation itself carries mortality risk comparable to well recognized physical risk factors. Screening without linking the person to something real adds burden and yields nothing, so the answer has to include a concrete connection. Sleep hygiene and delayed therapy treat a symptom while the cause continues unchanged. Assessing her husband shifts attention to a different patient and to a placement decision she has not raised.
  30. The correct answer is B (Verify your own jurisdiction's legislation before completing any involuntary document): mental health legislation is provincial and territorial, and the instrument, the criteria, the permitted duration and the professions authorized to sign are not interchangeable across Canada. Ontario uses an application for psychiatric assessment allowing up to 72 hours, while British Columbia uses medical certificates and Alberta uses admission certificates. Voluntary admission is preferable when genuine, but steering a person toward consent to sidestep the legislation is neither voluntary nor safe. Whether a nurse practitioner may sign varies by jurisdiction, since British Columbia permits a nurse practitioner to complete the first medical certificate while Ontario restricts the application to physicians, so a blanket statement that NPs never can is also wrong.
  31. The correct answer is C (Establishing withdrawal risk, since a prior seizure predicts dangerous withdrawal): alcohol and benzodiazepine withdrawal can be fatal, unlike opioid withdrawal, and a previous withdrawal seizure strongly predicts recurrence. Combined daily alcohol and benzodiazepine use with a plan for unsupervised abrupt cessation tomorrow makes this the immediate safety question. A complete substance inventory and infectious disease screening both belong in the assessment but neither prevents a seizure this week. Motivational stage matters for engagement and does not change the physiological danger of the plan he has already made.
  32. The correct answer is C (She appears capable of this decision, so her refusal should be respected): capacity is decision specific, and she has demonstrated both understanding of the relevant information and appreciation of the reasonably foreseeable consequences of declining. A guardian of property has authority over finances, not over treatment decisions she remains capable of making. A dementia diagnosis lowers the likelihood of capacity for complex decisions without removing it for simple ones. Formal capacity assessment is reserved for genuine doubt, and requiring it whenever a clinician disagrees would undermine the presumption of capacity.
  33. The correct answer is D (Explain that an understood and appreciated refusal is a capable decision): a refusal that others disagree with is not evidence of incapacity, and he meets the test by understanding the information and appreciating the consequences of his choice. Treating the content of the decision as proof of incapacity inverts the legal standard and strips a capable adult of autonomy. A substitute decision maker has no role while he retains capacity for this decision. Screening for depression is reasonable clinical care but must not be used as a mechanism to delay or override a capable refusal.
  34. The correct answer is A (Treat the pain, optimize his hearing and vision, and reassess at his best time): capacity fluctuates with pain, sedating medication, fatigue and sensory impairment, so the obligation is to maximize capacity before concluding that it is absent. Plain language, correction of hearing and vision, an interpreter where needed and reassessment at the person's best time of day are all part of that step. Recording incapacity under avoidable conditions produces a finding that reflects the setting rather than the person. A cognitive screening score does not determine capacity, and proceeding on a substitute decision is premature until capacity has been properly assessed.
  35. The correct answer is B (Evidence is insufficient for universal screening, so use indicator based case finding): the Canadian Task Force on Preventive Health Care found the evidence insufficient to recommend universal routine screening for intimate partner violence, so the practical standard is recognizing indicators and then asking directly in private. Indicators include injuries inconsistent with the stated mechanism, delayed presentation, chronic pain and a controlling companion who will not leave the room. A mandatory documented field also creates a record that a controlling partner may later access, which can increase danger. Asking is never prohibited, and restricting the question to pregnancy misses most people at risk.
  36.
