The correct answer is A (Create a clinical reason to see her alone with a professional interpreter): repeated vague presentations, an injury and a companion who will not leave are recognized indicators, and the question must be asked in private through an interpreter who is not a family member. Using the suspected person as interpreter guarantees an unusable answer and can put her in danger. A coded signal asked in front of him is visible to him and unsafe. Postponing for three months leaves the risk unassessed and wastes the contact you already have.
  37. The correct answer is D (The neck petechiae, anterior neck tenderness and hoarse voice together): that combination describes non fatal strangulation, which is one of the strongest known predictors of later homicide by an intimate partner. Delayed presentation and bruises of differing ages are genuine indicators of abuse but do not carry the same predictive weight for lethality. A normal radiograph reflects the imaging performed and says nothing about future danger. The immediate work is seeing her alone, assessing danger including weapons, threats to kill and stalking, then safety planning and documenting her own words.
  38. The correct answer is C (Assess immediate danger, safety plan, document her words and offer referral): a capable adult decides for herself whether to involve police, and the clinician's role is danger assessment, collaborative safety planning, accurate documentation in her own words and an offer of referral. Reporting against her wishes removes her control and can escalate the danger she is managing. Directing her to leave is the classic error, since separation is the period of highest lethality and the decision belongs to her. Making care conditional on leaving abandons the one relationship that may keep her safer.
  39. The correct answer is B (Report yourself now, because the duty is personal and needs only suspicion): every province and territory imposes a personal legal duty on professionals to report a child who may be in need of protection, triggered by reasonable grounds to suspect rather than by proof. That duty cannot be delegated to a supervisor, a social worker or anyone else, and waiting for confirmation misstates the legal threshold. A changing mechanism with a spiral long bone fracture in a young child already meets reasonable grounds to suspect. Documenting and rebooking leaves a possibly unsafe child at home while the duty goes unmet.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (A duty exists everywhere, but ages and receiving bodies differ by jurisdiction): all provinces and territories impose a duty to report a child who may be in need of protection, yet the upper age and the body that receives the report are not uniform. Ontario's mandatory duty applies to children under 16 with a discretionary duty for 16 and 17 year olds, while the receiving body may be a children's aid society, an Indigenous child and family well being agency, a Director of Child Welfare or the Director of Youth Protection in Quebec. Assuming one province's threshold or one province's agency name applies nationally is a common and consequential error. Verifying the local statute and the correct receiving body is part of meeting the duty.
  2. The correct answer is A (Make a new report, because the duty is ongoing with each new suspicion): the duty to report is continuing, and a fresh suspicion requires a fresh report regardless of how any previous investigation was resolved. A closed file means the earlier information did not meet the agency's threshold, not that this child is safe now. Informal contact does not discharge a statutory duty and leaves no formal record for the agency to act on. Waiting for a pattern or for a failed parental explanation both apply a higher bar than reasonable grounds to suspect.
  3. The correct answer is C (The approach shapes care for everyone and requires no disclosure at all): trauma and violence informed care assumes trauma is common and that health care itself can re-traumatize, so it changes how you practise with every client whether or not anyone discloses. Its elements are awareness of trauma and violence, safety and trustworthiness, choice and collaboration, and a strengths based approach. A mandatory intake question compels disclosure, which is the opposite of choice and can itself cause harm. Restricting the approach to people with a diagnosis misses most of those affected and defeats its purpose.
  4. The correct answer is B (Refer for psychoeducational assessment and request school accommodations meanwhile): a mismatch between apparent ability and academic output, avoidance of schoolwork and somatic complaints confined to school days is the recognized pattern of a specific learning disorder. Diagnosis requires psychoeducational assessment, and advocating for accommodation while the family waits is part of the primary care role. Reassurance alone leaves the child failing and the symptoms unexplained. Vision and hearing have already been excluded, and abdominal investigation before addressing an obvious school pattern subjects the child to unnecessary testing.
  5. The correct answer is A (Investigate and treat the vitamin B12 deficiency contributing to his presentation): every positive depression screen should be paired with a medical differential, and a low serum B12 with a raised mean corpuscular volume in a man taking metformin and a proton pump inhibitor is the abnormality that fits his symptoms. Thyroid function is within the reference range, so there is no thyroid abnormality to treat. Ferritin is normal and the red cells are macrocytic, so iron deficiency does not explain the anemia. Starting an antidepressant without addressing a treatable mimic risks months of ineffective treatment.
  6. The correct answer is D (Cultural safety is judged by the person receiving care, not by the provider): safety is an outcome experienced by the client, so a provider's belief that he was clear and even handed cannot establish it. Competence is knowledge and humility is self reflection, but neither one guarantees the outcome, which is why training alone is not the answer. Identical treatment for everyone ignores racism as a determinant of health and the history that shapes whether a service feels safe to attend. Recording non compliance turns a systemic issue into a fault of the client and forecloses the question worth asking.
  7. The correct answer is B (Intergenerational trauma affects health now and shapes his avoidance of care): residential schools, forced relocation, the Sixties Scoop, tuberculosis evacuation to southern sanatoria and ongoing systemic racism produce health effects transmitted across generations through disrupted parenting, family separation, cultural loss and chronic stress. Treating that history as background rather than as a present determinant misreads both his pain and his avoidance. A behavioural contract frames avoidance as his failure rather than as a reasonable response to unsafe care. Truth and Reconciliation Commission Calls to Action 18 through 24 point toward recognizing Indigenous healing practices and building safety, not toward routing him to psychiatry first.
  8. The correct answer is C (Act on her concern now, since parental concern predicts genuine delay): surveillance is continuous and includes eliciting and acting on parental concerns, and a parent's worry about speech is itself a strong predictor of true delay. Deferring to a fixed screening age converts surveillance into a scheduling rule and misses children between visits. Pointing and eye contact are reassuring for social communication but do not exclude an expressive language delay. An autism specific tool is used when that specific concern exists, and reduced vocabulary alone is not its defining feature.
  9. The correct answer is A (Audiology now and early intervention referral alongside the diagnostic assessment): loss of previously acquired skills is a red flag at any age, hearing loss is the most treatable cause of language delay and must be excluded early, and early intervention should run in parallel with diagnostic assessment rather than after it. Waiting fourteen months for a label before starting therapy wastes the period when intervention works best. A wait and see approach is never appropriate with regression, a language delay and a worried parent. Holding all other referrals until audiology returns adds delay for no clinical gain.
  10. The correct answer is D (Connect him to housing and drug coverage navigation while keeping him safe): the housing loss and the absence of drug coverage are driving both the untaken medication and the low mood, so the upstream condition outranks a further prescribing step. A medication he cannot afford or safely store will not be taken, and titrating it changes nothing. Screening for social conditions creates an obligation to link the person to something real, such as income support navigation or a community health centre. His mood and diabetes still need treatment, but they follow rather than precede the intervention that makes treatment possible.
Case C021

A 74-year-old woman comes to your clinic with three months of increasing fatigue

and reduced exercise tolerance. She walks with a cane, lives alone, and has

osteoarthritis treated with naproxen 500 mg twice daily. She has no overt bleeding,

no weight loss, and no abdominal pain. Her blood pressure is 128/76 mmHg and heart

rate 92 bpm. Conjunctivae appear pale. You order a complete blood count, ferritin

and renal function.

  1. The correct answer is C (Iron deficiency anemia, which at her age requires investigation for bowel malignancy): a hemoglobin of 92 g/L with a mean corpuscular volume of 73 fL, ferritin 8 micrograms/L and transferrin saturation 9 percent is unambiguous iron deficiency. Anemia of chronic disease does not drive ferritin below 30 micrograms/L, because ferritin rises as an acute phase reactant. The indices are microcytic, so neither aging nor a macrocytic cause fits these numbers. Any new iron deficiency anemia after age 50 requires colorectal investigation even when naproxen offers a convenient alternative explanation.
  2. The correct answer is A (Colorectal cancer is the must not miss diagnosis, outweighing its modest probability): a differential is ranked by probability and by consequence together, so a diagnosis that would seriously harm the client if missed earns investigation even at modest probability. Oral iron does raise the hemoglobin, so a rising count would falsely reassure everyone while a tumour continued to bleed. Gastritis from naproxen is plausible but remains a diagnosis of exclusion and is not a reason to stop looking. Funding rules do not determine whether new iron deficiency after age 50 is investigated.
  3. The correct answer is D (Refer her to the emergency department today for assessment and transfusion): hemoglobin below 70 g/L is a critical result requiring same day action however well the client appears, and she is now symptomatic on exertion. Falling from 92 to 66 g/L in two days signals ongoing brisk blood loss rather than a slow chronic decline. Oral iron takes weeks to raise hemoglobin and cannot address acute loss. Waiting to repeat the count or simply bringing a procedure forward leaves a rapidly dropping hemoglobin unmanaged overnight.
  4. The correct answer is D (Naming the working diagnosis and stating what finding would change it): premature closure is countered by making the working diagnosis explicit and defining in advance the result that would overturn it, which forces the search to continue past the first plausible answer. Broader untargeted testing adds false positives without imposing any discipline on the reasoning itself. Deferring all diagnosis until specialists reply is neither practical nor safe in primary care. Fast intuitive recognition is usually accurate, and the failure here was not using it but failing to switch to deliberate analysis.
Case C022

A 56-year-old man attends for a periodic health review. He drinks four to six beers

most evenings, has a body mass index of 33, and takes rosuvastatin and metformin.

He feels well, denies abdominal pain, and has no jaundice or pruritus. His abdomen

is soft with a liver edge palpable two centimetres below the costal margin.

Screening bloodwork was drawn last week and the results are now available for review.

  1. The correct answer is B (A hepatocellular pattern with a ratio favouring alcohol related liver injury): aminotransferases are raised well above alkaline phosphatase, which defines a hepatocellular pattern, and an aspartate to alanine ratio above two with a markedly raised gamma glutamyl transferase points strongly to alcohol. Cholestatic injury would instead show alkaline phosphatase raised disproportionately, which is not the case at 118 U/L. Albumin of 41 g/L and an INR of 1.0 show synthetic function is preserved. Statin hepatotoxicity is uncommon and typically produces an alanine dominant rise, not this ratio.
  2. The correct answer is A (Liver ultrasound with hepatitis B surface antigen and hepatitis C antibody): chronic viral hepatitis and steatosis are the common treatable causes that sit alongside alcohol in a man with obesity and diabetes, and both ultrasound and serology would change what you do next. Inherited metabolic causes are far rarer and are pursued only once common causes are excluded in a younger client. Invasive biliary imaging is illogical when the pattern is hepatocellular and there is no cholestasis. Waiting a year abandons a persistently abnormal result rather than resolving it.
  3. The correct answer is C (Continue rosuvastatin, since this enzyme pattern reflects alcohol rather than statin injury): his aspartate aminotransferase of 160 U/L is above three times its upper limit, but the aspartate dominant ratio alongside a gamma glutamyl transferase of 288 U/L identifies alcohol as the cause, whereas statin injury is characteristically alanine dominant, so stopping rosuvastatin would remove proven cardiovascular protection in a man with diabetes without touching the real problem. Mild transaminase elevation is not a contraindication to statin use and is more often explained by steatosis or alcohol. Dose reduction without a threshold breach treats the clinician's anxiety rather than the client. Fibrates are not hepatically neutral and do not substitute for statin therapy here.
  4. The correct answer is D (Albumin falls to 29 g/L and INR rises to 1.6 without enzyme change): albumin and INR measure synthetic function rather than injury, so a falling albumin with a rising INR signals failing hepatic function that steatosis and reduced drinking do not explain. Enzymes can be normal while synthetic function deteriorates, which is exactly why the pattern must be read across both groups of tests. Partial improvement in aminotransferase with a residually raised gamma glutamyl transferase is the expected trajectory after reducing alcohol. Alkaline phosphatase of 120 U/L and a stable bilirubin are within the reference interval and reassure rather than alarm.
Case C023

A 68-year-old woman with type 2 diabetes and hypertension attends for routine review.

She takes metformin 1000 mg twice daily, ramipril 10 mg daily and hydrochlorothiazide.

She feels well, has no urinary symptoms, and her blood pressure today is 138/82 mmHg.

She started ibuprofen three weeks ago for shoulder pain. A previous creatinine eight

months ago was 92 micromol/L with an eGFR of 56 mL/min/1.73 m2. New bloodwork and a

urine albumin to creatinine ratio have returned.

  1. The correct answer is D (An acute decline superimposed on chronic kidney disease, needing same day action): her estimated filtration rate has fallen from 56 to 32 mL/min/1.73 m2, a drop of more than 25 percent, which is itself a critical result, and it arrives with a potassium of 5.6 mmol/L and a falling bicarbonate. That combination demands medication review and repeat testing within days, not at an annual visit. Calling this stable ignores the prior value, which is the single most informative number on the page. Assuming laboratory error while the potassium climbs is an unsafe way to resolve an unexpected result.
  2. The correct answer is A (Ibuprofen added to a renin angiotensin blocker and a diuretic): the combination of a nonsteroidal anti inflammatory, an angiotensin converting enzyme inhibitor and a diuretic is a well described triple insult that reduces glomerular perfusion and raises potassium, and the timing matches her three weeks of ibuprofen exactly. Metformin does not injure tubules; it accumulates when filtration falls, so it is a consequence rather than a cause. Obstruction is possible but she has no urinary symptoms and no supporting findings. Albuminuria of this degree reflects long standing diabetic kidney disease, not an acute glomerulonephritis.
  3. The correct answer is A (Hold ramipril temporarily and reduce metformin, with dietary potassium counselling): holding the renin angiotensin blocker while the filtration rate and potassium are unstable is standard sick day guidance, and metformin needs dose reduction between 30 and 44 mL/min/1.73 m2 because it is renally cleared. The word permanently is wrong, since these agents are usually restarted once perfusion recovers and they remain kidney protective in diabetic nephropathy. Continuing unchanged ignores a potassium already above the reference interval. Substituting an angiotensin receptor blocker keeps the identical mechanism and the identical hyperkalemia risk.
  4. The correct answer is C (She has chronic kidney disease stage G3a with severe albuminuria, so restart ramipril): an estimated filtration rate of 49 mL/min/1.73 m2 falls in the 45 to 59 band, and an albumin to creatinine ratio above 30 mg/mmol is the most severe albuminuria category, and her filtration rate has stayed below 60 mL/min/1.73 m2 for more than three months in her record, which is what establishes chronicity. Recovery to baseline does not erase chronic kidney disease, because the prior value was already reduced with persistent albuminuria. The 15 to 29 band would be needed for the more advanced stage, and she is well above it. Renin angiotensin blockade reduces albuminuria and slows progression, so it is restarted rather than withheld.
Case C024

A 62-year-old man presents with six weeks of intermittent palpitations, mild

breathlessness on his usual dog walk, and one episode of light headedness. He has

hypertension treated with amlodipine and no known cardiac history. He drinks two

coffees daily and no alcohol. Today his blood pressure is 134/84 mmHg, oxygen

saturation 97 percent on room air, and his pulse feels irregular. He is alert,

comfortable, and has no chest pain. A twelve lead tracing is obtained.

  1. The correct answer is B (Atrial fibrillation, with absent P waves and an irregularly irregular ventricular response): the strip shows no organized P waves and an irregularly irregular ventricular response, which is diagnostic of atrial fibrillation and fits palpitations, exertional breathlessness and light headedness. Sinus rhythm with ectopy retains discernible P waves before conducted beats. Progressive PR lengthening before a dropped beat requires visible and measurable P waves, which are absent here. Ventricular tachycardia is a regular broad complex rhythm and would not leave him comfortable and alert for six weeks.
  2. The correct answer is D (Serum thyroid stimulating hormone, since thyrotoxicosis can present exactly this way): thyrotoxicosis is a common, cheap to detect and fully correctable precipitant of new atrial fibrillation, so it sits high on the differential on treatability rather than on raw probability. Invasive coronary imaging is not a first line investigation in a comfortable client with no chest pain. Catecholamine secreting tumours are rare and would usually present with paroxysmal hypertension, headache and sweating. Troponin rises with rate related demand ischemia and therefore does not discriminate between the mechanisms being considered.
  3. The correct answer is C (A direct oral anticoagulant, since hypertension is present below age sixty five): Canadian guidance directs oral anticoagulation for anyone aged 65 or older, and below that age for anyone with hypertension, heart failure, diabetes or prior stroke, so his hypertension alone qualifies him. Acetylsalicylic acid is no longer recommended for stroke prevention in atrial fibrillation because it is far less effective and still carries bleeding risk. Preserved systolic function does not lower the thromboembolic risk conferred by the arrhythmia. Antiplatelet combinations increase bleeding without providing adequate protection against cardioembolic stroke.
  4. The correct answer is B (Attend emergency immediately for one sided weakness, slurred speech or black stool): safety netting names the specific findings that mean the situation has changed, states what to do, and names where to go, which is exactly what stroke and major bleeding warnings provide for someone newly anticoagulated. Asking him to call about anything worrying leaves the judgement of what counts as worrying with the person least able to make it. Recording his pulse is useful monitoring but tells him nothing about the two events that could kill him. Directing medication questions to a pharmacy is sensible practice but is not safety netting.
  5. The correct answer is A (Repeat thyroid stimulating hormone with free T4 in six to eight weeks): a mildly raised thyroid stimulating hormone with a normal free T4 is subclinical hypothyroidism, and a substantial proportion of these results normalize on repeat, so confirmation before six to eight weeks of watching is the standard step. Starting replacement now commits a well woman to lifelong therapy on a single unconfirmed value. Naming primary hypothyroidism requires a low free T4, which she does not have. Uptake scanning investigates hyperthyroidism and has no role in an elevated stimulating hormone.
  6. The correct answer is D (Overt hyperthyroidism, which her symptoms and the supplement history both support): a suppressed thyroid stimulating hormone of 0.02 mIU/L with a free T4 of 34 pmol/L and a free T3 of 12.5 pmol/L is biochemically overt hyperthyroidism, and it matches weight loss, heat intolerance and palpitations. Subclinical disease requires the free hormones to be normal, which they clearly are not. Hypothyroidism would show the stimulating hormone raised and the free T4 low, the opposite pattern. Unregulated online supplements can contain thyroid hormone, so the history matters and the source should be identified.
  7. The correct answer is B (Repeat a confirmatory glycated hemoglobin or fasting glucose before making the diagnosis): Diabetes Canada requires a confirmatory repeat test in an asymptomatic person when a single result crosses a diagnostic threshold, because laboratory and biological variation both produce isolated abnormal values. Since 6.8 percent sits above the 6.5 percent cut point, this is not prediabetes, and rescreening in a year would leave probable diabetes untreated. Starting a drug on one unconfirmed result labels him permanently. Glucose tolerance testing is a reasonable alternative confirmatory test but is not needed to exclude a false result specifically.
  8. The correct answer is C (Iron deficiency can falsely raise glycated hemoglobin, so glycemia needs different assessment): glycated hemoglobin depends on red cell lifespan, and iron deficiency prolongs it so that the measured percentage overstates true average glycemia, which explains a value of 6.6 percent alongside a fasting glucose of 5.1 mmol/L. Diagnosing diabetes on an unreliable test in a woman with profound iron deficiency would commit her to lifelong treatment on a laboratory artefact. Two internally consistent results explained by a known interference are not laboratory error. Anemia does not dilute plasma glucose, which is measured in plasma and not in whole blood.
  9. The correct answer is C (Two abnormal glycemic tests confirm diabetes, and the raised transaminase needs workup): a glycated hemoglobin of 7.4 percent and a fasting glucose of 8.2 mmol/L are two different tests both above their diagnostic thresholds, which confirms diabetes without a further sample, and the alanine aminotransferase of 52 U/L must not be dismissed once the glycemic question is answered. Stopping at the first abnormality is search satisficing, and hepatic steatosis commonly accompanies new diabetes. An albumin to creatinine ratio of 1.2 mg/mmol is normal and does not indicate nephropathy. Both glycemic values sit above the prediabetes range, not within it.
  10. The correct answer is A (Use non HDL cholesterol of 5.5 mmol/L, since Friedewald is unreliable here): the Friedewald estimate becomes unreliable once triglycerides exceed roughly 4.5 mmol/L, which is why the laboratory suppressed the LDL value, and non HDL cholesterol of 6.4 minus 0.9 is a validated alternative that remains valid at any triglyceride level. Applying the formula anyway produces a number that looks precise and is not trustworthy. Non fasting sampling is accepted for routine Canadian lipid screening and does not invalidate the panel. The figure of 1.6 mmol/L cannot be derived from these numbers by any method.
  11. The correct answer is D (Search for other causes of delirium rather than treating the bacteriuria): asymptomatic bacteriuria is common in older residents and should not be treated outside pregnancy and specified urological procedures, a Choosing Wisely Canada position, and she has no urinary symptoms or fever. Treating the culture is a classic diagnostic momentum error that closes the search while medication effects, constipation, pain, dehydration or infection elsewhere go unexamined. Repeating the culture answers a question that does not need answering and delays the real assessment. Antibiotics here add adverse effects and resistance without treating her delirium.
  12. The correct answer is C (Second degree atrioventricular block, so arrange urgent cardiology assessment today): the tracing shows intermittently non conducted P waves, which defines second degree atrioventricular block, and combined with near syncope this carries a real risk of progression to complete block, so specialist assessment cannot wait. Sinus bradycardia conducts every P wave and would not produce dropped beats. The rhythm has visible organized P waves, so atrial fibrillation does not fit. Calling symptomatic conduction disease a normal variant in a man with presyncope risks an unmonitored collapse before the next review.
  13. The correct answer is A (Measure methylmalonic acid, since this cobalamin level is in the borderline range): a level between roughly 150 and 220 pmol/L is indeterminate, and methylmalonic acid rises early in true tissue deficiency, so it resolves the ambiguity before committing to lifelong replacement. Neurological deficiency can occur with a normal hemoglobin and a normal cell volume, so a reassuring blood count does not exclude it, particularly with two drugs that reduce absorption. Nerve conduction studies describe the neuropathy but do not identify its cause. Treating first makes the confirmatory level uninterpretable afterward.
  14. The correct answer is B (A critical sodium requiring same day emergency assessment, not outpatient management): a sodium below 120 mmol/L is a critical value demanding same day action whatever the person looks like, and she already has slowed cognition and a fall, both consequences of cerebral edema. The measured osmolality of 248 mmol/kg is genuinely low, which confirms true hypotonic hyponatremia rather than a laboratory artefact. Calling 118 mmol/L mild misreads the number by a wide margin. Both her drugs are plausible contributors, but attributing the value to an expected effect and waiting a week risks seizure in the interval.
  15. The correct answer is D (High anion gap acidosis of 27, indicating ketoacidosis and emergency transfer): the anion gap is sodium 137 minus the sum of chloride 99 and bicarbonate 11, which is 27 and well above normal, and with a glucose of 26.8 mmol/L and large urine ketones this is diabetic ketoacidosis needing hospital care now. Vomiting alone produces a metabolic alkalosis or a normal gap picture, not a gap of this size. Her rapid breathing is respiratory compensation for the acidosis rather than its cause. Ketosis is the defining feature of decompensated type 1 diabetes, so the alternative hyperglycemic syndrome does not fit.
  16. The correct answer is C (The corrected calcium is 2.74 mmol/L, indicating hypercalcemia of malignancy): adding 0.02 mmol/L for every 1 g/L that albumin falls below 40 g/L means adding 0.02 times 16, which is 0.32, so 2.42 becomes 2.74 mmol/L and his thirst, constipation and confusion are explained. Reading the uncorrected total in a man with an albumin of 24 g/L systematically underestimates the true ionized fraction and is the error the correction exists to prevent. Subtracting rather than adding the correction reverses the relationship. Total calcium diverges from the ionized fraction precisely when albumin is abnormal.
  17. The correct answer is B (Hold warfarin, stop clarithromycin and recheck the INR within 24 hours): an INR above 5.0 is a critical result requiring same day action, and the correct step in a client who is not bleeding is to withhold warfarin, remove the interacting macrolide and recheck quickly. Clarithromycin inhibits warfarin metabolism, so leaving it in place lets the INR keep climbing whatever is done to the warfarin dose. Full dose intravenous vitamin K over corrects and risks valve thrombosis in a woman with a mechanical valve. Small dose reductions with a monthly recheck respond far too slowly to a value this high.
  18. The correct answer is A (Arrange interval computed tomography follow up of the incidental pulmonary nodule): the report contains a second finding beyond the one that prompted the film, and a 9 mm noncalcified nodule in a 64 year old smoker requires structured surveillance imaging rather than being filed with the pneumonia. Stopping at the abnormality that explains the symptom is search satisficing, the exact error this report is designed to expose. Plain radiography does not adequately characterize a nodule of this size. Immediate bronchoscopy is disproportionate for a nodule that has not yet been staged or characterized on cross sectional imaging.
  19. The correct answer is C (About 1.4 percent, low enough to stop investigating for pulmonary embolism): pre test odds of 0.1 divided by 0.9 are 0.111, and multiplying by 0.125 gives post test odds of 0.0139, which converts to a probability of roughly 1.4 percent, below the threshold at which further testing is justified. Imaging beyond this point exposes a young woman to contrast and radiation to chase a risk already excluded. Negative results cannot raise probability above the pre test value, so a higher post test figure is arithmetically impossible. Negative likelihood ratios exist precisely to quantify the value of a negative result.
  20. The correct answer is D (Positive predictive value is about 8 percent, because most positives are false): in 10,000 people, 100 have the condition and 90 test positive, while 10 percent of the 9,900 without it produce 990 false positives, so 90 divided by 1,080 is about 8 percent. Sensitivity and specificity are properties of the test and stay roughly stable regardless of how common the condition is, which is exactly why prevalence must be supplied separately. Specificity alone cannot yield predictive value without knowing prevalence. The calculation shown needs only sensitivity, specificity and prevalence.
  21. The correct answer is B (Explain that untargeted testing generates false positives and cascade investigations): Choosing Wisely Canada advises against routine annual blood work in asymptomatic adults, against untargeted vitamin level testing and against thyroid testing without risk factors, because a positive result in a low probability person is usually false. Each false positive then generates repeat tests, imaging, referral, cost and anxiety, so overtesting is not a neutral act of reassurance. Selecting one low value test from the list applies the same flawed logic on a smaller scale. No age threshold converts untargeted panels into indicated testing.
  22. The correct answer is A (Direct him to the emergency department now for an ECG and treatment): a potassium of 6.5 mmol/L is a critical result carrying immediate risk of malignant arrhythmia, and how well he feels does not modify that risk, so he needs cardiac monitoring and treatment today rather than a plan for the week. His estimated filtration rate of 28 mL/min/1.73 m2 means his kidneys cannot excrete the load, so dietary and dose measures act far too slowly. Repeating bloodwork in three days leaves him unmonitored through the highest risk period. Medication changes are necessary but follow emergency management rather than replace it.
  23. The correct answer is D (Iron deficiency is likely, because inflammation raises ferritin and saturation is low): ferritin is an acute phase reactant, so a value up to about 100 micrograms/L can mask true deficiency when the inflammatory markers are raised, and a transferrin saturation of 11 percent is well below the 20 percent threshold that supports deficiency. Applying the usual 30 micrograms/L cut point to an inflamed client is the specific trap this result sets. Inflammatory anemia alone is typically normocytic and would not drive saturation this low. Thalassemia trait produces microcytosis with normal or raised iron studies, not a saturation of 11 percent.
  24. The correct answer is C (Giant cell arteritis threatens vision, so start corticosteroids and refer urgently): jaw claudication, scalp tenderness and a sedimentation rate of 88 mm/h in a woman over 65 make giant cell arteritis the must not miss diagnosis, and irreversible blindness can occur within days, so treatment begins before confirmation. Biopsy remains diagnostic and stays positive for one to two weeks after starting steroids, so waiting buys nothing and risks sight. Attributing a new headache with these specific features to tension type headache is premature closure. Raised intracranial pressure does not produce jaw claudication or this inflammatory picture.
  25. The correct answer is B (Send her to hospital now for febrile neutropenia and stop methimazole): a neutrophil count below 0.5 x 10^9/L with fever is a critical result requiring same day action, and antithyroid drug induced agranulocytosis can progress to overwhelming sepsis within hours. The offending drug must stop immediately rather than be reduced, because the reaction is idiosyncratic rather than dose dependent. Oral antibiotics prescribed from the community do not provide the broad spectrum intravenous cover and monitoring she needs. Waiting for a swab result treats a sore throat while ignoring the count that makes it dangerous.
  26. The correct answer is A (Sinus bradycardia is an expected training adaptation, so no further testing needed): the strip shows a slow but regular rhythm with a P wave conducting to every QRS complex, which is sinus bradycardia and is a normal finding in a highly trained endurance athlete with no symptoms. Bradycardia only becomes a pacing question when it produces syncope, presyncope or exercise intolerance, none of which he reports. Complete block shows P waves marching independently of the ventricular complexes, which is not the pattern here. Regular rhythm with consistent P waves excludes atrial fibrillation.
  27. The correct answer is C (The corrected QT is 480 ms and prolonged, so review medications and correct electrolytes): at a rate of 60 the RR interval is 1.0 second and its square root is 1.0, so the Bazett corrected value equals the measured 480 ms, which is prolonged and demands action. Both her drugs prolong repolarization and both hypokalemia and hypomagnesemia lower the threshold for torsades de pointes, so the electrolytes are part of the problem rather than incidental. Calling the value normal ignores a correction that happens to leave the number unchanged at this particular rate. Abrupt cessation of a serotonergic antidepressant causes a discontinuation syndrome and neglects the electrolytes entirely.
  28. The correct answer is B (Mean corpuscular volume, because cell size sorts anemia into distinct causal groups): cell size divides the differential immediately, with values under 80 fL pointing to iron deficiency or thalassemia trait, normal values to chronic disease, blood loss or renal disease, and values over 100 fL to cobalamin or folate deficiency, alcohol, hypothyroidism or medications. Reticulocytes are genuinely useful but subdivide within those groups rather than defining them. Ferritin is the right second test once microcytosis is established but wastes information if ordered blind. Sedimentation rate is nonspecific and rarely changes what is done next.
  29. The correct answer is D (The label is unsupported, so the differential for her breathlessness must be reopened): a ratio of 0.84 that does not change after bronchodilator excludes airflow obstruction, and with normal lung volumes and diffusion there is no evidence for the diagnosis she has carried for six years. This is diagnostic momentum, where a label applied by someone else becomes progressively harder to question, and the countermeasure is to ask what objective evidence ever established it. Escalating inhaled therapy treats a disease she does not have. Normal spirometry with normal diffusion does not establish asthma either, so heart failure, deconditioning and anemia now need consideration.
  30. The correct answer is C (Acknowledgement by a named clinician with a documented action and client notification): a closed loop system tracks each ordered test to a result, requires a named clinician to acknowledge it, and generates a documented action plus a documented message to the client, and every one of those steps failed here. The laboratory did flag the result, so flagging was present and by itself achieved nothing. Moving filing rights to administrative staff removes clinical accountability rather than adding it. Repeating every abnormal result before filing multiplies work without ever closing the loop with the person who needs to know.
  31. The correct answer is A (Restate the presentation without labels and apply an explicit embolism rule): the framing effect means the triage wording has raised your threshold for investigating genuine tachycardia and tachypnoea in a woman four weeks postpartum, and the countermeasure is to restate the case in neutral terms and then use a structured rule rather than a felt sense of probability. Pregnancy and the postpartum period are strong risk factors for venous thromboembolism, which is the must not miss diagnosis here. Asking a colleague to read the same labelled chart imports the same framing. Broad untargeted testing and an anxiolytic trial both leave the anchoring intact.
  32. The correct answer is B (Ask her to restate in her own words the problem and next steps): teach back asks the client to restate the information in her own language, and her answer shows what was actually understood rather than what was said, which is precisely what the practice statement on ascertaining understanding requires. Nodding and saying yes are social responses and are poor evidence of comprehension. Asking whether there are questions places the burden on someone who does not yet know what she has missed. Repeating more slowly or handing over written material delivers information again without ever testing whether it landed.
  33. The correct answer is D (Arrange review within twelve hours with named findings prompting earlier attendance): planned reassessment turns time into a diagnostic test, letting an evolving illness declare itself under supervision, and pairing it with specific return criteria gives him a threshold he can actually apply at home. Advice to return if worse is not safety netting, because it leaves the judgement entirely with the person least equipped to make it. Admission for every undifferentiated abdominal pain is neither feasible nor necessary for a well young man living close by. Empiric antibiotics blunt the signs that the reassessment depends on and delay diagnosis by a week.
Case C025

A 68 year old woman is booked for a routine visit. Her chart lists COPD, diagnosed by a previous clinician four years ago after a chest infection. She has never had spirometry. She reports worsening breathlessness on stairs over three months, waking at night short of breath, and two new pillows. She has smoked five cigarettes daily for twenty years and takes salbutamol and a combination inhaler. Blood pressure is 152/94 mmHg.

  1. The correct answer is A (Elevated jugular venous pressure with bibasilar inspiratory crackles at both lung bases): raised venous pressure with dependent crackles points to volume overload from heart failure rather than to airflow obstruction. Orthopnea, nocturnal breathlessness and the need for extra pillows already fit that pattern, and this finding would strengthen it substantially. A prolonged expiratory phase, wheeze and hyperresonance are features of obstructive lung disease and would support the label already sitting in the chart. Clubbing belongs to neither condition and would redirect the search toward malignancy or interstitial disease.
  2. The correct answer is D (Airflow obstruction is absent, so the recorded diagnosis is not supported): a post bronchodilator FEV1 to FVC ratio of 0.78 sits well above the 0.70 threshold, so fixed airflow obstruction is not present and chronic obstructive pulmonary disease cannot be diagnosed. Continuing inhaled therapy for obstruction that does not exist simply perpetuates the original labelling error. A normal FVC alongside a normal ratio gives no evidence of restriction, so lung volumes are not indicated. Both pre and post bronchodilator values appear on the report, so reversibility was in fact assessed.
  3. The correct answer is B (Heart failure is likely; arrange echocardiography to characterize ventricular function): a natriuretic peptide of 1840 ng/L in a client with orthopnea and nocturnal breathlessness makes heart failure very probable, and echocardiography defines ejection fraction and structural cause. Mild iron deficiency at a hemoglobin of 116 g/L is real and needs its own workup, but it does not produce orthopnea or nocturnal breathlessness. Age raises natriuretic peptide modestly, not to fourteen times the upper limit. Calling the panel non diagnostic would carry the original respiratory label forward unexamined.
  4. The correct answer is A (Acknowledge her frustration, explain the new findings, then ask her to restate the plan): responding to emotion before adding facts is what allows information to be heard, and teach back is the only way to confirm what actually transferred. Moving straight to treatment dismisses a legitimate reaction and leaves her understanding untested. Written material and a follow up appointment are useful additions but neither checks comprehension in the room. Defending the previous clinician or the inhalers answers a question she did not ask and still verifies nothing.
Case C026

A 34 year old woman attends a walk in clinic with one day of right sided pleuritic chest pain. She has no leg swelling, no hemoptysis, no prior venous thromboembolism, no recent surgery or immobility, and no malignancy. Heart rate is 88, respiratory rate 18, oxygen saturation 98 percent on room air, and blood pressure 118/74 mmHg. She takes a combined oral contraceptive. Chest examination is normal.

  1. The correct answer is C (She fails the rule because she uses exogenous estrogen, so testing is required): the rule out criteria are only negative when every single element is absent, and use of exogenous hormone is one of those elements. Her age, heart rate, oxygen saturation, absence of leg swelling, absence of hemoptysis and absence of prior clot or recent surgery are all reassuring, but one positive element defeats the whole rule. Respiratory rate is not one of the rule out criteria at all, so her rate of 18 is irrelevant to whether the rule is satisfied. Her pre test probability is low, which is precisely the setting where the rule may be applied.
  2. The correct answer is A (Approximately 1.4 percent, low enough to exclude embolism without any imaging): the negative likelihood ratio is one minus sensitivity divided by specificity, which is 0.05 divided by 0.40, equal to 0.125. Pre test odds are 0.10 divided by 0.90, which is 0.111, and multiplying by 0.125 gives post test odds of 0.014. Converting back gives about 1.4 percent, below the accepted threshold for imaging. Quoting the false negative rate of five percent confuses a test property with a post test probability, and a likelihood ratio is never multiplied by 100.
  3. The correct answer is B (Diagnose musculoskeletal chest wall pain, give analgesia, and provide specific safety netting): once embolism sits below the testing threshold, chest wall pain is the most probable remaining explanation in a well young adult, and safety netting manages the residual uncertainty. Imaging after a negative result in a low probability client adds radiation, cost and incidental findings without changing management. Leg ultrasonography in someone with no leg symptoms and an excluded embolism has a very low yield. Anticoagulating a client with no confirmed thrombosis exposes her to bleeding for no established benefit.
  4. The correct answer is D (Availability bias, addressed by applying an explicit validated decision rule): two recent cases made the diagnosis easy to recall, which inflated the felt probability well beyond what this presentation warranted. Replacing a felt sense of likelihood with a scored rule is the specific countermeasure for that error. Anchoring involves fixing on an early feature of this client and failing to adjust, which did not happen here. Search satisficing describes stopping after the first abnormality found, and no abnormal result had been generated at the point the impression formed.
Case C027

An 84 year old man is brought in by his daughter with two days of confusion. The referral note from the walk in clinic reads treat urinary tract infection, urine dipstick positive. He has hypertension, atrial fibrillation and mild cognitive impairment. He denies dysuria, frequency and flank pain. Temperature is 36.9 degrees Celsius, heart rate 104 and irregular, blood pressure 128/76 mmHg. He started oxybutynin ten days ago.

  1. The correct answer is B (Reframe the presentation as undifferentiated delirium and rebuild the differential from scratch): restating a case in neutral language, without the label another clinician attached, is the direct countermeasure for framing and for diagnostic momentum. The presenting problem is new confusion in an older adult, which has a broad differential including medication effects, metabolic derangement and stroke. Waiting for a culture still keeps infection as the organizing hypothesis and leaves the framing intact. Confirming the dipstick result verifies a number that was never diagnostic, and starting antibiotics acts on the inherited conclusion outright.
  2. The correct answer is C (Hyponatremia and acute kidney injury are present and need explanation before any infection label): the sodium of 129 mmol/L and a creatinine that has risen from 96 to 148 micromol/L are both plausible contributors to delirium and both demand a cause. He is afebrile with a normal white cell count and no urinary symptoms, so a positive dipstick most likely reflects asymptomatic bacteriuria. Chronic kidney disease cannot be diagnosed from a single elevated value, since the abnormality must persist for three months. Calling this panel normal would ignore two clinically significant derangements.
  3. The correct answer is A (Stop the oxybutynin, review all anticholinergics, rehydrate cautiously and reassess cognition): an anticholinergic started ten days before the onset of confusion in an 84 year old is a highly probable and fully reversible cause, and removing it costs nothing. Treating bacteriuria in a client with no urinary symptoms is explicitly advised against and would not address the delirium. Nitrofurantoin is also a poor choice when renal function has fallen acutely. Head imaging without focal neurology and psychiatric referral for a medical delirium both delay the intervention most likely to work.
  4. The correct answer is D (Ask what she already understands, explain the new findings, then use teach back): establishing her current understanding first shows exactly which belief needs to change, and teach back confirms that the revised explanation landed. Prescribing an antibiotic she does not need to keep the peace exposes her father to harm and reinforces the wrong model. A correct explanation delivered without checking comprehension often leaves the original belief intact, and a handout does not verify anything. Waiting for a culture postpones a conversation that the current findings already support.
  5. The correct answer is C (Address the raised corrected calcium urgently, because it requires same day action): a corrected calcium above 3.0 mmol/L is a critical result demanding action on the day it is reported, whatever the client looks like. Fatigue and constipation fit hypercalcemia at least as well as they fit the thyroid result, so stopping at the first abnormality is the classic search satisficing error. A TSH of 9.8 with a free T4 inside the reference interval is subclinical and warrants a repeat rather than immediate treatment. A hemoglobin of 129 g/L is normal and generates no referral. The creatinine of 104 umol/L is mildly above the female reference interval and needs follow up, including a check for hypercalcemia related dehydration, but it is not the same day priority.
  6. The correct answer is B (Does it start at a predictable level of exertion and settle with rest?): a reproducible exertional threshold with prompt relief on rest is the defining feature of stable angina and is rarely produced by chest wall pain, so the answer changes the probability substantially whichever way it falls. Chest wall tenderness is common in people who also have coronary disease, so reproducing pain on palpation does not exclude ischemia. Nocturnal worsening points toward reflux or heart failure without separating the two hypotheses in question. Antacid response is unreliable, since ischemic pain sometimes eases coincidentally.
  7. The correct answer is D (Positive predictive value rises from about 8 percent to about 79 percent): among 10,000 people screened at 1 percent prevalence the test yields 90 true positives and 990 false positives, giving a positive predictive value of 8.3 percent. In the clinic population there are 2,700 true positives and 700 false positives, giving 79.4 percent from the identical test. Sensitivity and specificity are properties of the test and do not shift with prevalence or disease severity. Negative predictive value moves in the opposite direction, falling as prevalence rises.
  8. The correct answer is A (Anchoring, because the initial label was never revised as new findings appeared): the migraine hypothesis was fixed at the opening sentence, and fever with neck stiffness was filed as incidental rather than allowed to move the diagnosis toward meningitis. Naming the anchor and asking which finding would move you off it is the countermeasure. No recent case is described as driving the estimate of likelihood, so recall based judgment is not the mechanism here. No abnormal test result was generated and no behaviour of the client was blamed.
  9. The correct answer is A (Second degree atrioventricular block is present; the triage wording framed the assessment): intermittently non conducted P waves identify second degree block, which readily explains dizziness and syncope in this man. Labelling him as anxious and hyperventilating before assessment raises the threshold for investigating a cardiac cause, which is exactly how framing operates. The rhythm is not irregularly irregular and shows organized atrial activity, so fibrillation does not fit. The complexes are narrow at a modest rate, so ventricular tachycardia is not present and emergency cardioversion is not indicated.
  10. The correct answer is C (Switch to analytic reasoning, because key features sit outside the recognized script): night sweats and weight loss over three weeks do not belong to the illness script for streptococcal pharyngitis, and an atypical feature is the signal to move deliberately from fast intuitive processing to slow hypothesis testing. Staying with the initial pattern would carry the mismatch forward unexamined. A scoring tool addresses only the probability of streptococcal infection and cannot evaluate the systemic features. Fast reasoning is accurate most of the time, so replacing it with reflex broad testing is neither necessary nor safe.
  11. The correct answer is B (A positive second test shifts probability enough to change management; the first does not): a positive likelihood ratio above 10 usually moves probability across a decision threshold, while a ratio between 0.5 and 2 leaves the estimate essentially where it started. A ratio of 1.3 therefore buys almost nothing for the same time and cost. A low ratio signals a weak test, not a clean one, so it does not mean fewer false positives. A powerful ratio is most valuable at intermediate probability, where a decision genuinely hangs in the balance.
  12. The correct answer is A (Premature closure, because the search stopped once a plausible answer had appeared): a positive positional test produced an acceptable diagnosis, and the evaluation ended there even though persistent unsteadiness between attacks does not belong to that condition and suggests a central or cerebellar process. Asking what else this could be, and what the worst thing it could be, is the countermeasure. Anchoring involves failing to adjust from an early feature rather than closing after confirmation. No recent case and no external label are described, so recall driven and wording driven errors do not apply.
  13. The correct answer is C (Address the apical pneumothorax as well, and arrange urgent reassessment today): the findings section records a pneumothorax that the impression omits, and a traumatic pneumothorax needs observation and repeat assessment rather than routine discharge. Reading only the summary line is search satisficing carried out by the reporting radiologist and then repeated by the clinician. Treating this as an isolated rib fracture with a one week review risks enlargement at home. Delaying all decisions for six hours without acting, and referring an undisplaced single rib fracture for surgery, both misjudge urgency.
  14. The correct answer is D (Explain that no result would change management, and offer age appropriate screening instead): a test is indicated only when some result would alter what you do, and Choosing Wisely Canada advises against routine annual bloodwork, routine vitamin D testing, thyroid testing in asymptomatic low risk people and routine electrocardiograms in low risk clients. Ordering the panel generates false positives, incidental findings, cascade testing, cost and anxiety. Choosing one test from the list because it is cheap or because the disease is serious applies neither probability nor consequence correctly. Redirecting to cervical screening addresses her underlying concern usefully.
  15. The correct answer is B (Residual risk after a negative result stays unacceptably high at the higher probability): with a negative likelihood ratio near 0.125, a 10 percent pre test probability falls to about 1.4 percent, which sits below the testing threshold, while a 60 percent pre test probability falls only to about 16 percent. A one in six residual chance of embolism is not a safe discharge, so that client goes directly to imaging without a D dimer being drawn. Sensitivity and specificity are stable properties of the assay and do not differ between clients. Likelihood ratios apply at every pre test probability.
  16. The correct answer is D (The anemia is macrocytic from B12 deficiency; stop the iron and treat the deficiency): a mean corpuscular volume of 106 fL with a B12 of 118 pmol/L identifies the mechanism, and both metformin and long term proton pump inhibitors lower B12. The ferritin of 168 micrograms per litre shows iron stores are replete, so more iron cannot help and the original label has simply been carried forward for two years unquestioned. Anemia of chronic disease is typically normocytic and does not explain the macrocytosis. The cause is identified, so hematology referral before treatment is unnecessary.
  17. The correct answer is A (Name it a working diagnosis, say what would change it, and arrange review): naming the diagnosis as provisional is honest about where the reasoning actually stands, and it tells her the conclusion may move. Stating the findings that would change it gives her something concrete to watch for and makes her a participant in the diagnostic process. Booking review closes the loop so an evolving arthritis is caught rather than lost. Presenting an uncertain diagnosis as settled risks anchoring both of you, while refusing to say anything or reciting the whole differential leaves her with no usable plan.
  18. The correct answer is C (Fundamental attribution error, because the cause was placed in the person, not circumstances): recording nonadherence attributes the poor control to a personal choice when the actual driver is loss of drug coverage. Asking about cost, access, housing and understanding before concluding that someone declined to follow a plan is the countermeasure, and it changes the intervention from education to a coverage application. No prior hypothesis was being defended against contrary evidence, so selective weighting of evidence is not the mechanism. No inherited label and no recall of recent cases feature in this scenario.
  19. The correct answer is B (Base rates rank the list, while must not miss items stay despite low probability): iron deficiency, poor sleep, mood disorder and recent viral illness dominate the probability list for three weeks of fatigue in a well 24 year old, and leukemia stays on the list only as a low probability item to be kept in view. Rare conditions remain rare even when a presentation is slightly atypical, so unusual features alone do not promote them. Ordering purely by danger would put catastrophes at the top of every list and drive indiscriminate testing. Deleting unsupported possibilities discards the must not miss category entirely.
  20. The correct answer is A (A normal strip taken between episodes does not exclude paroxysmal atrial fibrillation): the tracing shows regular sinus rhythm with a P wave before every narrow complex, which tells you only about this moment and says nothing about an intermittent arrhythmia. Her description of rapid irregular episodes lasting an hour warrants ambulatory or patient activated monitoring across a longer window. The rhythm is regular with visible P waves, so fibrillation is not present today and anticoagulation is not decided on this strip. Every P wave conducts, so no block is demonstrated.
  21. The correct answer is D (Attend emergency today for a non blanching rash, drowsiness, or refusal to drink): real safety netting names specific findings, a time frame and a destination, so the caregiver can recognize deterioration without having to interpret it. Advice to return if worse leaves the judgment entirely with the family and is not safety netting at all. Height of fever is a poor marker of serious illness in this age group and would delay presentation of a drowsy child whose temperature has fallen. A routine appointment in one week is far too slow for an evolving bacterial infection.
  22. The correct answer is B (Deliberately seek the finding that would disprove depression and weight it equally): explaining away a normal screening score and dismissing a ferritin of 18 micrograms per litre are both instances of discounting evidence that contradicts a favoured hypothesis. Actively hunting for disconfirming evidence and giving it the same weight as supporting evidence is the specific countermeasure. Repeating the screening tool or seeking a colleague's endorsement of the same hypothesis both search for confirmation rather than challenge. Ordering broad untargeted testing substitutes volume for reasoning and generates incidental findings.
  23. The correct answer is C (Explain the finding and arrange biochemical assessment for hormone excess as recommended): an adrenal nodule of 22 mm found incidentally requires functional testing even when the client has no clinical features, because subclinical hormone secretion is common and treatable. Low attenuation favours a benign adenoma, so escalating straight to functional imaging or surgery investigates a lesion that has not yet been characterized biochemically. Withholding the finding removes her ability to participate in her own care and is not defensible. The cascade risk of incidental findings is managed by following a defined pathway, not by ignoring them.
  24. The correct answer is A (Feeling rushed, which is a recognized trigger for taking a diagnostic pause): time pressure is one of the named conditions under which fast reasoning fails, alongside atypical presentations, a client who is not improving as predicted, an inherited label and high stakes. A ten second stop before committing is the highest yield habit available in that moment. A close script match with no unusual features is precisely when fast reasoning performs well. Familiarity with a client is not itself a trigger, and intuitive reasoning is accurate most of the time rather than something to avoid.
  25. The correct answer is D (Give the result in plain language, then pause and respond to his emotion): after the warning shot the next step is delivering knowledge in small plain chunks, and emotion must be addressed before any further information is added. Silence after bad news is a clinical intervention rather than an awkward gap. Treatment detail and survival figures given at this moment will not be retained, and a single number is a poor way to convey prognosis. Asking about support is important, but the invitation to have others present belongs before disclosure begins, not after the warning shot.
  26. The correct answer is C (Acknowledge the emotion, then offer a range and what it means functionally): responding to distress before adding information is what allows the answer to be heard, and prognosis is best conveyed as a range with functional meaning rather than as a single number. Checking how much detail he wants keeps the disclosure matched to his stated preference. A precise figure in months conveys false certainty and is often remembered as a deadline. Refusing to answer abandons a direct request, and redirecting to treatment avoids the question he actually asked.
  27. The correct answer is B (Treat the iron deficiency and confirm glycemia with a repeat fasting plasma glucose): HbA1c is unreliable whenever red cell turnover is abnormal, and iron deficiency falsely raises it, which explains why the HbA1c sits in the diabetes range while the fasting glucose of 6.4 mmol/L sits in the prediabetes range. Glucose based testing is the trustworthy measure in this person. Diagnosing diabetes on a single unreliable value in an asymptomatic client ignores the requirement for confirmation. Labelling prediabetes or repeating the HbA1c both continue to rely on the compromised measure.
  28. The correct answer is A (Track every ordered test to a result acknowledged by a named responsible clinician): safety in results management comes from a closed loop system rather than from individual vigilance, and naming an accountable clinician for every pending result is what survives holidays and locum cover. The loop also requires a documented action and a documented message to the client. Depending on the laboratory or imaging service to telephone leaves the practice with no record of what remains outstanding. Putting the responsibility on clients assumes they know a test was ordered, and asking one person to look harder is not a system change.
  29. The correct answer is C (High sensitivity, accepting that many positives will need confirmatory testing afterwards): a highly sensitive test misses few true cases, so a negative result meaningfully lowers probability and can exclude the condition when the pre test probability is already low. The cost is a lower specificity, which generates false positives that a second more specific test must sort out. A specific test is designed to confirm rather than exclude, so it is the wrong first step here. Positive predictive value varies with prevalence rather than staying stable, and a ratio near one carries no information at all.
  30. The correct answer is D (The differential considered, why dissection was judged unlikely, and the safety netting given): a note recording the working diagnosis, the differential, the reasoning that ranked it, what was excluded and how, and the planned follow up is both better practice and stronger medicolegal protection than a clean note naming only the answer. A bare assertion that dissection was excluded records a conclusion without the evidence behind it. An exhaustive list of every possible cause is unusable and does not show reasoning. Pertinent negatives are exactly the findings that demonstrate the differential was worked through.
  31. The correct answer is D (Ventricular tachycardia; this is an emergency requiring immediate transfer and monitoring): the strip shows a rapid regular broad complex rhythm without visible P waves, which in a 71 year old with dizziness and a borderline blood pressure must be treated as ventricular tachycardia. Accepting the triage label of dehydration is diagnostic momentum operating on a life threatening rhythm. Sinus tachycardia would show narrow complexes with a P wave before each one, and fibrillation would be irregularly irregular. Second degree block produces intermittently dropped beats at a slower rate, not a sustained broad complex tachycardia.
  32. The correct answer is B (About 69 percent, a shift large enough to change management in most settings): pre test odds are 0.20 divided by 0.80, which equals 0.25, and multiplying by the positive likelihood ratio of 9 gives post test odds of 2.25. Converting back, 2.25 divided by 3.25 is about 69 percent. Adding the ratio to the percentage is not how the calculation works and badly underestimates the shift. A ratio of 9 is powerful but does not carry a 20 percent starting probability to near certainty, and it moves probability far more than modestly.
  33. The correct answer is D (Giant cell arteritis, because any delay risks irreversible and permanent visual loss): new temporal headache after age 50 with jaw claudication and polymyalgic stiffness is the classic script, and the consequence of missing it is sudden permanent blindness that treatment prevents. A normal examination and normal vision today provide no reassurance, since visual loss occurs abruptly. Tension type and cervicogenic patterns do not produce jaw claudication and do not carry that consequence. Medication overuse requires a history of frequent analgesic use, which is not described here.
  34. The correct answer is C (Arrange a planned reassessment within 24 hours with explicit return instructions): a scheduled recheck turns time into a diagnostic test, letting an early appendicitis or other evolving pathology declare itself under supervision instead of at home. The return instructions cover deterioration between now and that visit. Imaging an equivocal presentation immediately exposes a young adult to radiation and to incidental findings when the examination may well be diagnostic tomorrow. Empiric antibiotics blur the evolving picture, and discharge with no planned follow up leaves the uncertainty entirely unmanaged.
  35. The correct answer is A (Ask what objective evidence established the original diagnosis, and arrange cardiac assessment): the note shows the label was applied clinically with no electroencephalography and no cardiac evaluation, and collapse while standing with brief jerking is a recognized presentation of arrhythmic or vasovagal syncope. Asking what evidence established an inherited diagnosis is the countermeasure for diagnostic momentum, and continued events despite treatment is itself a signal the diagnosis may be wrong. Increasing or switching the medication assumes the diagnosis is right. Referring for pregnancy planning without revisiting the label carries the original error into a pregnancy.
  36. The correct answer is B (Estimate probability using exposure risk and case definition, not your recent recall): ease of recall is inflating the felt likelihood, and the countermeasure for that is an explicit rule, here the established risk area map and the clinical case definition. Gardening in an area with no established tick population makes exposure improbable, and a non specific rash is not erythema migrans. Testing on a low pre test probability yields mostly false positives and starts a cascade. Ruling the diagnosis out because other cases occurred is the mirror error, since recent cases neither raise nor lower this client's individual risk.
  37. The correct answer is C (Investigate for colorectal malignancy alongside iron studies and renal function testing): any new anemia in an adult over 50 keeps colorectal malignancy on the differential until it is excluded, because the consequence of missing it is severe and occult bleeding often produces no visible blood. Iron studies and renal function cover the common and treatable causes at the same time, so probability and danger are addressed together. Empiric iron treats a mechanism that has not been demonstrated and delays diagnosis by weeks. Labelling it chronic disease without investigation, or sending him to hematology before basic testing, both skip the necessary work.
Case C028

Marisa Cardinal, 58, has type 2 diabetes diagnosed nine years ago and hypertension.

Her eGFR is 44 mL/min/1.73 m2 and her urine albumin to creatinine ratio is 38 mg/mmol.

Her A1C is 8.6 percent on metformin 1000 mg twice daily, and she takes ramipril 5 mg daily for her blood pressure. She works casual shifts at a

grocery store, has no private drug plan, and pays out of pocket for anything not on the

provincial formulary. She tells you her goal is to avoid dialysis and keep working.

  1. The correct answer is C (Her provincial drug coverage and the monthly cost she can sustain): Canada has no national drug plan, so coverage varies by province, age, income and private insurance, and a plan she cannot pay for will never be followed. Knowing what is listed and what needs a special authority request decides which agent can realistically be started today. Glucose logs, family history and weight trend refine the clinical picture but do not decide whether the prescription gets filled. Cost is the commonest reason a technically correct plan fails.
  2. The correct answer is A (Albuminuria with reduced eGFR makes kidney protection the deciding treatment factor): an eGFR of 44 mL/min/1.73 m2 with an albumin to creatinine ratio of 38 mg/mmol is albuminuric chronic kidney disease, so the second agent is chosen for proven organ protection rather than glucose lowering alone. Choosing on potency alone would favour agents with no kidney benefit. Duration of diabetes does not by itself mandate insulin. Metformin can continue with dose review at this level of kidney function.
  3. The correct answer is D (Submit the special authority application today and optimize her ACE inhibitor dose): the paperwork is the barrier, so it is started immediately rather than at the next visit, and the waiting interval is used to do something useful for her kidneys. Blood pressure control and maximal tolerated renin angiotensin blockade slow albuminuric disease while coverage is pending. A sulfonylurea adds hypoglycemia risk without kidney protection. Asking a client with no plan to pay retail predictably ends in one filled month and nothing after.
  4. The correct answer is B (Continue the drug, since an early eGFR dip with falling albuminuria is expected): SGLT2 inhibitors produce a small hemodynamic fall in eGFR over the first weeks that then stabilizes, and the falling albumin to creatinine ratio shows the kidney is being protected. Stopping the drug would forfeit that protection for a predictable and benign change. Nephrology referral is not triggered by an expected early dip in a well client. Dose reduction is unnecessary when the trajectory matches the known effect.
Case C029

Wendell Roy, 69, has COPD confirmed by post bronchodilator spirometry showing an FEV1 to

FVC ratio of 0.58. He has had two exacerbations in the past year, one requiring admission.

He is short of breath walking to his mailbox and has stopped attending his weekly card

game. He smokes fifteen cigarettes daily. He lives alone in a rural community sixty

kilometres from the nearest hospital and does not drive.

  1. The correct answer is B (His current inhaler technique and how he actually uses each device): much apparent treatment failure in obstructive lung disease is device failure, so technique is checked before therapy is escalated. Repeating spirometry adds nothing when the ratio is already confirmed below 0.7. A radiograph and a blood gas may matter later but neither changes what is prescribed at this visit. Escalating a drug he is not inhaling properly raises his cost and delays real improvement.
  2. The correct answer is D (Enrolling him in a pulmonary rehabilitation program he can actually attend): rehabilitation improves exercise capacity, symptoms and quality of life and reduces readmission after exacerbation more reliably than most inhaler adjustments, and it matches the goal he has named. An added bronchodilator may ease breathlessness but has a smaller effect on function. A rescue pack shortens exacerbations without improving exercise tolerance. Home oxygen is indicated for chronic hypoxemia, not for breathlessness alone.
  3. The correct answer is A (Arrange a virtual or community based rehabilitation program he can access locally): transportation is a named barrier, and a named barrier is solved rather than recorded. Virtual and community delivered rehabilitation produce meaningful gains for clients who cannot travel to a hospital program. Referring to a distant program he cannot reach guarantees a missed appointment. A handout is not equivalent to a supervised program, and recording refusal misrepresents a system problem as a client choice.
  4. The correct answer is C (Consult respirology with a specific question while remaining his responsible provider): consultation is asking a colleague for advice on a defined question while responsibility for the client stays with you, which is exactly the intention described. Referral sends him for another provider to manage a defined problem, and transfer hands responsibility over entirely. Neither fits a plan to keep managing him. Emergency assessment is for acute deterioration, not for a considered question about long term therapy.
Case C030

Helene Barbeau, 76, has heart failure with an ejection fraction of 30 percent, atrial

fibrillation, and stage 3 chronic kidney disease. She takes bisoprolol, furosemide and

apixaban. She lives with her daughter, who works days. Over three weeks she has gained

four kilograms, sleeps on two pillows, and has ankle swelling to mid calf. She has no

private drug plan and receives the provincial seniors benefit. Her goal is to stay out of

hospital.

  1. The correct answer is A (The four kilogram weight gain recorded over the past three weeks): retained fluid registers on the scale before it appears as breathlessness or on examination, which is why daily weights anchor every heart failure self management plan. Orthopnoea and ankle edema confirm the picture but appear later. Ejection fraction describes severity rather than current volume status. Atrial fibrillation and kidney disease shape management but do not track decompensation from week to week.
  2. The correct answer is D (Three of the four foundational disease modifying classes are missing): she takes a beta blocker but has no renin angiotensin system blocker or angiotensin receptor neprilysin inhibitor, no mineralocorticoid receptor antagonist, and no SGLT2 inhibitor. Furosemide relieves congestion without changing survival, and apixaban treats her atrial fibrillation rather than her heart failure. Adding a single agent would still leave two classes untouched. Canadian practice now starts all four classes early rather than perfecting one at a time.
  3. The correct answer is C (Start low doses of the missing classes together, choosing formulary listed agents): current heart failure practice favours early initiation of all foundational classes at low dose with later titration, because benefit appears quickly and sequential maximization delays it by months. Choosing agents already listed on the seniors formulary keeps the plan affordable and avoids abandoned prescriptions. Waiting for congestion to clear or for a cardiology appointment leaves her unprotected. Titrating one drug to maximum first is the superseded approach.
  4. The correct answer is B (Daily weights paired with a written action plan naming when to call): self monitoring combined with explicit instructions on what change triggers what action prevents more admissions than any scheduled visit, and it gives the daughter a concrete threshold rather than a judgement call. Monthly examination misses the two to three day window in which early congestion can still be treated at home. Imaging on demand does not change management. Blanket fluid restriction is not routine and can worsen kidney function in an older adult on diuretics.
Case C031

Devon Achebe, 47, has no chronic conditions and takes no medication. Averaged home readings

on a validated upper arm device over seven days are 134/82 mmHg. He does not smoke, his

body mass index is 27, his fasting glucose is 5.4 mmol/L, his LDL cholesterol is 2.9

mmol/L, and his calculated ten year cardiovascular risk is 6 percent. He has a family

history of stroke and asks whether he needs a pill.

  1. The correct answer is B (His readings meet the current diagnostic threshold for hypertension in adults): the 2025 Hypertension Canada guideline defines hypertension as a blood pressure of 130/80 mmHg or higher measured with a validated device under optimal conditions, and his averaged home readings of 134/82 mmHg sit above that. The 140/90 mmHg figure his father was given belongs to the superseded guideline. There is no holding category that defers the diagnosis. Averaged home readings on a validated device are an accepted basis for diagnosis.
  2. The correct answer is A (Healthy lifestyle change alone, with blood pressure reassessed in three to six months): the current guideline reserves pharmacotherapy for adults at 140/90 mmHg or higher, or for those with a systolic pressure of 130 to 139 mmHg who carry high cardiovascular risk.
