His systolic pressure sits in that middle band and his calculated risk is low, so lifestyle change with reassessment is the recommendation. Starting a drug because he crosses the diagnostic threshold confuses diagnosis with treatment indication. A year of monthly readings delays a decision already answerable.
  3. The correct answer is D (A systolic pressure below 130 mmHg, with no separate diastolic target): the 2025 guideline sets a systolic only treatment target of below 130 mmHg where treatment is well tolerated, and deliberately sets no diastolic treatment target. Quoting a paired value restates the diagnostic threshold rather than the target. The 140/90 mmHg figure belongs to the superseded guideline. Treating to a diastolic number alone has no basis in current Canadian recommendations.
  4. The correct answer is B (Begin pharmacotherapy, since his pressure now meets the treatment threshold): averaged readings of 142/90 mmHg reach the 140/90 mmHg level at which drug treatment is recommended regardless of calculated cardiovascular risk. His lifestyle work remains worthwhile and continues alongside the medication rather than instead of it. Another three months repeats a trial that has already been given a fair test. Uncomplicated hypertension sits well within nurse practitioner scope, and two more weeks of readings would only restate seven days of averaged data.
Case C032

Priya Nagra, 61, has knee osteoarthritis, a body mass index of 34, and well controlled

hypothyroidism. Radiographs show severe medial compartment narrowing. She takes

levothyroxine and buys ibuprofen over the counter, using it most days. Her creatinine is

96 umol/L. She works as a school custodian and tells you her goal is to finish her shifts

without limping, not to lose weight. She asks about a knee replacement.

  1. The correct answer is A (Her near daily oral ibuprofen use, which carries real risk at her age): sustained over the counter oral anti inflammatory use in a woman of 61 risks gastrointestinal bleeding, kidney injury and rising blood pressure, and clients rarely report it because it is not a prescription. Reviewing it comes before layering anything new on top. Levothyroxine at a controlled dose does not cause knee pain. Radiographic severity correlates poorly with reported pain, and her weight is not the immediate safety concern.
  2. The correct answer is A (Referral follows failure of conservative care, not the severity seen on imaging): surgical opinion in knee osteoarthritis is triggered by persistent symptoms despite a genuine trial of exercise, education and appropriate analgesia, and she has had none of these. Radiographic narrowing correlates weakly with pain and function. Requiring a particular body mass index before referral is not a guideline standard and acts as a barrier. Sending her now skips the base of the treatment pyramid entirely.
  3. The correct answer is C (Topical anti inflammatory plus a community based supervised exercise programme): topical agents give useful knee pain relief with far less systemic risk than oral anti inflammatories in an older adult, and exercise is the foundation of every osteoarthritis plan. A community programme is affordable where private physiotherapy is not. Adding gastric protection to daily oral ibuprofen still leaves the kidney and blood pressure risk untouched. Oral corticosteroid has no role here, and rest from work contradicts her stated goal.
  4. The correct answer is D (Explore what she wants to change and agree on health focused goals): the Canadian adult obesity guideline treats obesity as a chronic disease assessed by health impairment and function, with goals negotiated around health rather than a number on the scale. She opened the conversation, so it is welcome, but the content still has to be collaborative. Prescribing a target weight and calorie limit ignores root causes and reinforces weight bias. Dismissing her question shuts down a concern she raised, and surgical referral precedes any assessment of what she actually wants.
  5. The correct answer is B (Accept her consent, since capacity is specific to this decision at this time): capacity is decision specific, so a diagnosis of early dementia does not remove the ability to consent to a simple low risk treatment she can clearly describe. She demonstrates understanding of the nature, the purpose and the consequence of declining, which is the standard applied. A substitute decision maker acts only where capacity for that decision is absent. Delaying replacement she does not need assessed causes avoidable harm.
  6. The correct answer is C (Arrange three month dispensing intervals so she pays fewer dispensing fees): she has named the barrier precisely, and a fee charged per prescription per fill falls directly when the interval is extended. This keeps her whole regimen intact instead of trading glucose control for cost. Dropping an agent worsens an A1C that is already rising. Clinic samples are unreliable and unsustainable, and adherence counselling addresses a knowledge problem she does not have.
  7. The correct answer is D (Refer to nephrology, since both the eGFR and the ACR cross referral thresholds): an eGFR persistently below 30 mL/min/1.73 m2 and an albumin to creatinine ratio above 60 mg/mmol are each independent Canadian referral criteria, and he meets both on confirmed repeat testing. Waiting six months allows further loss that cannot be recovered. He already takes a renin angiotensin blocker, so starting one is not the gap. His metformin must also be stopped, since an eGFR below 30 mL/min/1.73 m2 is a contraindication. He is not in kidney failure, and dialysis planning is a nephrology decision.
  8. The correct answer is A (Transfer of care, because another provider has assumed responsibility for it): transfer occurs when a receiving provider accepts responsibility for the client or for an episode of care and the sending provider steps out of that part of the plan. Consultation keeps responsibility with you while a colleague advises. Referral sends the client for a defined problem while you remain accountable for chasing the outcome. Shared care describes a negotiated joint arrangement, which is not what has happened here.
  9. The correct answer is B (Review adherence, inhaler technique and whether the diagnosis is correct): Canadian Thoracic Society guidance requires those three checks before therapy is stepped up, because uncontrolled symptoms far more often reflect a device used badly or doses not taken than a genuine need for more drug. Her reliever use of five doses a week and her two lost work days confirm poor control without identifying its cause. Adding a long acting agent on top of a technique problem simply raises her cost. Prednisone treats an exacerbation, not chronic poor control.
  10. The correct answer is B (Choose a regimen that fits his living situation with supported follow up): no refrigeration, no fixed address and shelter living make a twice daily injectable plan unsustainable, and that is a plan problem rather than a client failing. Selecting an agent tolerant of his circumstances, with shelter based or outreach follow up, delivers actual glucose lowering. Written storage instructions do not create a fridge. Withholding treatment until housing improves leaves severe hyperglycemia untreated indefinitely.
  11. The correct answer is C (This LDL level is a statin indicated condition and bypasses risk calculation): Canadian Cardiovascular Society guidance names an LDL cholesterol of 5.0 mmol/L or higher as a statin indicated condition, so treatment is recommended without first calculating a Framingham score. Calculating one here would only delay a decision that has already been made. A six month lifestyle trial does not bring an LDL of this magnitude to target. Non HDL cholesterol is a secondary target and does not gate the initial decision.
  12. The correct answer is A (Confirm the diagnosis, counsel on lifestyle change, and reassess within six months): readings consistently at or above 130/80 mmHg on a validated device establish hypertension under the current guideline, while systolic values in the 130 to 139 mmHg band with low cardiovascular risk call for lifestyle change and reassessment in three to six months rather than a drug. Starting medication applies the pharmacotherapy threshold to someone who does not meet it. Calling the readings normal applies the superseded 140/90 mmHg standard, and ambulatory monitoring adds nothing when three validated sets already agree.
  13. The correct answer is D (Ask which problem is hardest now and plan around her answer): prioritization puts clinical urgency first, and with nothing life threatening present the next priority is the client's own stated concern, because engagement collapses when a client feels unheard. Food insecurity or untreated mood often blocks every other intervention, so solving it first makes the rest possible. Three simultaneous new prescriptions guarantee confusion and abandonment. Booking four visits ignores her explicit statement that she cannot attend more of them.
  14. The correct answer is C (Explain that the level reflects the new dose only after six to eight weeks): thyroid stimulating hormone takes six to eight weeks to reach a new steady state after a levothyroxine change, so testing at three weeks measures an unstabilized value and invites an unnecessary further increase. Raising the dose on symptoms alone risks overtreatment, atrial fibrillation and bone loss. Free T4 helps interpret an abnormal result rather than shorten the waiting interval. Her fatigue deserves attention but has many other possible causes.
  15. The correct answer is A (Consolidate the regimen toward once daily dosing using the equivalent alternatives): treatment burden directly determines adherence, and she has already identified which doses actually get taken. Switching to equally effective and equally priced once daily agents removes the mismatch without losing therapeutic benefit. A pill organizer aids organization but leaves four dosing times in place. Stopping drugs she still needs trades one problem for another, and paid blister packing adds a cost she has not agreed to.
  16. The correct answer is B (Respect the refusal, document the discussion, and keep the option open): a capable client who understands the benefits, the risks of declining and the alternatives may refuse any treatment, and the refusal stands even when the clinician disagrees. Documenting the conversation protects both parties and leaves room for a later change of mind. Requesting a capacity assessment because a decision seems unwise misuses that process. Acetylsalicylic acid is not equivalent stroke protection, and recruiting family to apply pressure undermines voluntariness.
  17. The correct answer is D (An SGLT2 inhibitor, because it also reduces heart failure hospitalization): when heart failure with reduced ejection fraction accompanies type 2 diabetes, the second agent is chosen for proven organ protection rather than glucose lowering alone, and her kidney function supports use. Coverage is not a limiting factor here, so the clinical argument governs. A sulfonylurea lowers glucose cheaply while adding hypoglycemia and no cardiovascular benefit. A dipeptidyl peptidase 4 inhibitor is tolerable but offers no heart failure benefit, and basal insulin does not protect the heart.
  18. The correct answer is D (Arrange the telephone interpreter now and proceed with the consent discussion): valid consent for insulin requires the client to understand the nature, the benefits, the risks and the alternatives, which needs a professional interpreter. A ten minute wait is trivial against a medication that can cause severe hypoglycemia. Using a child as interpreter is never acceptable, and any family member risks filtering and role reversal. Simplified English does not overcome a real language barrier, and rescheduling delays treatment when a solution exists today.
  19. The correct answer is C (Arrange post bronchodilator spirometry to establish the correct underlying diagnosis): obstructive lung disease cannot be diagnosed on symptoms alone, and two years of ineffective asthma therapy in a smoker with daily sputum and no variability strongly suggests the label is wrong. A post bronchodilator ratio below 0.7 would confirm COPD and change the entire plan. Escalating inhalers treats a diagnosis never established. A steroid trial and a radiograph do not substitute for spirometry, and referral without it wastes a specialist appointment.
  20. The correct answer is A (NT proBNP, because a normal level makes heart failure very unlikely): natriuretic peptide testing is most useful for ruling heart failure out in undifferentiated breathlessness, which is exactly the uncertainty described. A normal result redirects the workup toward deconditioning, obesity related restriction or lung disease. An electrocardiogram may show contributing abnormalities but neither confirms nor excludes heart failure. Troponin identifies myocardial injury rather than the cause of chronic dyspnea, and D dimer screens for venous thromboembolism.
  21. The correct answer is B (Ask permission before discussing weight, and address the presenting complaint): the Canadian adult obesity guideline explicitly requires asking permission before raising weight, because unsolicited advice reinforces weight bias and drives people away from care. Repeated unrequested advice already appears in her chart and has changed nothing. Delivering brief advice at every contact ignores both consent and the reason she attended. Ordering metabolic bloodwork or making a referral she has not asked for takes the decision out of her hands.
  22. The correct answer is C (COPD is confirmed, and management follows symptoms and exacerbation history): a post bronchodilator FEV1 to FVC ratio below 0.7 in a heavy smoker establishes fixed airflow obstruction, which confirms COPD. Canadian practice then guides therapy by symptom burden and exacerbation frequency rather than by the FEV1 percentage alone. The minimal change after bronchodilator argues against asthma and against a reversibility based plan. The report is diagnostic as it stands, so repeating it would only delay treatment.
  23. The correct answer is A (Start a high intensity statin now, since it is fully covered and effective): secondary prevention calls for high intensity statin therapy as first line, the drug is on the formulary, and nothing blocks starting today, so efficacy and feasibility point the same way. Ezetimibe is an add on for clients who miss target on a maximally tolerated statin and also carries a paperwork barrier. A low starting dose undertreats established disease. Waiting for an approval he does not need leaves him unprotected.
  24. The correct answer is D (Outstanding investigations with dates and who is responsible for the results): pending results are the classic item that falls between two providers, and a result nobody owns is the commonest source of harm at a handover. Naming the test, the date and the responsible person closes that gap explicitly. Sending every note buries the essential information instead of transmitting it. Appointment preferences are administrative, and a subjective judgement about motivation biases the receiving provider without adding clinical value.
  25. The correct answer is B (Stating that the client needs assessment within two weeks, and why): urgency communicates only when it is concrete, so a stated timeframe with the clinical reason lets the receiving service triage against its own criteria. The word urgent means different things in different offices and is used so often that it has lost force. A telephone call to a clerk leaves nothing in the written record. Describing anxiety conveys distress rather than the clinical risk that drives triage.
  26. The correct answer is A (Assessing her stroke risk and discussing anticoagulation options with her): the tracing shows an irregularly irregular rhythm without discernible P waves, and in an 80 year old with hypertension the dominant hazard from atrial fibrillation is stroke, which anticoagulation substantially reduces. Her rate is acceptable and she is asymptomatic, so nothing here is an emergency. Cardioversion is not indicated for well tolerated fibrillation of unknown duration without anticoagulation. Driving her rate below sixty risks bradycardia without benefit.
  27. The correct answer is C (Stop the naproxen and the salt substitute, then recheck the potassium): salt substitutes are potassium chloride, and non steroidal anti inflammatories raise potassium and impair kidney perfusion, so reversible contributors are removed before abandoning a drug that protects an albuminuric kidney. His stable eGFR argues against progressive disease as the driver. Stopping the ramipril forfeits proven kidney protection for a likely correctable cause. Nephrology referral for refractory hyperkalemia applies only once correctable causes have been removed.
  28. The correct answer is B (Find an effective controller she can afford and check available coverage programmes): cost is the barrier she named, and reliever only use with two prednisone courses this year signals real risk of a severe exacerbation. A cheaper inhaled corticosteroid, a different device, or provincial and manufacturer support programmes can restore controller therapy within her means. Repeating a prescription she cannot fill changes nothing. Reliever only treatment is unsafe, and specialist referral does not address why the medication goes unfilled.
  29. The correct answer is D (Keep current therapy, explaining that a relaxed target fits his situation): Diabetes Canada sets an A1C target of 7.1 to 8.5 percent for frail older adults, and 7.1 to 8.0 percent for those who are functionally dependent, because hypoglycemia becomes the greater danger, and at 7.8 percent he already sits within that target. Intensifying therapy for a number in this setting buys no benefit and risks falls and hypoglycemia. Insulin and sulfonylureas are the two classes most likely to cause exactly that harm. Stopping metformin is unnecessary when it is tolerated and prevents symptomatic hyperglycemia.
  30. The correct answer is C (Observe with periodic testing, since this pattern usually needs no treatment): a mildly raised thyroid stimulating hormone with a normal free T4 on confirmed repeat testing is subclinical hypothyroidism, which in an asymptomatic older adult is usually observed rather than treated. Treating on the number alone exposes her to overtreatment, atrial fibrillation and bone loss for no proven gain. Ultrasound images structure and does not guide this decision. Cholesterol is not the deciding factor for starting replacement here.
  31. The correct answer is C (Consult dermatology for advice now, and refer for assessment in parallel): consultation obtains guidance on a defined question while responsibility stays with you, which lets treatment begin long before an eight month appointment. Running the referral alongside secures the specialist assessment without leaving her untreated meanwhile. Transferring her skin care outright removes the coordination she needs across her other conditions. Continuing failed topical therapy for eight months is not a plan, and the emergency department is not a route to non urgent specialist opinion.
  32. The correct answer is A (Within one to two weeks of starting, before any further dose change): renin angiotensin blockade can raise creatinine and potassium within days, so kidney function and electrolytes are checked one to two weeks after initiation and again after each dose increase. Detecting a significant rise early allows the dose to be adjusted or a cause to be found before harm occurs. Three and six month intervals belong to stable maintenance therapy. Waiting for symptoms misses hyperkalemia, which is silent until it is dangerous.
  33. The correct answer is B (Ask him to explain in his own words what he will do daily): teach back is the standard method for confirming understanding where literacy is limited, because it tests recall and application rather than politeness. Nodding and an absence of questions are not evidence of comprehension. Printed material he cannot read shifts the problem to the next visit. A yes or no question about questions invites agreement, and delegating the whole explanation elsewhere never confirms that he understood.
  34. The correct answer is D (Complete the special authority form now and diarize the decision date): she meets the listed criterion of reduced kidney function with an A1C above target on metformin, so the form is the route to coverage she can actually use. Decisions take about fifteen business days, and tracking that date stops an application from quietly disappearing. Coverage is explicitly not retroactive, so telling her otherwise leaves her with a bill. Abandoning the indicated class or starting before approval both push cost back onto a client who cannot pay.
  35. The correct answer is A (Assess and treat the foot infection, then rebook the weight discussion): clinical urgency outranks the stated agenda when limb or life is threatened, and a diabetic foot ulcer with spreading cellulitis and fever threatens the limb. Explaining why the agenda is changing keeps her engaged rather than dismissed. Continuing the planned discussion delays treatment of an infection that can progress within days. Splitting the visit or arranging podiatry without treating today both leave the infection unaddressed.
  36. The correct answer is C (Group the visits on one day and use virtual contact between them): transportation is the binding constraint, so the plan is redesigned around it by consolidating everything needing an in person visit into one trip and handling the rest remotely. This preserves all three tasks rather than quietly dropping one. Three separate appointments triple a travel burden she cannot meet. Asking her to solve the transport herself or sending a neighbour with paperwork moves the problem rather than solving it.
  37. The correct answer is B (Increase the diuretic dose and review her again within a few days): a steady gain of nearly five kilograms with a rising resting heart rate and falling oxygen saturation is progressive congestion, and congestion is treated with more diuretic plus close review. Early outpatient escalation is precisely what keeps this client out of hospital. Raising the beta blocker during decompensation reduces cardiac output and is contraindicated. The renin angiotensin blocker carries prognostic benefit and her pressure is stable, and echocardiography would not change today's action.
  38. The correct answer is D (Name the serious risk and the alternatives briefly, then confirm her agreement): valid consent requires the client to receive the material risks and the reasonable alternatives, and a serious adverse effect is material even when it is uncommon. Delivering that briefly and plainly respects both her trust and the legal standard. A blanket waiver of information does not meet the threshold for informed consent. Handing over a sheet assumes she will read it, and refusing to prescribe punishes her for how she framed the conversation.
  39. The correct answer is B (The once daily agent, because a regimen actually taken works better): trial efficacy is measured under conditions of near perfect adherence, so a marginal advantage evaporates when a five times daily schedule collides with twelve hour shifts. Real world effectiveness is the relevant comparison when cost and coverage are equal. Alarms do not create opportunities to take a dose during an unbroken shift. Withholding treatment until adherence is demonstrated inverts the logic and leaves the condition untreated.
Case C033

A 34 year old woman is in your primary care clinic 10 minutes after receiving an

intramuscular antibiotic injection. She reports throat tightness, itching and nausea.

She has generalized urticaria, an audible wheeze and a hoarse voice. Blood pressure is

88/54 mmHg, heart rate 124 beats per minute, respiratory rate 26 breaths per minute and

oxygen saturation 92 percent on room air. She has no known drug allergies and takes no

regular medications.

  1. The correct answer is C (Tachycardia with urticaria, wheeze and hoarseness involving skin and airway together): Anaphylaxis is defined by acute onset involving two or more organ systems, and skin plus airway involvement with tachycardia meets that definition. Vasovagal episodes produce bradycardia, pallor and diaphoresis without urticaria or wheeze. Hypotension shortly after a painful injection occurs in both and does not discriminate between them. Mild hypoxemia alone can accompany anxiety or hyperventilation.
  2. The correct answer is A (Give 0.5 mg epinephrine intramuscularly into the anterolateral thigh now): Epinephrine is the only intervention that reverses airway edema, bronchospasm and vasodilatory shock, and nothing precedes it. Establishing intravenous access before giving epinephrine is the delay most often identified in fatal anaphylaxis. Antihistamines relieve urticaria and corticosteroids act far too slowly to alter the acute event. Calling for help follows immediately after the injection rather than before it.
  3. The correct answer is D (Repeat intramuscular epinephrine and give an intravenous crystalloid bolus while awaiting transport): Persistent hypotension and wheeze after one dose mean the response is inadequate, so epinephrine is repeated every 5 to 15 minutes while circulating volume is restored. Nebulized bronchodilator alone treats bronchospasm but not shock or airway edema. Titrated intravenous epinephrine infusions demand continuous cardiac monitoring and are not started in an unmonitored clinic. Corticosteroids do not reliably prevent biphasic reactions and never substitute for repeat epinephrine.
  4. The correct answer is B (Prescribe two auto injectors, teach their use, document the allergy, refer to allergy): Canadian practice after anaphylaxis is two auto injectors, demonstrated training in their use, clear documentation of the culprit drug, and allergy referral for confirmation and future planning. Supplying only one device leaves no margin when a second dose is needed before help arrives. Premedication with an antihistamine does not prevent anaphylaxis and can mask early warning signs. Deferring the auto injector until specialist assessment leaves her unprotected for months.
Case C034

A 78 year old man with type 2 diabetes is brought to your rural clinic by his daughter.

For three days he has had fever, back pain and burning on urination, and today he is

confused and unsteady on his feet. He normally lives alone and manages his own

medications. He takes metformin, ramipril and atorvastatin. The nearest emergency

department is 50 minutes away by road. His temperature is 38.9 degrees Celsius.

  1. The correct answer is D (New confusion with hypotension and a respiratory rate above 22 breaths): Sepsis is infection plus new organ dysfunction, and new confusion with hypotension and tachypnoea signals exactly that. Costovertebral angle tenderness with abnormal dipstick findings localizes the infection but says nothing about organ dysfunction. Fever with rigors and dysuria describes infection severity in symptom terms rather than physiological failure. Cloudy urine with frequency is common in uncomplicated cystitis and does not change disposition.
  2. The correct answer is A (He is deteriorating physiologically and needs immediate transfer with fluid resuscitation started): Falling blood pressure, rising pulse, rising respiratory rate and deepening drowsiness across 30 minutes describe progressive physiological deterioration, which mandates resuscitation and transfer rather than observation. Calling him stable ignores the direction of travel in every recorded parameter. Anxiety and pain do not lower blood pressure while consciousness declines. Waiting an hour in a clinic 50 minutes from hospital risks arrival in refractory shock.
  3. The correct answer is B (Draw blood cultures, give broad spectrum antibiotics and a crystalloid bolus immediately): Canadian sepsis care in the first hour is cultures if they cause no delay, immediate broad spectrum antibiotics, lactate measurement and a crystalloid bolus for hypotension. Waiting for culture results before antibiotics costs hours, and mortality rises with every hour of delay. Deferring antibiotics until hospital arrival adds at least 50 minutes of untreated sepsis. Fluids alone address perfusion without treating the source driving the shock.
  4. The correct answer is C (The antibiotic given with its time, the fluid volume infused, and cultures drawn): The receiving team's first hour decisions depend on what has already been given, so naming the antibiotic and its time, the fluid volume infused and whether cultures were drawn directly shapes whether they repeat, escalate or withhold treatment. Remote surgical history rarely alters immediate resuscitation. Pharmacy and family physician details matter for continuity rather than for the first hour. The daughter's contact information belongs in the record but does not change treatment.
Case C035

A 71 year old woman with metastatic pancreatic cancer is receiving care at home with her

husband. She has declined further chemotherapy. She takes long acting morphine 30 mg

twice daily with breakthrough doses, and reports that her pain is controlled. Over the

past week she has become breathless at rest, eats very little and sleeps most of the

day. Her husband asks whether it is time to stop her regular pain medication because

she is barely awake.

  1. The correct answer is A (Ask her to rate her breathlessness at rest and with minimal exertion): Breathlessness is a subjective symptom, and the most useful measure is the client's own rating at rest and on minimal exertion, repeated over time to judge response. Urgent imaging and blood gas testing impose burden without changing a comfort focused plan at this stage. Proxy reporting by family misestimates symptom severity when the client can still answer. Oxygen saturation correlates poorly with the sensation of breathlessness.
  2. The correct answer is D (Explain that drowsiness reflects disease progression and opioids also relieve her breathlessness): Progressive drowsiness in advanced pancreatic cancer reflects the disease trajectory, and opioids titrated to symptoms relieve breathlessness as well as pain without hastening death. Halving the dose risks uncontrolled pain and rebound dyspnea in someone whose pain is currently settled. Properly titrated opioids in palliative care do not shorten survival, and acetaminophen alone cannot control cancer pain. Framing her sedation as intentional misrepresents symptom directed titration.
  3. The correct answer is C (Prescribe an anticipatory symptom kit in the home with clear administration instructions): An anticipatory symptom kit with written administration instructions lets the family and visiting nurse treat pain, dyspnea, secretions and agitation within minutes at home, which is what prevents an unplanned ambulance transfer. Twice weekly nursing visits help but leave long unsupported gaps when a crisis occurs. Instructions to call emergency services produce the very transfer the family wants to avoid. Raising the long acting dose pre emptively risks toxicity without covering sudden crescendo symptoms.
  4. The correct answer is B (Explore what is driving the request and address any reversible sources of suffering): The first response to any request for medical assistance in dying is to explore what is driving it, because unrelieved pain, fear, loss of control and caregiver burden are often treatable and may change what she wants. Declaring eligibility before formal assessment misstates the legal process. Requiring family or oncologist presence breaches her confidentiality and her right to raise the request herself. Refusing to discuss it and simply redirecting her risks obstructing access, which regulators do not permit.
Case C036

A 19 year old man with asthma arrives at your urban clinic unable to speak in full

sentences. He has used his salbutamol inhaler eight times in the past three hours with

little relief. He has had two emergency department visits this year and takes no

controller medication. On examination he is sitting upright, using accessory muscles,

with widespread expiratory wheeze. Oxygen saturation is 89 percent on room air.

  1. The correct answer is A (A markedly quiet chest with new drowsiness and a falling respiratory rate): Air movement too poor to generate wheeze, together with declining alertness and a respiratory rate that falls because the client is tiring, indicates imminent respiratory failure. Loud widespread wheeze actually confirms that air is still moving through narrowed airways. Peak flow at 60 percent of personal best describes a moderate exacerbation rather than a critical one. Tachycardia and anxiety accompany beta agonist treatment and severe symptoms alike.
  2. The correct answer is B (He is deteriorating despite treatment and needs emergency transfer without further delay): Saturation stalling, peak flow falling further and alertness declining after a full hour of maximal therapy describe treatment failure with exhaustion, which requires transfer now rather than more clinic time. Calling this a good response contradicts the falling peak flow and the new drowsiness. The falling respiratory rate here reflects fatigue rather than recovery, which is the trap in this pattern. Tachycardia is an expected beta agonist effect and never a reason to withdraw bronchodilator in severe asthma.
  3. The correct answer is D (Prepare for assisted bag mask ventilation while continuing bronchodilator and oxygen): Exhaustion with worsening drowsiness means ventilation is failing, so assisted bag mask ventilation is prepared while bronchodilator and oxygen continue until the crew arrives. Further oral corticosteroid adds nothing within the transfer window, because systemic corticosteroids take four to six hours to act and an adequate first dose has already been given. Withholding oxygen applies to no asthma presentation, and hypoxemia kills faster than hypercapnia here. Sedation removes the respiratory drive that is currently keeping him alive.
  4. The correct answer is C (Start inhaled corticosteroid containing therapy, check technique, give a written action plan): Nearly all severe exacerbations in young adults follow reliever only treatment, so starting inhaled corticosteroid containing therapy, confirming technique and supplying a written action plan is the change that lowers his risk most. Supplying more reliever inhalers reinforces the pattern that produced the admission. Waiting for respirology advice leaves him untreated through his highest risk period. Spirometry is useful, but delaying controller therapy for six weeks after a life threatening event is unsafe.
  5. The correct answer is A (Swab and treat with penicillin only if the streptococcal test is positive): Her modified Centor score is high, but Canadian practice confirms streptococcal infection by swab before treating, and penicillin remains first line because resistance to it has not emerged. Amoxicillin clavulanate is unnecessarily broad for streptococcal pharyngitis. Azithromycin is reserved for penicillin allergy, and macrolide resistance is a genuine concern. Symptomatic care alone risks leaving a confirmed streptococcal infection untreated.
  6. The correct answer is D (Offer analgesia with a safety net prescription and review within 48 hours): Healthy children six months or older with non severe unilateral disease and reliable follow up may be managed with analgesia and a safety net prescription filled only if there is no improvement within 48 hours. Immediate antibiotics for every bulging membrane over treats a condition that usually resolves without them. Amoxicillin clavulanate is second line for treatment failure or recent antibiotic exposure. Tympanocentesis has no role in routine primary care management here.
  7. The correct answer is B (Call emergency services, prepare for synchronized cardioversion, and begin continuous monitoring): Wide complex tachycardia with hypotension, pallor and presyncope is unstable ventricular tachycardia, so emergency activation, continuous monitoring and readiness for synchronized cardioversion take priority. Nitroglycerin in a hypotensive client with this rhythm will worsen perfusion further. Acetylsalicylic acid may be indicated later, but non urgent cardiology review misjudges an immediately life threatening rhythm. Waiting for a full tracing delays treatment the strip already justifies.
  8. The correct answer is C (Check capillary blood glucose because hypoglycemia can closely mimic acute stroke): Hypoglycemia mimics stroke closely and is instantly reversible, so capillary glucose is measured before any imaging or reperfusion decision. Routine blood pressure lowering in the acute phase reduces perfusion to threatened brain tissue. Antiplatelet therapy cannot be given until hemorrhage has been excluded by imaging. Swallowing screening matters before oral intake but does not precede excluding a reversible mimic.
  9. The correct answer is D (Give 15 g fast acting carbohydrate, recheck in 15 minutes, then a snack): Diabetes Canada defines hypoglycemia as plasma glucose below 4.0 mmol/L, treated in a conscious client with 15 g of fast acting carbohydrate, a recheck at 15 minutes, and a snack or meal once the level is above threshold. Complex carbohydrate raises glucose too slowly to correct a symptomatic episode. Glucagon is reserved for clients who are unconscious or unable to swallow. Protein slows gastric emptying and delays the rise in glucose.
  10. The correct answer is A (Give intramuscular glucagon while a colleague attempts intravenous access for dextrose): An unresponsive client must have nothing placed in the mouth, so glucagon is given while access is attempted and intravenous dextrose follows as soon as a line is available. Buccal gel still risks aspiration in someone who cannot be roused. Offering juice to an unresponsive client is unsafe regardless of gag reflex. Nurse practitioners may administer dextrose within scope, and waiting for transport prolongs cerebral hypoglycemia.
  11. The correct answer is C (Open the airway and start assisted ventilation with oxygen, then give naloxone): Opioid overdose kills through hypoventilation, so opening the airway and delivering assisted ventilation with oxygen precedes naloxone and buys time while the drug takes effect. Giving naloxone first leaves a hypoxic brain unventilated throughout the onset period. Compressions are for pulseless clients, and this man has a palpable pulse. Intramuscular naloxone is well absorbed and is the standard route when no line exists.
  12. The correct answer is B (Explain that naloxone wears off before opioids do, and describe his legal protections): Naloxone has a shorter duration than most opioids, so re sedation is a real risk and he must be observed, while the Good Samaritan Drug Overdose Act protects people at the scene from simple possession charges. Telling him he may leave ignores the pharmacology that makes re sedation likely. Police attendance is not mandatory when naloxone is given clinically. Mental health legislation does not authorize detention merely to monitor an overdose.
  13. The correct answer is A (Arrange emergency transfer for surgical assessment and intravenous antibiotic therapy now): Pain out of proportion to the visible findings, rapid spread, bullae, crepitus and systemic toxicity together suggest necrotizing soft tissue infection, which needs emergency surgical assessment and intravenous therapy rather than an oral antibiotic. Marking the border and reviewing in 48 hours suits uncomplicated cellulitis, not these features. Outpatient ultrasound delays definitive care while the infection advances hourly. Oral clindamycin with nurse visits still manages this as an outpatient problem.
  14. The correct answer is D (The potassium value, since insulin will drive it down and provoke arrhythmia): Insulin drives potassium intracellularly, so a level already above the reference range will fall rapidly once treatment begins, and total body potassium in ketoacidosis is depleted despite the measured value. Correcting glucose quickly without attending to potassium and fluid is what causes arrhythmia and cerebral edema. The low sodium here is largely dilutional from hyperglycemia and corrects with fluid. Mild renal impairment reflects volume depletion and is an indication for fluid.
  15. The correct answer is C (Explain that treatment is not indicated and address hydration and continence care): Asymptomatic bacteriuria is treated only in pregnancy and before urologic procedures that breach the mucosa, and cloudy or strong smelling urine without urinary symptoms or cognitive change is not an infection. Nitrofurantoin exposes her to adverse effects and resistance with no benefit. Fosfomycin is still an antibiotic for a condition that does not require one. Repeating the culture invites the same inappropriate decision a week later.
  16. The correct answer is B (Explain that imaging now will not change care, and encourage activity and analgesia): Without red flags, imaging in the first six weeks of acute low back pain changes neither treatment nor outcome, and Choosing Wisely Canada advises against it while encouraging activity and simple analgesia. Radiographs deliver radiation and incidental findings without altering management. Occupation alone is not a red flag and does not justify advanced imaging. Referring to spine surgery for an imaging decision medicalizes a self limiting problem.
  17. The correct answer is D (No radiograph is needed, so provide analgesia, support and early weight bearing advice): The Ottawa ankle rule requires imaging only with bone tenderness at either malleolar zone, at the navicular or the base of the fifth metatarsal, or inability to bear weight for four steps, and she meets none of these criteria. Swelling alone is not a criterion in the rule. Adding foot views compounds unnecessary radiation when no midfoot criterion is met. Orthopedic referral for a low risk sprain consumes access others need.
  18. The correct answer is A (Give oral rehydration solution in small frequent amounts and provide clear return precautions): Oral rehydration in small frequent volumes is the intervention for viral gastroenteritis in a well hydrated child, supported by clear advice on when to return. Azithromycin is not indicated for non bloody watery diarrhea without systemic features. Antimotility agents are avoided in children and are contraindicated with bloody diarrhea or high fever. Intravenous fluid is unnecessary when perfusion, mucous membranes and urine output are all normal.
  19. The correct answer is B (Explain the expected course, treat symptoms, and give clear worsening precautions): Most upper respiratory illness is viral and already improving at six days, so explaining the expected course, treating symptoms and naming clear worsening precautions is both accurate and safe. Duration alone does not diagnose bacterial sinusitis, which requires persistent severe symptoms or worsening after initial improvement. Combining a nasal steroid with an antibiotic adds cost and harm without shortening a viral illness. Plain sinus radiographs cannot separate viral from bacterial disease.
  20. The correct answer is C (Treat as an outpatient with oral antibiotics, review in 48 hours, give precautions): She has radiographically confirmed pneumonia with normal mental status, stable hemodynamics, adequate oxygenation and social support, which places her in the low risk group managed at home with oral antibiotics and a 48 hour review. Lobar consolidation by itself does not determine the site of care. Withholding antibiotics for confirmed pneumonia in a 61 year old is unsafe. Computed tomography is reserved for non resolving pneumonia rather than the initial episode.
  21. The correct answer is D (Arrange a professional interpreter and obtain consent covering risks and alternatives): Valid consent requires the client to understand the nature of the procedure, its benefits, material risks and alternatives, which is achievable here only through a professional interpreter rather than a family member. Using his daughter risks filtered information and undisclosed family dynamics, and Canadian practice avoids family interpreters. Postponing indefinitely denies him timely assessment of a suspicious pigmented lesion. Handing him a translated form without an interpreter cannot support the dialogue that consent requires.
  22. The correct answer is A (Written aftercare naming infection signs, who to call, and pathology follow up): Most serious harm after minor surgery comes from unrecognized infection or a lost pathology result, so written aftercare that names infection signs, gives a named contact and states when results will be discussed addresses both risks. Verbal advice about keeping the wound dry is useful but easily forgotten and covers only one issue. Prophylactic antibiotics after a clean minor excision cause harm without benefit. Leaving the client to book suture removal risks it never happening.
  23. The correct answer is B (Calculating the maximum dose by body weight and aspirating before each injection): Local anesthetic systemic toxicity is prevented by calculating the maximum dose from body weight before starting and by aspirating before each injection to avoid intravascular delivery. Warming and slow injection improve comfort but do nothing about the total dose delivered. Needle gauge does not limit the total volume infiltrated during a repair. Bicarbonate buffering speeds onset and reduces stinging without affecting toxicity risk.
  24. The correct answer is D (Reduce stimulation, keep an exit path clear, and attempt verbal de escalation): Safety comes before diagnosis in acute psychosis, so reducing stimulation, keeping an exit path clear and attempting verbal de escalation with a single calm speaker is the first priority. Completing involuntary paperwork before any attempt at engagement escalates the encounter, and that authority differs by jurisdiction. Intramuscular medication is a later step once de escalation has failed. Collateral history is valuable but does not outrank immediate environmental safety.
  25. The correct answer is C (A medical cause is likely, and he needs urgent medical assessment and monitoring): Disorientation, fever and tremor three days after stopping alcohol point to a medical cause such as withdrawal or infection rather than primary psychosis, and delirium requires urgent medical assessment and monitoring. Treating this as a first psychotic episode risks missing a condition that can be fatal within hours. Fever in a confused client is never incidental and must be explained before psychiatric transfer. Attributing the tremor to anxiety leaves the underlying medical problem uninvestigated.
  26. The correct answer is A (Assess her stroke risk, discuss anticoagulation, and address ventricular rate control): The strip shows an irregularly irregular rhythm with no discernible P waves, and in a hemodynamically stable client with three weeks of symptoms the priority is stroke risk assessment, an anticoagulation discussion and rate control. Cardioversion after more than 48 hours of arrhythmia without anticoagulation or imaging risks embolic stroke. Rate control without addressing thromboembolic risk misses the intervention preventing the most harm. This rate and stability do not warrant emergency transfer.
  27. The correct answer is B (Arrange emergency transfer, establish intravenous access, withhold the anticoagulant and naproxen): Melaena with orthostatic symptoms, tachycardia and a borderline blood pressure in someone taking an anticoagulant and a non steroidal anti inflammatory is an active upper gastrointestinal bleed needing emergency transfer, intravenous access and withdrawal of both contributing drugs. Outpatient endoscopy within two weeks ignores current hemodynamic compromise. Waiting for a coagulation result delays resuscitation already indicated. Oral iron treats the consequence while bleeding continues.
  28. The correct answer is C (Giving sublingual nitroglycerin to relieve his ongoing ischemic chest discomfort): Nitroglycerin is withheld with hypotension, bradycardia and inferior ST elevation, because right ventricular involvement makes the circulation preload dependent and vasodilation can precipitate profound hypotension. Chewed acetylsalicylic acid is indicated unless truly contraindicated. Oxygen is appropriate once saturation has fallen below target. Calling emergency services for reperfusion is the definitive action in ST elevation infarction.
  29. The correct answer is D (Obtain a 12 lead tracing, give chewed acetylsalicylic acid, call emergency services): Canadian acute coronary syndrome care sets a 10 minute target from first contact to a 12 lead tracing, with chewed acetylsalicylic acid and emergency activation happening in parallel rather than in sequence. Waiting for a troponin result before calling for help delays reperfusion by hours. Routine oxygen for normoxemic clients offers no benefit and may cause harm. Repeated nitroglycerin titrated only to symptom relief ignores blood pressure and the reperfusion decision.
  30. The correct answer is A (Give a crystalloid bolus and arrange emergency transfer despite her normal blood pressure): Lactate of 4.6 mmol/L signals tissue hypoperfusion even when blood pressure looks normal, so a crystalloid bolus and emergency transfer are indicated for what is cryptic septic shock. Normal blood pressure is a late and unreliable reassurance in sepsis, particularly in younger clients who compensate well. Repeating the lactate in four hours wastes the window in which resuscitation changes outcome. Managing confirmed hypoperfusion at home is unsafe.
  31. The correct answer is C (0.22 mg intramuscularly into the anterolateral thigh, repeated every 5 to 15 minutes): Pediatric dosing is 0.01 mg/kg of the 1 mg/mL solution given intramuscularly into the anterolateral thigh, so 22 kg gives 0.22 mg, repeated every 5 to 15 minutes when the response is inadequate. Adult dosing into the deltoid is both the wrong amount and the wrong site, since thigh absorption is faster. Subcutaneous administration absorbs unreliably in shock. Intravenous epinephrine here risks arrhythmia and demands continuous monitoring.
  32. The correct answer is B (Chronic occult gastrointestinal blood loss requiring urgent endoscopy and iron replacement): Microcytic anemia with a ferritin of 6 ug/L, reactive thrombocytosis and a raised urea against a normal creatinine is the classic pattern of gastrointestinal blood loss with digested blood in the gut, so endoscopy and iron replacement are both needed. Anemia of chronic disease preserves or raises ferritin rather than depleting it. Hemolysis neither depletes iron stores nor raises urea selectively. Attributing profound iron deficiency in a 70 year old to diet alone risks missing malignancy.
  33. The correct answer is D (Start a low dose oral opioid titrated to the breathlessness, with bowel care): Low dose oral opioid titrated against the sensation of breathlessness is the evidence based treatment for refractory dyspnea in advanced disease, with laxatives started at the same time. Raising oxygen flow in someone already at 93 percent treats a number rather than the symptom. Benzodiazepines are second line and are added for anxiety when opioids alone are insufficient. Home non invasive ventilation is burdensome and is not first line here.
  34. The correct answer is A (Ask her what she understands and how much she wants to know): The conversation begins by asking what she understands and how much she wants to know, which respects both her capacity and her right to set the pace of disclosure. Following the son's instruction to withhold information from a capable client breaches her autonomy. Delivering a full prognosis without first asking permission skips the emotional preparation step. Deferring to the cardiologist abandons a conversation she has explicitly opened with you.
  35. The correct answer is B (A consultation, since you seek advice while retaining responsibility for the client): Seeking specialist advice on a defined question while remaining the most responsible provider is the definition of consultation. Transfer of care means another provider assumes responsibility, which requires their acceptance and a clear handover. Referral means sending the client to another provider to manage a defined problem, which is not what is described. Shared care describes an ongoing co management model rather than one request for advice.
  36. The correct answer is C (Address food access first, start one simplified medication, and arrange early review): Food insecurity is his stated priority and undermines every other intervention, so addressing food access, starting one simplified medication and arranging early review will achieve more than several simultaneous starts. Four new treatments in one visit with no drug coverage invites non adherence and lost trust. Deferring all treatment for months leaves both his glycemia and blood pressure untreated. Three specialist referrals delay care primary care can deliver.
  37. The correct answer is A (Confirm his Interim Federal Health Program coverage and choose within that formulary): Refugee claimants are covered by the Interim Federal Health Program, whose drug benefit follows the public formulary of the province or territory where he lives, so confirming coverage and prescribing within that list is what makes the plan achievable. Leaving coverage to the pharmacy usually ends in an unfilled prescription and a lost follow up. Choosing on price without checking coverage may cost him money for a drug that would have been covered. Delaying cardiovascular protection leaves him at avoidable risk.
  38. The correct answer is D (Activate the stroke pathway urgently for reperfusion assessment within the time window): Imaging that excludes hemorrhage in a client 90 minutes from last known well, with a normal glucose, means she sits inside the reperfusion window and the stroke pathway must be activated now. Aggressive blood pressure lowering before the reperfusion decision reduces perfusion to threatened tissue. Repeating imaging in six hours forfeits the entire treatment window. Dual antiplatelet therapy started now can preclude thrombolysis.
  39. The correct answer is B (Intravenous isotonic crystalloid to restore circulating volume before any insulin therapy): Diabetic ketoacidosis is a profoundly volume depleted state, and isotonic crystalloid restores perfusion, lowers glucose and improves acidosis before insulin is started. Giving insulin first with unreplaced volume worsens hypotension and drives potassium intracellularly. Bicarbonate is not used for the acidosis of ketoacidosis at this level. Oral fluids cannot correct a deficit of several litres in a vomiting client.
  40. The correct answer is C (Review adherence, inhaler technique and the diagnosis before changing her treatment): Reliever use five times weekly exceeds the Canadian Thoracic Society criterion of fewer than four doses weekly and marks her asthma as uncontrolled, even though waking twice monthly stays within the separate criterion of fewer than one night weekly, and that same guidance reviews adherence, inhaler technique and the diagnosis itself before escalating therapy, because much apparent treatment failure is device failure. Increasing the corticosteroid dose without checking technique treats the wrong problem. Adding a long acting muscarinic antagonist skips several recommended steps. Allergy testing may inform trigger avoidance but does not explain uncontrolled symptoms on treatment.
  41. The correct answer is A (Incise and drain the abscess, with aftercare instructions and a review date): Incision and drainage is the definitive treatment for a fluctuant abscess, and with only 1 cm of surrounding erythema in a well host, drainage with aftercare and a review date is sufficient. Antibiotics alone will not resolve a walled off collection. Warm compresses delay drainage and prolong pain in an abscess that is already fluctuant. Referral for general anesthetic is unnecessary for a superficial thigh abscess.
  42. The correct answer is D (Start systemic antibiotics guided by wound culture and arrange close reassessment): Spreading erythema, increasing pain and fever three days after adequate drainage indicate progression to cellulitis, so systemic antibiotics guided by wound culture with close reassessment is the appropriate escalation. Continuing unchanged for another 48 hours lets an advancing infection progress. Tighter packing impairs perfusion, and topical agents do not treat spreading soft tissue infection. Immediate debridement is reserved for necrotizing features or an undrained collection.
  43. The correct answer is B (Assess for urinary retention and relieve it before adding any sedating medication): Terminal restlessness has a reversible cause until proven otherwise, and ten hours without urine with a distended abdomen points to retention, which is relieved before any sedating medication is considered. Antipsychotics mask the distress without emptying a full bladder. Reducing the opioid in someone whose regimen is unchanged risks uncontrolled pain and does not explain the distension. Hospital transfer in the last days of life is avoidable when the cause can be treated at home.
Case C037

An 82-year-old woman with hypertension and heart failure attends for routine review.

Her regular medications are ramipril 10 mg daily, hydrochlorothiazide 25 mg daily and atorvastatin 20 mg daily.

Two weeks ago she began buying ibuprofen 400 mg three times daily from the pharmacy for low back pain.

She reports reduced urine output, ankle swelling and mild nausea.

Her blood pressure today is 104/62 mmHg and she weighs 54 kg.

  1. The correct answer is C (Order serum creatinine and electrolytes and compare with her results from three months ago): Her new symptoms began two weeks after a nonsteroidal anti inflammatory drug was added to an angiotensin converting enzyme inhibitor and a thiazide, so renal function and potassium are the measurements that separate drug induced acute kidney injury from progression of heart failure. Comparison against a recent baseline is what makes the result interpretable. Echocardiography and natriuretic peptide testing address ventricular function, which her clear lung fields and stable weight do not suggest has changed. Urgent referral before any bloodwork skips the cheapest and most informative test available today.
  2. The correct answer is B (Combined renin angiotensin blockade, diuretic and nonsteroidal drug have collapsed glomerular filtration): This is the triple whammy: the nonsteroidal drug blocks prostaglandin mediated dilation of the afferent arteriole, the angiotensin converting enzyme inhibitor prevents efferent constriction, and the thiazide reduces circulating volume, so filtration falls and potassium rises. The timing, two weeks after the only new agent, points to drugs rather than to disease progression. Heart failure progression would be expected to produce raised venous pressure and crackles, which she does not have. Statin myopathy causes muscle pain with a raised creatine kinase rather than this renal picture.
  3. The correct answer is A (Stop the ibuprofen and hold both the ramipril and the hydrochlorothiazide): All three agents drive the same mechanism, so the nonsteroidal drug she added stops permanently while the two prescribed agents are held until filtration recovers. Removing only one arm of the mechanism leaves the injury in place. Gastric protection does nothing for the kidney, because this injury is hemodynamic rather than mucosal. Waiting a week with a potassium of 5.7 mmol/L and an eGFR of 24 mL/min/1.73m2 risks arrhythmia and further loss of function.
  4. The correct answer is D (Restart ramipril alone once renal function stabilises and recheck bloodwork in two weeks): Ramipril has documented mortality benefit in heart failure and is reintroduced once filtration has recovered, with creatinine and potassium rechecked one to two weeks after the restart rather than at a distant interval. Reintroducing one agent at a time keeps any further deterioration attributable. Abandoning renin angiotensin blockade permanently removes proven benefit when the reversible cause was an over the counter drug she bought herself. Switching to a receptor blocker preserves the identical hemodynamic effect, so it does not remove the risk.
Case C038

A 74-year-old woman on warfarin for a mechanical aortic valve telephones the clinic.

Three days ago a walk in clinic prescribed trimethoprim with sulfamethoxazole for a urinary tract infection.

She now has bleeding gums, a nosebleed that took twenty minutes to stop, and extensive bruising on both forearms.

Her usual target range is 2.5 to 3.5 and her last INR eight days ago was 2.9.

She has started no other new medications.

  1. The correct answer is A (An urgent INR measurement obtained today rather than at her next scheduled visit): Bleeding gums, prolonged epistaxis and widespread bruising three days after a strongly interacting antibiotic point to a supratherapeutic international normalized ratio, and that value determines every subsequent decision. Nothing else on offer changes management before the number is known. Hemoglobin matters if bleeding becomes significant, but it does not tell you how much anticoagulation to reverse. Culture results and dietary vitamin K are relevant later and cannot be allowed to delay the coagulation result.
  2. The correct answer is D (Sulfamethoxazole inhibits CYP2C9 and displaces warfarin from albumin binding sites): The active S enantiomer of warfarin is cleared by CYP2C9, and sulfamethoxazole inhibits that enzyme while also displacing warfarin from albumin, so free drug rises and the international normalized ratio climbs steeply within days. Suppression of vitamin K producing gut flora adds to the effect. Warfarin itself, not the antibiotic, blocks vitamin K epoxide reductase. Warfarin is cleared by hepatic metabolism rather than renal tubular secretion, and acute illness reduces rather than induces clotting factor synthesis.
  3. The correct answer is B (Hold warfarin and give low dose oral vitamin K, rechecking the INR tomorrow): With an international normalized ratio of 7.4 and non major bleeding, warfarin is held and low dose oral vitamin K is given, with the value rechecked within about twenty four hours. Low dose vitamin K corrects the excess without over reversing someone whose mechanical valve requires anticoagulation to be restored quickly. High dose intravenous vitamin K with factor concentrate is reserved for major or life threatening hemorrhage and produces prolonged warfarin resistance. Holding the dose alone, or continuing any warfarin at this value, leaves her bleeding for days.
  4. The correct answer is C (Change the antibiotic to nitrofurantoin and continue close INR monitoring this week): Removing the interacting antibiotic while still treating the infection fixes the cause, and nitrofurantoin is a reasonable choice for uncomplicated cystitis at this level of renal function. Monitoring continues through the week because the effect of the original antibiotic persists after the last dose. Leaving a urinary infection untreated creates a new problem in place of the old one. Adjusting warfarin around a strong interacting drug is far less reliable than removing the interacting drug.
Case C039

A 78-year-old man is seen at home two days after discharge from hospital following a fall.

He lives alone, manages his own medications from the original vials, and has stopped using his blister pack.

He hands you the discharge summary and a bag of bottles.

He reports dizziness on standing and says he is taking everything he was given.

His daughter says he has been drowsy since coming home.

  1. The correct answer is D (Two formulations of the same beta blocker are being taken concurrently): Duplication of one drug under two different formulations is a classic unintentional discharge discrepancy, and taking both an immediate release and an extended release beta blocker doubles his beta blockade, which explains dizziness on standing in a man who has already fallen. That duplication carries the most immediate risk of syncope and further injury. Acid suppression without an indication and a hospital started hypnotic both deserve deprescribing, but neither is today's urgent correction. His acetaminophen total remains below the usual daily ceiling.
  2. The correct answer is A (Use at least two independent sources and verify the findings with him directly): The Best Possible Medication History is built from at least two independent sources, such as a structured interview, inspection of the actual vials and the community pharmacy dispensing record, and then verified with the person. Each source alone is incomplete, and the discharge summary is exactly the document that carried the duplication forward. Dispensing records show what was filled, not what was taken. Caregiver accounts are valuable but remain one source rather than verification.
  3. The correct answer is C (Stop the immediate release formulation and reassess standing blood pressure in one week): Only the extended release product was intended, so removing the unintended duplicate corrects the error while preserving the therapy he is meant to receive, and standing blood pressure is the parameter that shows whether it worked. Stopping both agents removes a drug he needs and risks rebound tachycardia and hypertension. Keeping the unintended product while reducing the intended one perpetuates the discrepancy. Adding a mineralocorticoid to treat the adverse effect of a duplicated drug is a textbook prescribing cascade.
  4. The correct answer is B (Taper the hypnotic slowly and stop the acid suppressant, one change at a time): Canadian deprescribing algorithms support tapering benzodiazepine receptor agonists gradually, roughly a quarter of the dose every one to two weeks, because abrupt cessation risks seizure and severe withdrawal, while a proton pump inhibitor without a maintained indication can be stopped or reduced with a warning about self limited rebound acid symptoms. Changing one drug at a time keeps any new symptom attributable. Stopping both at once removes that attribution and adds withdrawal risk. Continuing indefinitely leaves two potentially inappropriate medications in an older adult who has already fallen.
Case C040

A 3-year-old boy is brought to your clinic with two days of fever, left ear pain and poor sleep.

He has had no antibiotics in the past three months and no known drug allergies.

His weight measured on the clinic scale today is 15 kg.

Examination shows a bulging, immobile left tympanic membrane and a temperature of 38.9 degrees Celsius.

He is drinking well, alert and playing.

His mother asks for something for the pain and something to stop the infection.

  1. The correct answer is A (A bulging tympanic membrane with impaired mobility on pneumatic otoscopy): Bulging of the tympanic membrane with reduced mobility is the finding that distinguishes acute otitis media from an effusion without infection, and it carries more weight than any symptom reported at home. Fever and ear pain occur with viral upper respiratory illness and with effusion, so neither confirms the diagnosis. Recent antibiotic exposure guides which agent to choose rather than whether infection is present. Committing a child to antibiotics on symptoms alone is how unnecessary prescribing happens.
  2. The correct answer is B (Amoxicillin 675 mg, 13.5 mL, by mouth twice daily for five days): Ninety milligrams per kilogram per day for a 15 kg child is 1350 mg daily, and dividing that into two equal doses gives 675 mg per dose, which is 13.5 mL of a suspension containing 50 mg per millilitre. Writing the full daily total as each dose doubles the exposure, and halving it again underdoses the child. Prescribing in teaspoons invites a volume error because household spoons vary, so pediatric liquids are written in milligrams with the matching millilitre volume. Recording the weight and the calculation in the chart lets the pharmacist verify the arithmetic.
  3. The correct answer is D (Offer weight based ibuprofen and explain why codeine is unsafe in children): Codeine is not used in children because conversion by CYP2D6 is unpredictable, and ultrarapid metabolisers have suffered fatal respiratory depression after ordinary doses. Ibuprofen dosed by weight is effective for the pain of acute otitis media and can be alternated with acetaminophen. Sharing another child's prescription repeats the same hazard with an unverified dose and an unknown expiry. Exceeding the acetaminophen maximum risks hepatotoxicity and is never a legitimate route to extra analgesia.
  4. The correct answer is A (Expect improvement within 48 to 72 hours and return if symptoms persist or worsen): The monitoring plan names the parameter, the expected timeframe and the threshold that triggers contact, and improvement in fever and pain within 48 to 72 hours is the accepted benchmark for acute otitis media. Failure to improve within that window suggests a resistant organism or a different diagnosis and warrants reassessment. Expecting resolution within 12 hours sets a false alarm threshold and drives unnecessary return visits. Spontaneous perforation with discharge often relieves pressure and does not by itself signal treatment failure.
Case C041

A 61-year-old woman with seropositive rheumatoid arthritis is booked for an urgent visit.

Rheumatology started her on methotrexate six weeks ago, and she has since attended a walk in clinic for a urinary tract infection.

She now reports mouth ulcers, fatigue and a sore throat.

She has had no bloodwork since starting therapy.

She brings all of her medication bottles to the appointment.

  1. The correct answer is C (Methotrexate combined with a trimethoprim containing antibiotic, causing additive marrow suppression): Trimethoprim inhibits dihydrofolate reductase, the same enzyme methotrexate targets, so the combination produces additive antifolate effect and bone marrow suppression, and mucositis with sore throat and fatigue is the classic early presentation. Sulfamethoxazole also displaces methotrexate from protein binding and competes for renal tubular secretion, pushing concentrations higher. Folic acid reduces mucosal and hepatic toxicity without abolishing efficacy, which is why it is co prescribed. Nonsteroidal anti inflammatory drugs do raise methotrexate concentrations through reduced renal clearance, but calcium is not the interacting partner described.
  2. The correct answer is D (A complete blood count with differential, looking specifically for pancytopenia): Bone marrow suppression is the complication that kills, and mucositis with fever in someone taking methotrexate alongside a trimethoprim containing antibiotic demands an immediate count with differential to identify neutropenia. Methotrexate concentrations are not routinely measured or readily available for low dose weekly therapy in primary care. Streptococcal pharyngitis does not explain the fatigue and oral ulceration in this context and would not change the urgent decision. Liver enzymes matter for longer term monitoring but do not detect the acute threat.
  3. The correct answer is A (Stop methotrexate and the antibiotic and arrange urgent hospital assessment today): Fever with severe neutropenia is a medical emergency requiring immediate assessment, cultures and empiric intravenous antibiotics, and both the methotrexate and the interacting antibiotic stop at once. Managing febrile neutropenia at home forty minutes from hospital exposes her to overwhelming sepsis within hours. Folinic acid rescue is part of hospital treatment and does not substitute for admission, and continuing any methotrexate now is unsafe. Waiting days for repeat bloodwork ignores how quickly this deteriorates.
  4. The correct answer is B (A written card listing methotrexate and the antibiotics that must be avoided): The event happened because a prescriber who did not know she took methotrexate added an interacting antibiotic, so the intervention that prevents recurrence is one that travels with her to any walk in clinic or pharmacy and names the drugs to avoid. Interval blood counts detect marrow suppression after it develops rather than preventing the interaction. Extra folic acid reduces mucosal toxicity but does not offset an additive antifolate antibiotic. The day of the week is recorded to prevent accidental daily dosing, not to prevent interactions.
  5. The correct answer is C (Remove the trailing zero and replace the directions with an explicit dose and frequency): The trailing zero in 1.0 mg can be read as 10 mg if the decimal point is missed, and take as directed transfers the prescribing decision to a pharmacist who never assessed the client. Complete directions naming dose, route and frequency are a required element of a valid Canadian prescription. Two identifiers are already present, so a health number adds little safety here. Faxed prescriptions remain legal in most Canadian jurisdictions, so transmission method is not the urgent fix.
  6. The correct answer is A (Write out units and daily in full rather than using U and QD): The abbreviation for units has been misread as a zero or a four, turning 24 units into 240 units, and the abbreviation for daily has been misread as four times daily. Both appear on error prone abbreviation lists used across Canada because they alter dose and frequency, the two elements that cause insulin catastrophes. Brand naming and refill limits are reasonable additions but do not touch the tenfold error risk. Injection site rotation belongs in counselling rather than in the legal directions.
  7. The correct answer is D (The same diuretic is being taken twice under a brand and a generic name): Duplication of one drug under both a brand and a generic name is among the commonest unintentional discrepancies found at medication reconciliation, and here it doubles his daily diuretic to 80 mg. Volume depletion at that dose explains dizziness and near falls in an older adult. Bedtime statin dosing is conventional practice and does not cause falls. Antiplatelet therapy and a statin act through entirely different mechanisms and are not duplicates.
  8. The correct answer is B (The nurse records the order, reads it back, you confirm, and you countersign): Write down, read back, confirm and countersign within the organization's timeframe is the controlled process that makes a verbal order defensible. Read back by the receiver is the step that catches a misheard drug name or dose, so the prescriber simply repeating the order does not replace it. Witness signature does not substitute for prescriber confirmation of what was actually heard. Documenting only after the event removes the real time verification the process exists to provide.
  9. The correct answer is A (It sends the prescription from the medical record directly into the pharmacy system): PrescribeIT, operated by Canada Health Infoway, transmits a prescription from the prescriber's electronic medical record straight into the chosen pharmacy's management system, removing transcription and handwriting error and preventing misdirected faxes. It does not adjudicate provincial drug coverage, so formulary status still has to be checked separately. Controlled drug requirements are set by federal and provincial law and are not waived by the transmission method. Pharmacists still require prescriber authorization before changing a dose.
  10. The correct answer is C (Her authority is defined by the regulator of the province where she now practises): Nurse practitioner prescribing authority in Canada is set by each provincial and territorial nursing regulator, so scope is determined by where the practitioner actually practises. The federal New Classes of Practitioners Regulations opened controlled substance prescribing to nurse practitioners, but each jurisdiction then decides whether and how that authority is used and what added education or registration it requires. Authority does not travel with the original registration. Assuming national uniformity is the error this scenario tests.
  11. The correct answer is B (Reduce the total daily dose and recheck renal function more frequently): An eGFR between 30 and 45 mL/min/1.73m2 calls for a reduced metformin dose with closer monitoring rather than abrupt withdrawal, which is reserved for values below 30. Metformin is not started below 45, but continuation at a reduced dose is appropriate for someone already established on it. Glycemic control near target does not remove the need to adjust for falling clearance. Raising the dose in renal impairment increases the risk of lactic acidosis.
  12. The correct answer is D (Calculate creatinine clearance by Cockcroft and Gault using her actual body weight): Direct oral anticoagulant dose reduction criteria were validated against creatinine clearance from the Cockcroft and Gault equation using actual body weight, which is not the same number as an eGFR normalised to body surface area. In a small older woman the two values can differ enough to move her into a different dose band. Dabigatran is the most renally cleared agent of the group and demands the closest attention to clearance. Halving doses on the basis of age alone appears in no product monograph.
  13. The correct answer is C (Choose an antibiotic that does not inhibit CYP3A4 for this infection): Clarithromycin is a potent CYP3A4 inhibitor and simvastatin is a CYP3A4 substrate, so the combination raises statin concentrations and risks rhabdomyolysis. Selecting an antibiotic without that inhibitory effect removes the interaction entirely, which is safer than trying to manage around it. Weekly enzyme monitoring detects muscle damage after it has begun rather than preventing it. Permanent statin withdrawal sacrifices proven cardiovascular benefit for the sake of a short antibiotic course.
  14. The correct answer is D (Prescribe pantoprazole rather than omeprazole alongside his clopidogrel): Clopidogrel is a prodrug activated by CYP2C19, and omeprazole and esomeprazole inhibit that enzyme, reducing formation of the active metabolite and the antiplatelet effect. Pantoprazole has far less effect on CYP2C19 and is the preferred proton pump inhibitor alongside clopidogrel. Separating doses does not reliably overcome enzyme inhibition, because the inhibitory effect outlasts the plasma concentration. Replacing clopidogrel soon after stenting exposes him to stent thrombosis, which is not a reasonable trade for reflux control.
  15. The correct answer is B (Cumulative anticholinergic burden from three separate agents on her list): Oxybutynin, amitriptyline and diphenhydramine are all strongly anticholinergic, and their effects add together to produce confusion, constipation, dry mouth and urinary retention in an older adult.
