Anticholinergic burden is cumulative across ordinary drugs that each look harmless in isolation, and two of these were never reviewed because one is bought over the counter. Statins are not an established cause of this symptom cluster. Neither a renin angiotensin blocker nor a standard calcium supplement produces retention combined with confusion in this pattern.
  16. The correct answer is A (Fluoxetine inhibits CYP2D6, blocking conversion of codeine into morphine): Codeine is a prodrug requiring CYP2D6 to form morphine, and fluoxetine is a potent inhibitor of that enzyme, so someone taking fluoxetine may obtain no analgesia at all. Paroxetine and bupropion create the identical problem, and tramadol depends on the same activation pathway. Fluoxetine is not a CYP3A4 inducer, so accelerated clearance is not the mechanism here. Absorption of codeine is not meaningfully altered by antidepressants.
  17. The correct answer is D (Both the thiazide and the ibuprofen raise lithium concentrations toward toxicity): Lithium is reabsorbed alongside sodium in the proximal tubule, so thiazide induced sodium loss and prostaglandin inhibition by a nonsteroidal anti inflammatory drug both raise lithium concentrations. With a therapeutic index this narrow, a level of 0.9 mmol/L can climb past 1.5 mmol/L and produce toxicity. An agent that does not affect lithium handling, together with review of the daily ibuprofen, is the safer path. Angiotensin converting enzyme inhibitors and receptor blockers carry the same problem and are not the alternative.
  18. The correct answer is C (Measure serum potassium and creatinine before making any other change): Renin angiotensin blockade combined with a potassium sparing diuretic already raises potassium, and a potassium based salt substitute plus a supplement adds two further loads that he does not think of as medication. Hyperkalemia is usually silent until it causes an arrhythmia, so feeling well is not reassurance. Measurement precedes any prescribing change because the result determines whether this is an emergency. Stopping a mortality reducing heart failure drug or adding a loop diuretic blindly are both premature without a potassium value.
  19. The correct answer is B (Switch to an antihypertensive suitable in pregnancy and begin folic acid now): Angiotensin converting enzyme inhibitors cause fetal renal damage and oligohydramnios, and the exposure that matters can occur before a pregnancy is recognised, so the change is made preconception rather than at a positive test. Folic acid is started before conception whenever pregnancy is planned. Stopping treatment altogether leaves maternal hypertension untreated, which carries its own maternal and fetal risk. Ultrasound surveillance does not make continued exposure to a known fetal toxin acceptable.
  20. The correct answer is D (Check a lactation specific source, since monographs advise avoidance for legal reasons): Product monographs commonly advise against use in lactation to limit manufacturer liability rather than because measured infant exposure is harmful, so a lactation specific resource such as LactMed gives the clinically useful answer. Most drugs transfer into milk in trivial amounts, and every agent on this list is generally regarded as compatible with breastfeeding. Stopping effective treatment for postpartum depression exposes both mother and infant to far greater risk than continuing it. Pumping and discarding is unnecessary for drugs with a low relative infant dose and undermines her supply.
  21. The correct answer is A (Acetaminophen at a reduced maximum daily dose with an explicit stated ceiling): Acetaminophen remains usable in stable chronic liver disease at a reduced total daily ceiling and is safer here than any alternative on offer. Nonsteroidal anti inflammatory drugs precipitate variceal bleeding and hepatorenal syndrome in cirrhosis, and adding acid suppression removes neither the renal nor the variceal risk. Regular opioids accumulate through reduced first pass metabolism and precipitate hepatic encephalopathy. Naming the explicit daily ceiling is part of the prescription rather than an afterthought.
  22. The correct answer is C (The free phenytoin fraction is likely toxic despite a normal total concentration): Phenytoin is highly protein bound, so when albumin falls the bound fraction falls with it and the free, pharmacologically active fraction rises while the reported total still looks therapeutic. Nystagmus, ataxia and dysarthria are the classic toxic triad, and the clinical picture outranks the laboratory number. Warfarin and valproate behave the same way in hypoalbuminemia. Increasing the dose on the basis of the total concentration would deepen the toxicity.
  23. The correct answer is B (Gabapentin has accumulated in renal impairment and is producing these symptoms): Gabapentin is cleared unchanged by the kidney, so at an eGFR of 26 mL/min/1.73m2 a standard adult dose accumulates and produces sedation, ataxia and myoclonus. Onset within weeks of starting a new drug in an older adult points to the medication list rather than to a new neurodegenerative diagnosis. Attributing drug toxicity to a new disease is exactly how a prescribing cascade begins. Increasing the dose would worsen every symptom described.
  24. The correct answer is D (Reduce or change the calcium channel blocker rather than adding a diuretic): Dihydropyridine calcium channel blockers cause dose dependent peripheral edema through arteriolar dilation rather than fluid overload, and the absence of raised venous pressure, orthopnoea and weight gain argues strongly against heart failure. Treating that edema with a diuretic is the textbook prescribing cascade and exposes him to volume depletion and electrolyte loss without touching the cause. Reducing the dose or moving to another antihypertensive class resolves it. Imaging is not required when the temporal link and the examination findings are this clear.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (Arrange an electrocardiogram and urgent treatment of the potassium today): Potassium of 6.2 mmol/L is an emergency regardless of how well the person looks, because cardiac conduction can fail before any symptom appears, so tracing the heart and lowering the potassium come first. Stopping the nonsteroidal drug and stopping the spironolactone outright both follow immediately afterwards, but neither lowers potassium quickly enough today, and merely halving the spironolactone, increasing the furosemide or waiting a week to repeat the bloodwork would each leave her exposed to arrhythmia in the meantime. The underlying cause is renin angiotensin blockade plus a potassium sparing diuretic plus a nonsteroidal drug on a background of collapsing filtration. Reviewing that combination is the second step, not the first.
  2. The correct answer is C (Ask him to explain and demonstrate the whole regimen back to you now): Teach back, where the person explains the plan in their own words and demonstrates the device, is the only method here that verifies understanding rather than assuming it. Asking whether someone has questions reliably produces a no, particularly across a language difference and a power difference. Written material at an accessible reading level in his own language supports teach back but does not replace it. Delegating to the pharmacist tells you nothing about what he understood in your office.
  3. The correct answer is B (Ask openly about cost and coverage before assuming he chose not to take it): Filling once and collecting no refills points to a barrier rather than to treatment failure, and cost is the barrier most often present and least often asked about in someone without drug coverage. Naming it directly opens the conversation and leads to a formulary option, a generic, or an assistance programme. Labelling him non compliant records a judgement in place of a cause and changes nothing. Adding a second agent to a drug he is not taking compounds both the cost and the risk.
  4. The correct answer is A (The Canadian Drug Product Database maintained by Health Canada): The Canadian Drug Product Database records every drug product authorised for sale in Canada together with its strengths and dosage forms, which is precisely the marketing question being asked. Any compendium of therapeutics guides selection among available agents but is not the authority on what holds a Canadian market authorisation. Provincial formularies state what is publicly funded, which is a different question from what is marketed. Pharmacokinetic data cannot establish Canadian availability.
  5. The correct answer is D (Take a detailed allergy history and consider a beta lactam based on it): Most childhood rashes labelled as penicillin allergy are not immunoglobulin E mediated, and reflexive avoidance drives the use of broader, more toxic and often less effective agents. Structured history taking that covers the reaction, its timing and its severity often allows safe beta lactam use or referral for testing. Reserving glycopeptides and fluoroquinolones for people who genuinely need them is core antimicrobial stewardship. Rechallenging with the original drug before taking that history skips the assessment that would make the decision safe.
  6. The correct answer is B (Recheck creatinine and potassium in one to two weeks along with blood pressure): The monitoring plan names the parameter, the target, the interval and the action threshold, and for a new renin angiotensin blocker that means creatinine and potassium at roughly one to two weeks alongside blood pressure. That interval catches the early rise in creatinine and the climb in potassium before either becomes dangerous. Waiting three months allows an unrecognised acute kidney injury to persist. Hyperkalemia is usually silent until it produces an arrhythmia, so symptom triggered testing is not safe.
  7. The correct answer is C (The proton pump inhibitor, continued four years without a maintained indication): Proton pump inhibitors continued well beyond eight weeks without a maintained indication appear on the Beers list and have a Canadian deprescribing algorithm supporting dose reduction, on demand use or stopping. She has no reflux symptoms, no Barrett esophagus, no severe esophagitis and no ongoing bleeding risk, so none of the continuation criteria apply. Her statin is secondary prevention after myocardial infarction, which the deprescribing tools flag as therapy to keep. Nothing in her presentation suggests her blood pressure or diabetes therapy is causing harm.
  8. The correct answer is A (Do not treat, explain asymptomatic bacteriuria, and address her hydration instead): Asymptomatic bacteriuria is common in older women, and treating it does not prevent symptomatic infection while it does cause adverse effects, resistance and Clostridioides difficile infection. Pregnancy and planned urologic procedures are the recognised exceptions, and neither applies here. Cloudy or strong smelling urine is not a symptom of infection and usually reflects concentration. Repeating the culture simply invites treatment of the same harmless finding one week later.
  9. The correct answer is B (Check the monitoring programme, avoid benzodiazepines, offer naloxone, document a functional goal): Canadian college prescribing standards pair every opioid prescription with a check of the provincial monitoring programme where one operates, an offer of take home naloxone to anyone at elevated risk, and a documented functional goal against which continuation is judged, and the 2017 Canadian Guideline for Opioids for Chronic Non Cancer Pain adds that opioids and benzodiazepines should very rarely be prescribed together. Without a stated functional goal there is nothing to evaluate at follow up. Narcotic prescriptions are not written with repeats, and part fills are used instead. Concurrent benzodiazepine use multiplies respiratory depression risk rather than reducing the opioid requirement.
  10. The correct answer is C (Reduce by 5 to 10 percent every two to four weeks, slowing at lower doses): Canadian guidance supports a slow taper of roughly 5 to 10 percent of the morphine equivalent dose every two to four weeks, going slower as the dose falls, and pausing or reversing the taper if the person destabilises. Rapid or forced tapering causes harm, including withdrawal, loss of function and transition to the unregulated drug supply. Abrupt cessation with a withdrawal agent is not a taper and does nothing for her pain. Trials of tapering are recommended above 90 morphine milligram equivalents rather than avoided.
  11. The correct answer is D (The herbal product can reduce contraceptive effectiveness through enzyme induction): St John's wort is a potent inducer of CYP3A4 and of intestinal transport, accelerating clearance of ethinyl oestradiol and progestin, and it has produced breakthrough bleeding and unintended pregnancy. Natural health products belong in the Best Possible Medication History precisely because people do not report them as medications. Doubling a contraceptive dose is not an evidence based workaround and is not how this interaction is managed. Induction lowers rather than raises hormone concentrations.
  12. The correct answer is A (Advise against sharing medication and arrange assessment, considering topical treatment): Sharing another person's prescription is unsafe in itself, and fluconazole inhibits the enzymes that clear warfarin, so even short courses have produced marked rises in the international normalized ratio and serious bleeding. Assessing her also confirms the diagnosis rather than assuming it from symptoms reported by telephone. Topical therapy achieves negligible systemic exposure and sidesteps the interaction altogether. Holding warfarin without measurement swaps one uncontrolled risk for another and leaves her unprotected from stroke.
  13. The correct answer is B (Review each medication for a form that can be given safely by this route): Crushing a modified release tablet destroys the release mechanism and delivers the whole dose at once, which explains morning dizziness after a crushed extended release beta blocker. The correct step is a medication by medication review with a pharmacist for a liquid, dispersible, patch or immediate release alternative. Splitting a crushed modified release dose does not restore controlled release and still produces a peak. Stopping every medication or placing a feeding tube are disproportionate when a formulation change solves the problem.
  14. The correct answer is C (Offer cognitive behavioural therapy for insomnia as the recommended first line treatment): Cognitive behavioural therapy for insomnia is first line for chronic insomnia in adults and outperforms hypnotics in durability, and her caffeine timing and use of the bed are exactly what it addresses. Benzodiazepine receptor agonists including the z drugs appear on the Beers list, cause dependence and falls, and have a Canadian deprescribing algorithm because stopping them is so difficult. Sedating antihistamines add anticholinergic burden rather than avoiding harm. Trazodone has weak evidence in primary insomnia and causes orthostatic hypotension.
  15. The correct answer is A (Name the likely early adverse effects and state exactly what to do about each): Anticipating the common early adverse effects and giving a concrete plan for each turns a frightening surprise into an expected, manageable event, which is what changes persistence in someone whose last medication was abandoned for exactly that reason. Adherence fails because of cost, complexity, side effects, poor understanding and beliefs nobody asked about, and his history names the cause directly. Warnings about consequences raise fear without giving him an action to take. An exhaustive written list at a high reading level is unusable and can itself deter him from starting.
Case C042

Mrs. Okonkwo is 82 years old and lives alone. Over the past eight months she was started on amlodipine 10 mg daily for hypertension, then furosemide 20 mg daily for ankle swelling, then oxybutynin 5 mg twice daily for new urinary urgency. She also takes metformin, atorvastatin and a daily multivitamin.

Today she reports dry mouth, constipation, two near falls and mild confusion. Her blood pressure sitting is 128/74 mmHg and standing is 104/62 mmHg. Serum creatinine is 118 umol/L with an eGFR of 38 mL/min/1.73m2.

  1. The correct answer is C (Map the start date of each medication against the onset of each symptom): when a new symptom appears in an older adult, the first hypothesis is the medication list, and a timeline is what tests that hypothesis. Imaging the head investigates a structural cause that nothing in this history suggests and delays the obvious answer. Referring before doing the review you are capable of doing yourself adds months of exposure to the offending drugs. Sending urine in an older adult with a plausible drug cause invites treatment of asymptomatic bacteriuria.
  2. The correct answer is A (Amlodipine caused ankle edema, which was then treated by adding furosemide): dihydropyridine calcium channel blockers produce dose related peripheral edema from precapillary dilation, and treating that edema with a diuretic is the textbook cascade. Metformin does not cause urinary urgency, so that pairing describes an association rather than a drug effect. Reducing a drug is not a cascade, since a cascade requires a second drug to be added. Atorvastatin does not cause confusion, and a multivitamin is not a treatment for it.
  3. The correct answer is D (Stop oxybutynin, the drug carrying the highest anticholinergic burden here): dry mouth, constipation, confusion and falls are the classic anticholinergic cluster, and this drug was itself added to treat a symptom rather than a disease. Starting with the diuretic leaves the anticholinergic toxicity untouched and risks unmasking the edema before the cause is addressed. Swapping the calcium channel blocker is a reasonable later step but does not relieve her most troubling symptoms. Metformin needs dose reduction and closer monitoring at this level of function, since contraindication begins below an eGFR of 30.
  4. The correct answer is B (Check sitting and standing blood pressure plus ankle edema every two weeks): a monitoring plan names the parameter, the interval and the finding that would change the plan, and here the parameters are the postural drop and the edema the cascade began with. Annual bloodwork with an open invitation to call gives no defined interval and no threshold. Following renal function alone tracks neither the blood pressure effect nor the edema. A six month gap with no contact leaves a frail woman unmonitored through two sequential medication changes.
Case C043

Mr. Lavoie is 71 years old. Six years ago he was prescribed pantoprazole 40 mg daily after eight weeks of heartburn that resolved completely. The prescription has been renewed ever since without review.

He has no history of Barrett esophagus, upper gastrointestinal bleeding or severe esophagitis. He takes ramipril, rosuvastatin and clopidogrel following a coronary stent two years ago. He has had two falls this year and asks whether he still needs all of his pills.

  1. The correct answer is B (His reflux resolved after an eight week course and never recurred since): the Canadian algorithm targets exactly this person, someone who completed treatment for uncomplicated reflux and stayed on therapy with no maintained indication. Age and pill count raise suspicion but neither establishes that the indication has lapsed. The interaction with clopidogrel involves omeprazole and esomeprazole through CYP2C19, which is why pantoprazole is the preferred partner rather than a reason to stop it. Fracture risk is an argument about harm, not evidence that the original indication has ended.
  2. The correct answer is D (Endoscopically confirmed Barrett esophagus found during a previous investigation): the algorithm names Barrett esophagus, severe esophagitis and ongoing bleeding risk as the groups who continue indefinitely rather than taper. Occasional heartburn already answering to antacids argues for on demand therapy, not lifelong daily suppression. Low dose acetylsalicylic acid alone in someone with no bleeding history is not itself an indication for gastroprotection. A family history of gastric cancer changes surveillance discussions but does not create an indication for acid suppression.
  3. The correct answer is A (Halve the dose or use it on demand, and warn about rebound heartburn): the algorithm offers a reduced dose, on demand use or stopping, paired with counselling that rebound acid hypersecretion can occur and is self limited. Replacing one indefinite acid suppressant with another indefinite one simply moves the problem and creates a new drug to deprescribe later. A rigid fixed percentage taper belongs to the benzodiazepine receptor agonist algorithm, which steps down by about one quarter every two weeks rather than every four, and not to acid suppression. Renewing for another two years is what produced the six year prescription in the first place.
  4. The correct answer is C (Explain likely rebound acid secretion and offer an antacid or on demand dosing): symptoms appearing within weeks of dose reduction in someone whose original reflux had resolved are far more likely to reflect transient rebound hypersecretion than returning disease. Restoring the full dose permanently at the first symptom converts a predictable and self limited effect into another six years of therapy. Nothing here meets the alarm criteria that would justify urgent endoscopy, since he has no dysphagia, weight loss or bleeding. Layering a second acid suppressant on top adds a drug before the simplest explanation has been tested.
Case C044

Mrs. Belanger is 78 years old and has taken zopiclone 7.5 mg at bedtime for four years, originally prescribed during a bereavement. She now sleeps poorly despite the medication and feels groggy each morning.

She fell in the bathroom six weeks ago and sustained a wrist fracture. Her other medications are levothyroxine, calcium with vitamin D, and cetirizine for seasonal rhinitis. Her Mini Mental State Examination score is 27 out of 30 and her serum creatinine is 96 umol/L.

  1. The correct answer is A (A sleep history covering timing, alcohol, caffeine, naps and daytime function): the decision turns on whether the drug still delivers benefit and what modifiable factors are keeping her awake, and evening alcohol is a strong candidate here. Sleep laboratory testing is reserved for suspected sleep apnea or parasomnia rather than for chronic hypnotic use with obvious behavioural contributors. Serum concentrations of this drug are not used clinically and would not tell you whether it helps. Deferring any change for three months of cognitive testing leaves her exposed after an injurious fall.
  2. The correct answer is B (Morning grogginess with a fall and fracture while the sleep benefit has gone): a documented harm has already occurred, the mechanism linking it to the drug is residual sedation, and the benefit side of the equation is now empty. Long duration without review is a trigger to reassess rather than proof that the balance has tipped. A score of 27 out of 30 sits at the borderline and cannot by itself be called decline without a previous score to compare. Appearing on a potentially inappropriate medication list flags the drug for review but does not measure harm in this individual.
  3. The correct answer is C (Reduce by about one quarter every two weeks, adding cognitive behavioural therapy): the benzodiazepine receptor agonist algorithm pairs a slow stepwise reduction, with smaller decrements near the end, with the non pharmacological treatment that actually works for chronic insomnia. Substituting another sedating drug replaces one hypnotic with another and abandons the goal of the exercise. Halving weekly is faster than the algorithm advises and offers nothing to manage the insomnia that surfaces during withdrawal. Converting to a longer acting benzodiazepine adds an unnecessary switch and keeps her on a falls risk drug for months.
  4. The correct answer is C (Hold at the current dose, normalize the symptoms, and resume reducing when settled): mild rebound insomnia and irritability are expected, time limited and best managed by pausing rather than abandoning or accelerating the plan. Returning to the starting dose teaches her that the taper failed and makes the next attempt harder. Adding a benzodiazepine during a benzodiazepine receptor agonist taper reintroduces the exact class being withdrawn. Stopping the remaining dose abruptly is the maneuver that risks seizure and severe withdrawal, which is why the algorithm insists on gradual reduction.
Case C045

Mr. Singh is 84 years old and lives in a long term care home. Nursing staff sent a urine sample because his urine smelled strong and looked cloudy. The culture grew more than 100 million colony forming units per litre of Escherichia coli, sensitive to nitrofurantoin and to trimethoprim with sulfamethoxazole.

He has no fever, no dysuria, no frequency, no suprapubic or flank pain, and no change from his usual behaviour. He takes ramipril, metformin and warfarin, with a stable international normalized ratio of 2.4.

  1. The correct answer is D (New fever with rigors and acute worsening of his usual daily function): bacteriuria becomes urinary tract infection only when localizing or systemic features of infection appear, and a change from baseline function with fever is the signal that matters in a long term care resident. Repeating the culture confirms colonization rather than converting it into disease. Pyuria accompanies asymptomatic bacteriuria routinely in this population and is not a treatment indication. Urine odour and appearance reflect concentration and diet and have no diagnostic value for infection.
  2. The correct answer is A (Asymptomatic bacteriuria, which should not be treated with an antibiotic): significant bacteriuria with pyuria and no urinary or systemic symptoms defines this entity, and the recognized exceptions to leaving it alone are pregnancy and planned urologic procedures. Nothing about an afebrile, functionally unchanged resident suggests bloodstream infection. Calling it a complicated infection because he is old and institutionalized applies a label the clinical picture does not support. A pure heavy growth of a single uropathogen argues against contamination, and catheterizing him to repeat the test adds risk without changing management.
  3. The correct answer is B (Document the reasoning and set explicit criteria that would trigger reassessment): stewardship in long term care depends on a decision the whole team can follow, so writing down why no antibiotic is indicated and exactly which changes would warrant a new assessment prevents a weekend prescription. A delayed prescription is a tool for symptomatic self limited infections, not for a resident with no symptoms at all. Repeat culture in an untreated asymptomatic resident generates another positive result and renewed pressure to treat. Prescribing to relieve staff anxiety exposes him to adverse effects and resistance for no benefit.
  4. The correct answer is D (Nitrofurantoin, avoiding the interaction that would raise his international normalized ratio): this is a narrow bladder specific agent, his previous isolate was sensitive to it, and his renal function permits it, while the alternative sulfonamide combination is one of the classic warfarin interactions that drives bleeding admissions. That sulfonamide combination would also add potassium retention on top of his angiotensin converting enzyme inhibitor. A fluoroquinolone is far broader than this presentation needs and carries tendon and central nervous system risks in older adults. Beta lactam combinations are broader again and offer no advantage for uncomplicated cystitis.
  5. The correct answer is C (Beers Criteria list drugs to avoid but do not flag missing therapies): that tool catalogues potentially inappropriate medications in adults 65 and older, so a list containing none of them can still be badly incomplete. Deprescribing is not only subtraction, and the START half of the STOPP and START tool exists precisely to catch omissions such as an absent anticoagulant in atrial fibrillation. The tool applies across community and institutional settings rather than to one care environment. It is a screening aid a prescriber can apply directly, and it remains current alongside the 2023 European criteria.
  6. The correct answer is A (Absence of an oral anticoagulant despite permanent atrial fibrillation): among these omissions this one carries the largest absolute benefit, since anticoagulation in an 80-year-old with permanent atrial fibrillation prevents disabling stroke and her renal function permits a direct oral agent. Bone protection after a vertebral fracture matters and should follow, but a fragility fracture is less immediately catastrophic than a cardioembolic stroke. Secondary prevention with a statin is also indicated and is the next priority after anticoagulation. Vitamin D supports bone therapy rather than substituting for it, so it ranks lowest here.
  7. The correct answer is A (Cumulative anticholinergic burden from three separate drugs explains her presentation): a sedating first generation antihistamine, a bladder antimuscarinic and a tricyclic antidepressant each carry anticholinergic activity, and burden is additive across ordinary drugs rather than confined to one culprit. Confusion, constipation, dry mouth and urinary retention are the expected cluster, and the normal bloodwork removes the common metabolic explanations. A stool softener dose is not the reason a woman on three anticholinergics is constipated. Starting a cholinesterase inhibitor before removing the drugs causing the picture would begin a new cascade.
  8. The correct answer is D (Perindopril, hydrochlorothiazide and ibuprofen, producing acute kidney injury): the nonsteroidal drug blocks prostaglandin mediated dilation of the afferent arteriole, the renin angiotensin blocker prevents efferent constriction, and the diuretic reduces circulating volume, so glomerular filtration collapses. The timing fits exactly, because the third agent was bought without a prescription ten days ago. Lactate accumulation from metformin presents with acidosis and is a consequence of failing kidneys here rather than the initiating combination. Occult bleeding would present with anemia rather than falling urine output, and acid suppression does not reduce filtration.
  9. The correct answer is B (Serum creatinine and potassium measured at one to two weeks after starting): renin angiotensin blockade reduces efferent arteriolar tone and impairs potassium excretion, so both filtration and potassium must be rechecked within the first two weeks, and sooner in established kidney disease. Blood pressure and weight track effectiveness rather than the two harms that actually cause admissions. Hematology and liver enzymes are not the toxicities of this class. Waiting three months allows hyperkalemia or a substantial creatinine rise to go undetected through the highest risk window.
  10. The correct answer is C (Continue ramipril, since this rise sits within the expected range): a rise of roughly 17 percent reflects the predictable hemodynamic effect of reducing efferent arteriolar tone, and increases of up to about 30 percent from baseline with a stable potassium are tolerated rather than treated. Stopping at the first small rise denies him the renal and cardiovascular protection the drug provides. Halving the dose and waiting three months both reduces benefit and abandons monitoring during the period when a real problem would declare itself. Adding a diuretic would lower perfusion further and increase the risk of genuine injury.
  11. The correct answer is B (Begin a gradual dose reduction with monitoring for return of symptoms): the Canadian antipsychotic algorithm applies to drugs prescribed for behavioural and psychological symptoms of dementia, and once symptoms have been stable for roughly three months a monitored taper is attempted. He has been stable for five months and is now showing the sedation and gait instability that make continued exposure harmful. Indefinite continuation ignores the stroke and mortality risks of antipsychotics in dementia. Switching to another antipsychotic keeps him in the same class, and abrupt cessation forfeits the chance to detect early recurrence.
  12. The correct answer is D (Stop metformin, as this level of renal function is a contraindication): metformin is contraindicated once estimated filtration falls below 30 mL/min/1.73m2, and her result sits clearly under that line. Dose reduction with closer monitoring is the correct response in the band between 30 and 45, which she has already passed through. Leaving the dose alone because glycemic control looks acceptable ignores the risk of lactic acidosis in advancing kidney disease. Escalating any sulfonylurea in advancing kidney disease raises her hypoglycemia risk, because reduced renal gluconeogenesis and reduced insulin clearance prolong any hypoglycemic episode.
  13. The correct answer is A (Stop glyburide and reassess control against a relaxed individualized target): the antihyperglycemic deprescribing algorithm is driven by hypoglycemia risk rather than by glucose numbers alone, and this drug is the sulfonylurea most associated with prolonged hypoglycemia in older adults. A glycated hemoglobin of 6.5 percent in a frail man living alone represents overtreatment, since targets are relaxed in this group. Telling him to change his eating pattern places the burden on him rather than on the unsafe drug. Metformin does not cause hypoglycemia on its own, so reducing it would worsen control without removing the hazard.
  14. The correct answer is C (Propose a monitored trial of withdrawal given her stage and care goals): the cholinesterase inhibitor algorithm supports a withdrawal trial when dementia has advanced, when no benefit is observable, or when goals of care move toward comfort, and all three apply here. Gastrointestinal effects and anorexia from this class may also be contributing to her weight loss. Escalating the dose in someone with no measurable benefit adds adverse effects without a goal. Framing continuation as protection against sudden decline overstates the evidence and removes the family's real choice.
  15. The correct answer is D (A cholinergic effect of donepezil was treated with an opposing anticholinergic): cholinesterase inhibitors increase detrusor activity and can precipitate urgency and incontinence, and adding a bladder antimuscarinic creates two drugs working directly against each other. The anticholinergic then worsens cognition, which is the very outcome the first drug was prescribed to protect. Disease progression does not usually produce new urgency within six weeks of starting a new drug. Increasing the antimuscarinic dose would deepen the confusion and constipation rather than resolve the underlying conflict.
  16. The correct answer is B (The same calcium channel blocker appears twice under two different names): duplication of one drug under a brand and a generic label is among the commonest unintentional discrepancies found at transitions of care, and here it doubles his antihypertensive dose to 10 mg daily. Building the history from two independent sources is exactly what exposed it. A beta blocker at 50 mg twice daily is a conventional dose and does not by itself explain hypotension. Rare as needed hypnotic use and acid suppression are not credible causes of a sustained low blood pressure.
  17. The correct answer is C (Provide symptom advice and a delayed prescription with clear criteria for filling): four days of symptoms without fever, unilateral pain or purulence describes a viral illness, and the delayed prescription is a named Choosing Wisely Canada tool that reduces antibiotic use while giving the person a plan. Prescribing immediately treats her itinerary rather than an infection. Refusing without safety netting leaves her with no guidance about the small proportion who develop bacterial sinusitis. Sinus imaging cannot distinguish viral from bacterial disease at this stage and adds cost and radiation.
  18. The correct answer is A (Five days total, provided he has been clinically stable for 48 hours): current evidence supports short courses for uncomplicated community acquired pneumonia, with clinical stability rather than a fixed calendar deciding when to stop. Longer exposure increases adverse effects, Clostridioides difficile risk and resistance without improving cure. The belief that longer courses prevent resistance reverses the actual relationship. Radiographic changes lag clinical recovery by weeks, so treating until the film clears would mean weeks of unnecessary antibiotic.
  19. The correct answer is B (A booked review with a pain score and a function measure at set intervals): a non pharmacological intervention needs the same four part plan as a drug, naming the parameter, the target, the interval and the threshold that would change management. An open ended promise to return if it fails supplies no interval and no measurable parameter, which is how exercise programmes quietly lapse. Radiographic joint changes do not improve with exercise and would not reflect the outcomes she cares about. Inflammatory markers are not elevated in osteoarthritis and would monitor nothing.
  20. The correct answer is B (Trial bladder training first and avoid adding a second anticholinergic drug): behavioural approaches including timed voiding and pelvic floor training are first line for overactive bladder, and adding a second anticholinergic to a man who already has cognitive impairment stacks burden that accelerates decline and falls. Halving the tricyclic still leaves two anticholinergics on the list. Low dose initiation with six monthly cognitive checks accepts avoidable exposure and would detect harm long after it began. Substituting a first generation antihistamine swaps one strongly anticholinergic drug for another.
  21. The correct answer is D (Send her for urgent assessment and stop the potassium raising drugs): a potassium of 6.1 mmol/L with weakness and falling filtration is an emergency requiring an electrocardiogram and treatment, and three of her drugs raise potassium simultaneously. Trimethoprim blocks the epithelial sodium channel much as a potassium sparing diuretic does, which is why this antibiotic added to renin angiotensin blockade and an aldosterone antagonist is a recognized cause of admission. Repeating the test in a week or waiting three days accepts a risk of fatal arrhythmia. Dietary advice alone cannot correct a value in this range.
  22. The correct answer is C (Metronidazole raises the international normalized ratio, so earlier retesting is needed): this antibiotic inhibits the metabolism of the more active warfarin enantiomer, and the resulting rise is one of the interactions that produces bleeding admissions, so the ratio should be rechecked within a few days of starting. Treating the combination as harmless is exactly how those bleeds happen. Holding anticoagulation for an entire course exposes a man with a thrombotic indication to needless stroke or clot risk. Infection and inflammation tend to raise rather than lower the ratio.
  23. The correct answer is A (Taper by 5 to 10 percent of the daily dose every two to four weeks): the 2017 Canadian opioid guideline recommends a trial of tapering above 90 morphine milligram equivalents daily, done slowly and slower still at low doses, with the plan paused or reversed if she destabilizes. Halving the dose in one step is a forced rapid taper, which causes real harm including transition to the unregulated supply. Leaving her at a high dose with no functional benefit and daytime sedation abandons the reason for reassessment. Adding a benzodiazepine to an opioid is the combination most associated with fatal respiratory depression.
  24. The correct answer is D (Coordinate reduction of the clonazepam and provide take home naloxone): concurrent benzodiazepine and opioid use multiplies respiratory depression risk, so the guideline advises avoiding the combination, and naloxone matters most for someone alone and far from emergency care. Raising the opioid dose worsens the very risk being managed. Locked storage prevents diversion and accidental ingestion by others but does nothing about the interaction he takes deliberately. Urine testing monitors adherence and unexpected substances rather than preventing the overdose mechanism at work here.
  25. The correct answer is B (Gabapentin accumulation, because renal clearance fell while the dose stayed high): this drug is cleared unchanged in the urine, so halving the filtration rate roughly doubles exposure even when the prescription never changes, and sedation, ataxia and myoclonus are the classic toxicity picture. The timing matches the fall in filtration rather than any prescribing event. Statin adverse effects are muscular rather than myoclonic and do not cause this syndrome. The total daily acetaminophen dose here is 2600 mg, which is within safe limits, and renin angiotensin blockers do not depress the central nervous system.
  26. The correct answer is A (Low muscle mass makes the reported eGFR overestimate her true drug clearance): creatinine production depends on muscle, so a frail 42 kg woman can have a reassuring creatinine alongside badly impaired clearance. Direct oral anticoagulant dosing in Canada uses creatinine clearance from the Cockcroft and Gault equation with actual body weight, which is not the same number the laboratory reports. Advanced age and low body weight are themselves dose reduction criteria for this agent and must be applied exactly rather than approximated. All agents in the class need renal function checked at least annually.
  27. The correct answer is C (Repeat thyroid stimulating hormone in six to eight weeks and titrate to it): for a thyroid replacement change both effectiveness and safety are read from the same value, and it must be drawn only after a new steady state has been reached. Testing at two weeks samples a hormone that has not yet equilibrated and invites an unnecessary further change. Weekly free thyroxine measurement tracks a parameter that is neither the titration target nor a measure of her symptom. Waiting six months leaves her either undertreated or overtreated for months, with the bone and atrial fibrillation risks that overtreatment carries.
  28. The correct answer is C (Assess the symptom, check creatine kinase once, and consider a dose change): routine enzyme monitoring is not recommended for this class, but a single measurement is appropriate when muscle symptoms appear, and most people tolerate a lower dose or a different agent afterwards. Weekly testing over a month generates data that will not change the decision. Labelling him statin intolerant permanently after one trial denies him proven cardiovascular protection. Dismissing the symptom risks missing the rare progression to rhabdomyolysis and guarantees he will stop the drug without telling you.
  29. The correct answer is B (Stop lithium and the thiazide, and arrange urgent emergency assessment): a level of 1.8 mmol/L with coarse tremor, vomiting and ataxia is established toxicity, and toxicity risk rises above 1.5 mmol/L, so this needs hospital assessment for hydration and possible dialysis. The thiazide caused it by promoting sodium loss and proximal lithium reabsorption, which her low sodium confirms. Halving the dose or holding for two days at home leaves a woman who is already vomiting to dehydrate further and climb higher. Treating the vomiting symptomatically masks the sign that is telling you she is toxic.
  30. The correct answer is D (Combined antifolate effects can cause severe bone marrow suppression): both drugs interfere with folate pathways and the combination has produced fatal pancytopenia, so a different antibiotic such as nitrofurantoin should be chosen for uncomplicated cystitis. The interaction increases rather than decreases methotrexate exposure, partly through reduced renal tubular secretion. Converting a weekly antirheumatic dose to daily dosing is the classic fatal medication error with this drug and must never be done. Weekly folic acid reduces mucosal and hepatic adverse effects but does not neutralize this interaction.
  31. The correct answer is A (Nitrofurantoin 100 mg twice daily for five days without a urine culture): uncomplicated cystitis in a healthy non pregnant woman is a clinical diagnosis, culture is not routinely required, and this agent is narrow, concentrated in urine and spares gut flora. A fluoroquinolone is a far broader choice with tendon, aortic and central nervous system risks that stewardship reserves for situations with no alternative. A beta lactam combination is broader again, and delaying treatment for allergy testing is unnecessary when a non beta lactam option exists. Withholding treatment leaves a symptomatic infection untreated for days.
  32. The correct answer is B (Take a structured allergy history and prescribe cephalexin if he is low risk): most reported penicillin allergies are not immunoglobulin E mediated, a childhood rash without airway or circulatory features is low risk, and reflexive avoidance drives use of broader and less effective agents. A glycopeptide for uncomplicated cellulitis is unnecessarily broad and usually requires intravenous administration. Clindamycin carries a high rate of Clostridioides difficile infection and rising resistance among skin organisms. Skin testing has a role in complex cases but should not delay treatment of an active infection.
  33. The correct answer is C (No, because appropriateness matters more than the number of medications): the count of five or more is a screening trigger for review rather than a diagnosis, and a regimen where every drug has a current indication, a correct dose and no interaction is appropriate polypharmacy. Treating the number as the problem leads to withdrawing beneficial therapy, which is the mirror image error the START criteria were written to prevent. Interactions are identified by review rather than assumed from a count. The concept applies across adult ages and is not restricted to the very old.
  34. The correct answer is A (Stop metoclopramide and reassess the movement disorder over several weeks): this antiemetic blocks central dopamine receptors and is a well recognized cause of drug induced parkinsonism, which characteristically appears symmetrical and responds poorly to dopamine replacement. Prescribing levodopa for it created a cascade, treating a drug adverse effect as a new disease. Escalating the replacement therapy or adding a second dopaminergic drug compounds that error and adds adverse effects. Surgical referral is inappropriate before the reversible pharmacological cause has been removed and the picture reassessed.
  35. The correct answer is D (Weight, blood pressure, fasting glucose and lipid profile at set intervals): the dominant long term harm of second generation antipsychotics is metabolic, and this drug carries the highest risk in the class, so anthropometric and metabolic parameters are what the safety plan must track. Serum concentrations are not used to guide therapy with this agent, and routine hematology monitoring belongs to clozapine. Liver and muscle enzymes are not the recurring toxicities here. Thyroid and vitamin B12 testing addresses neither the drug nor the weight gain pattern described.
  36. The correct answer is C (Discuss a slow taper with her psychiatrist, weighing relapse against long term risk): sustained remission makes her a reasonable candidate for withdrawal, but the decision belongs in a shared conversation with the prescriber managing the psychiatric condition, and any reduction must be gradual. Stopping abruptly on the basis of age alone risks seizure, severe withdrawal and relapse of a treated disorder. Renewing indefinitely without review is how four years becomes twenty. Substituting a z drug offers no safety advantage, since that class carries the same dependence and falls concerns.
  37. The correct answer is B (Explain the viral cause, give a symptom timeline and specific return criteria): a well appearing child with clear rhinorrhea, a normal chest and normal tympanic membranes has a viral upper respiratory infection, and the stewardship task is a credible explanation with safety netting rather than a prescription. Antibiotics do not shorten viral illness and expose him to rash, diarrhea and resistance. Imaging and swabbing a child with a normal examination adds distress and cost without changing management. Macrolides are broader than amoxicillin, not narrower, and resistance among respiratory pathogens is substantial.
  38. The correct answer is A (Hold warfarin, give oral vitamin K, and recheck the ratio within 24 hours): a ratio in the 4.5 to 10 range without significant bleeding is managed by withholding doses with early repeat testing, and low dose oral vitamin K is added when bleeding risk is raised, as it is here by his age, his bruising and his borderline hemoglobin. The azole inhibited warfarin metabolism, and the effect persists for days after the antibiotic course ends. Continuing unchanged for a week leaves him at high bleeding risk through the most dangerous period. Reversal with clotting factor concentrate is reserved for serious bleeding or an urgent procedure, and restarting the interacting drug would repeat the problem.
  39. The correct answer is D (A serum potassium above 5.5 mmol/L confirmed on repeat measurement): combining an aldosterone antagonist with a renin angiotensin blocker is the classic route to dangerous hyperkalemia, and a confirmed value above 5.5 mmol/L is the point at which action is required. Weight gain over three days is a useful heart failure decompensation signal but says nothing about this drug's principal hazard. A systolic pressure below 120 mmHg is often the therapeutic goal rather than a problem. A creatinine rise of 10 umol/L falls within normal biological and analytical variation.
  40. The correct answer is C (Stop amitriptyline first, then plan a taper of the temazepam): a tricyclic used for insomnia has no good indication, and it contributes sedation, anticholinergic burden and orthostatic hypotension at once, so it is both the most harmful and the easiest to remove. The benzodiazepine follows, tapered rather than stopped, because abrupt cessation risks withdrawal. Beta blockade may be treating a cardiac indication and is not the leading contributor to a postural drop of this size. Adding a pressor drug to counteract an effect caused by other drugs would start a new cascade.
  41. The correct answer is B (Abrupt beta blocker withdrawal can trigger angina, arrhythmia or infarction): receptor upregulation during chronic blockade means sudden cessation produces rebound sympathetic activity, and in someone with angina that can precipitate a coronary event, so this drug must be tapered. Deprescribing changes one drug at a time with a plan, never everything at once. The principle applies at any age wherever a drug lacks benefit or causes harm. Halving every dose simultaneously is the same abrupt reduction applied across his whole regimen and makes any resulting problem impossible to attribute.
  42. The correct answer is A (Stop the naproxen and stop the potassium chloride supplement): the nonsteroidal drug added to a renin angiotensin blocker and a diuretic is the combination that produces acute kidney injury, and it also worsens fluid retention in heart failure. A potassium supplement alongside both an angiotensin converting enzyme inhibitor and an aldosterone antagonist is an avoidable hyperkalemia risk with no remaining indication. Withdrawing the aldosterone antagonist would remove a therapy with proven mortality benefit in heart failure. Neither metformin nor the loop diuretic is the immediate hazard on this list.
  43. The correct answer is D (Ask her to describe in her own words how she will take it): teach back is the method that verifies understanding, because a person can nod and agree while having understood very little. Written material supports recall but does not test comprehension, and reading level or language may make it inaccessible. Asking whether she has questions is the technique teach back exists to replace, since most people say no. Pharmacist reinforcement is valuable and should happen anyway, but delegating verification does not tell you what she understood in the room.
Case C046

A 46-year-old warehouse supervisor comes to your clinic for a sore shoulder.

Near the end of the visit he mentions that his wife has been complaining about his drinking.

He says, "She exaggerates. I have a few beers after a shift like everyone else, and my job is what is really wearing me out."

He shows no signs of withdrawal, has never had counselling for alcohol, and has attended your clinic for eleven years.

  1. The correct answer is B (Ask what he notices about his own drinking during the hardest weeks): evocation draws the reasons for change out of the person rather than supplying them, because arguments people generate themselves are the ones they act on. Siding with the wife's account invites him to defend himself and produces sustain talk. Labelling the pattern as risky use is unsolicited advice and is the righting reflex in action. A treatment referral handed to someone in precontemplation usually ends both the conversation and the relationship.
  2. The correct answer is D (His mention of having thought about cutting back to weekends only): contemplating a specific reduction is desire and ability language, which is speech in the direction of change. The claim that nothing else will change argues for staying the same and is sustain talk. Framing the job as the source of strain is also sustain talk, since it locates the problem outside the drinking. Dismissing a partner's concern reflects discord in the conversation rather than any movement toward change.
  3. The correct answer is A (Give the continuum of risk as information, then ask what he makes of it): Canada's Guidance on Alcohol and Health frames drinking as a continuum of risk and is delivered as information rather than as a judgement. Eliciting his own interpretation afterwards keeps his autonomy intact and invites change talk. Announcing a category or issuing a weekly target hands a braced client a verdict, which reliably produces sustain talk, and the guidance describes a continuum of risk rather than fixed categories. Declaring abstinence the only safe course overstates the guidance and reliably produces an argument for staying the same.
  4. The correct answer is C (Book a specific follow up date and name what you will review then): Arrange is the step of the 5 A's most often skipped and most often the keyed action, because it converts a single conversation into ongoing care. Careful documentation is necessary but changes nothing for him. Printed material handed over without a review date rarely alters behaviour and was not requested. Bloodwork may eventually inform care, but ordering tests instead of scheduling review leaves his new plan completely unsupported.
Case C047

A 29-year-old woman attends for a first appointment to establish primary care.

She asks whether the examination room door can stay unlocked and chooses the chair nearest the doorway.

When you begin taking a history she answers in single words and keeps her coat on.

She has not disclosed any history of trauma. A transferred record from a previous clinic notes two missed appointments and no other detail.

  1. The correct answer is A (Describe what the visit will involve and let her set the pace): trauma and violence informed care creates predictability, safety and choice, and it never requires the person to disclose a trauma history. Asking directly about abuse at a first meeting places the burden of disclosure on her before any trust exists. Cutting the visit short removes her chance to receive care and repeats a pattern of being turned away. Opening with the missed appointments reads as an accusation and confirms that the institution is watching her record.
  2. The correct answer is C (A dissociative episode, calling for grounding rather than continued questioning): a brief unresponsive episode during history taking in a guarded person, with regular breathing and no autonomic or motor signs, most often represents dissociation triggered by the content. Grounding restores present awareness by naming the room and the date and inviting her to feel her feet on the floor. A focal seizure would usually involve motor automatisms followed by a postictal period. Reading the episode as refusal misinterprets a nervous system response as a choice and would end care abruptly.
  3. The correct answer is D (Normalize what happened and ask what she would like to do next): naming the response as understandable removes shame, and offering a genuine choice restores the control that the episode took away from her. Pressing on with the history prioritizes your data over her regulation and risks a repeat episode. Brushing the event off as nothing dismisses a real experience and then proceeds to touch her. Making psychiatric care a precondition of primary care withholds the very service she came for.
  4. The correct answer is B (She corrects your errors and brings her own questions to the visits): raising new concerns, correcting the clinician and arriving prepared are the signs that a person feels safe enough to participate as a partner. Attendance on its own can reflect necessity, geography or fear rather than trust. Disclosure of trauma is never the measure of a working relationship, since the model does not require it at all. Ready agreement with everything proposed often signals deference to your power rather than genuine collaboration.
Case C048

A 34-year-old man attends for renewal of his asthma inhalers.

He has smoked fifteen cigarettes daily for sixteen years and has never made a quit attempt.

When you ask about smoking he says, "I know what you are going to say. My father smoked until he was eighty. I am not quitting, and I do not want the patch."

His asthma is otherwise well controlled and he has no psychiatric history.

  1. The correct answer is D (Ask permission to share what you see, then leave the door open): in precontemplation the tasks are gentle awareness raising, asking permission before advising, and preserving the relationship for a later window. Prescribing medication and setting a quit date is a preparation stage intervention and will simply be refused. Handing over cancer material he did not ask for is the righting reflex and generates sustain talk. A referral offered before any interest has been expressed is the classic mismatch that ends the conversation.
  2. The correct answer is A (Reflect both sides of his ambivalence and explore what matters most): he is in contemplation, where the work is exploring ambivalence and building discrepancy between his values and his current behaviour. Treating this as a decision already made skips past the ambivalence and usually triggers a retreat into sustain talk. Attaching a diagnosis to his cough is unsolicited and moves him into defending himself. Declaring that willpower fails removes his agency at the precise moment his own reasons are surfacing.
  3. The correct answer is B (Pharmacotherapy plus behavioural support, which outperforms either alone): combining a cessation medication with behavioural support produces higher abstinence rates than using either component by itself. Varenicline, combination nicotine replacement pairing a patch with a short acting form, and bupropion are all effective options to discuss with him. A single patch without support underuses what is available at the moment he is ready to act. Withholding medication until an attempt has failed wastes the preparation window he has built.
  4. The correct answer is B (Frame the event as expected, then plan for the next risky situation): return to use is treated as a normal event within the cycle of change rather than a failure, and the maintenance task is identifying high risk situations and rehearsing responses. Telling him to start over implies that the previous four weeks counted for nothing. Stopping his medication removes support at the point where relapse risk is highest. Dismissing the health significance also dismisses the permission giving thought that preceded the cigarettes.
  5. The correct answer is C (The righting reflex, correcting him with information he did not request): the urge to fix, warn and correct is the most common error in this domain and shows up as accurate information delivered to someone who never asked for it. Asking permission before sharing would have preserved the collaboration. Health literacy is not the barrier here, since he understands monitoring well enough to be frightened by it. Nothing in the exchange minimized his anxiety, because the anxiety was never acknowledged at all.
  6. The correct answer is A (Using has become how you claim something that belongs to you): a complex reflection adds meaning or emotion beyond what was said, offering a hypothesis about what the behaviour does for the person. Restating the timing and the sleeping children is a simple reflection that repeats content without deepening it. A count of nights per week is a closed question and shifts the exchange into data gathering. Suggesting what other parents do supplies your solution and abandons the reflective stance entirely.
  7. The correct answer is D (You got here in a week that took your housing away): an affirmation names a specific real strength the person demonstrated, tied to her actual circumstances, rather than offering general approval. Generic praise carries no information about what she actually did. Statements built around the clinician's pride centre the clinician and reinforce the power imbalance in the room. Reminding her that missed appointments complicate care is a reproach dressed up as feedback.
  8. The correct answer is C (Acknowledge that his pain has gone untreated and ask what he wants): responding to discord means declining the argument, aligning with the person's experience, and handing the direction of the conversation back to him. Defending the thoroughness of your assessment turns the exchange into a contest he will win by disengaging. Introducing opioid induced hyperalgesia is accurate information that he did not request. Inviting him to consider that substance use distorts his pain confirms the very accusation he has just made.
  9. The correct answer is B (Arrange, because no follow up contact has yet been scheduled): the visit as described completed Ask, Advise, Assess and Assist, leaving Arrange as the missing step and the one most often skipped in practice. Telling her how drinking affects her sleep and blood pressure is personalized advice already delivered. Learning that she is willing to cut back is an assessment of readiness. A written trigger and response plan is a legitimate form of assistance even when no medication is offered.
  10. The correct answer is A (Selective screening based on appearance is where bias enters clinical care): the S in SBIRT means screening the whole practice population with a validated tool, precisely because deciding who looks like they need it reproduces stereotypes. Implicit bias is fast and largely invisible to the person holding it, so the countermeasure has to be structural rather than personal vigilance. Validated tools such as AUDIT-C and the ASSIST perform well across populations and do not lose validity in subgroups. No federal statute mandates universal substance use screening, so that reasoning would collapse under scrutiny.
  11. The correct answer is A (Cultural safety is judged by the person receiving care, not the provider): in Canadian nursing the standard is set by the recipient's experience, so a self declared claim of safety carries no weight against a client's account. Completing training is useful but does not certify that any particular encounter was safe. Identical treatment ignores the different starting positions people occupy and is not the same thing as equity. Dismissing her report as subjective repeats the exact dynamic she has described.
  12. The correct answer is D (Discuss the findings with her and challenge the language with the author): stigmatizing documentation follows a person through the record and demonstrably pushes other clinicians toward more punitive decisions, so both her care and the note itself need attention. Reading the wording aloud without context exposes her to the slur with no support and no correction. Withholding the outcome entirely removes her right to information about her own care. Sending her elsewhere leaves the original wording uncorrected and still influencing everyone who reads her chart.
  13. The correct answer is C (Urge surfing, riding out a craving that rises, peaks and then falls): he has already described the defining feature, a craving that passes when he outlasts it, and urge surfing teaches that cravings typically resolve within twenty to thirty minutes. Behavioural activation targets a low mood loop rather than a time limited craving. Cognitive restructuring addresses a permission giving thought that he has not described. A trigger and response rule suits a predictable social situation better than a nightly wave of craving.
  14. The correct answer is B (Name the poor fit plainly and arrange transfer to a named colleague): when a relationship is no longer therapeutic the professional response is a warm referral, stated openly, with information transferred by consent and the door left open. Abandonment is failing to arrange the alternative rather than the act of transferring care, so continuing out of fear of that label helps nobody. Written discharge without an arranged alternative is precisely the abandonment being avoided. Thinning out appointments while still prescribing preserves the risk and removes the oversight.
  15. The correct answer is D (Stay calm, keep distance, offer a concrete choice and locate an exit): escalating anger is managed by lowering stimulation, keeping more than an arm's length of space, avoiding arguments about facts, restoring control through a real choice, and always knowing your way out. Explaining the rationale in detail means arguing facts with someone whose reasoning is not currently available to him. Leaving at once when no threat has been made escalates the encounter and abandons it. Reversing a clinical decision under pressure teaches that escalation works and is unsafe practice.
  16. The correct answer is C (Arrange a professional interpreter and ask her privately who should stay): a family interpreter both filters content and inherits distress, which is a serious problem for sexual health information, and only she can decide who remains in the room. Her nodding cannot be read as consent when the person interpreting is also the person she might wish to exclude. Postponing the appointment delays assessment of a possible infection. A translation application cannot manage clinical nuance or confirm that she has understood a sensitive history.
  17. The correct answer is A (Her avoidance may be an accurate response to inherited institutional experience): intergenerational trauma and documented Indigenous specific racism in Canadian health care make guardedness toward institutions a reasoned position rather than a deficit. British Columbia's In Plain Sight review described widespread stereotyping of Indigenous patients, with missed diagnoses as the consequence. Labelling the pattern as non adherence and imposing a contract punishes her for the system's own history. Assuming she does not understand the referral substitutes a knowledge deficit for the safety concern she has already named.
  18. The correct answer is B (Clinicians given stigmatizing labels recommend more punitive responses to identical vignettes): the strongest rationale is the demonstrated effect on clinician decision making, since the same case described with a label draws harsher recommendations than the same case described person first. Calling the terms merely outdated implies a matter of fashion rather than measurable patient harm. Threatening regulatory consequences may change charting without changing thinking. Losing clients to other providers is a real service concern but is not the clinical mechanism.
  19. The correct answer is C (Name the power imbalance and describe what you plan to document): stating the imbalance openly and making the record transparent tends to reduce it, and it addresses the specific thing she is watching. Writing the note later hides the process rather than opening it up. Promising complete confidentiality is inaccurate, since disclosure obligations exist, and an overstated promise damages trust when it is broken. Turning the screen toward her is a gesture that never explains what a recorded diagnosis will mean for her.
  20. The correct answer is D (Naltrexone, which precipitates withdrawal in anyone taking an opioid agonist): naltrexone is an opioid antagonist and is contraindicated in anyone receiving opioid agonist therapy, because it displaces the agonist and precipitates acute withdrawal. Acamprosate would have been the appropriate first line choice for alcohol use disorder in a person on this regimen. A stable antidepressant dose does not explain an abrupt deterioration of this pattern. Thiamine is well tolerated, and an angiotensin converting enzyme inhibitor does not produce sweating, muscle aches and agitation together.
  21. The correct answer is B (State the limits of confidentiality now, before asking any sensitive questions): an adolescent needs the boundaries stated at the start of the visit, before any question that might trigger them, so she can make an informed decision about what to say. An unconditional promise cannot be kept and destroys trust at the moment it is broken. Telling her that confidentiality depends on her disclosures leaves her guessing at the rules while being asked to disclose. Bringing her mother in removes the private space she deliberately sought.
  22. The correct answer is A (The CRAFFT, developed and validated for adolescent substance use screening): the CRAFFT was designed specifically for young people and is the usual Canadian choice when screening this age group. The AUDIT-C is a brief alcohol screen validated in adults and covers only one substance. The ASSIST screens multiple substances but was developed for adult populations rather than adolescents. A withdrawal scale measures physical signs in a person already known to use opioids and has no screening role in a well adolescent.
  23. The correct answer is D (Ask how his weekends fit with the kind of father he wants to be): building discrepancy means inviting the person to compare his own stated values with his current behaviour, so the gap is one he identifies rather than one you announce. Telling him he is missing his children delivers the conclusion and reliably produces a defence. A sleep log gathers data without ever touching the value he named. Explaining sleep architecture is unsolicited information that moves away from what matters to him.
  24. The correct answer is C (It helps at work, the licence worries you, and you doubt any link): a summary gathers the strands of ambivalence together and hands them back, which is exactly what this response does with all three elements she raised. An open question about readiness is the first OARS skill rather than the fourth. Naming how long she has carried this is a reflection of one feeling, not a collected summary. Elaborating the consequences of losing a licence adds your emphasis to one strand and drops the rest.
  25. The correct answer is A (Sit down, lower the stimulation and reflect the feeling before content): intense emotion is clinical data rather than an interruption, and the response is to reduce stimulation, come down to her level, and name what is happening before returning to any agenda. Handing over tissues and pressing on treats the crying as something to be managed away. Leaving the room can read as withdrawal at the moment she is most exposed. Normalizing grief and offering a pamphlet supplies information instead of presence and closes the subject.
  26. The correct answer is B (She was in preparation and needed a concrete plan, not awareness raising): she arrived with a decision and a date, so the matched intervention is a specific plan with dates and supports, and revisiting awareness wastes the window when she was ready to act. A decisional balance exercise belongs to contemplation, a stage she had already moved through. Rating confidence is useful but is not what stalled someone who had already committed. No pharmacotherapy is approved for stimulant use disorder, so that explanation does not hold up.
  27. The correct answer is D (Explain each step, ask permission before it, and let her stop anytime): the model gives the person control of pacing through explanation before touching, permission for each part of the examination, and an unconditional right to pause or stop. Working quickly removes her control entirely and prioritizes your discomfort over hers. A chaperone is good practice, but silence about the steps leaves her unable to anticipate anything. Sedation medicalizes a situation that calls for collaboration and removes her capacity to consent moment to moment.
Case C049

A 27-year-old man attends your community clinic and says he has been smoking fentanyl daily for about two years.

He has never been offered treatment of any kind.

He lives in a rooming house, uses alone most days, and had one overdose reversed by a friend six months ago.

He is not in withdrawal today and has no other medical history.

He asks whether there is anything at all that would stop him from dying.

  1. The correct answer is C (Give naloxone, teach the sequence, and plan for not using alone): take home naloxone is the baseline offer for every person who uses opioids, and the highest immediate risk in this history is an unwitnessed overdose in a rooming house. The taught sequence is stimulate, call for help, open the airway and support breathing, give naloxone, then support breathing and repeat, because naloxone wears off before most unregulated opioids do. Confirming substances by urine testing changes nothing about his risk before the next visit. A referral takes weeks, and using less offers no protection against a supply of unpredictable potency.
  2. The correct answer is A (Buprenorphine would displace the fentanyl and precipitate an acute withdrawal): buprenorphine binds the opioid receptor with high affinity but only partial activity, so given while a full agonist is still bound it displaces that agonist and abruptly drops receptor activation. Standard induction therefore waits for objective withdrawal, conventionally a Clinical Opiate Withdrawal Scale score of about 12 or more. The drug is far from inert in this window, so calling an early dose harmless is wrong. The ceiling effect makes fatal respiratory depression less likely rather than more likely.
  3. The correct answer is D (Offer low dose initiation, with small increasing doses overlapping continued use): low dose initiation was developed for exactly this situation and allows buprenorphine with naloxone, the Canadian first line agent, to be started without a period of untreated withdrawal. Refusing any route other than standard induction loses a person who has just asked for treatment. Methadone can be started without withdrawal but is the alternative rather than the first line agent, and it needs closer monitoring including for QT prolongation and accumulation. Deferring treatment leaves him in an unregulated supply that kills people while they wait.
  4. The correct answer is B (Store the doses in a locked box, out of sight, and never shared): a locked box, storage out of sight and reach of others, and a firm rule against sharing are the core client teaching points, and shared accommodation makes secure storage the immediate concern. Returning unused medication to a pharmacy take back programme is correct advice but addresses the end of therapy rather than his daily risk. Refrigeration is not required and is not a safeguard against diversion. Reporting loss or theft to the Office of Controlled Substances within ten days is the practitioner's obligation under the narcotic regulations, not the client's.
Case C050

A 52-year-old woman joins your primary care roster after losing her family physician.

She drinks about a bottle of wine each evening and more on weekends.

Two years ago she had a seizure two days after stopping abruptly on her own.

She lives alone in stable housing, works full time, takes no medication, and has no other medical conditions.

She says she wants to stop drinking completely this time and asks for help.

  1. The correct answer is A (The seizure that followed her last abrupt cessation of drinking): a past withdrawal seizure is among the strongest predictors of complicated withdrawal and pushes management toward a supervised or inpatient setting. Heavy daily intake and significant comorbidity also add risk, but the weekday to weekend difference in volume is a much weaker signal. Living alone increases the danger if a seizure occurs, yet it is the seizure history itself that establishes the risk in the first place. Choosing abstinence over gradual reduction is a treatment goal, not a risk factor.
  2. The correct answer is D (Give parenteral thiamine before any glucose containing fluid is infused): glucose given to a thiamine depleted person can precipitate Wernicke encephalopathy, so thiamine goes first and by a parenteral route when absorption cannot be relied on. Benzodiazepines are the correct treatment for the withdrawal syndrome itself and will be needed, but they do not carry this time critical sequencing. A serum ethanol level does not change immediate management in someone who is already symptomatic. Oral supplements are unreliable in a person who has just vomited twice.
  3. The correct answer is C (Naltrexone, which reduces heavy drinking and suits her clinical situation): naltrexone and acamprosate are the first line agents for alcohol use disorder, and naltrexone fits here because she takes no opioids and has no contraindication to it. Acamprosate would also be reasonable and is renally cleared, which her normal filtration rate would allow. Disulfiram is a later option that requires supervision and full informed consent. Continuing benzodiazepines beyond the withdrawal period creates dependence and does nothing to treat the underlying disorder.
  4. The correct answer is A (Treat the episode as expected, review the triggers and continue the medication): return to use is a normal event in the cycle of change, and the clinical task is identifying the high risk situation and rehearsing a response while keeping effective treatment in place. A repeat withdrawal assessment is not indicated when she is not in withdrawal and has already resumed abstinence.
