Stopping the medication after a single episode removes support at the point of greatest vulnerability. Escalating to residential treatment ignores that she recovered the plan herself within days.
  5. The correct answer is B (The Good Samaritan Drug Overdose Act protects callers from simple possession charges): the federal Good Samaritan Drug Overdose Act shields the person who calls for help, and others present at the scene, from charges for simple possession of a controlled substance. The protection is not blanket immunity, since outstanding warrants and offences such as trafficking are not covered by it. Nothing in the Act conditions the protection on waiting for police to arrive. Criminal law is federal, so a provincial rule does not decide whether possession charges apply.
  6. The correct answer is D (A federal exemption under the Controlled Drugs and Substances Act permits it): section 56.1 allows the Minister of Health to grant an exemption so a supervised consumption service can operate lawfully, a route created in 2017 to replace a far more onerous application process. The 2011 Insite decision held that refusing an exemption to that particular service breached the Charter, but it did not legalize the model across the country. Federal criminal law is not displaced by provincial health legislation. A time limited decriminalization pilot changes personal criminal exposure and does not authorize a service to operate.
  7. The correct answer is C (A benzodiazepine adulterant is likely, so monitoring and airway support continue): adulterants such as etizolam are common in Canada's unregulated supply and cause prolonged sedation that naloxone does not reverse, so breathing returns while consciousness does not. Repeating naloxone once respirations are adequate adds nothing and can worsen withdrawal distress. Leaving an unrousable person unmonitored risks airway obstruction and a return of respiratory depression as the naloxone wears off. Declaring hypoxic injury at this point is unsupported and would justify withholding the monitoring that is still needed.
  8. The correct answer is C (Explain that uneven mixing means a negative strip cannot make a sample safe): substances are not evenly distributed through a sample, so a strip testing one portion can easily miss fentanyl present elsewhere in the same supply. Drug checking still has real value in informing decisions, so telling her it is not worth using discards a useful harm reduction tool. Spectrometry gives richer information than a strip but does not detect everything and cannot make any sample safe. Pairing the correction with not using alone addresses the risk she is actually carrying.
  9. The correct answer is A (Reduced non beverage alcohol consumption and fewer emergency service contacts): managed alcohol programmes supply measured regular doses of beverage alcohol and are associated with less use of non beverage alcohol such as hand sanitizer, together with reduced emergency service use. The model is not designed to produce abstinence and is not compared favourably with residential treatment on that outcome. Organ damage already present is not eliminated by stabilizing a pattern of drinking. Participants typically drink less overall rather than more, since consumption becomes measured and scheduled.
  10. The correct answer is B (Client reported opioid use and had a positive urine drug test today): person first wording describes the behaviour and the test result without moral framing, using positive rather than dirty and use rather than abuse. Describing what a client denies imports the language of accusation into the permanent record. Labelling someone a known user attaches an identity to the person instead of describing a behaviour. Calling a return to use a relapse and a result dirty preserves the exact terms shown to shift clinicians toward more punitive decisions.
  11. The correct answer is D (Five criteria indicate a moderate disorder, and withdrawal is not required): the eleven criteria span impaired control, social impairment, risky use and pharmacological features, with two to three indicating mild, four to five moderate, and six or more severe. No single criterion including withdrawal is necessary for the diagnosis to be made. Holding a job does not exclude social impairment, which can appear as abandoned obligations or conflict outside work. Assigning severe severity at five criteria overstates the count and would misdirect the intensity of treatment offered.
  12. The correct answer is A (Authority for opioid agonist therapy varies by province and college requirements): the New Classes of Practitioners Regulations open the door federally, but each province, territory and regulatory college then shapes what a nurse practitioner may actually initiate or prescribe. The federal exemption once needed for methadone was removed in 2018, moving that decision to the provinces. No single national rule grants identical authority, and any option claiming one should be treated as suspicious. Methadone prescribing by nurse practitioners is permitted in some jurisdictions, so a blanket physician restriction is inaccurate.
  13. The correct answer is C (The loss and theft clause, since the recipient and the timeline are wrong): loss or theft of a controlled substance is reported to Health Canada's Office of Controlled Substances within 10 days under the narcotic regulations, not to the regulatory college within 30 days. A locked cupboard with access restricted to authorized staff is an accepted secure storage arrangement. Periodic counts with reconciliation satisfy the record keeping obligation without a count after every single dose. Writing quantities in words and figures is correct practice, and local requirements govern when it applies.
  14. The correct answer is B (Offer cessation now, combining pharmacotherapy with structured behavioural support): tobacco causes much of the long term mortality in this population, treating it does not destabilize opioid agonist therapy, and medication combined with behavioural support outperforms either component alone. Varenicline, combination nicotine replacement pairing a patch with a short acting form, and bupropion are all reasonable choices to discuss. Delaying a year loses the moment when he has actually asked, which is the strongest predictor of an attempt. Counselling alone and unstructured gradual reduction both produce lower abstinence rates.
  15. The correct answer is D (Explain no approved pharmacotherapy exists, then target driving and potency): no medication is approved for cannabis use disorder, so counselling addresses frequency, potency, route, early adolescent initiation, driving, and psychosis risk in vulnerable people. Driving after use and a family history of psychosis are the two immediate hazards in this history. Nabilone is a synthetic cannabinoid used for nausea and has no role in reducing cannabis use. Bupropion and naltrexone have established roles in tobacco and alcohol care respectively, not in cannabis use disorder.
  16. The correct answer is A (It is one option within a full plan, offered with informed consent): prescribed safer supply provides pharmaceutical alternatives to people at high risk of death from the unregulated supply, and it remains genuinely contested, with supporters citing reduced deaths and critics raising diversion. The defensible position treats it as one component of care alongside opioid agonist therapy, harm reduction supplies and social support. Offering it universally overstates the evidence and abandons individual assessment. Ruling it out entirely dismisses the mortality risk that the approach was designed to address.
  17. The correct answer is B (These programmes reduce HIV and hepatitis C without increasing injection rates): needle and syringe distribution has consistently reduced bloodborne transmission, and no evidence supports the claim that it increases injecting. Conceding a modest increase in injection frequency accepts the premise that the evidence contradicts. Describing the evidence as mixed misrepresents a well established finding and hands a health decision to sentiment. The benefits are documented in communities with and without supervised consumption services, so local co location is not a condition.
  18. The correct answer is D (Seven or more drinks each week falls in the increasingly high risk band): Canada's Guidance on Alcohol and Health describes a continuum, with two drinks or fewer weekly low risk, three to six moderate, and seven or more carrying increasingly high risk as each additional drink adds harm. Calling eight drinks low risk contradicts the 2023 guidance, which replaced the older low risk drinking guidelines. A weekly count does not by itself trigger a treatment referral in someone with no disorder. Weekly amounts remain part of the guidance and are delivered as information rather than a verdict.
  19. The correct answer is C (Each can is just over one standard drink, giving about twenty two weekly): a Canadian standard drink is 341 mL of 5 percent beer, so a 355 mL can of the same strength is slightly more than one, and three every evening totals close to twenty two drinks per week. Doubling each can overstates the alcohol content by a factor of two. Halving it understates her intake and would place her in a falsely reassuring risk band. A standard drink is defined by the amount of pure alcohol, 17.05 mL, rather than by the container.
  20. The correct answer is A (Presentations must be assessed before intoxication is assumed, using standardized triage): treating a presentation as intoxication before assessment is the documented pathway to missed diagnoses, and the countermeasure is a standardized process applied to everyone rather than personal vigilance. British Columbia's In Plain Sight review described exactly this pattern and its consequences. Removing front desk observation entirely discards useful information instead of structuring how it is used. Framing the event as one person's poor judgement protects the system that allowed an assumption to determine care.
  21. The correct answer is C (Return leftovers to a pharmacy, and offer her a naloxone kit today): unused controlled medication goes back to a pharmacy take back programme rather than the toilet or the garbage, and every opioid prescription should be paired with an overdose risk conversation and a naloxone kit. Flushing puts the drug into the water supply and is no longer advised. Keeping leftovers for future pain leaves an accessible supply in a household with teenagers. Household garbage disposal creates both an environmental problem and a diversion risk.
  22. The correct answer is B (Address the combined sedative burden and provide her a naloxone kit): an opioid layered onto a benzodiazepine, a Z drug and gabapentin in an older adult living alone multiplies the risk of respiratory depression and falls, so reducing that stack and supplying naloxone are the immediate priorities. Increasing the opioid frequency worsens the exact interaction that makes this profile dangerous. A statin can cause myalgia but is not the source of fracture pain, and stopping it addresses nothing urgent. Bone density testing matters for her longer term care and does nothing about tonight's risk.
  23. The correct answer is D (Continue care, supply harm reduction resources, and keep the offer open): harm reduction reduces the harms of substance use without making abstinence or treatment a precondition of receiving care, and continued engagement is what allows a later window to be used. Conditioning wound care on treatment uses access to health services as leverage and drives people away. Repeated declining is not evidence that a more intensive service is needed, only that he is not ready yet. Restricting him to acute problems removes the preventive and relationship work that makes future treatment possible.
  24. The correct answer is A (The partial agonist ceiling makes fatal respiratory depression much less likely): Canadian guidance places buprenorphine with naloxone first line primarily on safety grounds, because the ceiling on respiratory effect makes overdose during treatment far less likely than with a full agonist. Standard induction still requires objective withdrawal, so a claim that no withdrawal is needed is inaccurate. The federal exemption for methadone prescribing was removed in 2018 and that decision now sits with the provinces. Methadone retains real advantages for some people, so a blanket claim of superior craving suppression is wrong.
  25. The correct answer is D (Physical dependence is present, but criteria for a disorder are not met): tolerance and withdrawal are excluded from the diagnostic count when a medication is taken as prescribed, and none of the impaired control, social impairment or risky use features are described here. Physical dependence is an expected pharmacological consequence of sustained opioid therapy rather than a disorder in itself. Duration of prescribed use is not a diagnostic criterion. Urine drug testing may serve other monitoring purposes but cannot establish or exclude a diagnosis based on behaviour.
  26. The correct answer is B (Decriminalization changes criminal exposure and not the toxicity of the supply): removing criminal penalties for possession does nothing about contamination, because the substances still come from the same unregulated market. British Columbia's exemption was time limited and was narrowed in 2024 to exclude public places, so unrestricted application is inaccurate. Only small amounts were covered and only for adults, so possession of any quantity was never lawful. No quality standard attaches to a decriminalized but still unregulated substance.
  27. The correct answer is C (His tolerance has fallen, so his previous amount could now be fatal): a period without opioids sharply reduces tolerance, and the weeks after release are among the highest risk for fatal overdose in Canada. He also needs a naloxone kit, a plan not to use alone, and an open offer of treatment whenever he wants it. The supply can change rapidly rather than slowly, and potency varies between doses from the same source. Naloxone reverses opioid effect but not benzodiazepine or xylazine adulterants, so it cannot be presented as full protection.
  28. The correct answer is A (Xylazine exposure, a veterinary sedative that causes severe skin wounds): xylazine is increasingly present in Canada's unregulated supply, causes severe ulceration at sites unrelated to injection, and is not an opioid, so naloxone does not reverse its sedative effect. Bacterial cellulitis would usually bring fever, raised inflammatory markers and spreading erythema rather than indolent necrotic ulcers. Pressure necrosis follows a dependent body position and would not appear symmetrically on both forearms over weeks. No skin reaction of this kind is attributed to naloxone.
  29. The correct answer is B (Deliver a brief intervention in clinic and arrange a follow up review): in SBIRT the brief intervention is five to fifteen minutes of feedback, motivational conversation and a menu of options, and referral is reserved for risk beyond what primary care can manage alone. Nothing in his history suggests that threshold has been reached. Repeating the screen in six months treats a positive result as though it were negative and wastes the opening. Starting medication while handing the counselling elsewhere drops the component that makes brief intervention effective.
  30. The correct answer is D (Repeat the dose, support breathing, and stay until help has arrived): the taught sequence is stimulate, call for help, open the airway and support breathing, give naloxone and repeat if there is no response, because naloxone acts for a shorter time than most unregulated opioids. Withholding a further dose to avoid withdrawal prioritizes discomfort over survival. Waiting until breathing stops completely delays treatment well past the point where ventilation is already inadequate. Recovery positioning helps but does not substitute for repeat dosing and rescue breaths.
  31. The correct answer is C (Screen him with a validated alcohol tool and ask about his morning symptoms): falls, a morning tremor and hyponatremia in an older adult who minimizes a nightly drink form a recognizable pattern, and a validated screen used as part of universal screening avoids the bias of deciding who looks like they need it. A tremor that settles after the first drink suggests withdrawal between drinks. Assigning everything to frailty closes the assessment before it begins. Bloodwork supports a history but never replaces it, and asking a family member first bypasses the person in front of you.
Case C051

Mrs. Dhillon is 81 and is being discharged home today after five days in hospital for a heart failure exacerbation. She lives alone in a second floor walk up, uses a walker, and her daughter visits on weekends. Furosemide was increased and her ramipril was held on day two for a rising creatinine. A repeat creatinine drawn this morning is still pending. She fills prescriptions at two different community pharmacies.

  1. The correct answer is C (Her pending creatinine has no named clinician and no follow up date): a result that returns after discharge belongs to nobody unless the discharge summary assigns an owner and a timeframe. The walk up, the weekend only support and the two pharmacies all matter and all belong in the plan, but each of them is visible to any clinician who later reads the chart. A pending creatinine in a woman whose ACE inhibitor was held is both invisible and time sensitive, so accountability for it has to be assigned before she leaves the building.
  2. The correct answer is A (The over the counter naproxen was never addressed in the discharge orders): a non steroidal anti inflammatory drug taken twice daily in a woman with heart failure, a rising creatinine and a held ACE inhibitor will worsen fluid retention and renal function, and she will keep taking it because nobody told her to stop. Doubling the diuretic is an intentional change whose reason is evident from the admission itself. The supplement carries no comparable hazard, and the unchanged beta blocker dose is an appropriate continuation. Purchased products are the discrepancy most often missed because they never appear on an order sheet.
  3. The correct answer is D (Repeat creatinine pending, Dr. Osei to review and phone client tomorrow morning): a safe discharge summary names a specific person and a specific timeframe for every outstanding result, because responsibility spread across a team is responsibility held by nobody. Directing the client to her pharmacist shifts the work onto someone with no authority to act on the value. Stating a conditional plan for the ramipril describes what should happen without saying who will see the number that triggers it. Naming an owner and a date is the entry that would survive review after an adverse outcome.
  4. The correct answer is B (Have her read every bottle aloud now and reconcile the list with her): reconciliation captures what the client is actually taking rather than what the chart says she should take, and the telephone allows you to use her as your hands while the bottles are in front of her. Stopping all diuretic and sending an ambulatory woman with orthostatic symptoms to hospital is disproportionate before you know what she has swallowed. Delegating the sort to a weekend visitor leaves five more days of double dosing. Faxing a corrected list is a necessary later step but corrects the pharmacies rather than the kitchen.
Case C052

You are a self employed nurse practitioner running a small primary care practice in Saskatchewan. On Monday your administrative assistant tells you that a referral package for one client, including a consultation note and recent laboratory results, was faxed on Friday to a construction company rather than to the specialist's office. The receiving number differs from the correct one by a single digit. The client has not been told.

  1. The correct answer is D (Telephone the construction company and arrange retrieval of the faxed package): breach response runs contain, notify, investigate, prevent, and containment comes first because every hour the package sits in an unsecured office widens the exposure. Notifying the client matters greatly and follows immediately, but it does nothing to limit the disclosure already in progress. Reporting externally and analysing the cause both belong to later steps and cannot be completed properly until the scope is known. Retrieving or securing the information is the only action that changes how much harm is still possible.
  2. The correct answer is B (You are the trustee and remain accountable for the acts of your employees): a self employed nurse practitioner personally holds custody and control of the health information in the practice, so safeguards, training, breach response and access requests all land on the owner. Staff who handle records act for the trustee rather than as independent trustees in their own right. Nothing transfers accountability to a specialist who never received the package. Employees can face their own consequences, but that does not divide or dilute the trustee's statutory duty.
  3. The correct answer is A (What was disclosed, what you have done, and how to complain externally): notification exists so the affected person can judge her own risk and pursue an independent remedy, which requires knowing the content involved, the containment steps taken, and the route to the privacy commissioner. Identifying an employee and describing internal discipline is her employer's private business and adds nothing to the client's ability to protect herself. Offering a legal conclusion about reporting thresholds gives her nothing she can act on, and assessing the risk of harm is a judgment the trustee must make and document rather than a verdict to deliver on the telephone. Promising that no breach will ever recur is an assurance no practice can honestly give.
  4. The correct answer is C (Send referrals through the encrypted provincial system using stored verified numbers): the failure was manual entry of a destination address, so eliminating hand keyed numbers and moving to encrypted transmission removes the hazard rather than managing it. A confidentiality notice asks a stranger to behave well after the information has already arrived. Annual retraining does not change the moment at which a digit is mistyped by a busy person. A witness step adds a second human to a task that humans reliably get wrong and degrades as soon as the clinic is short staffed.
Case C053

Mr. Chartrand is 54 and books a same day video visit for two days of abdominal pain. You have never met him. On video he is sitting in his parked car, appears pale, and shifts position frequently. He reports pain around the umbilicus that has moved to the right lower quadrant, one episode of vomiting, and no appetite. He has no thermometer and no blood pressure cuff at home. He asks you to prescribe something so he can avoid the emergency department.

  1. The correct answer is B (Migrating right lower quadrant pain requires examination to exclude appendicitis): virtual care is inappropriate whenever a physical finding would change management or the presentation could plausibly be a surgical emergency, and periumbilical pain migrating to the right lower quadrant with vomiting and anorexia is the classic example of both. The parked car limits privacy and the absent devices limit data, and both are genuine problems, but each could in principle be worked around. A first encounter is a relative caution rather than an absolute barrier. Here the missing abdominal examination is the piece that cannot be substituted.
  2. The correct answer is D (Delay, because an assessment needed today would be postponed until tomorrow): delay is the virtual encounter that pushes back an assessment the client needs now, and analgesia with a next day review in possible appendicitis is exactly that. Fragmentation is a real hazard of one off virtual prescribing but describes information failing to reach the longitudinal record, which is a separate problem from timing. Privacy in the car and an incomplete consent discussion are both worth correcting and neither is what a twenty four hour postponement creates. Naming the hazard correctly is what drives the right next step.
  3. The correct answer is C (Explain why in person assessment is needed today and arrange it directly): the safest virtual visit is sometimes the one that ends with a same day in person appointment, and leading a transition means arranging the assessment rather than handing the client a recommendation and hanging up. Booking three days out and relying on safety netting accepts a surgical presentation deteriorating unobserved. An antiemetic masks the vomiting that is helping you track him. Serial temperatures cannot be taken by a man with no thermometer and would not change the need for an abdominal examination.
  4. The correct answer is A (Modality, both locations, identity verification, and the limits on your assessment): a virtual note carries everything a face to face note carries plus the elements that describe the distance encounter itself, including where each party was, how identity was confirmed, consent to virtual care and what you could not assess. A history and differential belong in every note regardless of modality. Safety netting and a review interval are good practice in person as well. Consent to treatment and the client's goals are also universal, so none of those distinguish a virtual record.
Case C054

You saw a 27 year old woman in a walk in clinic eleven days ago for headache. You remember examining her cranial nerves and discussing red flags, but the visit was busy and your note records only the vital signs, the word headache, and a prescription for naproxen. Today the clinic manager tells you the client attended hospital yesterday with a subarachnoid hemorrhage, survived, and her family has written a formal complaint to the college.

  1. The correct answer is A (Add a clearly labelled late entry dated today describing the original encounter): late entries are legitimate, including after a complaint is known, provided they are labelled as late, time stamped with the actual time of writing, and state when the events described occurred. Saying nothing in the chart and raising the examination only in a written defence leaves the record permanently silent and invites the inference that nothing was done. Amending the original entry so it reads as contemporaneous is what the audit trail will expose as concealment. Delegating the addition to a manager who was not present produces hearsay in a legal document.
  2. The correct answer is C (Testimony about usual practice is permitted but weighs poorly against a silent record): the working assumption in Canadian regulatory proceedings, inquests and civil actions is that care not documented was care not provided, and while a clinician may testify to a habitual routine, that evidence is weak against a contemporaneous record showing nothing. Treating the note as irrelevant overstates how much weight recollection carries years later. Calling the gap conclusive proof overstates the rule in the other direction, since it supports a strong adverse inference rather than shifting the burden of proof. Nothing obliges a panel to prefer an uncorroborated memory.
  3. The correct answer is B (Your reasoning connecting the negative red flag screen to the discharge decision): documenting rationale is the specific expectation, and rationale is the reasoning that links what you found to what you decided. A defensible chart does not show that you were right, it shows that a reasonable clinician with the same information could have decided the same way. Dose and quantity are useful data but say nothing about why discharge was safe. Timing entries and a note that the client accepted the plan both describe the encounter without ever explaining the judgment that is now under scrutiny.
  4. The correct answer is D (Build a headache template prompting red flag screening and the disposition rationale): the defect was that a busy clinician recorded actions without reasoning, so a structured prompt at the point of charting makes the rationale the default rather than an act of discipline. Annual education competes poorly with time pressure on a ninety client day. A monthly audit measures the problem after the fact without changing behaviour during the encounter. Lengthening every appointment is expensive, unlikely to be approved, and would still leave the content of the note entirely to memory and habit.
Case C055

Mr. Beaulieu is 88 and transfers this afternoon from your inpatient unit to a long term care home. He was admitted with delirium secondary to a urinary tract infection, has moderate dementia, and became physically aggressive twice during the admission when staff attempted personal care. His daughter, who is his substitute decision maker, has asked you not to include his prior treatment for alcohol use disorder in anything sent to the home.

  1. The correct answer is D (Please have him assessed by your team within twenty four hours of arrival): a recommendation states what you want done and by when, and it is the element that converts a report into an owned next step. Describing a resolving delirium and an improving score is situation reporting. Recounting the two aggressive episodes during personal care is background the receiver needs but does not act on by itself. Offering your interpretation that the aggression was delirium related is assessment, and a handover that stops at interpretation leaves the receiving team to guess what you are asking for.
  2. The correct answer is A (Withhold it, and tell the home that some information has been withheld): where a restriction on disclosure applies, the withheld content may not be shared even inside the circle of care, and the receiving clinician must be told that something has been withheld without being told what, so they can ask the client or substitute directly. Sharing it anyway treats circle of care as an override rather than a default that a valid restriction displaces. Saying nothing at all leaves the receiving team believing they hold a complete history and unable to seek the gap. Completeness does not defeat a lawful restriction.
  3. The correct answer is C (Replace the injection order with documented triggers and non drug strategies first): a plan copied from an inpatient unit is not a transfer plan, and an open ended parenteral antipsychotic order handed to a single overnight nurse for forty residents predictably becomes the first response rather than the last. Carrying both orders forward unchanged ignores that the setting, staffing and monitoring capacity have all changed. Removing everything leaves the home with no guidance if agitation recurs in a man with dementia. Stretching screening to weekly would miss a recurrence long before anyone noticed.
  4. The correct answer is B (Name yourself as reviewer, state when, and give the home your contact): pending results are the highest risk element of any transfer document because a value that returns after the client has moved belongs to nobody unless a named person and a timeframe are recorded. Relying on the laboratory to telephone assumes a callback pathway that only exists for critical values and reaches a unit the client has left. Delaying a scheduled transfer for routine results is disproportionate and destabilising for a man with dementia. Pushing retrieval onto a receiving clinician who may lack portal access converts an owned task into an assumption.
  5. The correct answer is B (No recommendation was stated and the colleague has not accepted the next step): transfer of accountability is complete only when the receiver holds the information, has had the chance to ask questions, and has accepted responsibility for a specific action. Ending at assessment leaves the next step unowned no matter how accurate the description was. A verbal handover is normal practice and charting it afterwards does not create the missing acceptance. Family presence is not a component of clinician to clinician handover, and a written tool without a stated ask has the same defect.
  6. The correct answer is D (The summary did not name who owns the two results still pending): an unassigned pending result is the single most dangerous gap in a discharge document, because the value returns into a system where no clinician has been told to look for it and no date has been set. Preferred language, contact details and functional status are all genuine components of a safe summary and each would improve this one. None of them, however, produces the specific failure of a clinically actionable result going unread. Naming an owner and a timeframe is the correction.
  7. The correct answer is A (Submit a Jordan's Principle request so the assessment proceeds without jurisdictional delay): Jordan's Principle exists precisely so that a First Nations child receives the service first and governments settle the question of who pays afterwards. Referring into a nine month queue and recording the wait documents the barrier without removing it. A drug benefit program funds medications and has no mechanism for an assessment of this kind. Advising a family to purchase the service privately shifts a public obligation onto them and ignores the funding route that was created for this situation.
  8. The correct answer is C (The held anticoagulant stays invisible until someone compares lists at this transfer): reconciliation is required at admission, at every internal transfer and at discharge, and skipping the transfer step is what allows a drug held for a procedure to be quietly never restarted. A held order does not present itself to the receiving team the way an active order does, which is exactly why the comparison has to be deliberate. A duplicate order would at least be visible on the administration record. Sharing one electronic system creates a false sense that the list has already been checked.
  9. The correct answer is C (Where he is going should shape clinical decisions made from day one): planning that begins near the discharge date produces readmissions, because destination is clinical information that changes choices made days earlier, such as which antibiotic route he can manage alone and whether a regimen requiring assistance is realistic. Referrals can and should be initiated on an anticipated date rather than a confirmed one. Reconciliation is a process repeated at each transition rather than something that waits for a course to end. A late start may also delay discharge, but the deeper harm is decisions made without knowing the setting.
  10. The correct answer is B (One source only was used and the client herself was never interviewed): a best possible medication history requires at least two sources, and the client or family is the only source that reveals what is actually swallowed rather than what was dispensed, including over the counter products, herbals, cannabis, inhalers and injections given elsewhere. A dispensing record cannot show that a tablet is skipped or doubled. Indications, tablet appearance and start dates all add useful detail to a history that is already anchored in reality. Here the anchor itself is missing, with an alert client sitting at the bedside.
  11. The correct answer is D (Decline remote prescribing, and actively arrange a receiving provider before she moves): registration follows the client's physical location during the encounter, so prescribing into another province without licensure there is not available to you, and leading a transition means securing the receiving clinician rather than leaving the client to find one. Continuity is a reason to work harder on the referral, not a reason to practise unregistered. Sending a woman with unstable bipolar disorder and monthly lithium monitoring to a walk in clinic guarantees fragmented care. A three month arrangement has the same licensing defect and simply postpones it.
  12. The correct answer is A (A bridging plan with physiotherapy, analgesia review and scheduled reassessment while waiting): a referral into a fourteen month queue with nothing holding the client in the meantime fails quietly, and escalating non steroidal use over that period carries its own gastrointestinal, renal and cardiovascular risk that needs active review. Confirming how she will learn her appointment date is administrative courtesy rather than clinical safety. Asking for expedited triage may or may not succeed and leaves her unmanaged either way. Attaching the radiograph report improves the referral's quality without changing what happens during the wait.
  13. The correct answer is A (Select a dressing suitable for once daily change and revise the order): modifying the plan so it fits the receiving setting is the core of leading a transition, and choosing a product matched to the frequency the setting can actually deliver preserves the clinical goal instead of compromising it. Leaving the order unchanged and assigning the second change to a woman with limited dexterity sets up a wound infection and blames her for it later. Recruiting an untrained neighbour for sterile technique is neither safe nor consented. Occupying a hospital bed for a service that will not materialise wastes the resource and deconditions her.
  14. The correct answer is D (Ask her to read the order back to you before you hang up): read back of critical numbers and doses is the step that catches a mishearing at the moment it occurs, and a nod or an acknowledgement cannot do that on a poor connection. Entering the order later corrects the record after the first dose may already be running. An independent double check verifies the arithmetic but assumes the transcribed number was heard correctly in the first place. A follow up message is useful reinforcement yet arrives after the opportunity to correct the transfer has passed.
  15. The correct answer is B (Name a specific covering clinician, document it, and inform the client): an outstanding result requires a named owner and a stated timeframe, and an unmonitored shared inbox is the mechanism by which reports are lost rather than a plan. Placing the burden on the client assumes she will remember, will reach someone with authority to act, and will understand what she is being told. Routing health information to a personal email address is a privacy failure and still leaves nobody reviewing it for six weeks. Cancelling an indicated scan converts an administrative gap into a clinical delay.
  16. The correct answer is C (Use a professional interpreter for teach back and provide translated written instructions): a client whose language differs from the instructions she was handed has not received discharge instructions, and teach back through a trained interpreter is what confirms comprehension by the person who has to follow the plan. Relying on a family member to interpret and then to translate in writing places clinical responsibility on an untrained relative and hides errors from you entirely. Simplified English and pictures help low literacy but not a different language. A follow up call the next day arrives after the first doses and the first dressing change.
  17. The correct answer is D (Takes metformin three days weekly due to diarrhea, requested refill nine days early): objective documentation records observable behaviour and the client's stated reason, which lets any later reader draw their own conclusion and often reveals a clinical problem hiding behind a label. Calling adherence poor and behaviour drug seeking replaces one judgment with two softer judgments. Describing adherence challenges and frustration is vague enough to be useless to the next clinician. Saying she is not taking the drug correctly and wants opioids ahead of schedule still characterises her rather than recording what happened.
  18. The correct answer is A (Red flags screened and absent, imaging not indicated, rationale discussed, review planned): rationale matters most for decisions that can look wrong in hindsight, and this entry records the screening performed, the conclusion drawn from it, the discussion held and the safety net set. Citing a national recommendation states an authority without showing that this client was assessed against it. Recording that imaging was declined and advice given documents actions without the reasoning that connects findings to decision. A diagnostic label with conservative management describes the plan but leaves a reviewer unable to see what you ruled out.
  19. The correct answer is C (The held warfarin has no documented reason and no plan for restarting): an intentional change becomes dangerous the moment the intent is left undocumented, and a man with a mechanical mitral valve who is off anticoagulation with no stated reason and no restart date is exposed to valve thrombosis and stroke within days. Naming a role instead of a person weakens follow up accountability but does not create an immediate hazard when he already has a nurse practitioner reading the document. The antibiotic duration and a missing saturation value are quality gaps rather than threats. Undocumented intent is what stops the next clinician from restarting safely.
  20. The correct answer is B (Draw one line through it, write the correction beside, initial, date, time): the original entry must remain readable so that anyone reviewing the chart can see what was written, what replaced it, who made the change and when. Correction fluid destroys the original and reads as concealment even when the motive was innocent. Removing and replacing a page is worse still, because the audit trail vanishes with the paper. Marking the entry as an error without a legible correction beside it, an identity and a time leaves the record incomplete and the change unattributable.
  21. The correct answer is C (Label it a late entry, time stamp now, and state when events occurred): late entries are acceptable and sometimes unavoidable, and the requirements are that they be labelled as such, carry the real time of writing, and identify when the described events took place. Backdating to make an entry appear contemporaneous is fraud and the audit trail will show it. Having a colleague chart under her own name attributes your assessment to someone who did not make it. Leaving the encounter unrecorded until a future visit creates a permanent gap in the very document that would show the care was provided.
  22. The correct answer is D (Use the amendment function, keeping the original visible in the audit trail): the amendment tool exists to reconcile two duties at once, correcting information that is wrong while preserving what was originally written, who changed it and when. Deleting the sentence removes evidence and looks like concealment regardless of the motive. Declining outright confuses permanence with accuracy, since a permanent record can still be amended transparently. Filing her objection alongside an uncorrected entry leaves a false clinical fact in circulation for every future reader who does not open the second note.
  23. The correct answer is B (Her statute does not govern you, and Alberta's own legislation must be checked): Ontario's health privacy act is one province's statute and not a national rule, while Alberta operates under its own health information legislation with its own retention, consent and breach provisions. Federal legislation applies to commercial activity and federally regulated work and steps back where a province has enacted substantially similar health privacy law, so it does not harmonize the field. There is no opt out mechanism of the kind described. Choosing whichever statute seems stricter is not how jurisdiction works, since only one applies to you.
  24. The correct answer is A (The employed one acts as an agent, the self employed one is custodian): a nurse practitioner employed by a hospital works as an agent of the organization that holds custody and control, so the access request is processed through the hospital's own release of information process. A self employed practitioner personally holds that role, which means access requests, safeguards, retention and breach response all land on her directly. Creating a note does not by itself make the author a custodian. Individuals in independent practice clearly can hold the role, so restricting it to organizations is wrong.
  25. The correct answer is A (The pharmacist and the physiotherapist, who are both inside the circle of care): implied consent to share covers clinicians and staff directly involved in providing that client's care for that purpose, which includes the dispensing pharmacist and a clinician you referred her to. An insurer sits outside the circle entirely and needs express consent. A family member who holds no substitute decision making authority is also outside it, however close the relationship. Restricting sharing to dispensing alone would break the referral you yourself initiated and misreads how the circle of care operates.
  26. The correct answer is B (A privacy breach, because access without a care purpose is unauthorized use): a breach includes any unauthorized collection, use, disclosure, loss or theft of personal health information, and looking at a record when you are not part of that person's care is unauthorized use whether or not anything is repeated. This behaviour, commonly called snooping, is the most frequent privacy offence committed by Canadian health professionals. Valid credentials grant technical access to a system, not permission to read any chart in it. Concern for a neighbour explains the motive without changing the classification.
  27. The correct answer is D (Investigations and medications are referenced but not included for the triaging clinician): transferring accountability means sending the information the receiver actually needs, and pointing a triage office toward a chart it cannot open means the serology and the medication list have not been transferred at all. Without those results the request will be triaged as routine, which is precisely the wrong queue for six months of symmetric small joint synovitis. Naming an individual specialist, adding functional detail and supplying a callback number would each improve the letter. None of them changes whether the triager can see the data that sets urgency.
  28. The correct answer is C (Smith v. Jones permits disclosure where serious imminent harm is clearly identifiable): the Supreme Court of Canada set out the framework for breaching confidentiality in this country, turning on a clearly identifiable person or group, a risk of serious bodily harm or death, and imminence. Tarasoff is an American decision and is not the authority in Canada, so citing it misstates the law even though the practical conclusion overlaps. Confidentiality is not absolute and does not wait for an offence to be committed. Statutory and common law exceptions operate without a court order, which is a separate exception in its own right.
  29. The correct answer is B (Destruction must render the information unreconstructable, and be logged with details): secure destruction of paper means cross cut shredding, and the practice must record what was destroyed, when, how and by whom, none of which a recycling contractor provides. A locked bin controls access for a few days and then hands intact records to a third party. Retention periods come from legislation, college standards and employer policy and differ across the country rather than being set nationally. Neither a college authorization nor individual client consent is the mechanism that governs routine destruction after retention expires.
  30. The correct answer is A (The record belongs to the practice, while she controls access to the information): the Supreme Court of Canada in McInerney v. MacDonald separated the physical record, which belongs to the clinician or facility that created it, from the information within it, which the client may access and whose disclosure she controls. That is why she is entitled to a copy but cannot remove the original. Assigning ownership of the chart to the client reverses the holding. Provincial legislation regulates access and retention without taking ownership of the document, and the question is settled rather than open.
  31. The correct answer is C (Duties differ by jurisdiction, with many statutes triggering at real risk of harm): breach reporting duties to a privacy commissioner and to a regulatory college vary across the provinces and territories, and several statutes require a report whenever there is a real risk of significant harm, which two hundred unencrypted records plainly reaches. Health privacy is mostly provincial rather than federal, so a single national reporting rule does not exist. Colleges in several jurisdictions do receive breach notifications, so calling reporting discretionary everywhere is wrong. Notifying the affected individuals is a separate duty that does not extinguish the reporting one.
  32. The correct answer is D (Upcoding, which carries regulatory, contractual and potentially criminal consequences): billing must be accurate and honest, meaning only for services actually provided, by the person who provided them, under the code matching what was done. Submitting a comprehensive assessment code for a brief renewal misstates the service and exposes her to college discipline, recovery of the payments, and prosecution. Business independence does not soften any of the standards that apply to employed practitioners. Framing it as a private disagreement with the payer, or as a bookkeeping error to be netted out later, misdescribes a deliberate misrepresentation.
  33. The correct answer is D (A list of her credentials, services offered, hours and uninsured service fees): advertising must be truthful and verifiable, and factual information about earned credentials, the services available, opening hours and the cost of uninsured services meets that standard while also giving clients the price disclosure they are owed in advance. Client testimonials are restricted or prohibited by many Canadian health regulators because they cannot be verified and they trade on the trust relationship. Unverifiable superlatives such as leading or best are not permitted. Guaranteeing a specific clinical outcome is the clearest violation of all.
  34. The correct answer is C (Nothing records the modality, so a reader assumes an in person examination occurred): a note that hides the fact that the visit was by telephone misrepresents what the decision actually rested on, and a reviewer reading this objective section will believe a chest was auscultated and a saturation measured by the clinician. Missing safety netting and an uncalibrated home device are both genuine defects, and both flow from the same root once modality is concealed. Smoking history and exacerbation frequency would strengthen the assessment without changing what a reader assumes was examined. Modality, locations, identity verification and assessment limits all belong in the record.
  35. The correct answer is A (State the fee in writing before the service, since it is genuinely uninsured): where a service falls outside public insurance, the cost must be disclosed in advance and in writing so the client can decide before incurring it, and this examination is a recognised uninsured service. Billing the public plan for a visit that was really an uninsured assessment is billing for a service not rendered. Charging for uninsured work is permitted but remains subject to college standards on fairness, transparency and not tying access to insured care. Providing it free is not required and misstates the rule.
  36. The correct answer is B (Records must be retained and held by a named successor custodian, not destroyed): closing a practice does not end the retention obligation, so clients must be notified, given the opportunity to obtain a copy or have the record transferred, and a successor custodian named to hold the remainder for the balance of the retention period. Destroying records still within retention is the failure that cannot be undone. How clients are notified matters and a door notice alone is weak, but the method is a lesser defect than losing the records. Cross cut shredding is in fact correct for paper once retention has genuinely expired.
  37. The correct answer is C (Limits on assessment, platform privacy risks, the in person alternative, technology failure plans): consent to virtual care is separate from consent to the treatment itself, and it covers what this format can and cannot do, the privacy and security risks of the platform, the availability of an in person visit instead, and what will happen if the connection drops. Diagnosis, options and side effects belong to treatment consent, which is still required but is not what makes this discussion a virtual care one. The right to refuse and withdraw applies to all care. Administrative details about cost and cancellation are not consent content.
  38. The correct answer is A (Licensure follows the client's physical location, so British Columbia registration may apply): you must be licensed where the client is physically located during the encounter rather than where you happen to be sitting, and a three month stay in another province brings that jurisdiction's regulator into the picture. The origin of the therapeutic relationship does not extend your registration across a provincial boundary. The absence of a physical examination changes the assessment, not the requirement to be registered. Labour mobility supports registration in another province through an application process rather than making one licence valid everywhere automatically.
  39. The correct answer is D (Ask whether anyone else is present and offer yes or no questions): virtual privacy runs on the client's side as well as yours, and a client who cannot speak freely will answer screening questions untruthfully unless you build a way for her to respond safely. Establishing who is in the room and switching to closed questions she can answer without being understood by a listener is the technique for exactly this situation. Asking an explicit question about abuse while an unseen person listens can escalate the danger immediately. Calling police without her involvement removes her control, and a routine appointment next week ignores what you are seeing.
  40. The correct answer is B (A new limp with unilateral calf swelling after a long flight): unilateral calf swelling following prolonged immobility raises deep vein thrombosis, which requires examination, measurement and imaging arranged the same day, and no history taken at a distance can substitute for that. Reviewing self measured blood pressure is one of the presentations virtual care handles best. Mental health follow up in an established relationship is well suited to video. Communicating a normal screening result is straightforward remotely, provided the note reaches the client's record.
  41. The correct answer is B (Whether the anticoagulant held for the procedure was deliberately left off): a drug held for a procedure and never restarted is the classic unintentional discrepancy at an internal transfer, and it is invisible unless someone compares the lists, which is why reconciliation is required at every transfer and not only at discharge. Two other oral agents were restarted three days ago while this one was not, and four days without anticoagulation in atrial fibrillation carries real stroke risk. Converting a proton pump inhibitor to oral therapy and reviewing a statin are reasonable but not urgent. A dose increase is a new decision rather than a reconciliation gap.
  42. The correct answer is C (Speak in shorter segments with longer pauses, and use teach back): connection latency destroys the usual rhythm of turn taking, so shortening each segment and pausing deliberately prevents the overlapping speech, while teach back replaces the visual cues of confusion that video strips away. Speaking louder does not help a client whose hearing is intact and a written summary arrives after the misunderstanding. Dropping to telephone removes the most useful channel you have, and adding a relative addresses neither problem. Asking whether she has questions invites the same nod that already failed to signal her confusion.
  43. The correct answer is A (Documented safety netting naming the symptoms that require emergency care tonight): a virtual encounter closes with what should improve and by when, which specific symptoms mean seeking in person or emergency care, and how to reach you, and recording that advice is what shows the family was equipped to act on a deterioration you could not see. Consent to the virtual format belongs in the note but does not help the child at two in the morning. Identity and location verification matter for calling the right emergency service, not for the parent's decision. Symptomatic advice does not tell anyone when to seek help.
  44. The correct answer is D (Fragmentation, because the prescribing encounter never reached her longitudinal record): the one off virtual prescription with no note sent back to the regular provider is the case that both the Canadian Medical Protective Association and the Canadian Nurses Protective Society flag as a recurring source of claims, because recurrence patterns, resistance history and allergies are all invisible to whoever prescribes next. Delay describes a needed assessment being postponed, which is not what happened when she was treated promptly. Nothing here indicates unauthorized handling of her information. Billing conduct by the platform is unrelated to the continuity problem she now has.
Case C056

An NP in a rural Ontario family practice reads a published randomized controlled trial of a new oral agent added to usual care in adults with type 2 diabetes.

Over three years, heart failure hospitalization occurred in 8% of the usual care arm and 6% of the treatment arm.

The abstract leads with a 25% relative risk reduction, p = 0.01.

Enrolment required an eGFR above 60 mL/min/1.73 m2, and the primary analysis was per protocol, with 14% of those enrolled lost to follow-up.

  1. The correct answer is C (2 percentage points, the difference between the two reported event rates): absolute risk reduction is the control event rate minus the treatment event rate, so 8% minus 6% gives 2 percentage points, or 0.02. The 25% figure is the relative risk reduction, calculated as 1 minus 6 ÷ 8, and it stays identical no matter how low the baseline risk falls. The value 0.75 is the risk ratio itself rather than any risk difference. Loss to follow-up threatens validity but has nothing to do with the size of the effect.
  2. The correct answer is A (50 patients, calculated as 1 ÷ 0.02, the absolute risk reduction): number needed to treat is the reciprocal of the absolute risk reduction, and 1 ÷ 0.02 = 50 across the three year trial period. Using the relative risk reduction of 25% instead yields 4, an error that overstates the benefit more than tenfold. Taking the reciprocal of either single arm event rate answers a different question, since neither one is a difference between groups. Quoting the figure alongside the three year horizon is what makes the benefit meaningful to a patient.
  3. The correct answer is D (Locate the intention to treat analysis, which keeps the randomized groups intact): analysing participants in the group they were randomized to preserves the balance that randomization created and gives the more honest estimate of effectiveness in ordinary practice. Restricting analysis to completers breaks randomization, because people who finish a treatment differ systematically from those who stop it. Losing 14% of participants compounds that concern rather than removing noise. Confirming the sample size speaks to statistical power, not to the bias introduced by excluding non-completers.
  4. The correct answer is B (The result may not transfer, since this patient was excluded from enrolment): the trial required an eGFR above 60, so a patient at 38 sits outside the studied population and external validity is genuinely uncertain. Randomization protects internal validity inside a trial and says nothing about whether a finding generalizes beyond the people enrolled. Analysing completers only weakens the estimate without making the trial worthless. No legitimate arithmetic adjustment converts a trial result into an estimate for a population the trial deliberately excluded.
Case C057

An NP reviews a case control study examining whether a commonly prescribed medication is associated with a rare cancer.

Investigators recruited 240 people with the cancer from a tertiary oncology clinic and 240 controls from hospital staff who volunteered.

Past medication use in both groups was established by participant recall during a single interview.

The reported odds ratio was 2.4, 95% CI 1.3 to 4.4.

The discussion recommends that clinicians stop prescribing the medication.

  1. The correct answer is B (Controls were hospital staff volunteers, unlike the population the cases came from): controls must represent the source population that produced the cases, and hospital staff differ from tertiary oncology patients in health literacy, medication use, comorbidity and access, which is textbook selection bias. A rare outcome is the reason to choose this design rather than a weakness of it. A wide interval signals imprecision, a smaller problem than assembling a comparison group from the wrong population. Matching the group sizes affects precision, not the direction of the estimate.
  2. The correct answer is D (Sampling on the outcome leaves baseline risk unknown, so risk cannot be computed): investigators chose how many people with and without the cancer to enrol, so the proportion carrying the outcome is an artefact of recruitment rather than a real incidence. Without incidence there is no risk to compare, which leaves odds as the only ratio available. Recall of past exposure introduces measurement bias but does not dictate which ratio can be calculated. Odds approximate risk only when the outcome is uncommon, so the two measures are not interchangeable in general.
  3. The correct answer is A (The association reaches statistical significance, but the estimate carries limited precision): for a ratio measure the null value is 1, and an interval running from 1.3 to 4.4 excludes 1, so the result is statistically significant. That same interval spans a more than threefold range, meaning the true effect could be modest or large, which is imprecision rather than certainty. A confidence interval describes the behaviour of the procedure across repeated samples and assigns no probability to one exact value. A single observational study with these sampling problems cannot establish causation.
  4. The correct answer is C (Search for cohort or synthesis evidence before changing any prescribing practice): a single case control study with poorly chosen controls and recall based exposure generates a hypothesis, and the next step is to see whether stronger designs agree with it. Halting an established therapy on that evidence exposes patients to the harms of undertreatment. Dismissing the design outright is equally wrong, since studies of this kind are the practical way to examine rare outcomes. Withholding an emerging safety signal from a patient who asks about it would undermine informed decision making.
Case C058

An NP appraises a meta analysis of 11 randomized trials of a supervised exercise programme for chronic low back pain.

The pooled result favours the programme, standardized mean difference 0.42, 95% CI 0.18 to 0.66.

Heterogeneity is reported as I squared of 82%.

The funnel plot is visibly asymmetric, with small trials reporting no benefit largely absent.

Seven of the 11 trials enrolled fewer than 60 participants and none blinded the outcome assessor. The primary outcome is patient reported pain.

  1. The correct answer is A (Substantial inconsistency across trials makes a single pooled average hard to interpret): an I squared of 82% indicates that most of the variation between trial results reflects real differences rather than chance, so one summary number hides very different underlying effects. The right response is to hunt for the source, such as differing programme intensity, populations or pain scales, and to consider subgroup analysis. Inconsistency of that size weakens rather than confirms robustness. A pooled estimate inherits every flaw of the studies inside it, so it does not automatically outrank one good trial.
  2. The correct answer is B (Publication bias, so the pooled estimate probably overstates the true benefit): small trials with unfavourable results are the least likely to be submitted or accepted, which empties the lower left corner of a funnel plot and drags the pooled average upward. Random error scatters small trials symmetrically around the true value rather than to one side only. Problems in how participants were allocated within a trial show up in risk of bias assessment, not in plot asymmetry. Differential dropout is likewise a within trial issue and would not produce this shape.
  3. The correct answer is C (Measurement bias, since expectation shapes how a subjective outcome is rated): pain is reported by the participant, so knowing which group they are in can move the score without any real change in pain, and blinding matters most precisely when the outcome is subjective. Randomization already handles baseline confounding, measured and unmeasured, provided allocation was concealed. Dropout is a separate concern judged from the flow of participants rather than from blinding. Whether allocation was concealed at entry is a distinct item from whether assessors were blinded afterwards.
  4. The correct answer is C (Very low certainty, downgraded for inconsistency, risk of bias and publication bias): randomized evidence starts at high certainty, and three serious limitations are documented here, so three downgrades carry this body of evidence to the lowest tier. Remaining at the starting tier ignores the substantial heterogeneity, the unblinded assessment of a subjective outcome and the asymmetric funnel plot. Downgrading for inconsistency and imprecision credits a problem that is not present, since the pooled interval does not cross the null, and it still misses both the risk of bias and the publication bias. Randomized design sets the starting point only, and nothing prevents further downgrading from there.
  5. The correct answer is D (In adults with mild depression, does supervised aerobic exercise reduce 12 week symptoms): this version names the population, the intervention, the implied comparison of usual care, the outcome and the timeframe, which is what makes a search strategy reproducible and its results relevant. Asking whether exercise improves mood leaves both intervention and outcome undefined, so nothing can be narrowed. Asking for the single best option requests a conclusion rather than a searchable question. Framing the comparison across all adults removes the population boundary that keeps retrieved studies applicable.
  6. The correct answer is A (Internal validity is strong while external validity to her caseload is limited): concealed randomization, blinded verification and complete follow-up mean the trial almost certainly got the right answer for the people it enrolled. Those people were young, motivated and free of mental illness, so the result may not carry over to older clients living with schizophrenia, who face different barriers and different pharmacokinetics. Rigorous method never guarantees transferability, because the two questions are separate. Volunteers can be randomized perfectly well, and cessation outcomes vary widely between populations.
  7. The correct answer is B (People who attend routinely differ in health behaviours from those who do not): attenders tend to be more affluent, more health literate, less frail and more likely to exercise and not smoke, and each of those factors independently lowers mortality. That is confounding compounded by a healthy user effect, and it is the standing weakness of any exposure that people choose for themselves. Concluding that the visit itself caused the survival difference simply assumes that explanation away. Cohorts are not randomized, and mortality is common enough for a cohort to measure directly.
  8. The correct answer is A (Differential dropout may bias the result, so the reported effect is unreliable): losing 27% from one arm and 9% from the other means the two groups compared at the end are no longer the groups that were randomized at the start. Those who left the comparison arm may be systematically different, perhaps the least successful or least motivated, which distorts the comparison in an unpredictable direction. Loss above roughly 20% in any arm already warrants suspicion, so a threshold of half is far too permissive. Heavier withdrawal from one arm is a warning sign rather than evidence of benefit.
  9. The correct answer is D (Residual confounding remains from variables unmeasured or measured imprecisely): statistical adjustment can only handle variables that were actually collected, so frailty, function, socioeconomic position and prescriber judgement continue to differ between the groups. Even a well chosen variable adjusted using crude categories leaves some imbalance behind. No amount of modelling reproduces what randomization does, which is to balance unknown factors alongside known ones. Adjustment also does nothing about how the exposure or the outcome was ascertained in the first place.
  10. The correct answer is D (Selection bias, by preventing recruiters from steering patients into an arm): allocation concealment hides the upcoming assignment at the moment of enrolment, so a recruiter who believes in the intervention cannot delay or divert a sicker patient into the comparator. Randomization only balances groups when nobody involved can see or predict what comes next. Keeping the outcome assessor unaware is blinding, a separate safeguard operating after allocation. Prospective registration and retention are both worthwhile, but neither is what a concealed sequence accomplishes.
  11. The correct answer is C (It gives the probability of data this extreme if no true difference existed): a p value is calculated on the assumption that the null hypothesis holds, and it quantifies how unusual the observed data would be under that assumption. It says nothing about how likely the null hypothesis itself is, which is precisely the mistake behind the 97% claim. It also carries no information about effect size, so a trivial difference in a very large sample can reach significance easily. Replication depends on the true effect, the sample size and chance, none of which a single p value captures.
  12. The correct answer is B (The hazard ratio is not significant, while the mean difference is significant): for a ratio measure the null value is 1, and an interval running from 0.74 to 1.05 includes 1, so no difference remains compatible with the data. For a difference measure the null value is 0, and an interval from 6 to 42 metres excludes 0, so that second result is statistically significant. Applying the zero rule to a ratio, or the one rule to a difference, produces the other readings offered here. Whether 6 metres of walking distance is clinically worthwhile is a separate judgement from significance.
  13. The correct answer is A (50, so roughly one bleed for every 50 treated, against one stroke per 60): the absolute risk increase is 2.5% minus 0.5%, which is 2 percentage points or 0.02, and 1 ÷ 0.02 = 50. Because harm arrives slightly more often than benefit on these figures, the trade needs an explicit conversation about which outcome the patient fears more. Taking the reciprocal of either single event rate answers a different question, since neither one is a difference between groups. The fivefold relative increase in bleeding sounds alarming but describes the very same 2 percentage point gap.
  14. The correct answer is C (Likelihood language belongs to the risk ratio of 0.60, a 40% lower risk): risk is 30 ÷ 100 = 0.30 on treatment and 50 ÷ 100 = 0.50 on control, giving a risk ratio of 0.60 and a 40% relative reduction in risk. Odds are 30 ÷ 70 = 0.43 against 50 ÷ 50 = 1.00, so an odds ratio of 0.43 is a different quantity, and translating it into how likely an outcome is overstates the effect. Odds ratios move further from 1 than risk ratios whenever the outcome is common, converging only when it is rare. Randomized trials can and routinely do report odds ratios, particularly from logistic models.
  15. The correct answer is B (500 patients, the reciprocal of an absolute risk reduction of 0.002): the absolute risk reduction is 0.8% minus 0.6%, which is 0.2 percentage points or 0.002, and 1 ÷ 0.002 = 500 over the five year follow-up. The 25% headline is the relative risk reduction, which stays constant however small the baseline risk becomes, so its reciprocal carries no clinical meaning. Misplacing a single decimal turns 0.002 into 0.02 and shrinks the answer tenfold. The reciprocal of one arm's event rate describes neither benefit nor harm.
  16. The correct answer is D (25 patients, using the 4 percentage point gap between the curves at 24 months): at the end of follow-up the usual care curve sits at 12% and the intervention curve at 8%, so the absolute risk reduction is 0.04 and 1 ÷ 0.04 = 25. The relative reduction is 1 minus 8 ÷ 12, which is one third, and its reciprocal of 3 vastly overstates what any individual patient can expect. Reading a single curve gives the risk within that arm alone and answers a different question. Quoting the figure alongside the two year horizon is what keeps the counselling honest.
  17. The correct answer is A (Exposure and outcome were measured together, so temporal sequence cannot be established): a cross sectional design captures a single moment, so depression could equally well be causing the short sleep, or a third factor such as chronic pain could be driving both at once. Prevalence and association are all this design supports, which makes the causal language in the conclusion the central flaw. A sample of 1,240 is ample to detect an association of the size reported. Odds ratios are routinely derived from survey data, and self-report introduces measurement error rather than making analysis impossible.
  18. The correct answer is A (Credibility, transferability, dependability and confirmability of the interpretive account): qualitative work is judged on whether the interpretation fits the data, whether context is described richly enough for a reader to judge transfer, whether the process is auditable and whether findings arise from participants rather than the researcher. Sample size in this tradition is governed by depth and information power, not by a power calculation. Randomization and confidence intervals belong to designs that estimate effects in a population. Applying quantitative yardsticks to a question about meaning is a category error rather than rigour.
  19. The correct answer is C (A 2 mmHg difference is real but too small to justify the programme alone): with 9,400 participants a trial can detect differences far below the size that changes outcomes for any individual, and a p value of 0.004 attached to a 2 mmHg gap is exactly that situation. Statistical significance answers whether a difference is likely to be real, never whether it is large enough to matter. A p value is also not the probability that the programme works, since it is calculated assuming no true difference exists. Both arms sitting above the systolic target argues for intensifying therapy rather than for calling a small real effect nonexistent.
  20. The correct answer is B (A prospective cohort following newly diagnosed patients over many years): prognosis is a question about what happens over time to a defined group assembled at diagnosis, and only a design that starts there and follows people forward can describe that trajectory. Trials answer whether a treatment changes an outcome, which is different from the natural history this client is asking about. Starting from the outcome and looking backward cannot generate risk over time. A snapshot of current function mixes people at every disease duration and so cannot show any trajectory.
  21. The correct answer is D (A cross sectional study applying a reference standard to consecutive pregnant clients): diagnostic accuracy is measured by giving both the index test and an independent reference standard to an unselected consecutive series of patients in whom the diagnosis is genuinely uncertain. Recruiting people already known to have or lack the condition inflates accuracy, because the difficult intermediate cases are missing. A randomized trial can compare testing strategies and their downstream outcomes but does not itself yield sensitivity and specificity. Following clients until disease appears answers a prognostic question rather than an accuracy question.
  22. The correct answer is C (Rigour of development, together with editorial independence from funding interests): rigour captures how the evidence was searched, selected, appraised and linked to each recommendation, which is the core of whether a document can be believed at all. Editorial independence exposes whether industry funding or panel conflicts shaped the wording. Scope, clarity, stakeholder involvement and applicability are genuine domains worth scoring, but a beautifully presented guideline built on a weak search is still not trustworthy. Citation count is not a domain and says nothing about how evidence was used.
  23. The correct answer is B (Identify the point of divergence and decide with the client using both values): when two rigorous panels disagree because they weighted outcomes differently, the disagreement is about values rather than facts, and the person whose values count is the client. Isolating the exact recommendation that differs, then examining the primary evidence behind it, keeps the conversation concrete. Publication date alone cannot settle a values conflict, and a later document may have used the same trials. Organizational size confers no evidentiary weight, and appraising conflicting guidelines sits squarely within NP scope.
  24. The correct answer is A (Certainty and strength are separate judgements, and clear net benefit permits this): GRADE deliberately separates confidence in the estimates from how firmly a course of action is recommended, because benefits, harms, values, feasibility and cost all feed that second judgement. A strong recommendation on moderate certainty is appropriate when benefits clearly outweigh harms and most informed people would choose the same thing. Requiring the highest certainty for every strong recommendation would leave most of clinical practice unadvised. A conditional recommendation signals that the right choice genuinely varies with patient values.
  25.
