The correct answer is D (Assess barriers and facilitators, then adapt the guideline to this local context): the cycle places local adaptation and a barrier assessment before any intervention is chosen, and here the barriers are open disagreement among prescribers and a record system that makes the right action harder. Naming those two problems determines whether the team needs facilitated discussion, a template, or both. Sending the document addresses awareness, which is not the barrier described. Monitoring use and evaluating outcomes are real stages of the cycle, but they follow an intervention that has been tailored and implemented.
  26. The correct answer is B (Building the screening tool into the routine visit template as a default): the audit already shows knowledge and agreement, so the barrier is workflow, and the intervention has to make the right action the easy action. Embedding the tool where the work happens removes the second login that costs clinicians time at every visit. Education and literature summaries address an awareness gap this team does not have. Audit and feedback helps when the problem is inertia or unrecognized variation, but it will not remove a structural obstacle.
  27. The correct answer is C (Cost per patient, staffing implications and the effect on downstream emergency visits): a funding decision maker needs the resource case and the system level consequences, so the message has to be built around cost, staffing and risk. Statistical detail belongs in a manuscript or a journal club rather than a budget discussion. Patient stories add power to a presentation but cannot carry a resource argument on their own. Rehearsing the background literature explains why the clinic was started rather than why it deserves to continue.
  28. The correct answer is D (Integrated knowledge translation, which raises the odds findings will be usable): involving knowledge users from the moment the question is framed is what defines this approach, and it makes the chosen outcomes the ones decision makers actually need. Activity that begins once results exist, such as publication and conference presentation, arrives far too late to shape the study. Program evaluation assesses a service already running rather than designing new inquiry. Peer review is an external check on method, not a partnership with the people who will use the results.
  29. The correct answer is A (The gap is in implementation, so the work belongs in a local improvement cycle): the evidence for hand hygiene is settled and the staff already agree with it, so reading more literature cannot close the distance between what is known and what is done. What remains is a delivery problem in this setting, which is the definition of a quality improvement question. Repeating the audit generates the same number without changing anything, and further education addresses a knowledge deficit nobody has. Testing small changes to supply placement, workflow and feedback is what moves a compliance rate.
  30. The correct answer is D (The large rigorous trial may provide stronger evidence than the pooled biased studies): a synthesis can only be as sound as the studies inside it, so pooling six small unblinded trials multiplies their bias rather than cancelling it out. Position on the pyramid describes design in the abstract and is routinely overturned by study quality in any real comparison. A larger combined sample improves precision while doing nothing about systematic error. Both approaches estimate the same treatment effect, so they are directly comparable once quality has been taken into account.
  31. The correct answer is C (End each session with a named practice change and a person accountable): translation requires a decision and an owner, otherwise appraisal produces conversation and nothing ever reaches patients. Attaching a specific change, a start date and a person who will report back converts discussion into an implementable step. Rotating presenters and circulating articles early improve the quality of the appraisal without changing whether anything happens afterwards. Restricting the reading to syntheses narrows the reading list rather than closing the gap between discussion and practice.
  32. The correct answer is B (Offer it differently, since the numbers needed to treat are 40 and 400): a quarter off 10% leaves 7.5%, an absolute reduction of 2.5 percentage points and a number needed to treat of 1 ÷ 0.025 = 40. The same quarter off 1% leaves 0.75%, an absolute reduction of 0.25 percentage points and a number needed to treat of 1 ÷ 0.0025 = 400. Identical relative effects therefore mean tenfold different effort and tenfold different exposure to cost and harm, which is why baseline risk drives the conversation. The relative figure does apply in the low risk group, it simply buys very little there.
Case C059

An NP works at a community health centre serving 4,200 rostered adults.

An electronic record query shows that 46% of eligible adults aged 50 to 74 are up to date with colorectal cancer screening, against a regional average of 64%.

The interprofessional team agrees to run an improvement project using the Model for Improvement.

Staff include two NPs, a family physician, three registered nurses and a medical office assistant.

No additional funding is available for the work.

  1. The correct answer is B (Raise screening in eligible adults from 46% to 65% by March 31): an aim is only useful when it names the population, the current baseline, the target and the date, because that combination is what tells a team whether it is succeeding. Wanting improvement as soon as possible sets no measurable finish line at all. A target of every eligible adult is unattainable, since some will decline after a properly informed conversation. Naming the intervention inside the aim commits the team to one solution before a single test has been run.
  2. The correct answer is D (Mean appointment length and the number of visits running past scheduled time): a balancing measure watches for harm appearing somewhere else in the system, and the obvious risk of adding a screening conversation to every visit is that other care gets squeezed out. Positive kit results and completion within 90 days both describe the process or the result the team is already trying to improve. Counting adults never offered screening is another process measure of the same activity. Without something tracking the unintended consequence, a team can hit its target and still make the clinic worse.
  3. The correct answer is A (One provider, one afternoon, five clients, with a written prediction beforehand): a first Plan Do Study Act cycle is deliberately tiny, because its purpose is to learn quickly what breaks and to compare the result against what the team expected to happen. Spreading an untested idea across every provider risks a visible failure that hardens people against the whole project. Collecting nothing for three months discards the learning that early cycles are designed to produce. Allocating clients by chance in order to answer a research question moves the work off the improvement pathway and onto the research ethics pathway, which changes both the timeline and the approvals needed.
  4. The correct answer is C (A shift signalling real change, so the team should adopt and spread it): six or more consecutive points on one side of the median is a recognized signal of non random change, so seven points above the baseline median is very unlikely to be chance. Coming after changes the team deliberately introduced, that is evidence the intervention worked and is ready to be spread more widely. Random fluctuation scatters points on both sides of the median rather than stacking them on one side. A single extreme value is a different signal, and useful run chart interpretation begins well before 20 points.
Case C060

A 71 year old client with a documented penicillin allergy causing angioedema attends an urgent care clinic for cellulitis.

The allergy is recorded in the electronic record.

The prescribing screen displayed an allergy alert, which the prescriber dismissed because alerts had fired on every order she had written that day.

The client received one dose of amoxicillin, developed facial swelling and stridor within 20 minutes, and was treated with intramuscular epinephrine.

He recovered fully and was observed for six hours.

  1. The correct answer is A (A harmful incident, since the client reached actual injury requiring treatment): the drug reached the client, caused airway compromise and required epinephrine, which is harm regardless of the eventual full recovery. A near miss is an event intercepted before it reaches the person at all, and nothing was intercepted here. The no harm category describes an event that reaches the person without detectable injury, which does not describe angioedema and stridor. Full recovery affects severity and follow-up rather than whether the event must be reported.
  2. The correct answer is B (The known facts, the clinical consequences, the care plan and an expression of regret): initial disclosure happens as soon as the client is stable and covers what is known, what it means clinically, what happens next and a sincere expression of regret. Speculating about cause or naming who is at fault before any analysis risks stating something that later proves untrue. Delaying the conversation until a review concludes leaves the client without information he has a right to receive now. Telling him the reaction had no cause is inaccurate and forfeits trust that later conversations will need.
  3. The correct answer is D (At risk behaviour, so the response is coaching plus fixing the alert burden): clicking through alerts is a drift into a shortcut whose risk is no longer perceived, which is precisely the middle category, and it calls for coaching alongside removal of the system pressure that created the habit. Human error is an inadvertent slip, whereas dismissing alerts is a repeated choice even when the risk was never consciously weighed. Reckless behaviour requires conscious disregard of a substantial and unjustifiable risk, which alert fatigue does not describe. Disciplining a predictable response to a badly designed alert system drives reporting underground without reducing risk.
  4. The correct answer is A (It targets one individual and leaves the alert design unchanged for everyone): a recommendation asking a person to try harder produces no change in the conditions that made the error likely, and every other prescriber in the clinic remains exposed to the same alert fatigue. Strong recommendations from an incident analysis change the system, for example by tiering alerts so that allergy warnings interrupt while trivial ones do not. Adding a completion date to a weak recommendation only schedules its failure. Incident analysis conducted for local improvement does not require ethics board review.
Case C061

An NP in a northern Ontario community wants to test whether a monthly group visit model improves glycemic control for adults with type 2 diabetes.

She plans to allocate 90 of her own rostered clients by chance to group visits or to usual individual appointments, measure A1c at 12 months, and publish the results.

Roughly one third of her roster are members of a nearby First Nations community.

She intends to recruit clients herself during their regular appointments.

  1. The correct answer is C (Review is required, because the project seeks new knowledge beyond local improvement): TCPS 2 defines research as an undertaking intended to extend knowledge through a disciplined inquiry or systematic investigation, and its Article 2.5 exclusion reaches only work used exclusively for local assessment, management or improvement, which testing an unproven model to find out whether it works is not. Intent at the outset determines the pathway, so this project needs board review before recruitment begins. Whether the model is used elsewhere does not change what is being done to these particular participants. Deciding to publish afterwards cannot retroactively supply the oversight and consent that should have been in place from the start.
  2. The correct answer is D (Have an independent person seek consent, since the dual role creates pressure): a client whose ongoing care depends on this NP may feel unable to decline an invitation that arrives from her, in her office, in the middle of a treatment discussion. Separating the consent conversation from the therapeutic relationship protects the voluntariness that free and informed consent requires. A signature documents consent but does nothing about the pressure that produced it. Payment can shade into undue inducement, and selecting participants by A1c changes the science rather than the ethics of recruitment.
  3. The correct answer is B (Before the protocol is finalized, through engagement about design, benefit and data use): Chapter 9 expects engagement proportional to how far the research reaches into collective interests, and reporting results by Indigenous identity clearly does, so the conversation belongs at the design stage. Engagement covers the question being asked, how data will be governed, what the community gains and how findings will be reported. Presenting completed results is dissemination rather than engagement, and it arrives after every decision has already been made. Collective interests are not something an individual participant can waive for a community.
  4. The correct answer is A (The community owns and controls its data and decides on access and stewardship): ownership means the community collectively owns information about its members, control means it directs every stage of research affecting it, access means it can reach that information wherever it is held, and possession is physical stewardship of the data itself. Placing the sponsor in charge and granting reading rights inverts the relationship these principles describe. The principles are asserted by First Nations, while Inuit and Métis Nations articulate their own governance approaches, so a single blanket agreement is not appropriate. Removing identifiers does not extinguish a collective interest in community data.

Practice Test 4 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (A shift, since six consecutive points fall above the median after the change): the final six months all sit above the median of 58%, and six or more consecutive points on one side of the median is a recognized signal that something other than chance is at work. Arriving immediately after a change the team introduced on purpose, that is evidence the change did something. A trend requires five or more consecutive points each higher than the one before, and these later points rise and fall rather than climbing steadily. Randomness would scatter points on both sides, and no single value sits far outside the rest.
  2. The correct answer is C (Adapt the call to two days earlier and run a second small test): the cycle produced a specific piece of learning about timing, and adapting the change then retesting on a small scale is exactly what this step exists for. Abandoning the idea discards a change that has never yet been tested in its improved form. Adopting a change that missed its prediction spreads something the team already knows does not work as intended. Scaling an unmodified test to 200 clients buys precision about the wrong version of the change.
  3. The correct answer is B (Reacting to common cause variation as a special event usually worsens performance): a process showing only common cause variation is stable, and month to month wobble carries no information about any specific assignable cause. Adjusting a stable process in response to noise, sometimes called tampering, adds variation rather than removing it. Reducing common cause variation requires redesigning the process itself, not reacting to individual points as they appear. Limits are set from a stable baseline and then held fixed rather than recalculated with every new point, and the guidance on how many points are needed concerns calculating stable limits rather than barring action once a genuine signal appears. Staff perceiving blame is a real risk, but it still does not explain why adjusting a stable process backfires.
  4. The correct answer is D (Failure modes and effects analysis, used prospectively to design out likely failures): the method is forward looking, applied before a process goes live, and it multiplies severity, occurrence and detectability to rank which failure modes deserve redesign first. Root cause analysis works backwards from an event that has already caused harm. A test cycle is a way of trialling a change rather than a structured hazard scoring exercise. Incident analysis is the retrospective family of methods that root cause analysis belongs to, so it also looks the wrong way.
  5. The correct answer is A (Review is not required, since the work is exclusively for local improvement): quality assurance and improvement activities, program evaluation and performance reviews used only for assessment, management or improvement purposes sit outside the requirement for board review. Sample size does not convert improvement work into research, and neither does the clinical topic being studied. What would change the pathway is a shift in purpose toward producing new knowledge rather than improving care in this clinic alone, so consulting the board when the boundary is unclear remains the safe habit. Improvement work still requires privacy safeguards, but research consent is not the mechanism for that.
  6. The correct answer is B (Disagree, since near misses expose failing systems without a wounded client): a near miss reveals the same latent weakness as a harmful event at no cost to the person receiving care, which makes it the cheapest safety data any organization can obtain. Forty six of them in three months is a rich picture of exactly where the process is fragile. Setting a harm threshold before caring about these reports waits for someone to be injured before learning anything. Report volume reflects reporting culture rather than actual safety, so it cannot serve as a performance indicator on its own.
  7. The correct answer is A (Month 9 reflects common cause variation, so no special investigation is warranted): the points scatter on both sides of the median with no run of six, no five point trend and nothing sitting far outside the usual range, which is the picture of a stable process. Chasing one ordinary low point spends effort on noise and often adds variation to a process that was behaving predictably. A special cause has an assignable reason and announces itself through a recognized signal such as a shift, a trend or a point beyond the limits, not merely by being a low value. Genuinely improving this measure means redesigning the process and then watching whether the median moves.
  8. The correct answer is C (Meet, share what the analysis found and what will change, and express regret): post analysis disclosure closes the loop by telling a family what was learned and what the organization is changing, which is the part most families say matters most to them. Apology legislation in most provinces protects an expression of sympathy or regret from being treated as an admission of liability. Withholding the findings or repeating only the original facts breaks the commitment made at initial disclosure. Telling a family that no cause was found when two contributors were identified is a false statement, whatever the motive behind it.
  9. The correct answer is D (Stratify the rate by language, geography and Indigenous identity to expose gaps): an aggregate that meets target can conceal a group screened at half the rate of everyone else, and only disaggregated data will reveal it. Stratification also points the team toward the specific barrier, since a language gap and a distance gap call for entirely different interventions. Repeating the same aggregate monthly reproduces the same blind spot every month. Benchmarking against a provincial average or testing capacity may be interesting, but neither locates an inequity inside the region's own population.
  10. The correct answer is B (Act on the documented system factors, since the same conditions will recur): the report names a handwritten order, a trailing zero and two insulin concentrations stored in the same drawer, and every one of those will produce another tenfold error until it is fixed. A near miss caught by a double check tells the team its defences worked once, not that the hazard has gone away. Counselling the individual who prepared the dose changes nothing about the conditions described in the report. The classification as written is accurate, since the drug never reached the client.
  11. The correct answer is C (Respect for persons, because the payment may amount to undue inducement): free and informed consent requires a decision that is genuinely voluntary, and an amount large enough to override a person's own judgement about risk destroys that voluntariness. The concern is not payment itself, which fairly compensates time and travel, but a sum that becomes impossible to refuse for someone with no income. Distress during an interview and the fair distribution of research burden are real considerations addressed by other principles. Protecting sensitive information matters here but is not what the objection to the payment is about.
  12. The correct answer is A (She may withdraw at any point, and her clinical care will continue unchanged): consent in research is an ongoing process, and the right to withdraw without penalty or loss of entitlement to care is something a participant must be told at the outset and reminded of afterwards. Making withdrawal wait for a data collection window turns a right into a scheduling concession. Whether data already collected can be retained depends on what the consent form specified, so an absolute promise of destruction is not accurate. Treating the original signature as binding misunderstands consent as an event rather than a process.
  13. The correct answer is D (Review is needed, since the project pursues new knowledge rather than local improvement): the charter sets out to discover whether the change works rather than to improve care in these two clinics alone, which is an undertaking intended to extend knowledge rather than the exclusively local improvement that Article 2.5 excludes. That combination places the project on the research pathway regardless of the label the team has chosen, and the board must be approached before any allocation happens. Whether the intervention is already recommended does not matter, because it is being withheld from one group to create a comparison. The number of participating organizations affects which board reviews the work, not whether review is needed.
  14. The correct answer is B (Staff have stopped reporting, since reporting now carries a personal risk): a reporting system depends entirely on people believing that speaking up leads to system change rather than to sanction, and visible discipline after ordinary error destroys that belief very quickly. The hazards have not disappeared, they have simply become invisible to leadership. Attributing most errors to two individuals contradicts everything known about how error arises from the conditions of work. Six months is long enough to see a real signal, and the direction of this one is a warning rather than reassurance.
  15. The correct answer is A (Recommendations that change the system, each with a named owner and date): an analysis is only useful when it produces changes to the conditions of work, and an owner with a deadline is what turns a recommendation into something that actually happens. Cataloguing who was present, or naming a single person to blame, ends the inquiry precisely where the real learning begins. Blanket training is the classic weak recommendation, because it asks people to be more careful inside a system that has not changed at all. Strength is judged by how much a recommendation reduces reliance on individual vigilance.
  16. The correct answer is C (The percentage of respiratory visits that resulted in an antibiotic prescription): the outcome measure is the result the project exists to change, and reducing unnecessary antibiotic use is the stated purpose of this work. Offering a delayed prescription is one of the steps being tested, which makes it a process measure of whether the intervention is actually happening. Return visits within seven days watch for harm caused by the change itself, which is a balancing measure. Counting attendance at an education session measures activity rather than any of the three.
  17. The correct answer is B (Whether the development population resembles the community the tool will serve): an algorithm learns the patterns present in its training data, so a model built on urban American utilization can misestimate risk badly where the population, the geography and the access barriers all differ. Deploying it unexamined can direct scarce resources away from the people who need them most and widen an existing gap. Integration, training and how output is displayed all affect uptake, but none of them makes an inaccurate prediction safe. Local validation before deployment is the step that answers what actually matters.
  18. The correct answer is D (Two averages cannot separate real change from the normal variation over time): a process fluctuates from month to month, so comparing one month with another can show a difference that would have appeared with no intervention at all, in either direction. Plotting the measure in time order against a median reveals whether the pattern contains a genuine shift or trend. Switching to a median is a sensible refinement but does not solve the underlying problem with two data points. A significance test applied to two aggregates inherits the same weakness rather than correcting it.
  19. The correct answer is A (Console the first and coach the second, since the behaviours differ, not outcomes): a just culture judges the behaviour that led to the event, so an inadvertent slip during a rushed handover calls for support plus a hard look at labelling and handover, while a deliberate shortcut calls for coaching and removal of whatever made skipping the check seem reasonable. Responding identically because the outcomes matched abandons that distinction entirely. Uniform discipline drives reporting down without reducing risk at all. Doing nothing because nobody was hurt leaves the same conditions in place to produce a harmful event next time.
  20. The correct answer is C (OCAP is a First Nations framework, and Inuit assert their own governance approaches): the principles were created by First Nations in 1998 and are stewarded by the First Nations Information Governance Centre, which holds OCAP as a registered trademark, so applying the label to Inuit or Métis participants misrepresents whose framework is being followed. Inuit self determination in research is articulated through Inuit Tapiriit Kanatami's national strategy, and Métis Nations set out their own expectations separately. Chapter 9 of TCPS 2 sets ethics requirements for research involving First Nations, Inuit and Métis Peoples and sits alongside these frameworks rather than replacing them. Who holds the funding does not determine which governance framework applies.
  21. The correct answer is D (The Study step, since results were never compared against the team's prediction): learning comes from the gap between what a team expected and what actually happened, so skipping that comparison means the group repeats an assumption instead of testing it. A prediction that turns out wrong points directly at the part of the theory of change that needs revising. Moving to a rollout is the deciding step, which this team has in fact taken, just without the learning that should inform it. Aim setting and small scale testing are separate concerns from the missed comparison described here.
  22. The correct answer is B (A no harm incident, still reported so the staffing gap gets addressed): the event reached the client, because the dose really was given late, yet produced no detectable injury, which is exactly what this category describes. A near miss is caught before it reaches the person, and this one was not caught at all. Calling it harmful stretches a term that should be reserved for actual injury and blunts the usefulness of the whole classification. Declining to report it hides a staffing problem that will eventually produce a far more serious delay.
  23. The correct answer is A (Audit and feedback comparing each prescriber's own rate against the group): the barrier is neither awareness nor tooling, since everyone endorses the protocol and the template already exists, which leaves unrecognized variation in individual practice. Confidential feedback showing a clinician their own rate beside their peers is the intervention designed for exactly that gap. Repeating training addresses knowledge the group demonstrably already has. Removing the template or resending the document would take away a facilitator or repeat a message, neither of which touches the variation described.
  24. The correct answer is C (Alternatives, limits to confidentiality, funding source and the right to withdraw): informed consent requires that participants know what else they could do instead, where confidentiality has limits, who is paying for the work and that they may leave at any time without penalty. Those four elements are the ones most often omitted from an otherwise complete looking form. Promising a personal health benefit would be misleading, since a study exists precisely because the benefit is unknown. Investigator credentials and the analysis plan belong to the protocol and the ethics submission rather than to the consent conversation.
  25. The correct answer is B (Special cause variation with an assignable reason worth investigating specifically): a point beyond three standard deviations is very unlikely to arise from the routine noise of a stable process, so something specific happened that month and finding it is the entire purpose of the chart. That is exactly the situation where investigation is warranted, unlike the ordinary wobble inside the limits. Dismissing it as regression to the mean discards the one signal the chart was built to detect. A shift is defined by a run of consecutive points on one side of the centre line, which is a different signal altogether.
  26. The correct answer is D (The design extracts information without benefit or ongoing control for the community): Chapter 9 expects research to be conducted with communities rather than on them, which means engagement, respect for local codes of practice, recognition of knowledge holders and tangible benefit where the data came from. A plan that takes data away and never returns is the very pattern the chapter was written to end. Sample size is a methodological question that review does consider, but it is not the central objection here. Consent logistics and privacy legislation matter without touching the collective interests at stake.
  27. The correct answer is C (Natural frequencies, such as 3 in 100 against 5 in 100 over five years): absolute numbers expressed as counts out of a fixed group are the format lay readers interpret most accurately, and pairing them with a timeframe stops the benefit being read as lifelong certainty. A relative reduction quoted alone sounds identical whether it prevents one event in 20 people or one in 1000. Ratio measures with intervals belong in a clinical summary rather than a waiting room handout. A p value tells a reader nothing at all about how much benefit to expect.
  28. The correct answer is D (The change was never embedded, so sustaining use requires rebuilding the process step): improvement holds only when a new behaviour is built into the routine work, and here the template prompt that carried it has been removed, so the process reverted to what it was before. Sustaining use is an explicit stage of the cycle rather than something that happens automatically after a successful project. A gain that persisted throughout the project and then reversed when its mechanism disappeared is not a chance finding. Nothing here suggests the measure itself changed, and decline is common but never inevitable.
Case C062

You are a nurse practitioner at a family health team in a mid sized Ontario city.

Over three months, several patients with hypertension have received conflicting dose advice from you and from the team pharmacist, who also adjusts antihypertensives.

Two patients stopped their medication because of the confusion.

Staff describe the situation as a personality clash between the two of you.

Both of you are working within your legislated scope, and neither has raised the issue directly with the other.

  1. The correct answer is B (Role ambiguity about who adjusts antihypertensive doses for which patients): overlapping scope becomes conflict only when nobody has stated out loud who owns which decisions. Both clinicians are practising within scope, so no regulatory breach has occurred and a college notification would be unfounded. Naming it a personality clash hides the structural cause, and mediation would leave the same ambiguity in place. The shared record captured both changes accurately, so the problem is not a charting failure.
  2. The correct answer is C (Overlapping competence with no written agreement makes each read the other as intruding): shared competencies let teams cover for each other, but with nothing written down each clinician sees the other acting on their patients. Differences in training do not by themselves generate conflict when roles are clear. Canadian hypertension guidance is not ambiguous about titration, only silent about which profession does it in a given clinic. A shared record does exist and captured both changes, so information availability was not the barrier.
  3. The correct answer is A (Meet the pharmacist, name the overlap, and draft a written working agreement): the leadership response to structural ambiguity is to convene the affected roles and write down who does what, including how disagreements about a patient get settled. Handing titration to physicians wastes two competent prescribers and does not address the unnamed ambiguity. Careful documentation records the contradiction without preventing it. A college report is disproportionate when the pharmacist has not practised outside scope.
  4. The correct answer is C (Track reports of conflicting advice and review the agreement on a set date): a partnership that works has an agreed indicator and a scheduled review, which is what catches quiet reversion before patients are affected. Circulating a document and waiting for complaints relies on the failure occurring first. A standing monthly meeting consumes time whether or not anything needs discussing. Asking every patient at every visit is burdensome and captures only those who noticed the contradiction.
Case C063

You are a nurse practitioner on the medical unit of a small rural hospital.

On three shifts this month you have watched a locum physician prescribe a benzodiazepine to an eighty one year old woman already receiving hydromorphone, each time without a documented reason.

Tonight she is difficult to rouse, with a respiratory rate of eight and oxygen saturation of eighty nine percent.

The physician is off site.

The hospital has a stop the line policy and a just culture framework.

  1. The correct answer is B (Hold the dose, assess the patient fully and escalate for urgent review): when the risk is immediate the patient is protected first, which is exactly what a stop the line policy authorizes any team member to do. Waiting for the prescriber's rationale allows a sedating dose to reach an already hypoventilating patient. An incident report is necessary later but documents harm rather than preventing it. Reassessing at the next round accepts an unmonitored interval this patient cannot safely tolerate.
  2. The correct answer is C (Coach him and remove the incentive, since this is at risk behaviour): a shortcut taken without recognizing the risk it creates is met with coaching and a change to whatever made the shortcut attractive. Discipline is reserved for conscious disregard of a substantial known risk, which does not describe a prescriber unaware of the interaction. Consoling fits a slip or lapse by someone who intended the correct action. Just culture addresses both systems and individual behaviour, so doing nothing misapplies it.
  3. The correct answer is D (Report the specific events and dates in writing to the medical lead): escalation moves to the next level once the direct conversation has failed, and it is made in writing with facts and dates rather than characterizations. Restating the concern more forcefully repeats a step already tried without effect. A college report is premature while an internal channel remains untested. Collecting colleagues' impressions delays action and converts documented events into hearsay.
  4. The correct answer is B (Escalate in writing to the next authority and consider a college report): a duty to protect patients is not discharged by one report to a level that has not responded, and nursing practice standards across the provinces expect a nurse to keep escalating and, where internal channels have failed and patients remain at risk, to report to the colleague's regulatory college, while statutory mandatory reporting duties are narrower and fall mainly on employers and facility operators. Treating silence as a considered decision leaves a documented risk in place. Advising nurses informally to ignore orders creates a covert workaround without addressing the prescribing. Another verbal conversation repeats the approach that has already failed.
Case C064

You are three months into your first nurse practitioner position at a small northern health centre.

Reviewing your own charts, you find that four patients discharged from the regional hospital this quarter had no follow up appointment booked, and two of them returned to the emergency department within ten days.

You hold no formal authority over the booking clerks, the visiting physicians or the centre manager.

No one has raised this pattern before.

  1. The correct answer is B (Bring the pattern to the next team meeting with the specific cases): leadership does not require a title, and naming a pattern with concrete cases in front of the people who can fix it is available to any team member. Waiting for someone senior to notice is the response the blueprint treats as an abdication. Phoning the patients yourself repairs four charts and leaves the process untouched. A complaint to the health authority blames clerks for a system nobody has designed.
  2. The correct answer is A (Leadership roles are available at every level and do not require a title): leadership in place holds that influence does not flow only from hierarchical position, so the absence of authority is not a reason to stay silent about a pattern that is harming patients. Regulatory standards do not make nurse practitioners the supervisors of booking clerks. Accountability for system problems is shared rather than weighted toward the newest member. Responsibility does not transfer away from a manager because progress has been slow.
  3. The correct answer is D (Form a small working group, test a discharge checklist, then build it in): durable change needs a guiding coalition, a tested method and a route into everyday routine, which is precisely what two failed memos lacked. A third memo repeats an intervention with a known result. Reviewing the list personally creates a fix that disappears on your days off and on the day you leave. Posting numbers may raise awareness but supplies no process for booking the appointment.
  4. The correct answer is C (Write the checklist into orientation, job descriptions and the discharge policy): change that is never anchored in routines, orientation and role expectations reverts quietly within about a year, and anchoring is the step most often skipped. Celebrating a short term win sustains momentum but does not make the practice the new default. Monthly monitoring detects reversion after it happens rather than preventing it. Requesting new funding depends on a decision outside your control and outside your timeline.
  5. The correct answer is A (Set explicit expectations, audit compliance daily and address gaps immediately): clear expectations, monitoring and consequences suit routine, high volume, safety critical work during an outbreak, where variation is the hazard. Vision and inspiration change culture over months and are far too slow for a closed unit today. Asking about obstacles supports a demoralized team but does not produce uniform practice this week. Delegating the decision to the most experienced staff member abandons the standardization the situation requires.
  6. The correct answer is D (Ask each staff member what blocks their work, then remove those obstacles): putting the growth and needs of the team first suits a group that is depleted and quietly working around broken processes. Performance metrics add monitoring to a team whose problem is not effort. A bold vision lands poorly with staff who currently see no evidence that anything changes. Close direction of every task treats experienced clinicians as novices and deepens the disengagement.
  7. The correct answer is B (Provide close direction for bladder scanning while continuing to delegate wound care): the right level of support depends on competence and commitment for a specific task, not on the person overall. Delegating a brand new psychomotor skill because she is capable elsewhere is the most common misapplication of this model. Supervising all of her work closely punishes established competence and wastes clinic time. Letting the learner set her own supervision level abandons the accountability that comes with supervising practice.
  8. The correct answer is A (The respiratory therapist takes the airway while the physician runs the resuscitation): leadership is a property of the team, and in a resuscitation the person who leads a task is whoever holds the relevant current skill. Assigning the airway by seniority puts credential and recent practice second to hierarchy at the worst possible moment. Waiting for an anesthetist from another site is not survivable for a patient in arrest. Independent sequential attempts remove any single point of coordination.
  9. The correct answer is C (Name the omission at the table and offer to help redraft that section): leadership roles are available regardless of title or tenure, and the expected response is to raise the issue, own a piece of the work and bring the right people in. Routing the concern to a manager afterward removes your knowledge from the room where the decision is made. Withholding endorsement pending a more senior colleague reinforces the idea that leadership requires rank. Implementing a flawed model and cataloguing the fallout accepts avoidable harm.
  10. The correct answer is D (Conflict over roles is an expected phase that should be worked through): competing over roles and influence is a normal developmental stage, and a leader who suppresses it produces a team that looks calm while its disagreements go underground. Reconstituting the team restarts the same process with new people. Treating the friction as a selection error misreads a group phenomenon as an individual failing. The polite early phase was tentative rather than productive, so restoring it would not improve performance.
  11. The correct answer is A (Changes in membership and leadership can move a team back a stage): teams do not progress in a straight line, and a new member, manager or mandate can return a high performing group to renegotiating roles and expectations. Concluding that two years of smooth functioning was superficial discards real evidence. Blaming the arriving staff individualizes a predictable group response to change. Development is not linear, so treating any regression as pathology misleads the team about what is happening.
  12. The correct answer is B (Thank her, ask what made the error possible, and report the near miss): thanking people for raising concerns and meeting bad news with curiosity are the behaviours that make a team willing to surface its own errors. Agreeing that no harm means no report teaches staff that near misses are not worth mentioning. Reciting the five rights returns the focus to the individual and ignores the look alike packaging. Saving the event for a performance review converts a safety report into a disciplinary matter.
  13. The correct answer is D (Name your own missed referral openly and ask how the process failed): admitting your own mistakes is the strongest signal that speaking up is safe, and pairing it with a process question keeps the debrief from becoming about blame. Discussing system factors while concealing your own role models exactly the silence you are trying to prevent. Taking it privately to the manager keeps the learning away from the team that needs it. Waiting for someone else to notice makes disclosure conditional on being caught.
  14. The correct answer is C (Agree explicitly which parts of the teaching each of you will deliver): overlap becomes conflict only when nobody has said out loud who does what, so the fix is a stated division of the work rather than a winner. Absorbing all the teaching because you prescribe discards a competent colleague's contribution and worsens the resentment. Alternating by week leaves each patient's teaching to chance and still duplicates content. Framing the nurse's frustration as conduct treats a structural problem as a personality problem.
  15. The correct answer is A (Clarify the legislated roles together and document your working agreement): scope and most responsible provider authority differ by jurisdiction, so the useful step is to establish what the legislation actually permits and write down how the two of you will work. Accepting an unnecessary countersigning arrangement misrepresents accountability and sets a precedent. Referring the question to the board before the two clinicians have spoken escalates something they can resolve. Sending her to the college avoids the conversation the working relationship depends on.
  16. The correct answer is B (Ask each party why the timing matters before discussing any discharge date): positions collide while the interests behind them often do not, and surfacing why each side wants what it wants generates options neither position contained. Referring the decision upward settles it by authority and leaves both interests unaddressed. Splitting the difference compromises between two positions without learning anything about either. Presenting evidence to one party treats the disagreement as a knowledge deficit in the family.
  17. The correct answer is A (What outcome does this patient most want to avoid, given both risks): when clinicians disagree about a tradeoff, the patient is a party to the decision rather than its object, and his own values resolve what neither risk estimate can. Deciding by specialty seniority substitutes authority for the patient's voice. A risk score quantifies one side of the tradeoff and cannot weigh it against bleeding. Identifying the most responsible provider records who signs the order without addressing what should be ordered.
  18. The correct answer is D (Discuss her reasoning with her, and raise it at team rounds if needed): negotiation is the right tool when a disagreement is about clinical judgment within the range of acceptable practice, and looser targets for frail older adults sit inside that range. Escalating to the clinical lead treats a defensible judgment as a deficiency. A college report is reserved for unsafe or unprofessional practice, which this is not. Altering another clinician's plan in her absence undermines both the patient's care and the working relationship.
  19. The correct answer is C (Speak with her privately, naming the specific documentation and the specific risk): escalation starts with a direct private conversation that names the behaviour and the risk, unless the patient faces immediate harm or the approach is unsafe, and neither exception applies here. An anonymous report skips a step that often resolves the issue and prevents any explanation. A college report is premature before internal steps have been attempted. Auditing her charts collects evidence while the documentation practice continues unchallenged.
  20. The correct answer is A (Say stop immediately and state that the consent identifies the other knee): when the risk is immediate the patient is protected first, and a stop the line moment authorizes any team member to halt a procedure over a safety concern. Waiting for the injection to finish allows the wrong site procedure to be completed. Leaving to find the clinical lead removes the only person present who can prevent it. Questioning the patient afterward gathers information after the harm has occurred.
  21. The correct answer is B (Support the nurse and separate the look alike products in the storage area): a slip by a competent person calls for consoling the individual and redesigning the condition that allowed it, because the next nurse will make the same error against the same packaging. A written warning punishes a person for a system design and suppresses future reporting. Repeating an education module addresses knowledge the nurse already had. Recording the event without changing the storage leaves the hazard fully intact.
  22. The correct answer is D (Apply a disciplinary response, since the risk was knowingly disregarded again): conscious disregard of a substantial and acknowledged risk is the one category that just culture answers with discipline. Consoling fits an unintended slip rather than a repeated deliberate choice. Further coaching has already been tried twice and the clinician has confirmed understanding of the hazard. Concluding that just culture rules out discipline entirely misreads a framework designed to make the response proportionate rather than absent.
  23. The correct answer is C (Standardize the unit to a single infusion pump model with common defaults): the report identifies two pump models with different default rate units as the condition that made the error possible, and only an individual counselling response has been taken. Education repeats what staff already know while leaving the conflicting defaults in place. Continuing individual counselling is the response that just culture treats as unjust and ineffective for a design problem. A universal second signature adds workload to every infusion without addressing the source of the confusion.
  24. The correct answer is A (Restate the concern clearly and directly, asking him to stop and verify): the two challenge rule holds that a safety concern dismissed once must be restated unambiguously before any deferral, and the stop the line step is available to any team member. Deferring after a single dismissed statement is the failure mode the model exists to prevent. Leaving the room removes the only person raising the concern at the moment it can still be acted on. Documenting and watching records a wrong site procedure rather than preventing one.
  25. The correct answer is D (Add a brief standing safety item to the existing weekly team huddle): building improvement into a meeting that already happens costs nothing, needs no authority and creates the repeated low stakes forum where near misses become discussable. Requesting an external specialist places the work outside the team and outside your control. An anonymous survey collects a snapshot without creating any ongoing route for raising concerns. A formal committee with terms of reference is heavier than a small clinic will sustain and is easily deferred.
  26. The correct answer is B (She may call a safety stop herself and the procedure must pause): the policy extends the authority to any member of the care team including students, requires the procedure to pause, and states that no supervisory approval is needed. Waiting to tell a preceptor afterward defeats a mechanism designed to work in the moment. Requiring preceptor agreement inserts a permission step the policy explicitly removes. Routing the concern to a committee handles it long after the procedure has finished.
  27. The correct answer is C (State the situation, background, assessment and the specific action you need): a structured handover format gives a busy listener a shape they can absorb in the time available and ends with an explicit request rather than a hope. Detailing staff frustration conveys feeling without the data or the ask. Sending raw data transfers the analysis to the person with the least time to do it. Asking what she wants first delays a concern that has already gone unaddressed through several conversations.
Case C065

You lead the rollout of a routine food insecurity screening tool at an urban community health centre.

Six weeks after launch, only twelve percent of eligible visits include the screen.

Registered nurses tell you the tool adds about seven minutes to a visit, repeats a question already asked at intake, and that two previous centre initiatives were abandoned partway through.

No additional visit time or staffing was added when the tool was introduced.

  1. The correct answer is B (Meet the nurses to understand the specific barriers before changing anything): resistance usually carries accurate information about the work, and the people doing the screening have already named three concrete obstacles worth understanding in detail. Auditing individual completion measures a tool nobody has examined. Handing the task to intake without redesign moves the duplication rather than removing it. Presenting outcome evidence assumes the nurses doubt the purpose, which is not what they said.
  2. The correct answer is A (The tool repeats a question already asked during the intake process): duplication is a defect in the instrument itself, verifiable against the intake form, and removing it recovers time without losing any information. Abandoned past initiatives explain why staff are sceptical but describe the centre's history rather than this tool's design. Lack of consultation is a failure in how the rollout was run, not in the questions asked. Higher rates elsewhere show what is possible without identifying what is wrong here.
  3. The correct answer is A (Remove the duplicated intake question and let nurses redesign the workflow): objections that carry real information about the work become a better design when the people who raised them are asked to fix it, and removing duplication frees the time the screen needs. A mandatory field forces a click without producing a conversation with the patient. An individual incentive rewards one person and leaves the workflow unchanged. Another memo repeats the approach that produced twelve percent uptake.
  4. The correct answer is C (Build the workflow into orientation, the visit template and the standard process): a new way of working becomes the default only when it is stabilized in the routines and tools people use every day, which is what stops a gain from fading once its champion leaves. Weekly reminders depend on someone remembering to give them. Reporting the rate to the board records the achievement without protecting it. Moving on immediately is precisely the pattern that produced the centre's two abandoned initiatives.
Case C066

A wildfire forces the evacuation of a remote northern First Nations community of about nine hundred people.

You are the nurse practitioner at the nursing station.

Air transport to a host city eight hundred kilometres away begins within four hours.

Residents include three people receiving dialysis, several elders with dementia, two oxygen dependent clients and eleven people on daily witnessed opioid agonist therapy.

The regional health authority has activated its incident management structure.

  1. The correct answer is A (Identify and prioritize residents whose treatments cannot be interrupted during transport): dialysis, oxygen and daily witnessed dosing are the needs that fail first and fastest when a community is moved, so identifying those residents shapes every later decision about aircraft, escorts and supplies. Securing the drug supply matters but can be delegated and does not determine who flies first. Household by household telephoning duplicates the community's own emergency communication and consumes the hours available. A closure report can wait until people are safe.
  2. The correct answer is C (Take direction from the role assigned within the incident management structure): a declared emergency replaces everyday reporting lines with a single chain of command so that unfamiliar staff and agencies can coordinate without competing instructions. Following the usual manager reintroduces exactly the divided authority the structure exists to prevent. Acting independently inside a coordinated response leaves your work invisible to the people allocating aircraft and supplies. A regulatory college does not arbitrate operational assignments during an active evacuation.
  3. The correct answer is B (Rebuild medication lists from the provincial drug record and the dispensing pharmacy): provincial dispensing records and the home pharmacy give a verifiable history within hours, which is what safe reinstatement of opioid agonist therapy and insulin requires. Sending residents to an emergency department creates long waits and repeats the same information problem at a busier site. Restarting from recall risks a dosing error in exactly the drugs where an error is most dangerous. Holding all dosing produces withdrawal and hyperglycemia.
  4. The correct answer is B (The community's stated priorities should direct the sequence of the health response): partnerships with Indigenous communities respect community governance and follow the community's priorities rather than the health authority's, which is the difference between working with a community and working on it. Finalizing a plan and presenting it for information is consultation in name only. Provincial officials sit further from the community than the health authority already does. Pausing until a formal request arrives abandons a partner mid crisis.
  5. The correct answer is D (Portability maintains the former plan's coverage during a waiting period after moving): provinces may impose a waiting period of up to three months for new residents, during which the former plan continues to insure the person, which is what this patient is experiencing. Comprehensiveness describes the range of insured services rather than coverage across a move. Universality concerns equal terms for the insured residents of one plan. Public administration governs how a plan is operated, not what happens when someone relocates.
  6. The correct answer is C (Submit a Jordan's Principle request so the service proceeds without delay): the principle exists because First Nations children were caught between governments, and it requires the government of first contact to pay and resolve jurisdiction afterward. Appealing and waiting continues the delay that is already harming the child. Asking a family to purchase equipment and seek reimbursement transfers a jurisdictional dispute onto them. Referring to a tertiary centre may move the child but does not settle who funds equipment at home.
  7. The correct answer is D (Submit an exception request to the federal benefits program with clinical rationale): the federal benefits program funds medications for eligible First Nations and Inuit clients and has an exception process for drugs outside its regular list, which is the route when a provincial plan does not cover the product. Substituting a less suitable drug accepts inferior care while an established route exists. Telling the patient no appeal is possible is inaccurate. Relying on samples creates an interrupted supply and no lasting solution.
  8. The correct answer is A (The destination province's legislation and its college standards for the role): delivery and regulation of health services is a provincial and territorial responsibility, so prescribing authority, admitting privileges and most responsible provider eligibility all vary by jurisdiction. A national competency framework describes expected abilities but grants no legal authority anywhere. Employer policies apply to one organization and do not travel with registration. Federal drug legislation permits certain prescribing but leaves the scope decision to each province and its regulator.
  9. The correct answer is C (Arrange scheduled paramedic home visits with findings reported back to you): community paramedicine adds value at the transition points where patients get lost, and scheduled proactive visits with a reporting loop to the primary provider are what reduce emergency use. Redirecting transport to the clinic still waits for deterioration before anything happens. A direct line to dispatch increases call volume without adding assessment. Independent paramedic discharge decisions sit outside their scope and outside any shared care plan.
  10. The correct answer is D (Build a referral partnership with local food security and income support programs): community partnerships address determinants of health that clinical care alone cannot touch, and recurrent deficiency despite adequate treatment points to the household resource problem rather than the prescription. Raising the dose treats the measurement while the cause continues. Better written counselling assumes the barrier is knowledge rather than money. Ad hoc referrals when families happen to ask reach only those willing to disclose need.
  11. The correct answer is B (No named contact on each side and no scheduled review were agreed): partnerships that work name a contact on each side, state what each party contributes, and set a date to review, and the summary records neither the contacts nor the review. A secure fax pathway was explicitly agreed and is workable, so the transmission method is not the gap. The volume of spaces would limit capacity but would not make referrals disappear. A shared goal was recorded, so the failure lies in the operating detail.
  12. The correct answer is C (Seek the community's direction on the use of its data before submitting): partnerships with First Nations communities respect community governance and the community's ownership, control, access and possession of its own information, so the decision belongs to the community rather than the health authority. Data already sitting in a provincial system confers no permission for a new use. Aggregating reduces identifiability but still uses community information without direction. A permanent refusal substitutes your judgment for the community's and forecloses partnerships it may want.
  13. The correct answer is D (Explain that the health calls to action apply to practitioners in every setting): the health related calls to action are addressed to health professionals wherever they practise, including the urban settings where most Indigenous people in Canada live. Accepting the manager's view leaves reported experiences of judgment unaddressed. Waiting for the health authority defers a responsibility that already rests with the practice. Collecting complaints for a year gathers further evidence of a problem patients have already described directly.
  14. The correct answer is A (No shared sense of urgency was created before the pathway was launched): change that begins without clinicians believing the current state is a problem is ignored, and this group explicitly sees nothing wrong with what they do now. A longer notice period would not create a reason to change. The medium matters less than the absence of any felt need. Anchoring in routines and job descriptions prevents reversion after adoption, which cannot be the failure when adoption never happened.
  15. The correct answer is B (Recruit a respected staff member at each site into a guiding group): a change spread across sites needs a coalition with credibility in each location, because a leader who cannot be present depends on people who can. Circulating a finished plan for comment invites reaction without creating ownership. Starting at the familiar site delays engagement with the three sites where the risk actually sits. An executive mandate produces compliance at best and supplies no local advocate when problems appear.
  16. The correct answer is D (Show the team how the current booking pattern produces their daily backlog): loosening an established way of working starts with making the case that it no longer serves the people using it, which has to precede any new design. A quiet pilot jumps to the transition before the team accepts that a problem exists. Writing new rules into policy attempts to stabilize a change nobody has agreed to. Announcing a start date sets a schedule without supplying a reason.
  17. The correct answer is C (Treat the objections as design feedback and redesign the screen with them): resistance usually carries accurate information about the work, and duplicated questions plus seven unfunded minutes are real design problems rather than attitudes. An audit measures a tool the team has already identified as unworkable. Presenting outcome evidence assumes the nurses doubt the value of fall prevention, which they have not said. Making the screen optional abandons the safety purpose instead of fixing what makes it unusable.
  18. The correct answer is A (Role clarification and a shared care plan with an identified coordinator): co location, a meeting and a common record produce parallel practice, where each professional sees the same patient and writes a note nobody reads. What is absent is an agreement about what each profession contributes and one plan with someone accountable for it. Administrative funding does not create shared goals. More meetings add time to a team that already meets. Another messaging channel duplicates the record they share.
  19. The correct answer is D (Structure it around patients needing input from more than one profession): structured communication about shared patients is what turns proximity into collaboration, and a huddle that names who needs joint attention gives every attendee a reason to be there. Mandatory attendance compels presence at a meeting with no value. Shortening an announcement list makes it briefer rather than useful. Adding a video platform widens access to the same content without changing what the meeting does.
  20. The correct answer is B (Convene both to work through the disagreement using an agreed resolution process): conflict resolution is a core collaborative competency rather than an unfortunate necessity, and an unresolved rift that has already degraded documentation will not repair itself. Waiting leaves a patient's plan split between two professionals who are not reading each other. Removing one profession from the patient's care sacrifices needed expertise to avoid a conversation. Escalating it as a professionalism complaint punishes the disagreement rather than resolving it.
  21. The correct answer is C (Staffing and the systems to coordinate them, which the plan omits entirely): surge capacity rests on staff, stuff, structure and systems, and this plan has addressed only the middle two, leaving nobody assigned and nothing to coordinate the response. More supplies deepen an element already covered. A second overflow site duplicates structure rather than filling the gap. Public messaging matters during an event but cannot substitute for the people delivering care and the systems directing them.
  22. The correct answer is A (Emergency scope provisions with defined roles, orientation and named clinical oversight): the staff element of surge capacity expands the workforce through recognized emergency provisions rather than informally, and defined roles with oversight keep the expansion safe. Waiving registration entirely removes the accountability that makes expanded practice defensible. Leaving task assignment to each site lead produces inconsistent practice across the response. Restricting vaccination to currently registered nurses protects standards but leaves the surge unmet.
  23. The correct answer is B (Follow the incident commander's assignment, which supersedes your usual reporting line): in a declared emergency the incident structure creates a single chain of command, and clinicians take direction from the role they have been assigned rather than from their everyday manager. Following the routine reporting line reintroduces the competing instructions the structure exists to eliminate. Splitting time between two masters leaves both tasks half covered at the worst moment. A regulatory college does not adjudicate operational assignments during a response.
  24. The correct answer is D (Treat the three with controllable bleeding and give the other comfort measures): mass casualty triage seeks the greatest good for the greatest number, so patients with catastrophic injury and negligible survival chance are designated expectant and receive comfort care while salvageable patients get the resources. Treating the sickest first is everyday practice and is precisely what this situation inverts. Dividing resources evenly produces four inadequate treatments. Using one of two ambulances for an unsurvivable injury removes transport from patients who would survive.
  25. The correct answer is C (Reassess all casualties at intervals and move patients between categories as needed): triage is continuous because patients change category, and a category assigned in the first minutes describes a moment rather than the whole event. Holding the original categories makes the sequence predictable and wrong. Relying on crews to flag deterioration catches only the patients someone happened to watch. Transporting strictly in tagging order sends stable patients ahead of those who have since deteriorated.
  26. The correct answer is B (Staffing, infection control and outbreak support planning for long term care): the Canadian pandemic experience showed that the largest share of deaths occurred in long term care homes whose staffing and outbreak capacity collapsed, a setting this draft ignores entirely. More ventilators extend a hospital focus that is already the plan's only content. Public messaging supports prevention but does not protect the most affected setting. Additional critical care training strengthens hospitals rather than the homes where deaths occurred.
  27. The correct answer is A (Declare downtime and notify staff and the site lead by contact tree): the plan opens with a declaration and a notification because every later step depends on the whole team knowing that downtime procedures are in force. Telephoning the pharmacy is required before any dose is given, but doing it while colleagues carry on as though the system is live leaves the rest of the clinic unprotected. Cancelling the day discards a plan written so that care can continue. Starting paper forms alone leaves notification to chance.
  28. The correct answer is C (Arrange air resupply and take home dosing arrangements with the pharmacy): continuity of supply for clients on opioid agonist therapy and insulin is a core part of business continuity, and an isolated community needs both a resupply route and a dosing arrangement that does not require daily attendance. Reducing doses to stretch stock destabilizes treatment and risks relapse and hyperglycemia. Advising clients to leave displaces people who may have nowhere to go. Waiting and treating withdrawal accepts a preventable interruption.
  29. The correct answer is D (Allocation decisions should rest with a triage team separate from treating clinicians): a defensible framework separates the person deciding who receives a scarce resource from the person at the bedside, which protects both the patient relationship and the consistency of the decision. Questioning attending physicians' competence misidentifies the problem, which is role conflict rather than skill. Predicting family reactions is speculative and not the structural flaw. A ministry cannot make individual allocation decisions in real time.
  30. The correct answer is B (Remove the social worth criteria and add an appeal route for decisions): defensible allocation frameworks rest on published clinical criteria set in advance, with equity safeguards ensuring that social worth, disability or age is not the deciding factor, and with a route of appeal. Publishing criteria and requiring unanimity leaves the objectionable factors in place. Adding age as a tiebreaker introduces exactly the criterion the safeguards exclude. Family selected decision makers belong to individual care decisions rather than population level allocation.
  31. The correct answer is A (Follow the protocol, explain it honestly and monitor him for any deterioration): under scarcity, allocation rests on transparent criteria set in advance rather than on individual bedside exceptions, and the clinician's obligation is to apply them openly while continuing to watch the patient. Making a private exception advantages the patient who complains over patients with equal or greater need. Referring anyway shifts the same decision to a colleague without changing the constraint. Refusing to discuss it removes the transparency the framework depends on.
Case C067

Marcel is 58 and was diagnosed with type 2 diabetes eight days ago at a walk in clinic.

He drives a commercial truck across Ontario four days a week and is worried about losing his licence.

He was started on metformin, has not filled the prescription, and says he already knows what diabetes is because his brother has it.

He arrives at your clinic for a first appointment with 20 minutes booked and no family present.

  1. The correct answer is C (Ask what worries him most about diabetes and his commercial driving licence): adults are problem centred, and new learning attaches to a live personal concern rather than to a curriculum. His licence is the hook that gives hypoglycemia and medication teaching real stakes for him. Opening with pathophysiology is subject centred and will slide off a learner watching the clock. Complication warnings and a booklet with a class booking both push content before anyone has established what he actually needs.
  2. The correct answer is A (His existing experience carries beliefs that must be surfaced and corrected first): adults filter every new message through prior experience, and here that experience predicts amputation, blindness and needles. Until those specific beliefs are named and addressed, any teaching about metformin lands on top of a fear that the diagnosis is a sentence. Treating his knowledge as adequate mistakes familiarity for accuracy. Nothing in the question indicates denial, and using his brother's outcome to create urgency would deepen the fear that is already blocking him.
  3. The correct answer is D (Have him perform the whole test himself now while you observe): experiential learning requires the learner to do the task, get feedback, and try again, and manual accuracy is never predicted by verbal accuracy. Watching him lance, load the strip and read the result exposes errors that no description would reveal. A pamphlet and a spoken walkthrough both leave the first real attempt unsupervised on a highway. A video sent home defers the skill past the point where he needs it.
  4. The correct answer is B (He brings a completed glucose log showing testing before each long drive): level three is behaviour, meaning observable change in what the person does after the teaching. A completed log is direct evidence of an action taken outside the clinic room. A satisfaction rating is reaction, the weakest level, and a correct verbal statement of the hypoglycemia threshold is learning. A falling A1C with no emergency visits is a results measure, which sits one level higher and takes longer to attribute.
Case C068

Doris is 79 and lives alone in a small town. She has heart failure and was discharged four days ago on a new diuretic and a revised medication list.

At today's visit she says she forgot her glasses when you hand her the discharge summary, and mentions she will show the papers to her daughter on Sunday.

Her pill organizer is empty for the new medication. She nods and smiles throughout your explanation and tells you everything is fine.

  1. The correct answer is B (She deferred the written material to her daughter and cited absent glasses): those are the classic indirect signals of concealed limited literacy, because the person avoids reading in front of a professional and moves the task to a trusted family member. Living alone and advanced age are risk markers in a population, not behavioural evidence in one person. Absence of breathlessness says nothing about her ability to use written health information. Roughly 88 percent of Canadians over 65 score below the level considered adequate, so this pattern deserves weight.
  2. The correct answer is D (Deliver all education in plain language for every client by default): this is the universal precautions approach, and it works because clinicians cannot reliably identify who struggles and clients actively conceal it. Teaching everyone at a level that works for limited literacy removes both the guessing and the shame. Formal screening consumes visit time, misses people who answer defensively, and labels the client rather than fixing the material. Waiting for visible difficulty guarantees that the best concealers, who are often at highest risk, are missed.
  3. The correct answer is A (I want to check I explained this clearly, so tell me your plan): teach back places responsibility on the clarity of the teaching, not on the intelligence of the learner, and it asks for the plan in the client's own words. Framed this way the technique carries no shame and clients accept it readily. Confirming a fact you have just supplied invites agreement and proves nothing. Asking whether she understands or has questions are closed questions that a client who is embarrassed, or who does not know what she does not know, will answer yes to.
  4. The correct answer is C (Explain the weight rule a different way, then check her understanding again): the teach back loop is only complete when the gap is re taught differently and rechecked, using a drawing, a demonstration or another comparison. A failed teach back means the explanation failed, so repeating identical words more slowly reproduces the same failure. Deferring a safety critical rule to the next visit leaves her without the trigger for early action on fluid overload. Handing the rule to her daughter outsources the learning and does not confirm that Doris herself can act.
Case C069

You precept a final year nurse practitioner student in a rural family practice.

On her third day she sees a 42 year old woman with three weeks of fatigue, presents the case to you in eleven minutes with every detail in the order she collected it, and ends by asking what you would like her to order.

She apologizes twice for being slow. Four more clients wait, and you have nine minutes before your next appointment.

  1.
