The correct answer is A (Ask her what she thinks is causing this woman's fatigue): the first step is getting a commitment, because a learner who states a position before hearing yours reveals her reasoning and becomes invested in testing it. Asking for a shorter summary addresses presentation style but still leaves her without a formulation. Supplying the differential yourself removes the very step that makes the model work. Jumping to investigations skips diagnostic reasoning and lets her order tests without a hypothesis to justify them.
  2. The correct answer is C (Ask how the heavy menstrual bleeding fits the picture she has described): probing for supporting evidence follows the commitment, and a question that points at the neglected finding lets the learner correct her own reasoning. That produces durable learning and preserves her willingness to commit next time. Ordering the tests yourself teaches her nothing about why they are indicated. Naming the miss flatly is correction without reasoning, and delivering a general rule now arrives before you know where her thinking actually broke down.
  3. The correct answer is B (Presentations ran past ten minutes today, so try leading with your impression): effective feedback is timely, specific, focused on observable behaviour, and paired with a concrete plan the learner can apply at the next encounter. Naming a duration and a replacement behaviour gives her something to do tomorrow morning. Telling her to work on presentations or read a chapter identifies a deficit without a usable action. A vague reassurance softened by a general suggestion is comfortable to give and impossible to act on, and simply timing her adds measurement without instruction.
  4. The correct answer is D (Voice your own uncertainties aloud and separate coaching from formal evaluation): hidden uncertainty is a safety risk, and it persists whenever a learner believes that admitting a gap will be scored against her. Modelling your own knowledge gaps normalizes asking, and separating day to day coaching from the formal grade removes the reason to conceal. Telling her that safety outweighs image restates the duty she already knows and adds shame. Extra dosing review treats one knowledge gap, and a general request to staff does not change the evaluation pressure driving the behaviour.
Case C070

You work at a nursing station in a fly in First Nation community of about 900 people.

Rates of untreated dental decay in preschool children are high, and the regional health authority has sent 400 glossy pamphlets on brushing, printed in English, showing an urban family beside a grocery shelf of fresh produce.

The community has one store, intermittent satellite internet, and an active health committee that includes two Elders and a community health representative.

  1. The correct answer is D (Bring the issue to the health committee and ask them to set priorities): community led education, where the community defines the problem and the format, consistently outperforms programs designed elsewhere and shipped in. The committee includes Elders and a community health representative who know what families face at the one store. Rewriting the reading level improves a resource nobody asked for. Distributing pamphlets to generate a count measures paper, not reach, and a clinician designed questionnaire is still an outside agenda entering the community first.
  2. The correct answer is A (Short in person demonstrations at the feast plus printed one page reminders): any plan that depends on continuous connectivity fails where broadband is intermittent, so design for offline use and for the gathering places the community itself named. Demonstration also suits a manual skill better than any text does. Streaming video and an online logging portal both collapse when the satellite link drops in the evening. Text reminders assume reliable cellular service and deliver prompts without ever teaching the technique.
  3. The correct answer is C (Rebuild the content with community members using local foods and images): cultural and linguistic appropriateness has to be designed in from the start with the target group, not patched onto material built elsewhere. Food examples that reflect what is actually on the shelf, and images of people learners recognize, are what make a resource credible. Translating or simplifying the same unusable content keeps the mismatch and only changes its wording. Explaining why the pictured foods are absent draws attention to the flaw without giving families anything they can act on.
  4. The correct answer is B (Proportion of preschool children with a completed dental screening this year): a screening rate is a population level uptake measure drawn from nursing station records, and it reflects what families did rather than what they thought or recalled, with the indicator and its release to the authority agreed by the health committee. Attendance combined with a usefulness rating is reaction, the weakest level of evidence. A count of kits and pamphlets measures distribution effort and says nothing about reach or use. An end of session quiz captures short term learning that often fades before any behaviour changes.
  5. The correct answer is A (Answer her question, give two written points, and telephone within 48 hours): readiness to learn collapses after bad news, and most of what follows a diagnosis like this is not retained no matter how well it is delivered. Answering the question she actually asked respects where her attention is, and two anchor points plus early follow up contact fit what she can hold today. Pushing through staging and surgical options fills the visit without producing learning. Sending her away with nothing at all leaves her days with no information and no contact point.
  6. The correct answer is B (Arrange time alone and state what stays private and what does not): Ontario's Health Care Consent Act sets no minimum age for consent and turns on capacity rather than years, so a young person who understands the nature and consequences of care can consent, and the teaching needs private time plus an honest boundary on confidentiality. Naming the limits, such as disclosures of harm, protects the relationship. Teaching in front of a parent who has not been invited into that conversation usually ends it. A blanket promise of secrecy will be broken the first time reporting is required, and a pamphlet with a return invitation postpones a need she raised today.
  7. The correct answer is D (About 1 in 100 people who take this medicine get this problem): plain language gives risk as a natural frequency, uses everyday words, and keeps the sentence short and active. Readers estimate risk far more accurately from a count out of a hundred than from a percentage. Both percentage phrasings carry technical vocabulary such as incidence and probability, and percentages are poorly understood by many adults. Vague terms like uncommon and small minority give no number at all and are interpreted very differently by different readers.
  8. The correct answer is C (The formula measures word and sentence length only, so test it with clients): readability formulas count syllables and sentence length and cannot judge layout, sequencing, images, cultural fit, or whether the actions are findable. Three real misuse events are direct evidence that something the formula cannot see is failing. Blaming the clients ignores the universal precautions principle that unusable material is the clinician's problem. A second formula would measure the same two variables again, and dropping the grade level shortens words without addressing structure or design.
  9. The correct answer is C (Ask him to dial and give the dose himself while you watch): teach back is not show back, and verbal accuracy does not predict manual accuracy for any device, injection or dressing. A return demonstration is the only way to see whether he can prime the pen, hold the needle in long enough and read the dial. An illustrated guide and a support number both assume competence that has not been observed. A home visit may help later but leaves the first dose unsupervised, and hypoglycemia teaching is necessary without being the immediate gap.
  10. The correct answer is A (Replace technical terms with everyday words and lead with the required actions): the draft opens with clotting factor synthesis and monitoring language, buries what the reader must actually do, and uses words like anticoagulant, refractory and surveillance that most readers cannot decode. Plain language leads with the action and puts background second. Adding brand and coverage detail lengthens an already dense document. A longer food list adds reading burden, and larger print on matte paper improves legibility of text that still cannot be understood.
  11. The correct answer is B (Ask what he read and what worries him most about this vaccine): the relationship preserving sequence starts by finding out what the person actually encountered and naming the concern underneath it, which is usually a legitimate worry about safety or about being dismissed. Only after that does asking permission to share the Canadian evidence have any chance of being heard. Leading with the retraction or with measles mortality is flat contradiction, which hardens belief and ends the conversation. Handing over a guideline summary and rebooking avoids the discussion he came prepared to have.
  12. The correct answer is D (Note which parts apply here and discuss the Canadian screening recommendation): most online health material a Canadian client finds is American, and screening intervals, drug availability and coverage advice all differ, so the useful move is to separate what still applies from what does not. That respects the effort he put into reading and keeps him bringing material to you. Dismissing the source as unreliable teaches him to stop sharing what he reads. Ordering a test purely on request skips shared decision making, and attributing the decision to provincial programs deflects a conversation he is entitled to have with you.
  13. The correct answer is A (Use the telephone interpreter for the teaching and document that it was used): professional interpretation is the standard for teaching that carries safety consequences, and telephone or video interpretation is an accepted alternative where in person service is unavailable. Family interpreters filter, soften and omit, particularly around prognosis and symptoms that would worry a parent. A stated preference does not remove the accuracy problem, though the son can still be present with her permission. Written translation alone leaves no chance to check understanding, and splitting the visit between the two applies the standard inconsistently.
  14. The correct answer is D (Participants will demonstrate spacer technique and state two reliever overuse signs): measurable objectives use observable verbs such as demonstrate, state, list and choose, and this one specifies both the action and the quantity to be produced. Naming a quantity, two signs, also tells an evaluator exactly what counts as success at the end of the hour. Understand, appreciate and know describe internal states that nobody can observe or score, so a manager reviewing the session has no way to tell whether they occurred. Rewriting each of those as a demonstration or a statement is what turns an intention into an evaluable objective.
  15. The correct answer is C (Proportion of participants bringing completed glucose logs to the next visit): behaviour is the third Kirkpatrick level and asks whether people did anything differently, measured by things like refill records, attendance, self monitoring logs, or observed technique. Evaluation forms rating the facilitator sit at reaction, the weakest level. Quiz scores capture learning and often fade without translating into action. A twelve month A1C change is a results measure, harder to attribute to the program and too slow to guide a decision about running the next group.
  16. The correct answer is B (Ask invited clients who did not attend what prevented them from coming): evaluation covers the delivery method, not only the learner, and attendance of 15 percent points at scheduling, transport, childcare or cost rather than at motivation. The people who can answer that question are the ones who stayed away. Abandoning the group or adding reminder calls both commit resources to a guess about the cause. Continuing on the strength of satisfaction ratings from three people mistakes reaction data from a self selected minority for evidence that the format works.
  17. The correct answer is C (Ask permission to raise it, state your concern briefly, leave the door open): matching the intervention to the stage of change is what matters, and a person in precontemplation responds to pressure with resistance. Asking permission preserves autonomy, a brief statement of concern plants the issue, and leaving the door open keeps a later conversation possible. Handing over a pamphlet or a quitline number treats him as though he were preparing to act. Reciting risks is the lecture he has already refused, and prescribing replacement therapy assumes a quit attempt he has not agreed to.
  18. The correct answer is A (Reflect her concerns back and ask what she sees as her options): the righting reflex is the urge to argue the other side, and it reliably pushes the client into defending the position she started with. Reflective listening followed by an open question lets her voice her own change talk instead. Answering each objection with evidence is exactly the argument that generates counter argument. Conceding and deferring abandons the conversation, and warning that the untreated risk is greater is the same correction delivered as a threat.
  19. The correct answer is D (Agree on one small daily step he can succeed at right now): self efficacy is the belief that one can perform a specific action, it predicts adherence better than knowledge does, and it is built by mastery experiences broken down small enough to succeed at. Naming past successes and booking a follow up where success gets recognized strengthens it further. A twelve week program with weekly targets offers a fourth opportunity to fail. Repeating the evidence adds knowledge he already has, and a referral three months out delays any experience of success.
  20. The correct answer is B (Turn off the fan, close the door, and check his hearing aids): older adults with sensory change need the environment adjusted before the content is adjusted, and background noise plus a possibly flat hearing aid battery explains the whole difficulty. Facing him at a normal pace matters, but shouting distorts speech sounds and adds nothing once the noise is removed. Large print helps a vision problem, which is not what is happening here. Rebooking with family postpones a fixable barrier and treats a hearing issue as a comprehension issue.
  21. The correct answer is A (Teach the child directly with demonstration, then confirm the plan with her mother): a school aged child can learn what a trigger is, use a spacer with a return demonstration, and say when a puffer is not helping, so she should be included rather than talked over. Confirming the plan with the parent afterward keeps the caregiver informed and responsible. Teaching only the mother wastes the child's real capacity and leaves her unable to act at school. Permanent supervision of every dose removes her developing independence, and excluding the parent leaves nobody supporting the plan at home.
  22. The correct answer is C (Address safe sleep position and surface, then ask what she has been told): when teaching a parent, limit each contact to about three messages and prioritize the ones that prevent death, and prone sleep on a soft surface is an immediate risk that has already been described in this home. Asking what she has been told matters because you are correcting an established family practice, not teaching into empty space. Sleep patterns, feeding and vitamin D are routine content that can wait a visit. The Rourke schedule and booking are administrative and do not touch the hazard.
  23. The correct answer is B (Captions on the video and a printed version of the same steps): accessibility is a design requirement rather than an add on, and captions serve people with hearing loss while a printed equivalent serves those without a device, without data, or with connectivity too poor to stream. Together they cover the two largest access barriers for this resource. A transcript alone assumes the person is already online and able to find it. Alternative narration speeds address neither hearing loss nor connectivity, and a telephone number routes people to staff instead of giving them the content.
  24. The correct answer is D (Teach her to check funding, sources, update date, and Canadian relevance): contact time is short, so teaching the client how to judge information extends your reach far beyond the visit. Who runs the site, how it is funded, when it was updated, whether it cites sources, whether it is selling something, and whether it is Canadian are the questions that separate usable material from marketing. Telling her to clear everything through you keeps her dependent on appointments. Telling her to stay offline will be ignored, and a blanket warning about American content gives her no way to evaluate anything herself.
  25. The correct answer is C (A group session combining a demonstration with structured peer discussion): the medium should fit the content, the learner and the setting, and groups are the format that delivers peer support while a demonstration handles the skill of reading and responding to a log. One session also uses far less clinician time than twenty appointments. Individual visits address the skill but not the isolation and consume the time you do not have. A mailed plan offers no demonstration or contact, and a video with a quiz tests recall while leaving people just as alone.
  26. The correct answer is A (Offer the app with a paper log alternative and confirm usability): the limits of digital tools are equity limits, since older adults, low income households and remote communities may lack devices, data or connectivity, so a non digital equivalent must always be available. Confirming that the person can actually use whichever option they choose is the step clinicians skip. Enrolling everyone assumes access nobody has verified. Excluding clients by age is both inaccurate and discriminatory, and delaying the whole rollout withholds a useful tool from those who can use it today.
  27. The correct answer is D (Arrange interpretation and give plain language material he can actually use): a 24 page English booklet and an online module are unusable for a man whose first language is Punjabi and who reports leaving school early, so nothing else in the plan can function until that is fixed. Professional interpretation, never a family member, plus material at a suitable level is the precondition for every other element. Measurable objectives and a teach back check are genuine improvements that still depend on content he can access. A follow up call in a week would repeat the same barrier by telephone.
  28. The correct answer is B (Arrange service in French, since the province is officially bilingual): New Brunswick is the only officially bilingual province and the obligation to offer services in both official languages is constitutional there, so arranging French service, including on a day when a French speaking colleague works, is the requirement rather than a courtesy. An informal verbal translation by an English speaking clinician is neither accurate nor documented. Asking family to translate abandons the professional interpretation standard. Sending her elsewhere transfers your obligation and adds travel she should not need.
  29. The correct answer is A (Ask the organization to define the priority and shape the program with you): community led education, where the community sets the priority and the format, consistently outperforms imported programs, and partnership with community organizations, Elders and community health representatives is the posture the Truth and Reconciliation Calls to Action point toward. Running the curriculum first and collecting feedback afterward makes the community a test audience for someone else's plan. Swapping images and food examples is cosmetic adaptation of an outside agenda. Your own training is worthwhile but does not transfer any decision making to the community.
  30. The correct answer is C (Safety is judged by those receiving care, not declared by the provider): cultural safety is defined by the person experiencing the encounter, so advertising a space as safe does not make it so and the participants' discomfort is the only measure that counts. Given residential school and hospital histories, the useful question is what would make this feel safe rather than an assumption that the setting is neutral. Blaming the venue or the participants' preparation shifts responsibility away from how the session was run. Adapting screening intervals addresses content when the problem reported was the experience.
  31. The correct answer is D (Confirm return demonstration and brief the community health nurse before he flies): a manual skill needs observed performance rather than recall, and clients travelling for care need the teaching to reach the people who will support them at home. Handing over to the community health nurse creates local backup where no in person service otherwise exists for three weeks. A virtual appointment next month is too late and depends on connectivity that may not hold. A booklet and a support line give no confirmation of technique, and verbal recall does not predict whether he can change the appliance.
  32. The correct answer is C (Note that satisfaction is the weakest level and suggest measuring learning next): reaction data tells you people enjoyed the session, and people can enjoy a session and learn nothing at all, so the next practical step is a teach back, return demonstration or short pre and post check. Treating satisfaction as a predictor of behaviour change is precisely the error the model exists to prevent. Repeating with a larger group collects more of the same weak evidence. Demanding outcome data before running the session again sets a bar most clinic projects cannot clear and stalls a promising program.
  33. The correct answer is B (Teach the two most safety critical items, then schedule a second visit): chunking means delivering one piece, confirming understanding, then moving on, and repeating across visits works better than piling everything into one session for a learner who is already saturated. Prioritizing by safety keeps the decision with the clinician who knows which gap could cause harm. Covering all four topics fills the appointment and produces almost no retention. Letting her choose what to drop risks losing a safety critical item, and a referral leaves her unsupported until that clinic can see her.
  34. The correct answer is A (Involving the target group in identifying needs and shaping the content): a resource built from a clinician's assumption about what people need is the most common failure in educational design, and a needs assessment asks what learners already know, what they believe, what the real barriers are and what they say they want. Involving the target group also fixes food examples, images and family decision making assumptions at the same time. Near zero uptake is the signature of a resource nobody in the neighbourhood was asked about. Reading level, translation and objectives are all real requirements, but each of them refines content whose entire premise was never checked with the people it was for.
  35. The correct answer is D (Ask what he knows, offer the evidence neutrally, ask what he thinks): the technique starts by finding out what the person already believes, offers information without persuasion attached, and then hands interpretation back to them, which keeps the conversation collaborative rather than instructional. The closing question is what converts information into the client's own reasoning. Leading with the evidence and then repeating it is a lecture with a pause in the middle. Goal setting is a later step, and handing over reading material defers the exchange entirely.
  36. The correct answer is B (Run a simulated event where she locates and prepares the epinephrine): correct verbal answers do not predict performance under time pressure, and a simulation tests whether she can find the ampoule or autoinjector in this clinic, draw the right volume and deliver it into the anterolateral thigh. It also reveals system problems such as a kit stored where nobody can reach it. Case discussion builds reasoning without testing execution. A signed acknowledgement documents that she read the policy, and a later quiz measures retained knowledge rather than skill.
  37. The correct answer is C (Acknowledge her concern, ask permission, then state briefly what the evidence shows): the underlying worry here is legitimate, and naming it before asking permission to share information keeps the relationship intact so a second conversation remains possible. Correcting briefly, without restating the false claim, avoids reinforcing the very message you want to displace. Repeating the claim first strengthens its familiarity in memory. Leading with the risk of stopping is a counter argument that invites defence, and asking her to restart and self monitor bypasses her concern altogether.
  38. The correct answer is A (Adherence is a motivation problem, so explore barriers rather than repeat teaching): information changes knowledge and rarely changes behaviour on its own, so a client who knows the facts, performs the skill correctly and still does not act is telling you the gap is motivational or practical, such as cost, beliefs about daily medication, or readiness. Motivational interviewing and a barrier discussion fit that gap. Twice yearly refills of a controller are a behaviour finding, not a knowledge finding. Reinforcing knowledge he demonstrably has, rechecking a technique already confirmed correct, and reissuing a plan he can already recite all repeat education that has done its job.
Case C071

You are a nurse practitioner at an urban community health centre.

A Cree woman aged 34 attends for follow up of poorly controlled asthma. She missed her last two appointments. At today's visit she is quiet and gives short answers. Near the end she says the previous clinician told her the plan would work if she simply followed instructions, and that she felt spoken down to. Your colleague insists the encounter was respectful because nothing rude was said.

  1. The correct answer is C (The client's experience of the encounter determines that it was not culturally safe): cultural safety is assessed by the person receiving care, not by the provider who delivered it. Unsafe cultural practice is any action that demeans, diminishes or disempowers a person's cultural identity, and feeling spoken down to and stripped of any say meets that description. The absence of rude language speaks to the clinician's intent, which is not the measure. Framing the missed doses as non adherence relocates the problem into the client and leaves the encounter unexamined.
  2. The correct answer is A (Self certification of cultural safety by the provider, which the concept excludes): declaring your own care culturally safe reverses the direction of the assessment, which belongs to the client. Documentation records what the clinician believes happened and cannot substitute for the client's experience of the encounter. Cultural humility would involve examining his own assumptions and the power he held, not defending the interaction. Intent does not neutralize harm, because the standard is the effect on the person receiving care.
  3. The correct answer is D (Acknowledge what she describes and ask what she wants from today's visit): naming what she has told you and handing her the agenda begins to redistribute the power in the room. Defending the previous clinician asks her to manage your discomfort and confirms that her account will not be taken seriously. Restating the same plan repeats the behaviour that led her to stop the inhaler. Passing her to a liaison worker outsources a relationship that is yours to repair.
  4. The correct answer is B (Bring the pattern to the centre as aggregate data and propose changes): a recurring barrier described by several clients is an organizational problem, and organizational advocacy is realistic entry level work. Completing a module changes nothing for the clients already leaving and treats a system problem as a personal knowledge gap. Requiring written complaints puts the burden of system change on the people with the least power. Concentrating Indigenous clients with one clinician relieves the rest of the team of any obligation to change.
Case C072

A 41 year old man with a documented history of opioid use disorder on methadone presents to your rural clinic with two days of severe right flank pain and vomiting.

The triage note reads drug seeking, wants something for pain. You are three hours behind and have not eaten. His temperature is 38.4 degrees Celsius and he appears unwell.

  1. The correct answer is A (Diagnostic overshadowing, where new symptoms are attributed to the known label): a new presentation is read as an extension of the recorded diagnosis rather than investigated on its own merits. Recording a relevant substance use history is clinically appropriate and is not a privacy breach; the problem is the judgmental framing attached to it. The documented pattern for clients who use substances is undertreatment and underinvestigation, not the reverse. Medication reconciliation matters but does not explain the risk this particular note creates.
  2. The correct answer is C (Your time pressure, fatigue and the ambiguity of undifferentiated flank pain): implicit bias operates hardest when decisions are made quickly with incomplete data and depleted attention. The recorded diagnosis is information; what makes it dangerous is the conditions under which you are interpreting it. Resource scarcity limits what you can order but does not explain a snap judgment made before any assessment. Holding egalitarian values offers no protection, because the associations that drive the error operate outside stated beliefs.
  3. The correct answer is B (Slow down and ask whether you would investigate a housed, employed client identically): running the counterfactual at the decision point is a structural check that works in real time, particularly when you feel certain. Taking a test after clinic does nothing for the client in front of you and stopping there is a documented weak intervention. Colour blindness suppresses the very information needed to notice differential treatment. Handing the client to a colleague avoids your own reasoning rather than correcting it, and in a rural clinic may not be possible.
  4. The correct answer is D (Document objective findings and the reasoning behind the investigations you ordered): objective, reasoned documentation gives the receiving team usable information without transmitting a judgment. Stigmatizing labels travel forward and lower the quality of care the next clinician provides, which is exactly what happened at triage. Deleting the opioid agonist therapy history would be clinically unsafe, since methadone affects analgesic planning and interactions. Reproducing the triage wording preserves the bias in the permanent record under your signature.
Case C073

You practise in a northern nursing station serving a First Nations community.

A four year old girl with cerebral palsy needs a specialized feeding pump and in home nursing support. The regional health authority states the family must first establish whether federal or provincial funding applies, and estimates the review will take several months. The mother tells you her daughter is losing weight while she waits.

  1. The correct answer is D (Jordan's Principle, so the government of first contact pays and resolves later): it is a child first principle named for Jordan River Anderson of Norway House Cree Nation, who died in hospital while governments argued over payment for his home based care. The service is provided first and the jurisdictional dispute is settled afterward, and it has been affirmed through Canadian Human Rights Tribunal rulings. The Indian Act governs status, land and governance rather than creating a funding guarantee for this equipment. Joyce's Principle addresses discrimination in service delivery, which is a different problem from a jurisdictional funding gap.
  2. The correct answer is B (The service proceeds now and the funding dispute is settled afterward): that sequencing is the whole point of a child first principle, and delay while responsibility is argued is the harm it exists to prevent. Residence on or off reserve does not determine eligibility for a First Nations child. Nor is the principle limited to services a province already insures, since that restriction would recreate the gap it was designed to close. Requiring the family to exhaust an appeal process first inverts the principle and pushes the burden onto the least powerful party.
  3. The correct answer is A (Initiate a Jordan's Principle request now and start the feeding support): the clinician's obligation is to know how to initiate a request and to avoid delaying a needed service while funding is sorted out. Advising the family to wait accepts the exact delay the principle prohibits and the child is already losing weight. Admitting her hundreds of kilometres away separates a young child from family for a problem that can be solved at home. Asking the governments to clarify jurisdiction continues the dispute rather than bypassing it.
  4. The correct answer is C (Raise the recurring delay with the authority and propose a process change): four families receiving identical wrong instructions is a defective process, and the pattern is the thing to fix. Continuing to solve each case one at a time helps those children while leaving the cause intact for the next family. Sending families to their member of parliament transfers the work to people already managing complex care at home. Regulatory colleges oversee individual registrants, not a health authority's funding process, so that complaint would go nowhere.
Case C074

An Atikamekw woman is admitted overnight to the small hospital where you round.

In the morning she tells you that a staff member commented on her weight and questioned whether her pain was real, and that she recorded part of it on her phone. She wants to leave against advice. Her abdominal pain is undiagnosed and she has a fever of 38.6 degrees Celsius.

  1. The correct answer is B (Believe her account, address her immediate safety, and keep her care available): a client who has just described degrading treatment needs to be believed and to see something change before she can safely accept care. Moving straight to a refusal form treats her departure as inevitable and repeats the message that her account does not matter. Demanding she delete the recording protects the institution and takes away the only evidence she holds. Investigating the staff member's version is necessary later but is not the priority while she has a fever and undiagnosed abdominal pain.
  2. The correct answer is D (Joyce's Principle, asserting equitable access to health services without discrimination): it arose after the death of Joyce Echaquan, an Atikamekw woman who died in a Quebec hospital in 2020 while recording staff mocking and degrading her, and it was put forward by the Atikamekw of Manawan and the Atikamekw Nation Council. It asserts the right to equitable access to health and social services without discrimination and to the highest attainable standard of health, including traditional medicines. The In Plain Sight report is an accurate description but investigated Indigenous specific racism in British Columbia specifically. Recruiting more Indigenous health professionals is a call to action directed at governments rather than a binding duty on a clinician, and aligning federal statutes with the Declaration is a legislative duty under the 2021 Act, so neither speaks as directly to this encounter.
  3. The correct answer is C (Seek an exception, since the policy creates an unequal burden here): a uniform visitor rule applied to a client who has just experienced racism on this unit produces a far worse outcome for her than for other patients, which is the difference between equality and equity. Making room for family and Elders is a concrete element of culturally safe practice. Excluding the Elder on the grounds that the role is not clinical imposes the institution's definition of who matters. Waiting for a manager leaves her without support through the night and effectively decides against her.
  4. The correct answer is A (Report the incident through the organization's process and request a systemic review): naming the event inside the organization and asking for a review of the pattern is what makes change possible beyond this admission. Apologizing personally matters but leaves the unit exactly as it was and answers her question with nothing. Sending her to police reframes a health system failure as a criminal matter and puts the work back on her. Waiting for a written complaint makes redress conditional on the effort of the person who was harmed.
  5. The correct answer is D (Competence implies an endpoint and leaves the power imbalance unexamined): competence frames culture as a body of knowledge that can be acquired and completed, which invites checklist thinking about what particular clients believe. Cultural safety instead shifts attention to the client's experience and to the power relationship inside the encounter. Lengthening the course or repeating it annually keeps the same flawed model and simply delivers more of it. Changing who teaches the content does not change what the content assumes about the nature of the problem.
  6. The correct answer is A (Cultural humility is a lifelong process of reflection, not a completed credential): there is no completion state, because assumptions and power operate in every encounter regardless of past education. Treating a course as an expiry dated certificate misses that the work is ongoing self examination rather than content delivery. Restricting reflection to unfamiliar cultures assumes bias only appears across obvious difference. An absence of complaints measures who felt able to complain, not whether care was experienced as safe.
  7. The correct answer is B (Ask what matters most to her and rebuild the plan around it): asking and then actually changing the plan is what redistributes power, whereas asking and proceeding unchanged is theatre. Simplified handouts and teach back both improve comprehension of a plan she had no part in setting. More time is useful but silence in a longer appointment is still silence if the dynamic does not change. The pattern across three visits points to the relationship rather than to a comprehension problem.
  8. The correct answer is B (It self certifies safety and confuses identical treatment with equitable care): the statement contains two errors, because the provider cannot declare his own care safe and sameness is not equity. Identical service delivered to people with different resources reliably produces unequal outcomes. Splitting the claim between clinical decisions and communication does not rescue it, since both are shaped by the same assumption. Complaint records reflect who felt safe enough to complain and cannot certify anything about the encounters themselves.
  9. The correct answer is C (Describe implicit bias as automatic associations that operate outside stated values): bias is not a synonym for prejudice, and treating the finding as an accusation of racism ends the conversation before any change is possible. The data show a differential outcome, which is not the same as proof of intent, so claiming intent is both inaccurate and guaranteed to escalate. Directing him to a self administered test and stopping there is a documented weak intervention. Removing him from analgesia decisions punishes an individual for a pattern the whole clinic produced.
  10. The correct answer is A (The language transfers bias to every clinician who later reads the chart): words like non compliant, difficult and drug seeking propagate an interpretation forward, which is how three clinicians came to write the same thing. The immediate risk is that new neurological symptoms will be read through that frame instead of investigated. Privacy legislation governs collection, use and disclosure rather than the tone of clinical language. Judgmental records remain admissible and in fact become evidence of how the client was treated.
  11. The correct answer is D (Apply defined imaging criteria and audit referral outcomes by client group): standardizing the decision removes the discretion where bias lives, and auditing by group detects patterns that recollection cannot. Awareness workshops raise knowledge but as a standalone measure do not reliably change behaviour. Colour blindness is counterproductive, because refusing to see race means refusing to see the differences the audit would reveal. Posting a values statement signals intent without altering any decision that gets made.
  12. The correct answer is C (Refusing to see race also means refusing to see racism and its outcomes): without disaggregated data, differential waits, referrals and outcomes remain invisible and therefore unfixable. Race is a social category rather than a biological predictor, and treating it as biology relocates the cause of inequity into the client's body. Data collection is governed by privacy and consent requirements but is not gated on ministry permission. Clinician recollection is a poor detector of population level patterns, which is precisely why the data are needed.
  13. The correct answer is B (Her wariness is an earned response to a documented pattern of child removal): residential schools, the Sixties Scoop and the ongoing overrepresentation of Indigenous children in care form a documented history of separation that entirely explains her reaction. Treating that response as an obstacle to be overridden repeats the dynamic that produced it. Distrust grounded in lived experience is not evidence of impaired capacity. The last federally supported school closed in 1996, within her lifetime, and the effects reach across generations regardless.
  14. The correct answer is A (The Indian Act's status provisions still shape eligibility for certain federal programs): first passed in 1876 and still in force in amended form, it gave the federal government control over identity, land, governance and family life, and its status rules continue to determine access to some benefits. It was not repealed; the United Nations Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples Act is separate legislation with a different purpose. Provinces deliver most health services, so federal law does not govern all care for Indigenous people. Residence matters for provincial insurance but does not by itself determine federal program eligibility.
  15. The correct answer is C (They call for closing measurable health outcome gaps with annual public reporting): the health related calls ask that gaps in health outcomes be identified and closed, with progress published, alongside recognizing Indigenous healing practices, increasing and retaining Indigenous health professionals and training all health professionals. Nothing in them requires wholesale transfer of provincial funding. Recognizing the value of Indigenous healing practices means working with Indigenous healers and Elders, not certifying every clinician in a practice that is not theirs. The calls are addressed broadly across governments and institutions, including provincial health systems and schools.
  16. The correct answer is D (The recorded vital signs, which are inconsistent with the tone of the notes): hypotension, tachycardia, fever and tachypnoea in a client with severe abdominal pain describe possible sepsis and demand resuscitation now, whatever the notes imply about her motives. The framing in the record is exactly how bias delays care, and the objective data contradict it. Reviewing a dispensing history is a reasonable later step but does nothing about the physiology in front of you. Frequent attendance and deferred reassessment are real quality concerns yet neither is the finding that changes management in the next five minutes.
  17. The correct answer is A (It found widespread systemic Indigenous specific racism in British Columbia health care): the review was led by Dr. Mary Ellen Turpel Lafond, drew on input from roughly 9,000 people and issued 24 recommendations. It described racism as systemic rather than the conduct of a few individuals, which is the finding that makes organizational change necessary. Its scope was the British Columbia health system, not federal nursing stations in the territories. It documented that a high proportion of Indigenous respondents experienced discrimination that discouraged them from seeking care.
  18. The correct answer is D (It requires alignment of federal laws with the Declaration and an action plan): the federal Act obliges the Government of Canada to take measures to bring federal laws into consistency with the Declaration and to develop an action plan to achieve its objectives. British Columbia enacted its own legislation to the same end in 2019, which is why the two are often mentioned together. The Act does not transfer jurisdiction over health services, nor does it immediately bind provincial authorities as directly enforceable law. Training requirements for individual professionals come from regulators and employers rather than from this statute.
  19. The correct answer is B (Ask about the medicine, discuss any interactions, and support both approaches): traditional medicine is a recognized right rather than an obstacle, and the clinical task is to understand what she is taking and watch for genuine interactions. Making continued prescribing conditional on stopping converts your prescribing power into leverage over her cultural practice. Recording her use of a healer as a refusal of care misstates what is happening, since her control is at target. Disclaiming responsibility abandons her while appearing to respect her choice.
  20. The correct answer is C (Collective decision making is his autonomous choice and should be accommodated): choosing who participates in a decision is an exercise of autonomy, not a surrender of it. Insisting on private individual decision making imposes one cultural model of the good patient and disempowers him. Wanting family and an Elder present is not evidence of impaired capacity and does not trigger a formal assessment. He remains the decision maker, so treating his daughter as a substitute would be both clinically and legally wrong while he has capacity.
  21. The correct answer is C (Arrange professional interpretation and continue the visit with that service): professional interpretation is the standard, and a child should never interpret, least of all for a sexual health and safety history. Consent given in front of the only available interpreter is not freely given, and the son's presence makes honest disclosure about violence impossible. Sending her away for another family member adds a barrier and keeps the disclosure problem intact. Written exchange assumes literacy in Tigrinya and removes the ability to ask follow up questions safely.
  22. The correct answer is A (Name the comment as stereotyping at the time and explain the clinical harm): being the person who names a colleague's stereotyping rather than letting it pass is an explicit element of building an inclusive practice environment. Left unchallenged in front of the team, the comment becomes the shared understanding that shapes how those clients are assessed. Complaining to the college is disproportionate as a first response and does nothing about the team norm today. Deferring to an education day months away, or speaking only to bystanders, leaves the statement standing.
  23. The correct answer is D (Describing oneself as having no culture is itself a marker of dominance): clinicians from dominant groups often experience their own norms as neutral, which is exactly what makes those norms invisible to them. Her assumptions about punctuality, who decides and how pain should be expressed are cultural and will shape every assessment she makes. Being born in Canada does not remove culture; it supplies one. Excluding cultural factors from clinical reasoning is not possible, since they are already operating in her judgments about what a good patient looks like.
  24. The correct answer is B (The finding matches a documented pattern of undertreated pain in racialized clients): systematic undertreatment of pain in Black, Indigenous and other racialized clients is one of the best documented effects of implicit bias in health care. Attributing the gap to different pain reporting relocates the cause into the clients and ignores that pain scores were comparable. Dismissing it as a coding artifact avoids the finding without testing it. Deliberate discrimination by a few individuals does not fit a pattern spread across a whole service, which is what makes it systemic rather than personal.
  25. The correct answer is A (Investigate for ischemic heart disease using the same criteria applied to men): women's cardiac symptoms are more often attributed to anxiety and investigated less aggressively, and applying identical criteria is the counterfactual check that corrects for it. Exertional fatigue with jaw discomfort and nausea is a recognized presentation of ischemia and warrants assessment now. Rescreening for anxiety before any cardiac workup repeats the previous clinician's ordering of priorities. Delayed follow up and reassurance both accept the anxiety explanation that has not been tested.
  26. The correct answer is C (Noticing that difference exists says nothing about how members are treated): cultural awareness is the shallowest level and coexists comfortably with stereotyping, which is why a poster changes nothing about the encounter. The initiative touches neither the power relationship nor the client's experience of care, so it cannot reach safety. Sensitivity sits above awareness but below safety on the continuum, so describing it as above safety inverts the order. Individual variation and language services are real concerns but neither explains why this sits at the bottom of the continuum.
  27. The correct answer is B (Ask openly what she would like you to know about her community and practices): asking rather than assuming leaves her in control of what is relevant and how much she wishes to share. Silence on the subject means her care is planned around your defaults and offers no opening to mention traditional practices. Studying generalized cultural content and applying it is competence thinking, which treats a diverse population as uniform. Sending a liaison worker to collect the information first makes her identity a data field rather than part of your relationship with her.
  28. The correct answer is D (Bias risk rises with time pressure and fatigue, regardless of stated values): implicit associations operate outside conscious awareness and often contradict what a person sincerely believes, so the risk is highest on the worst shift rather than in the worst person. Egalitarian values are not protective when decisions are made fast with incomplete data. Absence of a prior relationship removes personal history but leaves group level associations fully intact. Restricting bias to consciously prejudiced clinicians is precisely the misunderstanding that keeps teams from examining their own systems.
  29. The correct answer is C (Action 2, because identical treatment is not the same as culturally safe care): a public promise of sameness states the equality position as though it were an equity commitment, and it leaves the power relationship completely untouched. Disaggregated outcome reporting is one of the strongest elements here, since it detects patterns that recollection cannot. An advisory council with authority to require a response shifts real power toward the people affected rather than duplicating anything. The one time module is genuinely weak, but the flaw is that it treats safety as a completed credential rather than that the deadline is long.
  30. The correct answer is B (Connect the family with food, housing and Jordan's Principle supports first): the presenting problem is material deprivation, the parents are actively seeking help, and Indigenous children remain overrepresented in child welfare through exactly this pathway. Poverty is not itself neglect, so an automatic report misapplies the threshold while repeating a documented pattern of removal. Signed undertakings ask a family without money to solve a funding problem and create a record of failure when they cannot. Waiting for faltering growth allows preventable harm while help is available now.
  31. The correct answer is A (Recognize the value of Indigenous healing practices and solve the technical barrier): the health related Calls to Action ask institutions to recognize the value of Indigenous healing practices and use them in collaboration with Indigenous healers and Elders, which makes detection settings an engineering problem rather than a reason to refuse. Restricting ceremony to outdoors in a Canadian winter denies it in practice for the clients who are most unwell. Requiring a physician to certify a therapeutic indication puts a clinician in charge of approving a cultural and spiritual practice. Waiting for a provincial standard indefinitely defers a decision the hospital can make itself.
  32. The correct answer is A (Address retention conditions rather than only recruiting more Indigenous staff): the calls ask for both an increase in Indigenous health professionals and their retention in the communities they serve, and the exit interviews describe exactly the conditions that defeat retention. Replacing them with trained non Indigenous staff abandons the workforce commitment entirely. Funnelling all Indigenous clients to one nurse increases the very burden the interviews identified and excuses everyone else from responsibility. Pay matters, but treating turnover as inevitable accepts the isolation and lack of advancement as fixed.
  33. The correct answer is D (Negotiate a revised pace with her and document the shared reasoning): you hold the prescription pad, and addressing that imbalance means building the plan with her rather than delivering it to her. Explaining the evidence and proceeding unchanged asks for agreement without offering any influence. Sending the decision to a distant clinic removes your accountability and delays her care. Abandoning your clinical judgment entirely is not shared decision making either, since it withholds the expertise she came for.
  34. The correct answer is C (Investigate the new symptoms on their own merits without anchoring on psychosis): diagnostic overshadowing is the documented risk for clients with a known mental illness, and headache with vomiting and neck stiffness demands urgent assessment for meningitis or raised intracranial pressure. Routing him to psychiatry first uses the chart history to explain away a presentation that has not been examined. Two hours of observation delays time critical investigation for a potentially fatal condition. Adherence is worth knowing but is not the question when these three symptoms appear together.
  35. The correct answer is B (Collect client reported experience of the encounter, disaggregated by identity group): cultural safety is judged by the person receiving care, so the only valid measure asks clients about their experience, and disaggregating it reveals which groups are being failed. Training completion and handout counts measure activity by the organization rather than any effect on clients. Chart audits capture what clinicians chose to write, which is the provider's account again. Staff confidence is a particularly poor proxy, since the clinicians most certain of their own safety are often the least likely to examine it.
  36. The correct answer is D (A parallel initiative exists that applies a child first approach for Inuit children): a separate child first initiative covers Inuit children, so the family should not be told that no route exists. Advising them to use standard provincial processes would reproduce the jurisdictional delay the child first approach was created to prevent. Eligibility is not restricted by territory of residence. Provincial disability programs vary widely and none of them guarantee the immediate provision that a child first approach requires.
  37. The correct answer is A (The last federally supported school closed in 1996, within many clients' lifetimes): the system is recent enough that survivors are current patients, which makes the history clinically present rather than academic. Attributing inequities to genetics relocates the cause of colonization into Indigenous bodies and is the error the domain exists to correct. The settlement agreement and the formal apology did not end effects on parenting, language, community and trust. The intergenerational transmission of those effects means the harm reaches children and grandchildren who never attended.
  38. The correct answer is B (Would the client agree with my description of what happened in that visit?): it is the only question that tests the encounter against the client's experience, which is where the judgment of cultural safety sits. Guideline concordance evaluates the clinical content and can be perfect in an encounter the client found disempowering. Reviewing your own words for disrespect keeps the assessment inside your intent, which is not the measure. Training completion certifies attendance and says nothing about this visit.
Case C075

You work at a primary care clinic that offers every client a fifteen minute appointment, a printed handout in English, and online booking.

Your panel includes a woman who reads at a grade four level, speaks neither official language fluently, and travels by two buses. She has missed three appointments and her diabetes control has worsened. Clinic policy discharges clients after three missed appointments.

  1. The correct answer is B (The offering delivers equality, which produces a worse outcome for this client): giving everyone the same appointment length, handout and booking channel is equality, and equity means giving people what they need to reach a comparable outcome. Identical inputs are exactly what generates the gap when clients arrive with different literacy, language and transport. Interpretation available on request still places the burden of asking on the person least able to navigate the system. Translating the handout addresses one barrier of four and leaves the appointment length, the booking channel and the transport problem untouched.
  2. The correct answer is D (Treat the missed appointments as a system failure and arrange outreach contact): outreach rather than discharge is the equity response when the barriers are transport, language and literacy rather than choice. Applying the rule uniformly filters out the client who needs care most, which is how a neutral sounding policy produces an unequal effect. Granting a conditional exemption keeps the same rule and postpones the discharge to the next bus that runs late. Telling her to solve the transport problem herself assigns her a task the clinic created and she cannot fix.
  3. The correct answer is A (Longer appointments, funded interpretation, teach back, and telephone booking kept available): each element gives her something different from the standard offering so that she can reach the same outcome as other clients. Email reminders and a portal tutorial assume internet access and literacy she does not have, so they deepen the same barrier. More frequent visits multiply the two bus journey that is already causing the missed appointments. Signing a responsibilities agreement records the problem as hers and changes nothing about the conditions producing it.
  4. The correct answer is C (Bring aggregate data to the clinic and propose revising the discharge policy): nineteen discharges concentrated in one group is a policy problem, and organizational advocacy backed by aggregate data is realistic entry level work. Case by case exceptions rescue individuals while leaving the rule to discharge the next twenty. Referring clients elsewhere exports the barrier to another clinic and assumes an alternative exists. Requesting a new site is a large capital ask that does not address the rule actually removing people from care.
Case C076

A 22 year old trans man attends your practice to establish care.

His health card still shows his legal name and a female sex marker. He asks about starting testosterone and says a previous clinician required two psychiatric assessments before agreeing to discuss it. He has not had any surgery. He mentions that he avoided health care entirely for two years after that experience.

  1. The correct answer is C (Record both chosen and legal name so requisitions do not out him): recording both allows the chosen name to be used in speech, in the chart and in referrals while still permitting laboratory and billing matching. Using the legal name alone guarantees a repeat of the pharmacy counter experience that drove him away from care. Using the chosen name alone risks results being mismatched or rejected, which is a clinical safety problem. Making documentation conditional on a legal name change imposes a slow and costly process as the price of respectful care.
  2. The correct answer is A (An informed consent model, discussing benefits, risks and monitoring with him): informed consent rather than gatekeeping is the prevailing direction of practice, and hormone therapy sits within nurse practitioner scope in most Canadian jurisdictions. Requiring even one psychiatric clearance for a client with no untreated mental health condition treats his gender as a condition needing your approval. Referring him to a distant clinic before any conversation adds a long wait and repeats the message that you are not the person who can help. Mandating a documented social transition period imposes a milestone he did not choose and no clinical finding supports.
  3. The correct answer is D (Screening follows the organs he currently has, regardless of gender marker): he has not had surgery, so cervical screening remains indicated on the same schedule as for any other client with a cervix. Using the health card marker would drive screening off an administrative field that reflects paperwork rather than anatomy. Deferring screening while hormones are established creates a gap with no clinical justification. Screening by affirmed gender alone produces the error the previous clinic made and would miss a preventable cancer.
  4. The correct answer is B (Treat avoidance after mistreatment as a health risk requiring active outreach): avoidance of care following prior mistreatment is common among trans clients and is itself a documented health risk, as the untreated fracture demonstrates. Labelling it non adherence attributes a system failure to him and puts the same word in his chart that will shape the next clinician's reading. Defending the previous clinician's gatekeeping tells him his experience was acceptable and risks losing him again. Requiring a complaint as a precondition adds a task before care that he did not ask for.
Case C077

A 34 year old refugee claimant who arrived in Canada four months ago attends your clinic with her two children.

She is not yet eligible for provincial health insurance. She needs prenatal care, immunization catch up for the children, and a prescription she cannot afford. The receptionist tells her the clinic does not bill any federal program. She speaks Dari.

  1. The correct answer is A (The Interim Federal Health Program provides temporary coverage for refugee claimants): the program provides limited, temporary coverage to protected persons including resettled refugees and claimants not yet eligible for provincial or territorial insurance. Provincial insurance is exactly what she does not yet have, which is the gap the federal program exists to fill. Waiting for a final determination would leave a pregnant woman and two children without care for months. The coverage extends well beyond screening and emergencies, and depending on category includes supplemental and prescription drug benefits.
  2. The correct answer is C (Arrange professional interpretation in Dari before conducting the clinical assessment): professional interpretation is the standard, and a child must never be used, least of all for questions about pregnancy loss and violence. Placing an eleven year old in that role exposes him to his mother's trauma and makes honest answers impossible for her. Machine translation on a phone is unreliable for clinical detail and offers no confidentiality or accountability. Rescheduling delays prenatal care and immunizations while making access depend on a volunteer's availability.
  3. The correct answer is B (Register the clinic to bill the program and see her today regardless): the fact that not every provider bills the program is itself a barrier, and removing it is within the clinic's control. Prenatal care at 22 weeks is needed now, so the visit should not wait for administrative steps that take days. Sending her to find another clinic assumes she can identify one, reach it and communicate there. Charging her privately asks a claimant with no income to fund care she is already entitled to receive.
  4. The correct answer is D (Change clinic policy to bill the program and train staff on eligibility): seven families refused by the same script is a process failure at the front desk, and fixing the policy and the training stops it for everyone who comes next. Keeping a personal referral list depends on you being present and helps only the families who reach you. Telling claimants to apply sooner does not change eligibility rules and leaves the refusals intact. Asking the province to shorten its waiting period is legitimate policy advocacy but is slow and does not address the barrier your own clinic is creating.
Case C078

You are the only nurse practitioner in a community of 700.

Your neighbour, who repairs your truck, books an appointment for chronic back pain and asks for a refill of the opioid another prescriber started. Your sister in law works as your clinic's receptionist and has access to the schedule. The nearest alternate provider is 180 kilometres away.

  1. The correct answer is D (Manage the overlap openly and hold boundaries, since avoidance is impossible here): in a community of 700 the standard advice to avoid dual relationships cannot be followed, so the obligation shifts from avoidance to active management. That means naming the overlap out loud, keeping clinical information strictly inside the clinical setting and documenting to a higher standard. Sending him 180 kilometres for routine care denies him access for a problem you can safely manage. Proceeding silently leaves the risk unexamined, and disclosure on its own informs him without removing or controlling anything.
  2. The correct answer is A (Decline and arrange another prescriber, since objectivity cannot survive here): managing dual relationships in a small community still includes declining the encounters where independent judgment cannot be maintained, and prescribing a controlled substance to a close family member is the clearest example. Distance is a real barrier but does not convert an unmanageable conflict into a manageable one. Limiting the quantity and justifying it in writing is the same act with a better paper trail. Retrospective review by a colleague and transparent documentation both leave the prescribing decision in the hands of the person who cannot be objective about it.
  3. The correct answer is C (Tell affected clients about the overlap and offer an alternative booking route): naming the overlap and providing a real alternative both manages the conflict and returns some control to the clients who raised it. The signed agreement addresses her duty but does nothing about clients already avoiding care because of a perceived risk. Personal text booking creates an unsecured, undocumented channel and a second problem. Doing nothing because she is unregulated ignores that the barrier is the client's perception, and perceived conflicts damage trust on their own.
  4. The correct answer is B (Document to a higher standard, since the record proves the care was ordinary): where overlap is unavoidable, the chart is the evidence that the assessment, reasoning and prescribing were no different from any other client's. Shorter notes for people you know are exactly backwards, because familiarity is what makes shortcuts likely and unverifiable. Keeping a parallel personal file fragments the health record and breaches record keeping standards. Countersignature by a colleague is impractical where no colleague exists and does not substitute for a complete note in any case.
  5. The correct answer is A (Allocate funding according to each school's level of documented need): equity means giving different people different amounts so that outcomes become comparable, which is exactly what differential allocation does. Identical per student funding is the definition of equality and is what produced the shortfall. Monthly spending reports generate information without moving a single dollar to where it is needed. Uniform smaller portions spread the shortage to every school, and asking the poorest families to top it up shifts the cost onto those least able to carry it.
  6. The correct answer is D (Address the housing and medication cost barriers through referral and advocacy): the social determinants of health drive far more variation in outcomes than clinical care does, and mould exposure with unaffordable medication explains this presentation better than the regimen. Escalating therapy adds cost to a client who already cannot pay for what she has been prescribed. Written action plans and further technique review repeat interventions that have already been done correctly. Monthly visits monitor a deterioration that is being produced outside the clinic and cost her time she does not have.
  7. The correct answer is C (Organizational advocacy, bringing aggregate data forward to change the process): fifteen identical letters with a uniform six week delay means the process is the problem, and the delay originates inside your own clinic where it can be fixed. Continuing to appeal case by case solves each situation while leaving the cause intact for the sixteenth client. Waiting for a formulary review targets a body that is approving the requests anyway. Switching to a covered alternative subordinates the clinical decision to an administrative inconvenience.
  8. The correct answer is A (Decline to speak for them and support community members speaking themselves): advocacy is done with communities rather than over them, and speaking for a group that did not ask you to substitutes your voice for theirs. You can still contribute by supplying clinical context the community asks you to supply and by helping members reach the journalist. Handing over attendance data would breach privacy and expose the clients you intend to help. Refusing all engagement misreads advocacy as outside scope when coalition work with affected communities is explicitly part of the role.
  9. The correct answer is B (Respect her decision, document it, and offer options she can use later): advocacy means asking what the client wants advocated for and respecting a decision not to escalate, particularly when she carries the consequences. An anonymous complaint about a single unit is easily traced back to her and overrides her explicit request. Contacting the landlord as her clinician identifies her immediately and risks the eviction she fears. There is no duty to report a housing defect that overrides her wishes, so telling her otherwise would be inaccurate and coercive.
  10. The correct answer is C (The rule follows a funding decision that persists because the population lacks influence): the clinical barrier sits on top of a budget choice, and that choice survives because the people affected carry little political weight. Recognizing which lever actually holds the rule in place is what makes advocacy effective rather than merely sincere. Formulary decisions consider cost and budget impact alongside guidelines, so guideline change alone does not move them. Federal regulatory approval concerns safety and efficacy, which is a separate question from whether a province will pay, and generic entry lowers price without guaranteeing that the listing criteria change.
  11. The correct answer is D (Arrange daily dispensing, drop in access, and a plan that fits shelter timing): practical equity for clients experiencing homelessness means dispensing that accounts for storage and theft, access that does not depend on a booked slot, and a schedule built around competing survival priorities. Substituting a single dose regimen that does not treat the infection sacrifices the clinical goal, and discharging him from follow up removes the safety net. Handing over the full course repeats the loss that has already happened more than once. Admission is disproportionate, expensive and removes his shelter bed entirely.
  12. The correct answer is B (Provide the supplies and naloxone, keeping treatment available without preconditions): harm reduction is the standard of care in Canada, and abstinence is neither a precondition for service nor a realistic universal goal. Withholding supplies to manufacture motivation increases the risk of infection and fatal overdose while damaging the relationship that might later support treatment. Attaching a counselling requirement makes life saving supplies conditional on compliance. Deferring to another service delays access today and assumes he will attend a clinic he has not asked for.
  13. The correct answer is A (Speak directly to the client and arrange sign language interpretation for the visit): the client is the person you are speaking to, and professional interpretation gives her direct access to the conversation rather than a filtered version of it. Allowing the sister to answer removes the client from her own appointment and is the attitudinal barrier that clients with disabilities describe most often. Written exchange assumes a level of written English that many deaf signers do not use and slows clinical dialogue. Excluding the sister without asking substitutes your judgment for the client's about who she wants present.
  14. The correct answer is D (A plan she cannot reach is not a plan, so alternatives must be explored): distance, weather, thin services and caregiving obligations determine what is actually possible, so feasibility has to shape the regimen rather than be discovered after it fails. The trial evidence describes what works when the drug is received, which tells you nothing about a schedule she cannot attend. Travel funding addresses cost but not weather, childcare or the days lost to each trip. Monitoring adherence documents a failure the plan itself created.
  15. The correct answer is C (Language of service is a safety issue and a recognized right, not a courtesy): official language minority communities have recognized language of service rights, guaranteed constitutionally in New Brunswick and set out in French language services legislation covering designated areas and agencies in Ontario and several other provinces, and misunderstood insulin instructions cause real harm. Treating the request as impractical because the community is small is precisely how minority language populations lose access. Relying on a friend introduces error and removes confidentiality from a discussion of her health. Referring her to another city transfers the clinic's obligation onto a client who then bears the travel.
  16. The correct answer is C (Each provision filters out the clients who most need access to care): a discharge rule penalizes unstable transport and housing, a permanent address and photo identification requirement excludes people without either, portal only booking excludes those without internet, and an attend alone rule excludes clients who need an interpreter or supporter. Uniform requirements are the definition of equality, and applying them to this population is what produces the unequal effect. Revising only the missed appointment rule leaves three other filters operating. Explaining a barrier at registration informs the client without removing anything standing in her way.
  17. The correct answer is B (Evidence shows small gifts shift prescribing while recipients believe otherwise): the effect of even modest industry hospitality on prescribing is robustly documented, and clinicians reliably believe themselves to be the exception. Balanced looking evidence delivered by a party with a financial interest is still selected by that party. Disclosure informs clients of a conflict but leaves it operating, so it is not management. Nothing about the effect is limited to physicians, since it applies to every prescriber the representative visits.
  18. The correct answer is A (Offer a genuine list of alternatives and disclose your financial interest): self referral converts a clinical recommendation into a sales transaction, and offering real alternatives alongside disclosure removes the steering while keeping her informed. Referring to your own clinic and simply recording the ownership discloses the conflict without managing it, which leaves the responsibility with the person holding the least power. Confidence in your own service is exactly the reasoning a financial interest produces. Referring elsewhere silently avoids the sale but withholds information she needs and does not address the pattern for the next client.
  19. The correct answer is D (Disclosure informs but leaves the conflict operating and shifts the burden): telling a client about a competing interest does nothing to remove it, and it transfers responsibility for the decision to the person with the least power in the room. Management means recusing yourself, offering genuine alternatives, arranging independent oversight or declining the benefit, always in addition to disclosing rather than instead of it. Speaking fees are a recognized industry relationship and are not exempt from the obligation. No monetary threshold defines the duty, because the standard is whether a competing interest could reasonably be seen to influence judgment.
  20. The correct answer is B (Recommend the standard therapy and have another clinician discuss the trial): acting in the client's best interest means recommending what suits her, and separating the recruitment conversation from your financial interest removes the conflict rather than announcing it.
