Enrolling her with a disclosure attached leaves the payment influencing the recommendation she relies on. Framing monitoring as the benefit rationalizes a choice made for the wrong reason when a better treatment exists. Refusing all industry funded research overcorrects and would deprive future clients of access to trials that are properly managed.
  21. The correct answer is A (Provide the time the client needs and raise the target's effect with management): institutional throughput targets can conflict directly with a client's needs, and the obligation is to act in the client's interest while naming the systemic pressure so the pattern gets addressed. Splitting the encounter into several short visits multiplies the travel and interpretation cost for a client already facing barriers. Written material assumes literacy she does not have and substitutes paper for a conversation. Recruiting a family member to speed things along is not interpretation and puts a relative in a clinical role.
  22. The correct answer is C (Work through your professional association's submission to the regulatory consultation): individual nurse practitioners rarely change rules alone, and organized professional advocacy through consultations and briefs is how scope has actually expanded in this country. Your year of documented cases is most powerful as evidence inside that submission. Writing to the premier bypasses the body that is actively deciding the question. Public campaigning may be constrained by employment terms and is unlikely to move a technical consultation, while prescribing outside the restriction risks your registration and helps no one for long.
  23. The correct answer is B (Appeal the denial, citing the provincial rule that authorizes your signature): individual advocacy means appealing a denial that is simply wrong on the rules, and quoting the authority is the fastest route to reversal. Having a physician re sign concedes an error that was not yours and reinforces a false requirement for every client who follows. Waiting six months to reapply leaves the client without income while the same mistake waits to be repeated. Legal clinics may help later but are unnecessary when a straightforward appeal from you resolves it.
  24. The correct answer is D (Document the refusal and its clinical consequences so the pattern becomes visible): documenting a refusal of care makes it countable, and aggregated refusals are the evidence that changes an eligibility rule. She has consented to this and it also protects her by recording why treatment did not start. Omitting the refusal to keep the chart neutral erases the only record that the barrier exists. Writing a personal letter of disagreement carries no data and is easily set aside, and sending her to another region assumes she can travel while leaving the local rule untouched.
  25. The correct answer is A (Correct yourself briefly and carry on with the clinical conversation): a brief self correction acknowledges the error without making the client responsible for managing your discomfort. An extended apology, particularly one blaming the chart, turns the moment into the client's job to resolve and takes time from a worrying new symptom. Saying nothing signals either that you did not notice or that it does not matter. Asking the client to correct you each time transfers the work to the person already carrying it.
  26. The correct answer is C (Disclosure to staff without a clinical need is a documented barrier to care): unnecessary disclosure is one of the recognized barriers trans clients face, and it drives the avoidance of care that follows mistreatment. Twelve staff hearing details irrelevant to their work exposes her to further intrusive questions and unsafe treatment on the unit. Breaching the handover format describes a procedure problem rather than the harm to the client. Accuracy and time management are real concerns but neither carries the consequence that unnecessary disclosure does.
  27. The correct answer is D (Entries 2 and 4, because each removes or independently checks the influence): recusing yourself from a purchasing decision removes the conflict from the decision, and independent monthly review of referrals places an external check on judgment that could be swayed. The imaging referral and the supplement sales are recorded as disclosure only, which informs clients while leaving the self referral fully operating. Recording a conflict makes it visible but visibility is not management. Recusal is one valid method among several, which include offering genuine alternatives, arranging oversight and declining the benefit.
  28. The correct answer is B (A neutral sounding change produced unequal effects on those with least access): a uniform rule applied to people with different access to devices, connectivity and stable addresses concentrates the harm on the groups the data identify. Identical content delivered through a channel some clients cannot open is not identical care. Portal training assumes the barrier is skill rather than lacking a phone, data or a place to charge it. Severity is a general driver of readmission but cannot explain a rise confined to two specific groups beginning when the policy changed.
  29. The correct answer is B (Decline and help her access one of the providers within 90 kilometres): a dual relationship with an employee you supervise and evaluate is avoidable here, and where avoidance is possible it is preferred over management. Her ability to decline your advice or disclose sensitive information is compromised by the employment relationship regardless of good intentions. Restricting chart access protects privacy but does nothing about the power imbalance in the room. Periodic colleague review and an episodic care arrangement both keep the conflict in place while adding partial safeguards.
  30. The correct answer is A (A conflict exists when a competing interest could reasonably be seen to influence judgment): the test is reasonable perception and potential influence, not proof that a decision was altered. Perceived conflicts damage trust on their own, which is why they must be identified and managed rather than argued away. Requiring measurable behaviour change sets a standard almost nothing could ever meet and would leave every relationship unmanaged. Monetary thresholds and the supplier's stated expectations describe policy details and intentions, neither of which defines when the duty is triggered.
  31. The correct answer is C (Ask what she wants advocated for and how she wishes to participate): advocacy is done with clients rather than over them, and she may want to speak for herself, to stay anonymous, or not to escalate at all. Photographing the ramp is useful evidence but gathering it before asking assumes a course of action she has not chosen. An anonymous report decides on her behalf that she should be invisible. Going to a municipal office first skips the parties who can fix the ramp this week and removes her from a process about her own access.
  32. The correct answer is D (Depending on category, the federal program includes supplemental and prescription drug coverage): the Interim Federal Health Program covers basic health care and, depending on the person's category, supplemental and prescription drug benefits as well. Telling her to pay privately would impose a cost she does not have to bear for a benefit she already holds. The program exists precisely to bridge the provincial waiting period, so it does not begin after that period ends. Its scope is far wider than emergencies, screening or immunizations alone.
  33. The correct answer is A (It identifies a determinant that shapes outcomes more than clinical care does): income and its distribution sit among the strongest social determinants of health, and knowing about material hardship changes what you prescribe, how often you ask the client to travel and which supports you arrange. Framing the question as an accreditation or billing exercise treats the answer as paperwork rather than clinical information. Using it to lower expectations of a client converts a system level finding into a judgment about the person. The purpose is to change the plan, not to explain away a poor outcome.
  34. The correct answer is B (Accept it graciously, record it in the clinic's gift register, and change nothing clinically): a modest gift of cultural significance can be accepted where refusal would cause harm to the relationship, provided it is transparent and alters nothing about her care. Refusing outright misreads the standard and treats a culturally meaningful act as a bribe. Accepting it without any record removes the transparency that makes acceptance defensible. Offering priority booking in return converts a gift into an exchange and disadvantages every other client.
  35. The correct answer is C (Decline to discuss any client, explaining that this applies to everyone): keeping clinical information strictly inside the clinical setting is the core management strategy where dual relationships cannot be avoided, and stating the rule plainly protects every future client rather than only this one. Confirming what is supposedly already public still verifies information as a clinician and expands what the town believes it knows. Offering reassurance about progress discloses clinical status. Deflecting with a question avoids the disclosure but leaves the neighbour expecting an answer next time.
  36. The correct answer is A (Add fields for chosen name, pronouns, and gender identity separate from sex): intake forms that capture chosen name and pronouns are a concrete element of an inclusive practice environment, and separating gender identity from sex assigned at birth supports correct screening. Verbal confirmation at reception asks a client to declare her identity within earshot of a waiting room. Removing gender fields entirely erases information needed for organ based screening and for addressing the client correctly. One catch all category groups everyone outside two options into an afterthought and still fails to capture pronouns.
  37. The correct answer is D (Convene affected clients and local partners to seek a pharmacy access solution): a community wide loss of pharmacy access is a systemic problem, and working with the affected community and local partners is how the lever actually gets pulled. Six months of case by case improvisation has already shown it does not scale and depends entirely on you. Directing clients to online pharmacies assumes internet access, payment cards and a delivery route that does not exist. Withholding effective medications makes the clinical plan absorb a distribution failure.
  38. The correct answer is C (Whose interest does this serve, and would my clients agree with my account?): asking whose interest an action serves and whether the client would recognize your description of it is the closing test that runs through the whole domain. Asking whether the company would still want you if you prescribed elsewhere is a useful probe but stops at the company's motive rather than your clients' interests. Absence of payment does not remove a conflict, since profile, access and reciprocity all influence judgment. An employer policy sets a floor for declaration and cannot answer whether accepting is right.
